# The Long Way Home
*a novel*

---

## PART ONE — Arrival & Survival

---

# Chapter 1: Waking

The first thing I got wrong was thinking I'd fallen asleep on the job.

I came up out of nothing the way you do after a long shift, with my mouth dry and my neck wrong and the certainty that I'd missed something. My cheek was against the ground. The ground was cold and it had texture, little broken stalks pressing dotted lines into my skin, and I lay there working out, in a slow stupid way, that you do not fall asleep face down in a field. I had been in the stockroom. I had been counting deck screws into the little paper sacks because the scale at register three had been throwing tantrums all week, and I remember the buzz of the long fluorescent tube that flickered above the fastener aisle, the one that Marcus kept saying he'd replace and never did. That tube. The hum of it. I held onto the hum for a second because it was the last real thing I had, and then it was gone and there was only this.

I sat up.

My phone was in my hand. I was gripping it hard enough that my fingers had gone stiff, and the first thing I did, before I even looked around, was look at the screen, because that is what your hand does. Six fourteen. The battery was a fat green bar. Ninety-eight percent. No little dot of signal at the top left, just the empty bars and a small gray word where the carrier name should be. I remember thinking, in the flat dumb way you think before the fear arrives, that I must be way out somewhere. That somebody had driven me somewhere as a joke.

There were no roads.

I want to be careful here, because later on people would ask me about this morning and I would lie about how clear it was, how I knew right away. I did not know right away. What I knew was that the field went on. It was a harvested field, the wheat or whatever it was cut down to a stubble that came up maybe to my ankle, dry and pale gold, going on in rows to a line of trees a long way off. The rows were not straight the way our fields back home are straight. They wandered. And past the trees there were low hills the color of nothing in particular, brown going green, and over all of it the sky was doing that thing where it is the kind of blue that hurts to look at near the bottom and still gray-purple up at the top, the sun not up yet but close, lighting the edge of everything.

I stood. My knees were stiff. I had my work clothes on, all of them, which made no sense and at the same time was the only thing that did, because they were mine and they were familiar and I could touch them. Jeans. My gray T-shirt with the little hole at the collar. My work boots, the steel-toes, the brown leather ones I'd bought myself with the first paycheck so my feet would stop dying on the concrete floor, and I was even wearing the cheap nylon belt I hated. My keys were in my pocket. House key, the spare to my mom's Corolla, the brass one to the store's back door that I wasn't even supposed to have a copy of. They jingled. Such a small, ordinary, idiotic sound.

"Okay," I said out loud, to the field. My voice came out cracked. "Okay."

It smelled wrong. I think that was the first thing my body understood before my head did, because I had been standing there a while turning in a slow circle and trying to find a fence or a power line or the back of a barn, and underneath the dry-grass smell and the dirt there was something thick and low and animal, woodsmoke and something rotten and sweet at once, like a dumpster behind a restaurant in August, only spread out thin over everything. I had never smelled air with that in it. The air at home had a smell, sure, exhaust and cut grass and whatever, but it had a kind of nothing under it, a clean nothing, the smell of a planet with the windows open. This air was full. There was no clean nothing anywhere in it.

I told myself: you fainted. You hit your head. You are confused. I had read somewhere that a concussion can scramble you, can lose you a chunk of time, and I held onto that, I really did. I pressed my fingers around the back of my skull looking for a sore spot, a lump, blood, anything, and there was nothing. My head didn't even hurt. I was just standing in a field that went on forever, in my boots, at six fourteen in the morning, and the sun had not come up, and there were no roads, and the air was full.

Birds started.

That was the thing that made my chest go tight, and I couldn't have told you why at the time. Birds are birds. But these were not the birds from home. I don't mean I'm some birdwatcher who can tell a sparrow from a finch. I mean the sound was wrong in a way I felt in my teeth, too many of them, too loud, a racket coming up out of the trees all at once like somebody had thrown a switch, and not one of them was the flat two-note thing the mourning doves do on our gutter. I stood very still and listened to a thousand birds I did not know and I thought, with great calm, the way you think a thing right before you lose it: I am not where I was.

I started walking because standing was worse.

I picked the direction where the smoke smell was strongest, because smoke meant people and people meant a phone that worked or a road or a cop or my mom, somebody, anybody. The walking helped. I'm okay at staying calm if I have something to do with my hands or my feet, and I made myself a job: count steps, watch the ground, look for anything made by a person. The stubble crunched. After a while the field gave out into a strip of weeds and then a path, and the path was real, packed dirt with a rut worn down the middle and dried mud humped up on either side, the kind of path feet make, not machines. No tire tracks. I looked for tire tracks for a long time. There were ruts but they were the wrong width and there were no treads in them, just the smooth scoop a wheel makes, and beside them, pressed into the old mud, hoofprints. A lot of hoofprints.

I checked the phone again. Six twenty-six. I don't know what I expected. The signal was still the empty bars. I opened the dialer with my thumb shaking and I hit nine, one, one, and the green button, and it sat there a second saying it was calling and then it just stopped, the screen going back to the keypad with a little gray line of text I didn't read. No ringing. No operator. Nothing. I did it again. Same. I told myself the tower was far, that I'd walk to higher ground, and I put the phone away because looking at it was making it worse.

The settlement came up on me slow.

First it was one building off to the side of the path, and I almost laughed, because it was so clearly a fake. Mud walls, actual mud, dried tan and cracked, and a roof of red clay tiles that didn't sit flat, and no windows, just a low doorway with a cloth hung in it. A movie set. That was the word my brain reached for and grabbed and held with both hands. A movie set. They build whole towns and leave them out in the desert. I'd seen a video about it. Somebody was filming something and I had wandered onto the back lot, that was all, and any second a guy in a vest with a headset was going to come yell at me for being where I wasn't supposed to be, and I would have given anything in the world to be yelled at right then.

Then there were more buildings, and a smell of animals so strong it stung, and the cloth in a doorway moved and a woman looked out at me.

She was old, or she looked old, with a brown cloth over her hair and a long dress the color of dirt, and her face when she saw me did a thing I have never had a face do to me before. She did not look annoyed, the way you'd look at a kid who wandered onto the set. She went still, and then she pulled the cloth across the door so fast it snapped, and she was gone, and I heard her saying something behind it, low and quick, words I didn't catch and wouldn't have understood if I had.

"Hey," I said to the closed door. "Sorry. Hey. Do you have a phone?"

Nothing.

I kept walking into the place because what else was there. It was bigger than it had looked, a cluster of these mud and rough-stone buildings strung along the widening path, a couple of them with a second story, smoke coming out of holes in some of the roofs, and there were people now, and the people were the part I couldn't make a story out of. A man leading a donkey loaded with bundles. Two kids, filthy, barefoot, who stopped dead and stared at my feet. A man squatting by a wall doing something with a knife and a piece of leather, and when I came near he stood up and stepped back and held the knife where I could see it, not pointing it at me, just letting me know it was there. They were all in the same kind of clothes. Long shirts to the knee, belted, wool I think, dyed in colors that were really just shades of the dirt, brown and rust and grayish white, and sandals or bare feet, and not one zipper, not one logo, not one stitch of anything I recognized as having been made in a factory, and I work retail, I have refolded ten thousand T-shirts, I know what machine-made cloth looks like and this was not it. It was thick and uneven and it hung wrong.

They looked at my jeans and they looked at my boots and they looked at the gray shirt, and they did the woman's thing, the going-still, the stepping-back. A man said something to me. It had the shape of a question, sharp at the end, and then a word he said twice, hard, and the rest of it was just sound, fast and full of edges, nothing in it I could grab. Not Spanish. We get a lot of Spanish at the store and I can fake my way through a little and this was not it, not French from two years of it in school, not anything. I held up my hands the way you do, palms out, I come in peace, and I said, "I don't, I'm sorry, I don't understand, is there, do you have a phone? A telephone? Police?"

His face didn't change. Whatever I was making with my mouth was just more noise to him, the same as his was to me, and I felt the floor of it open up a little right then, the realness of it, that I could stand a foot from another human being and we were as cut off from each other as two animals.

I tried again, slower, louder, which is the stupidest thing you can do and I knew it even while I did it. "Help. I need help." I pointed at myself, at the ground, I mimed holding a phone to my ear. He took another step back and put his hand on a kid's shoulder and moved the kid behind him.

There was a thing painted on the wall above a doorway, a long word in letters, and I went to it because letters meant I could maybe at least figure out where I was, read a town name, anything. The letters were ones I knew. That was the worst part. They were our letters, an R and an A and what I thought was an N, capital, scratched on red, and I stood there and read them left to right and they did not make a word. They were letters and they were arranged like a word and they meant nothing, and I read them four or five times like the meaning would turn on if I just kept hitting the switch, and it didn't, and a feeling came up the back of my neck like cold water.

I needed to get higher. That was the plan, the only plan, get up high, find the signal, find the highway, see the city. The path ran on toward where the ground rose and I went that way fast, not quite running, past more of the still faces, past a pen of actual pigs, the pig smell hitting me like a wall, past a cart with wheels of solid wood and a wooden axle, no metal on it anywhere that I could see, and I got to the top of the little rise where the buildings thinned out and the land fell away in front of me into a long shallow valley, and I stopped.

There was a city.

It was a long way off, sitting in the haze at the bottom of the valley and up over the next hills, and it was big, bigger than it had any right to be out here in the middle of all this dirt and these mud huts, a smear of pale stone going on and on with smoke standing up over it in a hundred thin gray lines, and I felt a flood of relief so strong it almost put me on the ground, because a city meant everything, a city meant streets and signs and a hospital and a working phone, and I started down the path toward it and I was already half composing the thing I'd say to the first cop I found.

And then I saw the building on the near edge of it.

I want to be honest about what happened in my head because for a while nothing happened. I looked at it the way you look at anything. It was round. It was huge, even from here, a pale ring of stone sitting a little apart from the rest of the city, arches stacked in rows up its side, and it was clean, it was bright, the morning sun was just catching the top of it and the stone was so pale it was almost white and it had no cracks in it, no holes, no green stains, no scaffolding, it was whole and it was new and it was the most enormous thing made by people that I had ever seen with my own eyes. I had seen a picture of a building like that. I had seen a thousand pictures of a building like that, in a textbook, on a screen, on the side of a pizza box one time. Everybody has. And in every single one of them it was broken. It was a ruin, a ring of stumps, half of it fallen, the whole top gone, that's the only way it ever is, that's the only way it has been for longer than there has been a country I'm from.

And this one was whole.

I stood on the path with the wind moving the dry grass around my boots and I looked at the unbroken Colosseum and the bottom went out of everything.

I don't know how long I stood there. My knees did go, partway, and I ended up crouched on the path with my arms around them and my breath coming wrong, fast and shallow, the edges of my sight going sparkly the way they do when you stand up too quick except I was already down. I kept saying no. Just no, out loud, over and over, a little kid's word, the only one I had. Because there were explanations. There are always explanations. They built a new one. They built a fake one. It's a museum, a theme park, they reconstructed it, people do that, there's a fake Eiffel Tower in Las Vegas, this is fine, this is a thing people do. But the mud huts. But the cloth. But the letters that weren't words and the man who put his kid behind him and the pigs and the cart with the wooden axle and the air that was full and the birds I didn't know and the path with no tire tracks, none, not one, in the whole world that I could see, not a single straight line that a machine had made except the lines on the screen in my hand.

The phone.

I got it out. My hands were doing something I couldn't fully control, a fine fast shake, and I had to try twice to wake the screen. Six fifty-one. Ninety-eight percent, the green bar still fat and smug, the empty signal bars at the corner. I opened the camera because some animal part of me needed proof, needed to make the building be a real thing that a real machine had recorded, and I pointed it down the valley and there it was on the screen, the round white building, sitting in the little glass rectangle in my hand exactly the same as it sat in the world, and I took the picture and the fake shutter clicked and I had it now, I had a photo of the thing, and that did not help at all. It made it worse. The photo made it a fact.

I opened the maps. I knew it wouldn't work and I did it anyway. The little blue dot spun and spun looking for itself and the map underneath was just the gray grid it goes to when it has nothing, the rivers and roads it had bothered to save sitting there with no me on them, a memory of a world the phone could no longer find. Searching. Searching. I closed it. I opened the dialer and I called nine one one again, knowing, and it died the same as before, and I called my mom, my thumb finding her without me even looking because I have called my mom from that screen ten thousand times, and the screen said calling and then it said nothing and the keypad came back and I sat down hard in the dirt and made a sound I'm not proud of.

I'm good at math. I know that sounds like a strange thing to reach for sitting in the dirt outside a city that couldn't exist, but it's true, it's the one thing I've always had, and when everything else in me was screaming my brain went to numbers the way it always does, because numbers hold still. I started measuring. The building down there, I told myself, look at it, estimate it, you can estimate anything. Those arches, count them across the part you can see, the curve, if that's the curve of a circle then the diameter, the thing has to be six hundred feet across at least, more, and the height, four rows of arches, call it forty feet a row with the stuff on top, that's a hundred sixty, hundred eighty feet of stone, and you cannot fake that, you cannot build that for a movie, that is a real building of real stone holding up its own real weight and it is standing there finished and clean in the morning sun, and the only place in the entire history of the world where that building stands clean and finished and new is a place that stopped being two thousand years ago.

Two thousand years. The number sat in front of me. I could not make it mean anything. I tried to and it just sat there, too big, the way a number gets when it stops being a count of anything and turns into a wall.

The sun came up over the far hills while I was doing the math, full up, and the light went from gray to gold and the unbroken building threw a long blue shadow across the valley, and somewhere behind me in the settlement a dog started barking, and then another, and there were voices, men's voices, more than one, coming up the path the way I'd come, fast and getting louder, and the word I'd heard the man say twice, the hard one, I heard it again now, called out, an order in it.

I got up. My legs were jelly and I got up anyway and I turned around.

There were four of them coming up the rise toward me, men in the dirt-colored shirts but with more to them, leather over the chest on two of them, and they had spears. Real ones, long, the heads of them catching the new sun in four small wet points of light, and they came at a walk, spread out a little so they had me from more than one side, and the one in front had his arm out flat toward the others, holding them at a distance from me, watching my hands, watching my feet, watching the strange bright cloth of me like I was something that might go off.

I put the phone in my pocket. I put my hands up, palms out, the only word I had in any language they could possibly want.

---

# Chapter 2: The Cell

Nobody shot me, which is the bar I'd set for the morning and I cleared it.

The one in front kept his arm out toward the others and came at me sideways, slow, the way you'd come at a dog you weren't sure about. I kept my hands up. I kept them so far up my shoulders ached. He said something short and I said nothing because I had nothing, and he said it again louder, like volume was the problem, like I was a tourist and he could find the English under there if he shouted, and I shook my head, which I think is close to universal, and his eyes went flat and careful in a way I did not like.

He reached out and took a fistful of my T-shirt at the shoulder. Not to hurt. To feel it. He rubbed the cotton between his thumb and two fingers and his face did a thing, a small stalling, and he looked back at one of the others and said a word and the other one came up and felt it too, and now there were two grown men with spears standing close enough that I could smell them, woodsmoke and sweat and something sour and animal under that, fingering the hem of my sleeve and talking over my head in fast low voices about my shirt. The shirt is from a three-pack. It cost about six dollars. I wanted to tell them that and there was no version of me that could.

They tied my hands.

Rope, not cuffs. I'd half braced for the cold bite of metal because that's what my brain had filed under "arrest," and instead it was rough fiber, scratchy, smelling of hay, wound twice around my wrists in front of me and knotted hard by the second man while the leader watched my face the whole time. The rope hurt more than cuffs would have. It pinched in a narrow line and there was no give in it. I remember thinking, stupidly, with the clean idiot clarity of a person in shock, that I knew a better knot. I'd learned a trucker's hitch off a video to tie a kayak to the Corolla. I knew a knot these men didn't and it was the single most useless fact in the entire valley.

Then they walked me down.

I have tried a lot of times since to put the town in order and I can't, because I didn't go through it in order, I went through it the way you go through a wave, all of it at once and over your head. The path turned to a rutted lane and the lane filled up with people the way water fills a low spot. The smell came first and never left: smoke and dung, the dung of more animals than I could name, and under it a thick rotten low note I'd later learn was the tannery and a sharp one I'd learn was the fish sauce they put on everything, and woodsmoke again, always woodsmoke, and bodies. Heat off the walls. The walls were close and the upper floors leaned in and there were faces in the dark doorways and faces above in the windows and they came out to look at me, all of them, a kid with his hands tied being walked through by armed men, and that was a normal enough thing that nobody was alarmed by it, they were just curious, and what they were curious about was me.

A woman pointed at my feet and said something to another woman and they both looked at my boots. A boy ran alongside us for a while at a careful distance, staring, until a man cuffed him out of the way. Somebody laughed. Somebody else, an old man sitting on a step shelling something into his lap, looked at me for a long flat second and then deliberately looked away and spat, and that one I felt in my stomach, because there was a judgment in it I couldn't read and couldn't argue with. I was the strange thing. I was the thing that was wrong in the picture. Every single person on that lane knew how the world worked and I was the only one who didn't, and they could all tell.

I kept my eyes down after the old man, mostly. The ground was its own education. Mud, straw, a dropped fish head, a dead thing flattened that had been a rat, a gutter running down the center of the lane that I do not want to describe and that I stepped over without anyone telling me to because some things you just know. My boots, my good steel-toes, were already the color of the lane to the ankle.

The building they put me in was stone at the base and brick above, and the cell was a room off a yard, low, with one window high up and barred, real bars, iron, and a door of heavy planks with iron bands. So they had iron. They had iron for the door and rope for me, and I spent a while sitting on the dirt floor working out what that meant and the answer was that I didn't rate iron, I rated rope, and that was either good news or it meant they hadn't decided what I was yet, and I went back and forth on which.

They left my hands tied. The leader stood in the doorway after the others had gone and looked at me for a while, and I looked back, and I tried, I really tried, I said "Daniel." I tapped my own chest with my bound hands as best I could and I said it slow. "Dan-iel." He watched my mouth. Then he said something, four or five words, none of which I had, and he waited, and I had to do the head shake again, and whatever patience had been in his face packed up and left. He pulled the door shut. A bar dropped on the outside, a heavy wooden clunk that I felt in the floor.

I sat in the half-dark and shook for a while. I'm not going to pretend I didn't.

When it passed, or when it leveled off into a background hum that I could think over, I did the thing I do, the only thing, I started counting. The window was maybe a foot square. The room was maybe eight feet by ten, pacing it heel to toe with my hands tied, which is a stupid-looking way to measure a cell. The light coming down the high window had moved a hand's width along the far wall in the time I'd been in here. I could time the day off that wall if I had to. I didn't have to.

I had a clock.

I'd been so careful not to think about it. The whole walk down I'd had it pressed against my thigh through the denim and I'd been aware of it the entire time the way you're aware of a sunburn, and now I was alone behind a barred door and I got it out of my pocket, which is harder than it sounds with your wrists lashed together, a whole contorted business of fishing two-handed.

It woke up. Of course it woke up. Ninety-eight percent.

I almost called my mom again. My thumb was already moving and I caught it, physically stopped my own hand, because I'd watched the call die twice already and I knew it would die a third time and I knew something else now that I hadn't really let myself know up on the hill, which was that the battery was the whole game. The battery was the only piece of the future I'd brought with me that still worked, and it was a finite thing, it was draining whether I used it or not, and every dumb panic call to a tower that wasn't going to be built for nineteen hundred years was a piece of it I'd never get back.

I turned the brightness all the way down. I turned off the radios, all of them, airplane mode, which was a joke, an actual joke that I made to myself in the cell, airplane mode in 98 AD, and there was no one to tell it to and I think that was the first time the real shape of it landed on me, not the fear, the alone. I had a funny thing and the funny thing died in my chest because there was nobody.

Then I opened Notes and I started writing, fast, because the funny thing dying had scared me worse than the spears had.

I wrote like the building was on fire. I didn't organize it. I told myself I'd organize it later and later never came the way I meant it to. I wrote down the date the phone thought it was, which was wrong, which was my world's date, and then I wrote ROME 98 AD?? with the question marks because the Colosseum math was the only date I trusted and I trusted it about eighty percent. I wrote the things I was afraid I'd forget, and what scared me was how fast that list got long and how shallow each thing on it was. I knew water boiled at 212 and I wrote it. I knew it boiled lower up a mountain and I could not for the life of me sitting in that cell remember the rule for how much lower, I could see the shape of the graph in my head and the number wouldn't come, and I wrote down can't remember which is its own kind of horror. I wrote pi to the digits I had, 3.14159, and stalled, and wrote 26 and wasn't sure about the 26. I wrote the quadratic formula and got the sign wrong and fixed it and wasn't sure I'd fixed it right. I wrote GUNPOWDER and then sulfur, charcoal, and the third one, the white one, and I knew there was a third one and I knew it was the important one and I sat there with my thumbs over the glass and could not produce it. I wrote ??? and moved on, furious, because I could feel the percent ticking even with the screen dimmed, even though it wasn't, not really, not in a single session, but in my head every second of light was a coin I was burning.

I wrote down names. My mom's number. Our address. The fact of Marcus and the flickering light over the fastener aisle, which I will never understand why I wrote, except that it was real and mine and nobody could take it if it was on the glass. I wrote down that the President was, and stopped, because who was I writing it for.

I was writing it for whoever found my body. That was the answer and I knew it as soon as I asked, and I had to put the phone face down on my knee for a minute.

Then I picked it back up, because there was one more thing.

I took a picture of my own face.

I don't know exactly why. I held the phone out at arm's length, both hands, the front camera, and there I was, this kid, hair stuck to my forehead, dirt on me, eyes too wide, the high barred window a gray smear behind my head, and I looked at myself looking at myself and I took the picture, and the fake shutter clicked in the stone room, and now there was a record that on this day a person named Daniel Mercer who knew the quadratic formula and could not remember saltpeter was alive and seventeen and scared in a cell outside Rome. Two photos on the phone now. The white building and my face. The proof and the proven.

I turned it off all the way, holding the button, watching it go black, and that took a real effort of will, like letting go of a hand.

Now the hard one.

Because they were going to want to know what I was. The leader had asked me already, four or five words, and somebody bigger than him was going to ask again, and at some point the asking was going to stop being polite, and I was going to have to be something. I lay on my back on the dirt with my tied hands on my chest and I worked the problem the way I'd work any problem, which is to say badly and in circles, but I worked it.

Option one. The truth. I'm from the future. I come from two thousand years from now and I know how your story ends and I can read and I can do things you can't. I lay there and I ran it forward like a chess line and every branch ended badly. There was no way to prove it. Not one thing on me proved it. The phone proved I had a strange object, and a strange object that lights up and is never wrong about the hour and shows little pictures, in a world I already knew did augury off bird flight and read the future in animal guts, that did not say "scholar," that said something with a worse word attached, something you burn or stone or feed to the thing in the round building. The truth was unprovable and it was mad and it was the kind of mad that gets you killed by frightened people, and I was surrounded by people I had frightened just by walking down a lane. I crossed off the truth.

Option two. Lie. Be from somewhere. Somewhere far enough that no one could check, far enough to explain the clothes and the not-talking and the strangeness, somewhere that made me odd instead of damned. A traveler. Better, a scholar, because a scholar is a man with value and reasons, a man you keep and feed and ask things of, instead of a vagrant you throw away. From the edge of the world. From up north, way up, past wherever north ran out for these people, a cold far place at the end of the map where the clothes are fine and strange and the men know numbers. I didn't have the word Thule yet. I want to be honest, because the rules of this telling are honesty, I did not lie there in the dark and pick the perfect cover story like a spy. I lay there and got as far as I am from very far away and I am learned and I am worth keeping alive, and the rest of it, the name of the cold place and how to sell it, that came later and it came hard, with help, and it cost me. But the spine of it I decided on the dirt floor of that cell on the first day, which was this: never the future, never the truth, only the far country. A man from a far country can be ignorant of your language and your manners and still be a man. A man from the future is a horror or a god, and both of those die young.

I'd just gotten that far when the hunger arrived, and it arrived like a separate event, a fist closing slowly under my ribs, and I realized I had not eaten since the night before in a world that no longer existed. The hunger and the needing-to-pee and the thirst all showed up around the same time, the body keeping its own schedule, indifferent to the fact that everything else was over. There was a clay pot in the corner that I'd been ignoring and I understood, with great dignity, what the clay pot was for.

The light on the wall went orange and then gray and then it was just the square of the window, paler than the dark around it, and I sat against the cold stone with my knees up and my tied hands hanging off them and waited for nothing in particular, because waiting was the only verb I had left.

The bar on the door lifted near full dark.

A man came in carrying a small clay lamp and a bowl, and he was younger than the others, or harder-used in a different way, with a soldier's build and a soldier's economy of movement, and he had a strip of something dark, leather or braid, on his shoulder that the spear-carriers hadn't had, which I took, correctly as it turned out, to mean he ranked them. He set the lamp in a niche. He crouched down in front of me, balanced on the balls of his feet, and looked at me with a directness that wasn't kind exactly but wasn't cruel either, the look you give a horse you've been told to evaluate, and he held out the bowl.

It was warm. It was some kind of grain mush, gray, with the sour-sharp reek of the fish sauce cut into it, and it was, in that moment, the most beautiful object I have ever been handed. I went to take it and couldn't, my hands tied, and I made a fumbling useless grab and the bowl tipped and he caught it before it spilled, fast, no fuss, and he looked at my wrists, and then he looked at my face for a long moment, working something out.

He took a short knife off his belt. I went very still. He turned it and put the edge against the rope, not my skin, between my wrists, and sawed through it in two strokes, and the rope fell off and my hands came apart and the blood went back into them in a hot pins-and-needles rush that made me hiss.

He put the bowl in my hands. He stood up, picked up the lamp, and at the door he stopped and pointed at his own chest with two fingers and said a word, slow and clear, the way I'd tried to say my own name to the other one and failed. He said it again. Two syllables, the second one falling. Then he tipped his head at me, waiting, and I understood, and I tapped my chest with a hand that didn't fully work yet and said, "Daniel," and he tried it back, mangled, the middle of it gone soft and wrong, and something in his mouth almost moved toward a smile and didn't get there.

He took the lamp with him. The bar dropped. I sat in the dark with the warm bowl in both my freed hands and ate the worst food of my life with my fingers, fast, scraping the clay, and I licked the bowl after, and I was not ashamed.

---

# Chapter 3: The Map

They came for me in the gray part of morning, before the town had finished being asleep. Two of the spearmen this time, and the optio behind them with his hand resting on the doorframe like he owned it, which maybe he did. He looked at me the way you look at a dog you're not sure about. He'd fed me. That counted for something with me and probably nothing with him.

One of the spearmen made a short upward motion with his free hand. Get up. The other thing that was true about my situation, the thing I kept relearning every hour, was that I understood gestures fine and words not at all. A whole language was happening around me all the time, wet and fast and full of those hard k-sounds where I expected soft ones, and none of it stuck. It washed off. I'd spent the night listening to two men talk in the lane and I could not have told you whether they were arguing or joking.

I got up. My back had a knot in it from the dirt floor that I would have killed to crack. I thought, for one stupid second, about my bed at home, the way the mattress had a Daniel-shaped dent in it, and then I shut that door because there were better uses for the morning than crying in front of soldiers.

The phone was in my front jeans pocket, screen-in, the safest place I had. Eleven percent. I'd checked it in the dark and made myself put it away. Eleven percent of the only thing I owned.

They walked me through the town. By daylight it was smaller and uglier than the noises had made it sound, three lanes of leaning brick and timber with a gutter running down the middle of each one carrying things I didn't look at. People stopped to watch me go by. A woman with a baby on her hip pulled the baby's face into her shoulder. A man selling something out of clay pots said a word at me, sharp, and the men around him laughed, and I felt my ears go hot even though I had no idea what he'd said. That was the worst part, honestly. You can't even be properly insulted. You just stand there glowing while strangers enjoy themselves.

The building they took me to had a real door, painted, and two more spearmen outside it who came to a kind of attention when the optio appeared. Inside it was cooler and dim and smelled of lamp soot and old smoke and, under that, the same fish-rot smell that was apparently just the smell of the entire world now. There was a table. Behind the table sat a man.

He was maybe fifty, going soft in the jaw, in a tunic with a band of dull red at the hem, and he had the particular stillness of a person who is used to other people waiting on his mouth to open. A clerk sat to one side with a folding wooden frame across his knees, two leaves of it, the inner surfaces darkened. The clerk had a little bronze stick. I watched him press the flat end of it across the dark surface, smoothing it, and I thought, wax, that's wax, they write on wax, and felt absurdly pleased with myself for figuring out one true thing.

The optio said something to the seated man. The man looked at me for a while without saying anything. Then he started asking questions.

I know they were questions because of the shape of them, the little lift at the end, the pause after where he waited and I gave him nothing. He asked one. Pause. He tried again, different words, slower, the way you'd talk to someone old. Pause. He leaned back and asked the optio something and the optio answered at length, and I caught exactly one sound in all of it that I recognized, which was the noise the optio had made last night when he failed to say my name, that mangled middle thing that wasn't quite Daniel. So they were talking about me. Brilliant deduction. Sherlock lives.

The man pointed at my clothes and said a word and made a small circular motion taking in the jeans, the T-shirt, the boots. He wanted to know about the clothes. Of course he did. I'd want to know about the clothes too. I had no way to tell him they came off a rack in a store under fluorescent lights for nineteen dollars. I plucked at the hem of the shirt and turned both palms up, the universal sign for I cannot help you, and his face did something cold.

I was being graded. I understood that much. I'd watched enough of these scenes in movies to know the categories, even if the movies were all wrong about everything else. Spy. Lunatic. Runaway slave. There'd be some word here for each and the man was sorting me into one. None of them ended with me going home, but some of them ended with me alive, and I wanted very badly to push myself toward the alive ones, and I had no tools. No words. A dead language I'd never even learned, except it wasn't dead yet, it was extremely alive and sitting at a table judging me.

He asked something else, longer, with an edge on it now, and when I didn't answer he made a flat gesture toward the door, a take-him-away gesture, dismissive, done. The optio shifted his weight.

That's the thing that did it. The little movement of the optio's foot, the sense of a door closing. I'd been told no answer and I was about to be filed under whatever you file the useless under. I thought, in a rush, that the one thing I had that nobody else in this room had wasn't a thing you say. It was a thing you show.

I lifted my hand and mimed it. I held an invisible stick and dragged it across an invisible surface. I looked at the clerk, at the wax frame on his knees, and did it again. Writing. Drawing. Give me that.

Everyone went still. The man at the table narrowed his eyes. I thought I'd blown it, that I'd just done something that read as a spell or a threat, that miming was how barbarians cursed you. Then the man said one short word and flicked two fingers at the clerk, and the clerk got up, not happy about it, and crossed the room and handed me the folded frame and the bronze stick, holding them out at arm's length like I might be contagious.

The wax was the color of dark honey, pressed smooth, in a wooden surround about the size of a paperback splayed open. The stick had a point at one end and that flat blade at the other for smoothing out mistakes. My hands were not steady. I knelt down because there was nowhere to set it, balanced the frame on my own knee the way the clerk had, and pressed the point in.

It cut a thin clean line. Easier than I expected.

I'm not an artist. I want to be honest about that, because of what happened later and what people decided about me, and it all started with a drawing made by a kid who got a B-minus in the one art class he was forced to take. But I knew one thing cold, and it was this: I knew the shape of the world. I'd seen it ten thousand times. On the back of cereal boxes. On the wall of every classroom I'd ever sat in. On my own phone, the little blue planet you spin with your thumb. I had the map in me the way you have the alphabet in you, not as facts you recall but as a shape your hand already knows.

So I started with the part they'd know.

I drew the sea in the middle. I drew it as a long horizontal pool, fat in the center, and I put the boot in. Italy. I got the boot pretty good, actually, the kick and the heel, and as the point came down through it I heard the optio make a sound, a soft grunt, and when I glanced up his eyes had changed. He'd recognized it. He was a soldier, he'd seen maps, military maps, road maps, and there under my hand was the shape of the ground he stood on.

I kept going. I hooked the long top edge of the Mediterranean, the south coast of what I called France and Spain in my head and had no name for here, the heel and instep of the European underbelly. I put in the big island down by the boot's toe, Sicily, a fat triangle. I dropped Greece in, all those torn-up fingers of land reaching down, and I knew I was getting Greece wrong, too clean, too few islands, but it was the right idea in the right place. I ran the line east and curled it down toward where I was pretty sure Egypt sat, where the sea ended and the land took over.

The man had stood up.

He came around the table. He didn't say anything now. He bent over the frame on my knee and I had to angle it up for him, and I could smell the wine on him, the morning wine they all drank instead of water. He put one thick finger down near the boot, not touching the wax, hovering, and said a word, soft, almost to himself. A question in it. I didn't know the word. I knew what he was pointing at. I tapped the boot with the back of the stick. Yes. Here. Then I tapped the floor between us. Here. Same place.

His head came up and he looked at me, really looked, for the first time, and the contempt that had been sitting on his face all morning wasn't gone exactly but it had something else next to it now.

I should have stopped there. If I'd had any sense I'd have stopped at the sea they knew and let that be my whole magic trick. But there's a thing that happens when a room finally looks at you after a day of looking through you, and I was seventeen and I'd been afraid for two days straight, and I wanted more of it. I wanted them to keep looking like that.

So I kept drawing.

I took the land below the sea, the African coast they traded along, and instead of letting it trail off into rumor the way their maps did, the way I was learning their whole picture of the world did, I pulled it down. I drew the whole continent. The big shoulder of the west bulging out into the ocean, the long taper down to the point at the bottom, the way it kicks back in on the east side. I drew Africa as a whole shape, a thing with an end to it, a thing you could sail around, and I knew I was getting the proportions wrong even as I did it, too narrow probably, the bottom point too sharp, but the gestalt of it, the wholeness, was something nobody in that room had ever seen rendered, because nobody in that room knew it had a bottom.

The clerk had come close without my noticing. He said something to the seated man, fast, and the seated man answered without looking away from my knee.

I went east. This is where it got bad, where my hand was guessing, and I knew it. I put in the wedge of Arabia okay, the Red Sea as a long thin gash, and then past that I was running on fumes. I knew there was a big subcontinent that hung down, a triangle, India, and I put it in too small and too far north, I'm sure, jammed up against everything. I knew China was east of that and huge and I gave it a fat coastline curving up and away, and I have no doubt it was wrong in every particular except the one that mattered, which was that it was there at all and it was enormous. I drew islands off the east edge, Japan, a guess on a guess. The southeast, all those islands and peninsulas below China, I just sort of stippled in a scatter and hoped. It was the kind of map you'd draw for a friend to win a bet, fast and confident and fundamentally a lie in the details and true in the bones.

The seated man was pointing at India now, asking, and I didn't have the word and couldn't give it, so I just kept the point moving, because the point moving was the only sentence I could speak.

Then I came back to the western edge. To the boot, to the coast of France and Spain, to where their world ended at the open ocean. The edge of everything. I'd seen on some article, some half-watched video, the word they had for it, the ocean as the great encircling river at the rim of the map, the place you didn't sail past because there was nothing to sail to.

I left a gap of blank wax. A wide one, the width of two fingers, open sea.

And on the far side of it I drew two more continents.

I drew the long thin tail of South America first because it's the easy one, the way it dangles, the bulge on its east shoulder. Then North America above it, the wide top, the gulf scooped out underneath, the long western run. I connected them with the thin waist in the middle. I drew them small, off at the edge of the frame, badly, the coastlines vague because nobody draws the coast of Brazil from memory, but unmistakably two big lands, with shape, with an end and a far side, sitting out there in the ocean Rome thought was empty.

The room had gone very quiet. Even the noises from the lane outside seemed to have stepped back.

The seated man wasn't pointing anymore. He was just looking at the blank gap of wax and the two shapes beyond it, and his hand had come up to his mouth, and I watched something move across his face that I can only call calculation. The clerk said a word, and the seated man said one back, low and short, and it had a cutting-off quality, shut up, and the clerk shut up.

I knelt there with the frame on my knee and the bronze stick going slippery in my fingers and I thought, very clearly, in plain American English in the middle of all that noise I couldn't understand: you idiot. You absolute idiot.

Because here is what I knew that they didn't, and what I shouldn't have shown them I knew. There was nothing across that ocean for them. Not yet. Not for fourteen hundred years. I had just drawn a treasure map to two continents that no one alive would reach, and I'd drawn it for a man with the kind of face that was already doing sums, already thinking about who he reported to and what it was worth, and the part of me that read too much science fiction was screaming about consequences and timelines and things you don't put back in the box.

And the other part of me, the part that had been cold and bound and laughed at and one wax-flick away from the dungeon or worse, that part was just glad. Glad they were looking at me. It had felt important to draw it. It felt important the way a thing feels important when you're seventeen and you finally have leverage and you don't yet understand that leverage is also a debt. I'd shown them the world had more of it than they knew, and that I knew where, and now I was a thing worth keeping.

I didn't have a word for any of that. So I just held the map up a little so he could see it better.

The man took the frame out of my hands. He didn't ask, he took it, and he held it close to his face and tilted it toward the light from the door, and then he carried it back to his table and set it down and stood over it with both hands flat on the wood. He said something to the optio without turning around. A string of it, decisive, the way a man talks when he's stopped wondering and started arranging.

The optio answered with a single word that I would learn, later, was a kind of yes, the army yes, and he said it the way you say it to someone you don't argue with.

Then the man said one more thing, and it had a name in it. I'm sure of that much, because of the way it landed, the way a name sits differently in a sentence, a hard little stone in a stream of water. He said it and the optio repeated it back as a question and the man said it again, sharper, and made a gesture with his hand like he was sending something out the door and a long way off. Go get this. Go get him.

I didn't know who. I picked up, much later, that he'd said two things about the man he was sending for: one of them meant something like one who heals, and one of them I would eventually understand was just a slur for foreigner, the same flavor of word the guy at the pots had spat at me, except this time it wasn't aimed at me. There was someone else in this town, or near it, who was strange enough to be useful for understanding strange things. A man who knew bodies, and tongues, and the far edges of the world. They were going to bring him to me and see what the two strange creatures made of each other.

A spearman left at a fast walk. I heard his sandals slap off down the lane and fade.

The seated man looked at me one more time. The contempt was mostly gone now and what had replaced it was worse in a way, because contempt you can survive by being beneath notice, and this was the opposite, this was a man who had decided I was worth the trouble. He said something to the optio, shorter, and waved his hand toward me, and I understood I was being given back into the optio's keeping. A possession returned to its shelf.

They didn't tie my hands. I noticed that. Yesterday the rope, today nothing. The math of how they saw me had changed in the space of one drawing, and I'd done all of it on my knees on a dirt floor with a stick.

The optio touched my shoulder, not rough, and tipped his head toward the door. We were going. I stood up, and my knee had gone half-asleep under the frame, and I stumbled, and one of the spearmen snorted, and there it was, the glow in my ears again, the great inventor of worlds tripping over his own dead foot.

At the door I looked back. The clerk had crossed to the table and the two of them, clerk and master, were bent over my map, heads almost touching, and the clerk had his own stick out and was, I realized, copying it. Copying my coastlines onto a fresh leaf of his own. Already. Whatever it was, they weren't going to let it stay only in my crooked hand. It was loose now.

Outside the light had come up hard and white and the lane had filled with the day, donkeys and shouting and the smell of bread somewhere doing its level best to cut through the fish. The optio walked me back the way we'd come, slower this time, and partway along he stopped at a stall and bought something with a coin, two of those flat dense rolls, and put one in my hands without looking at me, like he didn't want it remarked on.

I ate it walking. It was better than the mush. I'd have eaten a tire.

I had eleven percent of battery left in my pocket and I'd spent none of it doing the thing I was about to need it for, which was making sure the next map was better, because there'd be a next map, I could feel it coming the way you feel a test coming. Tonight, in the cell, in the dark, with my back to the door and the brightness down as low as it would go, I'd open the dead useless map app, the one that couldn't find me but still remembered the coastlines it had loaded once, and I'd stare at the real shapes for as long as I dared. Refresh the boot. Fix Africa's bottom. Get India hanging where it actually hangs. It wasn't honest, drawing from a glowing rectangle and passing it off as something I carried in my head. But I was past worrying about honest. I was a learned man from somewhere cold and far away who happened to know the shape of the whole world, and a learned man checks his sources, and mine had eleven percent of battery left and was the last library on earth that spoke my language.

The optio said something to me as we reached the cell door. Easy, calm, four or five words. I didn't understand any of it. But he tapped his own chest and said a sound, the sound he'd given me last night for his name, and then he pointed at me and said the mangled middle of Daniel, the part he could manage, and waited.

I said it back to him. His sound, as close as I could get it. His mouth went sideways, not quite a smile, more like a man acknowledging a decent throw.

Then he opened the door and put me back in the dark, and slid the bar across, and I sat down against the wall with half a roll still in my hand and my heart going like I'd run a mile, and I thought about the two continents I'd hung in the empty ocean, and whether you can ever take a thing like that back, and decided you cannot, and ate the rest of the bread.

---

# Chapter 4: Heras

They came for me the next morning, which I worked out from the light and from the fact that I'd slept, badly, in two or three pieces, waking each time to the same dark and the same smell and the bar still across the door. Somewhere in the middle of it I'd done the map-app thing, hunched in the corner with the screen turned to the wall and dimmed to almost nothing, staring at the real coast of Africa until my eyes watered and the battery ticked from eleven to ten. Then I'd put it away and held it against my chest like an idiot and gone back to sleep.

The optio opened the door and stood there a second looking at me the way you look at livestock you've been told is valuable now, recalculating. He had bread again. I was getting fond of the bread, which is a sad sentence, but there it is. He jerked his chin. I came.

We didn't go far. Up the lane, around a corner, through a low door into a room with actual furniture, a long scarred table and a stool and a brazier in the corner that made the air swim. A window with real shutters. After the cell it felt like a hotel. The optio put me on the stool and crossed his arms by the door, and we waited, and I ate the bread and tried to look like a learned man from a cold far country and not like a kid one degree off pissing himself from nerves.

The physician came in out of the white morning light with a leather satchel over one shoulder and stopped just inside the door to look at me before he said anything to anyone.

I want to get him right, because he matters, and because for a long time afterward I got him wrong, kept expecting him to be kind in the way I wanted, and he never once was. Maybe forty-five, going gray at the temples, clean-shaven in a town where most men weren't, with a long face and a downturned mouth and eyes that did all the work. A plain good tunic, a darker wrap over it, no jewelry, sandals that had been resoled. His hands were the cleanest I'd seen since I got here. He looked at me like a problem he had not yet decided was interesting.

He said something to the optio. The optio answered short. The physician said something back, longer, dry, and the optio's jaw tightened, and I understood with a small private joy that the doctor had just been faintly rude to a soldier and the soldier had to take it. So this was somebody. A freedman, I'd learn, born a slave and freed, near the bottom of the ladder, except that he knew bodies and he knew Greek, and that bought a kind of room his birth didn't.

Then he came over and crouched in front of me, easily, knees not complaining, and looked into my face from a foot away, and started in on me.

He turned my head side to side into the light. He thumbed my lower eyelid down and peered at the red of it. He picked up my hand off my knee, turned it over, ran his thumb across the calluses and the little white scar from a box cutter at the store, and made a sound, not a word, an interested noise low in his throat. He pressed two fingers to the inside of my wrist and went still, counting, his eyes drifting to the middle distance, and I sat there with this stranger taking my pulse in Italy in the wrong millennium and felt, of all the absurd things, calmer. Because it was the first thing anyone had done to me here that I recognized. A guy was trying to figure out what was wrong with me. That's a thing people do where I'm from too.

He let go of the wrist and sat back on his heels and asked me a question.

I didn't understand it. Of course I didn't. But he asked it like a question, head tilted, eyebrows up, and I spread my hands the way I'd been spreading them for two days, sorry, nothing, and his mouth went down at one corner. He tried again, slower. Same nothing from me.

And then he said a third thing, and the shape of it was different, rounder, and one of the sounds in it caught on something in my ear.

I'd had two years of French and a lot of YouTube and the kind of vocabulary you absorb sideways from being decent at math and reading too much. And there was a word near the front of what he'd just said, and it sounded like *pou*, and it snagged on a memory I couldn't place for a second and then could. *Pou*. Where. Some root, some prefix that lived inside English words about place and distance. He was asking me where. Where are you from.

Something in my face must have moved, because he saw it land. He went very still, then said the word again on its own, with the question in it, and pointed at me, and made a small gesture out at the world.

He'd switched to Greek. I worked that out later, that the first stuff had been Latin and he'd gotten nowhere and tried Greek on a hunch, and that Greek gave me my first toehold in this entire world, because Greek had loaned my language ten thousand words and a couple had stuck in me deep enough to hear.

I didn't have an answer I could say. But I had the gesture. I pointed at myself, lifted my arm and pointed past the shutters, out, away, made my hand do a long traveling motion, far, far, far, then crossed my arms over my chest and shivered, hard, theatrically, brrr, cold, a cold place, and pointed up, north, though there was no reason he'd read my up as north, and did the shiver again.

He watched all of this without moving. Then he said something over his shoulder to the optio in Latin, flat, and the optio answered, and I caught nothing of it, and the physician turned back with the corner of his mouth doing its downward thing, and from the whole set of him I gathered he'd just said something like *well, he's not deaf and he's not an idiot, which is two more than I expected.*

---

What I had, going in, was the map and the numbers. The map had already done its work two days running, and I had a feeling that the man across from me was a harder audience than a magistrate. So I went for the numbers.

There was a wax tablet on the table, a good one, two leaves, and a bronze stylus beside it. I made the writing motion, scribbling on my palm, then pointed at the tablet, asking. He considered me. Then he reached over, smoothed the wax flat with the broad end of the stylus, and set it in front of me, and he didn't hand me the stylus, he set it down too, just out of arm's reach, so taking it was a small choice I had to make in front of him. I took it.

I wrote out the numbers. One through nine, left to right, the way I'd been making them my whole life, the lazy modern shapes, the 4 open at the top, the 7 with no crossbar because I'm an American. Then a 0, and sat back.

He looked at them a while without touching the tablet. He said a word and pointed at the 1, and I made the yes face back, and he pointed at the 2 and worked down the line, naming them, his words nothing like mine, and I didn't try to learn them yet. I just let him count along his own row in his own language while I had drawn it.

Then I showed him the trick. Off to the side I wrote his numbers, the Roman ones, because I'd been staring at them on walls for two days and knew the shapes if not the system, I, V, X, a clump of them like a tally, and under it the same value in my figures, two digits, and I tapped the long Roman clump and then my two little marks and held my hands wide for the first and pinched them close for the second. Long. Short. Same thing.

He got it. I watched him get it. His eyes went from the clump to the two digits and back and something happened behind his face, a recalculation, the problem becoming interesting. He picked up the stylus and wrote a number of his own the Roman way, a real one, big, the kind that takes a fistful of letters, and pushed the tablet at me. A test. Write that, your way.

I did. Place value. I wrote it in four digits, and then, because I'm seventeen and I had an audience and leverage is a drug, I drew a little vertical line between the columns and tapped each in turn and held up fingers, ones, tens, hundreds, thousands, each one ten of the last, this is the whole secret, this is why it's short, every slot worth ten of the slot to its right, and the little round nothing holds a slot open when there's nothing in it. I tapped the zero, made a circle with my fingers, then opened my empty hand. Nothing, but a placeholder nothing, a nothing that does a job.

I have no idea how much of that landed. Probably less than I felt. But he looked at the zero a long time, then said one quiet word to himself, and I would have given a year of my life to know what it was.

---

He should have stopped there. I should have stopped there. It was going beautifully. But he was a man who tested things, so he wrote two numbers his way, one over the other, drew a line under them, and made the flat gesture, *go on then.* Add them, I figured. I added them in my figures, fast, carried the one, sat back. He counted it his own way, lips moving, and his head did a small motion I chose to read as correct. Fine.

Then he wiped the leaf and wrote one big number, four figures, and beside it a smaller one, two figures, and between them a mark I didn't know, and he tapped it and looked at me, and I understood he wanted the big one broken up by the small one. Division.

And here is the thing about long division, which is that you do it for years in grade school and then never again because you have a phone, and the phone was in my pocket with ten percent of battery and a calculator I could not, could not, take out in front of this man, and I had not done a long-division problem by hand since I was about eleven.

I started fine. I set it up, the big number under the bracket, the divisor outside, and I bit my lip and started the dance, how many times does it go in, write the digit on top, multiply, subtract, bring down the next. I got two digits in. And then I subtracted wrong. I had a remainder that should have been smaller than the divisor and it wasn't, it was bigger, which meant my digit on top was too small, which meant I'd lowballed it, and I knew the instant I saw it sitting there that I'd screwed up. The number was glaring up at me wrong, and the heat came up the back of my neck and into my ears.

I looked up. He was watching me. Not unkindly. Worse than unkindly. He was watching me the way you watch a man who has just promised a marvelous short road and walked you into a ditch. His eyebrows had gone up a fraction. He said something short, two words, and even without the language I knew exactly the temperature of it, it was *is that so,* it was a teacher catching a kid who'd bluffed the homework.

My hand was shaking a little. I made myself stop. I scrubbed out the bad digit with the flat of the stylus, the wax smearing, went back to that step, and said out loud, in English, to nobody, "okay, it goes in more times than that, you absolute idiot, count it," and counted on my fingers under the edge of the table where he could half see, the divisor, once, twice, three times, until I had the right digit. I wrote it, multiplied again, slower, and this time the remainder came out small, smaller than the divisor, the way it's supposed to, and I let out a breath I hadn't known I was holding and brought down the next figure.

It took me a while. I made one more small slip near the end and caught it myself before he had to do the eyebrow, and at the bottom I had a quotient and a remainder, and I drew the remainder off the clumsy way, with a little r, because I wasn't going to try to explain fractions to a man I'd just face-planted in front of.

He took the tablet and checked it. He checked the whole thing, every step, his lips going, his own way, and it was the longest minute of my life, sitting there with my ears on fire, the great Thulean scholar who couldn't divide. And at the end of it he set the tablet down and looked at me and said something, and I braced for the contempt, and it wasn't contempt.

It was dry as a bone, whatever it was, with a thread of something in it I would spend the next year learning to recognize, somewhere between amusement and respect held at arm's length and the satisfaction of a man who has caught you out and found you slightly more interesting for it. He tapped the smear in the wax where I'd made the first error, then tapped the correct answer at the bottom, and looked at me, and the corner of his mouth went up, just barely, the first time it had gone up instead of down. He had decided something about me. Not that I was a fraud. That I was real, and fallible, and the fallible part was the part that convinced him. A liar's trick comes out clean. A real method has a man sweating over it and fixing his own mistakes.

---

We worked through what was left of the morning, the two of us, the optio shifting his weight by the door and the brazier dying down. It wasn't conversation. It was two men passing a stylus back and forth and watching each other's faces. He'd write a thing, I'd answer it. I'd draw a thing, he'd frown at it.

But Greek kept handing me crumbs. He'd point at the brazier and say a word and I'd hear *therm* sitting in the front of it, heat, like thermometer, like thermal, and I'd point at the fire and rub my arms warm and he'd see me catch it. He'd point at the window, at the light, and there was a word with *photo* buried in it, or near enough that my brain grabbed for it. Single words, snagged out of a river of sound I couldn't follow, the way you catch one familiar face in a crowd and lose it again instantly. But they were the first words this place had handed me that I could hold, and after two days of total deafness I held them like a man holds anything in a drowning.

I never once understood a sentence. People who tell you they picked up a language in an afternoon are lying or they're geniuses, and I was neither. I had *where*, and *heat*, and *light*, and the optio's name, and the mangled middle of my own. That was the entire kingdom of my comprehension. I built everything I did that morning on the map and the numbers and my hands.

At some point I got the tablet back and drew the map again. The small version, the boot and the sea and the coasts, and I'll admit I drew it better than I had for the magistrate because I'd been staring at the phone half the night. His eyes narrowed at the real shape of the bottom of Africa, and narrowed more when I came up past where their world ended. He'd seen maps. You could tell. This was a man who'd read things, been places, and the map was an argument he could weigh, not a wonder he had to gawk at.

And then I did the thing the rest of my life now demanded. I put the stylus point up at the top of the map, above everything, above the lumpy afterthought of Britain, into the blank wax at the very top edge, and tapped it, there, far up there, and crossed my arms and did the shiver again, the cold, and pointed at myself, and tapped the empty top of the world one more time. From there. The cold place at the top, past the last land you know, off the edge of the part you've mapped.

He looked at where I was pointing. The blank wax above Britain, where there's nothing, where on any map I'd ever seen there's just the word for ice and the rumor of land. He looked at it a long time, then looked at me, and said a word.

I didn't know it then. I caught it without holding it, a hard little two-syllable sound that landed in the blank space above the world like a stone dropped in a well. He said it once, looking at where I pointed, half a question. I didn't react because I had nothing to react with. He said it again, quieter, more to himself, looking at the empty top of the map, and there was no wonder in his voice and no belief in it either. There was the flat dry skepticism of a man who has read the books that call that place a sailor's lie, weighing a boy who claims to come from it.

I only knew he'd put a word on the blank place I was pointing at, that he didn't sound like he bought it for a second, and that not buying it didn't make him want me gone. If anything it made him lean in. A wonder you either believe or you don't. A lie that good is worth taking apart.

---

The phone died at the end of that day, in the new place they put me, and there's no way to make it bigger than it was.

They moved me that afternoon. Out of the cell for good, the physician saying something to the optio and the optio saying something back, the upshot of it walking me through the lane to a workshop of some kind, a long room off a courtyard, benches and tools and the smell of glue and wood shavings and somebody's lunch, a household attached, a loft above where I was meant to sleep. A pallet. A blanket that smelled of another man's sleep. Two men who worked the benches and looked at me sideways, a boy who brought water, and a sense, never stated, always present, that I could not leave, that the door at the courtyard end had someone near it, that I was a useful object moved to a better shelf and still, in every way that mattered, owned. The physician had a hand in it. The optio did. Somebody above both of them did. I was lodged, watched, fed, and not free, and it was a palace compared to the cell.

That night I lay on the pallet in the loft with the noise of the household below me and the single luxury of stretching out flat, and I took the phone out to look at the photos. I did that most nights. The Colosseum, intact, gleaming, the proof I wasn't insane. My own face in the selfie, the last face I knew. I'd told myself I'd ration it down to nothing, save the last of it for an emergency that was never coming, and I'd torched most of it the first day and now there was almost nothing to ration.

I woke it up. Ten in the morning, less now after the night before and the map app. The lock screen came up, the photo behind the time, and I swiped in my code without thinking, the way your thumb knows it in the dark, and the camera roll opened and there they were, and I looked at them.

I went to put it to sleep. I pressed the button on the side. The screen flickered. It went dark, then came halfway back, a gray ghost of the lock screen, and a small white symbol I'd seen ten thousand times sat in the middle of it for a moment, the battery, the thin red sliver. And then that went too. The glass went black.

I pressed the button again. Nothing. I held it down, the long press, the hard reset, eight seconds, ten, my thumb white on the side of it. The black stayed black. I held it up close to my face and could see my own eye in it, dim, reflected, and nothing behind the glass at all. No glow. No little red sliver. Off.

I pressed it a few more times. I knew it wouldn't. I did it anyway, the way you jiggle a key in a lock that's clearly broken, the way you say a dead person's name.

I put it back in my pocket. The blanket smelled like another man. Below me a woman said something sharp to the boy, and the boy answered, and a door scraped, and the household went on in its language all around me in the dark. I lay there with the dead glass against my hip and my hands behind my head and looked up at the underside of the roof tiles, the same red clay as the village, as the whole world now, and waited to feel something I could put in the notes, and I didn't write the notes, because the phone was the notes, and I lay there with my eyes open until I slept.

---

# Chapter 5: Latin and the Little Ones

The first word I owned outright was *aqua*. Water.

I got it the way I got everything that winter, by being thirsty and stupid in front of people. The boy who carried water for the workshop, a knock-kneed kid called Stichus, maybe ten, who did not like me, set a jug down by my bench, and I pointed at it and made a noise, and he looked at me like I'd asked him to explain fire and said the word slow, the way you talk to a dog. *Aqua.* I said it back wrong. He said it again. I got it closer. He picked up the jug and walked off, and I sat there turning the one word over like a coin, because it was the first thing I'd taken whole instead of guessing at the front of it.

That was how it went, for months. No wise old man teaching me by lamplight. Just being thirsty and cold and humiliated, pointing at things, getting the word wrong, getting laughed at by a ten-year-old, trying again. The brain does it whether you want to or not, if you're drowning in the sound long enough and the alternative is staying deaf. The workshop ran on talk, the two men at the benches arguing about a joint or a price, the woman of the household, the boy, the people who came through the courtyard, a river of sound that ran over me ten hours a day, and I stood in it like a stone until the stone wore into the shape of words.

I built a grammar in the loft at night, on the inside of my own skull, because I had no tablet of my own and nothing to write with that wasn't theirs. I'm decent at patterns. That's the math talking. Two years of high-school French turned out to be the most useful thing my whole education handed me, more than the calculus, more than anything, and it had nothing to do with the French words. It was one idea: that a word could *bend*, that *amo* and *amat* were the same animal wearing different collars, and the collar told you who was doing the loving. Latin bent harder. It bent six ways and then bent the bending. I called myself by the wrong collar for weeks, used the form that meant someone was doing a thing *to* Daniel when I meant Daniel was doing the thing, so I went around announcing myself as the object of every sentence, which, fair.

The sounds were their own private joke at my expense. Their *c* was always hard, a *k*, no exceptions, so every soft *c* I'd ever read came out wrong in my mouth and right in theirs, and I had to unlearn a thousand words I'd never said out loud, only read. And the *v*. The *v* killed me. They said it like a *w*. I'd point at the wine, *vinum*, and say it with the buzzing *v* of home, and they'd squint, and Heras would say it back soft, *winum*, and the whole shape of the language tilted half a degree away from everything I'd assumed. I kept slipping the *v* back in for a year. It marked me the way an accent always marks you, a little flag that said foreign before I'd finished the word, and all I could do was flatten it down a degree at a time.

---

Heras came every few days. I had his name by the time the rains set in, and getting it was its own small idiot triumph.

He'd been "the physician" in my head for weeks, the clean-handed Greek who'd caught me lying about long division and decided he liked me for it. He came and went and I never had the language to ask the one thing you ask a person, which is who you are. Then one grey afternoon he was leaning over my bench correcting a figure I'd written, and the man who ran the workshop called across the courtyard to him by name, and I heard it land clean. Two syllables. I'd heard it a dozen times and never cut it out of the noise. This time I had enough words around it to know it was a name and not a thing.

I pointed at him. "Heras."

He straightened up and looked at me the way you look at a parrot that's said something true by accident, and then he put a finger on his own chest and said it again, properly, the Greek shape of it, and made me say it until I had the vowels close, and then he touched my chest and said my name, the mangled middle of it, *Danihel*, the version they could all pronounce, and we stood there formally introducing ourselves four months after we met. He found this funny. He had a way of finding things funny that never once involved laughing. The mouth went up a hair on one side and the eyes did something dry, and that was the whole performance, and I'd already learned to chase it, because it was the only applause in my life.

He talked to me in Latin mostly, slow and clean, because Latin was what I needed to survive in the street. But when a Latin word annoyed him he slid into Greek, and over the months I picked up Greek too, slower, sideways, the way you learn a thing standing next to someone who knows it. Greek had the roots I'd half-recognized from the start, the *therm* and the *photo* buried in the long words, the bones of a hundred English words I'd never thought about. I'd reach for a Greek word and find an English one wearing it as a mask. Heras never knew the language he was handing me had already gone ahead of him by two thousand years and come back wearing my clothes. There was no way to tell him. There was no way to tell anyone anything, and that was the whole problem, and it got worse as the words came, not better.

Nobody warns you about that. The first weeks, being mute is just survival, thirst and cold and pointing. Then the words come and you think it'll get less lonely, and instead it gets worse, because now you can almost reach people and the almost is a knife. I could ask for water. I could not say that home wasn't even a place yet, that it was a thing that hadn't happened. I could count to a hundred and haggle for bread. I could not make one joke that anyone alive would get. I'd think something funny, a line from a movie, a thing my mom said, and it would come up the back of my throat and die there, because the only audience for it was eighteen centuries off and didn't know it was missing me.

---

The first real joke I made in Latin, I made by accident, and it nearly killed me, I laughed so hard from pure relief.

It was the marketplace. Heras had started taking me into the town, with the optio's blessing or somebody's, on a loose leash I never fully understood, a foreigner walking a half-pace behind a freedman physician through a street that stank of fish and piss and the woodsmoke of a hundred braziers and the wet wool of everybody's cloaks in the rain. He'd name things and make me name them back, in the open, in front of people, which was harder than the workshop because the market didn't slow down for me and would cheat a foreigner who fumbled his numbers. I fumbled them constantly. Knowing *seven* in your head and saying *septem* fast enough that a fishmonger doesn't see you flinch are two different skills.
