So there's a man selling eggs, and Heras is buying, and the man names a price, and I understand the price, which is itself a small miracle, and the price is robbery, double what eggs go for, because he's looked at my tunic worn wrong and my mouth that can't say the *v* and decided I'm the soft money walking behind the doctor. Heras starts to argue him down the normal way, and out of nowhere, out of four months of swallowed words, I hear myself say it. I point at the eggs. In my broken collar-wrong Latin, the case probably wrong, I ask whether the hen laid them or Caesar did. Because it was an emperor's price. That was the joke. It's barely a joke. But it lands, the case wrong and the *v* buzzing and all, because the fishmonger next door barks out a laugh and the egg man goes red, and Heras turns and looks at me with that dry uptick of the mouth gone wider than I'd ever earned. I got my eggs at the right price and laughed all the way back to the workshop like a man let out of a cell, because for one second in a market in a dead language I'd been funny on purpose and someone had heard it.

Up in the loft that night the high came off it and left the other thing, which was that the people I'd have called to tell about the egg man would never be born. I lay there with the dead phone against my hip out of habit and didn't take it out, because there was nothing on it anymore, and went to sleep counting eggs in Latin to keep from counting anything else.

---

The little ones nearly got me killed, and I walked into it on purpose, like an idiot, because a kid was dying and I thought I knew something.

It was deep winter, the wet kind, everyone coughing, and the boy who worked the next bench cut his hand badly on a chisel and the cut went wrong. A wound here was a coin flip with death on the other side, and this one came up death's face. The hand swelled and went red and the red ran up his arm in a thin angry line crawling toward the elbow, and the boy went hot and stupid with fever, and they sent for Heras, and I, with my four months of Latin and my half a memory of a science class, decided this was the moment.

I'd been trying to get the idea across for weeks, in pieces, badly. Now I had a sick boy and a reason. When Heras came I waited until he'd looked at the arm and done the things he did, the smelling, the pressing, the muttering about the humors, and then I got the wax tablet and tried to draw it. You can't draw what I was trying to draw. I drew dots. Crowds of them, swarming, going into the cut, into the red line, up the arm. I said the words I had. *Small. Living.* I made them with my fingers, tiny, crawling, walked my fingers up his arm like little legs, and said, in the Latin of a four-month-old, that they were small living ones, that they went in, that they climbed inside you and ate. I didn't have the word for invisible, so I said small past seeing. And because I'd spent four months listening to people swear by gods at every turn, I added the only cover I had. I pointed up. The gods send them. Tiny living things the gods send to climb inside you and eat you from the middle out.

Heras let me finish. He had the patience of a man watching a child build a tower of blocks so he can see exactly how it falls. And when I was done he took the stylus out of my hand, gently, and he took me apart.

I got maybe half the words and the whole of the contempt, which needs no translation. He pointed at the swollen arm and said the word I'd learned for bad air, the air of marshes, low and rotten. *This* is what sickens. You got fever from bad air and bad places and the wrong stars, ask anyone. He pressed his own four fingers to his own arm and named them, the wet and the dry and the hot and the cold, the humors, the balance of the body, and named the imbalance that made a fever, and this he said slowly and twice, because it was real to him, it was Hippocrates, the best medical thinking on the planet earth in that year, and he held it the way I held the quadratic formula. And then the gods angry, a curse, a thing done wrong at an altar. Air, humors, the gods' wrath. Three good frames, the frames every educated man from Britain to Syria used, and into the middle of them I'd dropped a story about invisible animals that crawl into cuts. He looked at my drawing of the swarming dots and said a word I didn't know, and the man at the bench snorted, and Heras said it at me again, and from the way he said it I understood it meant something near *witch-talk*, the thing old women in the hills believed. He was telling me, as a friend, that I'd just said the kind of thing that got people killed here, that invisible things sneaking inside you was the language of curses and the evil eye and the magic the law had teeth about, and I should, for my own sake, shut up about my little animals.

I'd known it was a risk. I hadn't known how much. I'd reached for the one true thing I had, the half-memory of a video, swarming dots under a microscope I'd seen once on a screen, and I'd reached for it because the kid was dying and I was *sure*, the way you're sure of a thing you've never seen and only half remember, and Heras had just shown me that being sure was worth exactly nothing. I had a story and no way on earth to make him see the dots, because the only thing that could show him the dots was a thing I could not build. I had the truth and no ladder to it. It was the loneliest I'd been since the phone died.

So I did the only thing left. I gave up the story and kept the chores.

---

I couldn't make him believe in the little ones. But I could make him *do the things*, because the things worked, and Heras, whatever else he was, would do a thing that worked even if he hated the reason.

I took the water he meant to wash the boy's arm with and I wouldn't let him use it cold from the jug. I made him boil it. I had no word for *sterilize* and there's no such concept here anyway, so I framed it the one way that didn't sound like witchcraft: hot water is clean, fire cleans, you wouldn't eat off a dirty plate, don't wash a wound with dirty water, the fire makes it clean past what the eye can judge. That, he could take. Cleanliness he understood. The Greeks were big on the regimen and the bath, and *clean* slotted in with no demons attached. He boiled it, let it cool, and we washed the arm properly and washed it again, and washed our own hands first, which I made a thing of, which he thought was fussy and did anyway because the fussy foreigner wouldn't stop until he did. We kept the wound open and clean instead of packing it with the muck they'd packed it with. He knew his own herbs, the ones that helped, and I didn't argue any of that, because he'd cut up more bodies than I'd seen and I wasn't about to play doctor at him. I owned the water and the hands. He kept everything else.

The boy lived. The red line hung at the elbow for two bad days and went back down. He kept the arm. I want to be honest about what that proves, which is nothing, exactly nothing, because boys with red lines up their arms sometimes live no matter what you do and sometimes die no matter what you do, and one kid is not a number you can stand on. I knew that even then, lying awake doing the math I couldn't do, no count, no control, no second boy untreated to set him against, just one kid and a coin flip and my story dressed up as cleanliness. I'd never, in this place, have proof. I'd have the thing that worked and the story that didn't, forever, and I'd have to learn to live in the gap.

But Heras saw the boy keep his arm. And weeks later, a different patient, he sent the household girl to boil the water before I'd said a word, and washed his hands first without being told, and when I caught his eye he gave me nothing, no credit, no concession, just went on as if he'd always done it this way. He never once said I was right about the little ones. To the end of that winter and probably the end of his life he thought my invisible animals were a superstition I'd carried down off the ice, a barbarian's ghost story. He just quietly started boiling his water and washing his hands, because he could *see* it helped, and the story he hung on it was his own business and the doing of it was mine. That was the only way anything was ever going to happen here. Give them a thing that worked and let them keep their own gods on it.

I told him the soldiers' *posca*, sour wine and water, washed a scrape cleaner than plain water, because I half-remembered the acid did something, and I couldn't tell him what and he didn't ask anymore. He'd stopped asking me why. He'd learned that *why*, with me, was where the witch-talk lived, and what to do was where I was worth keeping.

---

Spring came around again. I'd been here a year. Eighteen, now, by my own count off the seasons, though no one here cared and there was no candle and no cake and no one to tell.

I could talk, and I want to be precise about how much, because it would be a lie to make it more. I could hold a conversation about a thing in front of us, a tool, a price, a body, the weather, the work. I could follow Heras at his slow and lose him entirely when he got going at his real speed with another educated man, the Greek flying, the long words stacking, and I'd sit there catching one word in five and feel the deafness come back like a tide. I made errors that cost me, wrong cases that turned a sentence sideways, the *v* that buzzed and marked me foreign in the first three words. I could barely read, only the figures and a few words. A clever man behind a child's tongue is its own specific hell, because people hear the broken Latin and price your whole mind at the level of your worst sentence, and you have to claw their estimate back up joke by joke until they forget the accent and talk to the part of you that's actually there. Heras had skipped all that. He'd priced my mind off a long-division error in week one and never re-priced it down for the tongue. That was the whole of why I could stand it. One person on earth who heard past the accent.

But one person is not enough, and a year in I knew the shape of my problem with a clarity I hadn't had when I was just trying to live. I was lodged in a workshop off a household that wasn't mine, fed by people who watched the door, useful enough to keep and not free to leave, a foreigner with a homeland nobody believed in. Heras had a word for the place I claimed to be from. He'd said it that first day over the map, flat and skeptical, and over the year it had become a thing we both used, half a joke between us, the cold land at the top of the world past the last real coast. *Thule.* He'd read the men who called Thule a sailor's lie, and he'd as good as told me he thought I was a clever fraud about the where of me and a real thing about the what of me, and we'd both decided not to fight about it. I was the Thulean. It was a better cage than the cell and it was still a cage. The numbers had spread to the handful of people Heras showed them to and no further, and the map sat in a drawer somewhere as a curiosity, and that was the sum of my dent in the world after a year: a kid's arm, a fishmonger laughing, some accountants squinting at a zero.

It was never going to be enough this way, in pieces, useful to one doctor, owned by one household, talked down to by ten-year-olds. I needed a thing that wasn't an argument. Heras had taught me that cold with the little ones: you can't win them with a story, you can't win them with truth you can't show, you win them with a thing their own eyes won't let them call a lie. Something cheap, because I had nothing. Something mine, because everything else here belonged to someone. Something nobody could argue with, no haruspex, no skeptic, no fishmonger, a thing that just *happened* in front of them and ended the question of whether the Thulean was a fraud.

I was up in the loft when I landed on it. A draft came up under the tiles, the smoke off the household fire below, warm air pushing through the gaps so you could feel it on your face if you lay close to the floor, the heat rising, the smoke rising, always up. I'd known the why of that since I was twelve. Hot air is lighter. Hot air climbs. The gods don't send it up, it just goes, and nobody in this entire world knew the why and I did, I had that one for free, and I lay there with my hand in the rising warm and thought about a bag.

A bag full of hot air. Light enough, full enough of fire-warmed air that it would do what the smoke did, what I'd watched a thousand times over a campfire and a candle and never once thought about. It would go *up*. Off the ground, into the sky, in front of everyone, a made thing of cloth and fire rising on a warm wind the way the smoke climbed past my face in the dark. I didn't know how to build it. I didn't know what it cost or what it weighed or whether the cloth would hold or burn, and it would turn out I didn't know the half of how much I didn't know. But I knew it would *rise*, the way I'd known the eggs were an emperor's price, and this time the thing I was sure of was true, and better than true, it was visible, the most visible thing in the world, climbing into the open sky where every eye in the town would have to look up and watch the Thulean make the air carry fire, and not one of them would have a word for calling it a lie.

I lay there with my face in the warm draft a long time, doing the only math I had, not the engineering, that came later and cost me, but the simple animal arithmetic of a cornered man. Cheap. Mine. Impossible to argue with. The hot air gave me all three at once. I went to sleep with my hand still up in the rising heat, and for the first night in a year I didn't reach for the dead phone at all.

---

# Chapter 6: Cheap, Mine, Undeniable

I sat with it for three days before I touched a single thing, because the idea was the only part I owned outright and I didn't want to spend it cheap.

That sounds wise. It wasn't. I sat on it for three days because I was a slave in everything but the word, and a slave can't just walk out and buy cloth. Everything I would need belonged to somebody. The fire belonged to the household. The cloth belonged to the household. The rope, the oil, the time, the hands, all of it priced and watched and not mine, and I lay up in the loft turning the bag over in my head and running into the same wall every time, which was that I had nothing to build it out of and no money to get the nothing.

So the first work wasn't cloth. It was reasoning my way to why this and not something else, because I'd already burned a year on things that didn't take.

I'd given them the numbers, the best thing I had, a whole machine for thinking handed over for free, and what had it bought me. Heras showed them to a few men who kept accounts and they squinted and said the figures were clever and went on cutting their tallies into wood the way their fathers had. You couldn't make a man see the zero if he didn't want to. He'd just decide it was foreign and go home. Same with the little ones. I'd had the truth in my hands and Heras had taken it apart in an afternoon because I couldn't show it to him, and a truth you can't show is worth exactly what a lie is worth, which here was your neck. Everything I'd tried, a man could wave away. The numbers were a trick. The creatures were witch-talk. The map was a curiosity in a drawer.

A thing that flew, you couldn't argue with. That was the whole of it. A bag would go up into the open air over the town or it wouldn't, and if it did, there was no haruspex alive who could stand under it pointing up and tell the crowd it hadn't. You can call a man a liar about a drawing. You cannot call him a liar about the sky.

And it was cheap. That mattered more than the rest of it put together. I could not buy a workshop. I could not buy a single craftsman's day. But hot air was free, fire I could beg, and cloth, even scrap, even rag, I could maybe scrounge a piece at a time without anyone deciding I was worth stopping. I could build it slow, with my own hands, out of what fell off other people's work, and nobody had to give me permission for a thing that looked like a beggar collecting rags. Cheap, mine, and impossible to argue with. I'd said the three words to myself in the dark the night I thought of it and they were still the only three that held up in daylight.

What I did not have was the faintest idea how to actually do it.

---

I started with paper, except there's no paper, which I keep forgetting even now. There's papyrus, and papyrus is money, sheets of it, scraped and pressed and sold, and the household had exactly none lying around for a foreigner to set on fire. What they had was an old man's worth of accounts on wax and a few brittle scroll-ends too stained to keep, and I wasn't getting near those.

So the first model was nothing. Was rag. I begged Stichus for it.

The boy hauled water and ran errands and was ten years old and treated me, on the good days, like a half-broken dog he'd been told to feed. I'd spent the winter making him laugh, which is a thing you can do across a language if you're willing to be the joke, and I cashed some of that in. I wanted the scraps off the cloth the women cut, the trimmings, the bits too small to mend with, the ravelings that swept up off the floor. He thought I'd lost my mind, which was the going opinion, and he brought me a fistful of linen offcuts anyway because I'd promised him I'd show him a thing the gods do, and at ten a thing the gods do beats almost any reason not to.

Linen is wrong for this. I'd find that out the hard way at every scale, but the first time I found it out was small and almost gentle. I cut and I had nothing to cut with but a borrowed knife that I had to give back, and I tried to sew a little bag out of four scraps of linen the size of my hand, and it came out a sad puckered thing the size of a wineskin, and I held it over the household fire by the neck and felt the warm push up into it and the whole sorry article just sat in my hand and got hot. It didn't even try. Linen's heavy. The cloth weighs more than the warm air inside a thing that small can ever lift, and I knew that, I think, in some back part of my head, I'd just hoped the knowing was wrong.

Stichus laughed at me. Fair.

But it had pushed. That was the thing. Held over the fire it had filled, gone taut, wanted to go somewhere and only failed because it was too small and too heavy, and I lay awake that night oddly steadied, because the failing had told me something true. Bigger. Lighter. The bag had to be huge and the skin of it had to weigh almost nothing, and those two wants pulled against each other, and somewhere in the pull was the whole problem I'd spend the next years losing to.

---

I needed something lighter than linen and there isn't anything, not here, not that I could get. No silk a beggar can touch, no oiled paper, no nothing. So I did the only clever thing I managed that whole first month, and I'm still a little proud of it, which tells you how thin the month was for pride.

I made it out of the thinnest stuff in the building, which was the worn-through cloth nobody would miss, the parts of old tunics so threadbare you could see the fire through them. Stichus and I picked them apart for a week. I built the second model the size of a feed sack, light panels of see-through cloth, and I shaped it like the campfire smoke wanted to go, narrow at the bottom where the heat came in and belling out above. No equations. I want to be clear there were no equations, I had the vague idea that a shape like a teardrop standing on its small end was right because that was the shape smoke made when it rose calm, and that was the entire engineering, a memory of smoke.

I needed sustained heat under it and an open mouth to let the heat in, and that was the part that nearly ended the project on a charge of arson. You can't lift a hot-air bag with a brazier you carry over. The air cools the second it's in the bag and the thing wants to come down as fast as it went up, so you have to keep feeding warm into the mouth while it's tethered low, and the only way I had to feed warm was open flame, and open flame under a bag of see-through rag is precisely as stupid as it sounds.

The first time the thin model went up it was the best moment I'd had in the world in over a year.

I did it in the little yard behind the workshop, early, before the household was properly awake, with Stichus holding a string tied to the bag's mouth and me feeding a small fire under it through a cone of green wood I'd rigged to funnel the heat up. It took forever. I burned my knuckles twice getting the fire to behave, and the bag just hung there filling, going round, going taut, and then the string came up tight in the kid's fist and the whole light see-through thing stood up off the ground and pulled, lifted, hung in the morning air at the end of the line with the fire-shimmer rising into it, a made thing, my made thing, floating.

Stichus screamed. Good scream, the gods-do-a-thing scream. I started laughing like an idiot.

It flew for as long as I fed it and not one breath longer. The second the warm stopped coming the thing sagged and sank and settled back onto the dirt like it had never had a thought of leaving, and that taught me the cruelest thing about the whole project in one motion. The going up is free. The staying up is the whole fight. It's the heat, the endless feeding of heat, the fire you have to keep alive under a thing made of cloth, and there is no version of that which is safe and the model had just shown me both halves of it at once, the lift and the leak, married together, the warm pouring in the bottom and pouring straight out through every gap in the thin cloth as fast as I could make it.

Heras came around the corner of the workshop while it was still up. He'd been sent for the household, some morning complaint, and he stood there in the gate with his satchel and watched the rag bag hang at the end of the string over a small dangerous fire in a yard that wasn't mine, and I watched his face do the thing his face did, which was nothing, deliberately nothing, the held-flat nothing of a man not letting you see he's interested.

He said it was very pretty. He said it the way you'd tell a child his mud horse was very lifelike. And then he asked me, in the dry voice, whether I planned to burn down the household I was a guest in, and I looked at the green-wood cone scorched black on its inner face and the cloth gone brown at the bag's mouth where the heat licked it, and I let the fire die, because he had a point and the point was on fire.

---

The household had a point too, and they made it less kindly.

You cannot run an open flame in a city of wooden lofts without people noticing, and Rome's towns burn, everyone's terrified of fire, and rightly, the whole place goes up like kindling on a dry month. The woman who ran the kitchen, who I'd never gotten a name out of and who ran more of the place than the master did, came out the second morning when she smelled the smoke wrong and saw the scorch and the rag and the foreigner crouched over a fire too close to the wall, and she did not need Latin to make herself understood. I lost the yard. I lost the morning fire. I was told, in words and a thrown wooden spoon, that if I wanted to play with flames I could do it well away from anything that mattered, which meant the open lot past the midden where they slaughtered, and it meant I'd lost the easy fire and now had to beg coals and carry them.

It cost me, all of it, in the small steady way that everything cost me there. I'd burned through a month of Stichus's goodwill and a fistful of rag and the kitchen woman's tolerance, and what I had to show was a feed-sack of worn-out cloth that flew for as long as I fed a fire I wasn't allowed to feed anymore. I had a blister gone to a blood-blister across two knuckles where I'd grabbed the hot cone. Small wound. I cleaned it the way I'd taught Heras to clean things, boiled water gone cool, washed it, and watched it like a hawk for the red line, because I'd seen what a cut did here and I wasn't going to die of a balloon before the balloon could do anything.

But the thin model proved the idea, and now I had to make it big, and big meant linen.

---

There's no getting around the linen. Worn-out rag flies because it weighs nothing, and it weighs nothing because it's half holes, and half holes leaks the heat out as fast as you put it in, so a rag balloon can only ever be a toy on a string. A real one, a big one, one that might someday carry more than its own weight, has to hold the heat, and holding the heat means a tight weave, and a tight weave means linen, real linen, the good cloth, which weighs like sin and leaks at every seam and burns.

And linen, the good cloth, I could not just have. This was the wall again, the one the whole project ran into, the one a free man with a coin never even sees. I needed an arm's length of decent linen and the household was not going to hand a length of decent cloth to the foreigner to set on fire. So I bargained, and I bargained from a position of owning nothing, which is the worst seat at any table.

I went to Heras. He was the only man who'd ever paid full price for my mind and the only one with a denarius to his name who'd hear me out, and I made the pitch over a sick man's leg one afternoon, in pieces, in my bad Latin, with my hands. I told him I needed a length of close-woven linen and grease to seal it and rope and a brazier I could leave standing, and I told him why, the whole why, the flying, the man-cannot-call-the-sky-a-lie of it, and I watched him decide I was a fool and decide, separately, that he wanted to see it anyway.

He didn't fund me out of belief. I want to be honest about Heras, because the temptation is to make him generous. He bought me a single length of linen and a pot of mutton grease because he was curious the way a cat is curious, and because, I think, he'd watched the rag bag hang in the air over that yard and some part of the man who'd spent forty-five years explaining the world wanted, against his judgment, to see whether the Thulean could make fire fly. He gave me the cloth and he gave me a lecture with it. Linen burns, he said. Grease burns worse. You will weigh it down sealing it and then you will set the seal alight and then you will have a heavy bag that is also on fire, and I want, he said, to be standing well back when you do.

He was right about all of it. He was right about every single part of it, which I'd learn over the next hour in the slaughter-lot past the midden.

---

I sealed the linen with mutton grease rubbed in warm, because I half-remembered that you have to close the weave or the heat pours through, and grease was the only thing I had. It worked, in the sense that the cloth went from cloth to a heavy, stinking, greasy hide that the heat couldn't pour through. It worked the way Heras said it would, which is to say it solved the leak by making everything else worse. The sealed panels weighed nearly double. The bag I sewed out of them, big as a man could reach around, sat on the dirt of the lot like a dead animal, and when I got the brazier going under it through the green-wood cone and fed it and fed it and fed it, it filled, slowly, sullenly, and it did not lift. It got hot. It strained. The whole greasy mass swelled and trembled and wanted to and could not, because I'd added so much weight in the sealing that the warm air inside, even good and hot, could not carry the skin that held it.

So I made the fire bigger. Of course I made the fire bigger. That's what you do, you're cold so you build the fire up, you want lift so you make more heat, and a man with no equations and a thing that almost flew is going to feed the fire until the thing flies or stops him. The flame came up off the brazier higher than I meant, into the cone, up into the mouth of the bag, and the greased linen at the lip caught.

It didn't explode. I'd half feared a fireball and got something quieter and worse. The grease at the mouth lit with a low blue-edged crawl and the flame walked up the inside of the bag along the seam, a finger of fire climbing a wall, and the linen browned and split where it burned and the heat I'd worked an hour to trap came howling out the new hole and the whole thing collapsed onto the brazier and that was the end of an afternoon and a length of Heras's linen and a pot of grease and most of my faith in myself, all of it gone up in a stink of burning fat over a slaughter-lot, with me beating at it with a board and breathing smoke that smelled like the worst day at a kitchen.

I put it out. I burned my forearm doing it, a real burn this time, a line of shiny red up the inside of my arm where I'd reached past the flame, and it blistered and it hurt for a week and I cleaned it and watched it and it was the first time the project drew enough blood to scare me. I sat in the dirt next to the smoking ruin of it and I was eighteen and a long way from anywhere and I'd just set fire to a thing I'd begged for, and I wanted, badly, to be done.

Heras had, as promised, been standing well back. He came and looked at the wreck with the expression of a man whose prediction has been confirmed in every particular and who is too decent to enjoy it more than a little. He looked at my arm and made me show it to him and grunted at the cleaning, which from Heras is praise. And he said, in the dry voice, that the air had carried the fire very well. Which it had. The fire had flown beautifully. That was the joke and the wound of it at once.

---

I understood, sitting in that lot, the size of the thing I'd walked into, and I understood it as a list of walls, each one taller than I was.

The cloth had to be light or it wouldn't lift, and it had to be sealed and heavy or it wouldn't hold the heat, and those two could not both be true at my scale or with my materials and I did not know the trick that made them both true, if there was one. The seams leaked. The seal burned. The heat had to be sustained and the only heat I had was open flame, and open flame under cloth is a fire waiting for a reason. Bigger only made all of it worse, more cloth, more weight, more seam, more flame. A toy on a string I'd already managed. The real thing was an engineering problem stacked three deep, and I had no money, no workshop, no cloth, no metal, no hands but a ten-year-old's and a doctor's bemused curiosity, and I was not free to go get any of it.

I needed money. That was the bottom of it. I needed a free man's ability to walk into a market and buy good cloth by the bolt and a craftsman's day by the dozen, and the awful circular joke of it was that the balloon was supposed to be the thing that bought me the money, and the balloon needed money to build, and I sat in the slaughter-lot with my arm on fire and saw the loop close around me clean as a noose.

But.

I'd had it up. The little one, the rag one, it had flown, and the failing of the big one didn't unsay that. Hot air rises, I'd watched it carry a made thing off the ground at the end of a string, and the rest, the cloth, the seal, the heat, the money, the hands, all of it was a problem with a shape, and a problem with a shape is a thing you can chip at, year on year, the way you chip at a language until one morning you can hold a conversation. I'd done that once already. I'd walked into this place with no words and clawed my way to bad Latin a humiliation at a time, and the balloon was going to be the same, a long grind of small failures, scorched cloth and begged coals and a doctor lending me a length of linen because he wanted to see the trick.

---

I built one more before the spring was out, because I couldn't not.

Smaller than the burned one and bigger than the toy, a modest thing of linen I'd lightened by giving up half the seal, hoping I could split the difference between leaking and burning and find one breath of cloth that did neither. I made it in the lot in the long evening light with Stichus holding the line and Heras leaning on the midden wall pretending he had somewhere better to be, and I fed the brazier careful this time, low, no higher than the cone, feeding the warm in steady the way you feed a shy fire.

It filled. It went round and stood up and the linen drew taut and held, and the warm went in and stayed in long enough, this once, this one time, and the line came up tight in the boy's small fist and the bag lifted, the whole heavy honest linen weight of it, up off the trodden dirt of the slaughter-lot and into the gold evening air, a hand's height, then a forearm, then higher than the boy's head, hanging there on the warm with the fire-shimmer pouring up into it and my heart going like a fist on a door.

It held the air for the length of maybe ten breaths. I counted them. Six, seven, eight, the linen straining at the seam I'd sewn by lamplight with a borrowed needle, nine, and on the tenth the seam let go, a long soft tearing I heard before I understood it, the warm air punching out through the opening seam in a gust I could feel on my face, and the bag folded along the rip and came down, not fast, not a crash, just a tired settling, sinking back through the gold light it had climbed, down past the boy's wide face and onto the dirt, where it lay breathing out the last of its heat into the evening, torn open along one seam, still warm, still smelling of mutton fat and smoke, done.

I stood over it in the slaughter-lot with my burned arm and my fistful of string and I watched the warm air leave it through the tear, and I knelt down and put my hand against the cloth to feel the heat go.

---

## PART TWO — The Man Who Flew

---

# Chapter 7: Iterations

The seam was always where it died.

I figured that much out before the spring rains stopped, standing in the slaughter-lot with a flap of scorched linen in my good hand and the smell of old blood baked into the dirt under me. Every bag I made wanted to fly. Heat went in, the cloth bellied out, the whole stupid thing tugged at the string like it had somewhere to be. Then a seam would let go with a sound like someone tearing a sheet, and my afternoon was on the ground.

So I went after the seams. That was the spring.

A normal person sews two edges together and trusts the thread. I had done that, and the thread held fine. The cloth around the thread did not. Hot air found the little gaps the needle punched, every stitch a tiny door, and leaked out through a thousand doors faster than I could heat it. I needed the edge itself tighter than the field of the cloth, which should have been obvious and was not, to me, for about three weeks.

What got me there was watching an old man patch a grain sack down past the midden. He folded the mouth of the sack over on itself twice before he ran his cord through, so the strain pulled on a triple thickness and not on one. I stood there long enough that he looked up at me with the flat patient hate of a man who would like the foreigner to go be strange somewhere else.

I folded my seams after that. Edge over, edge over again, sew through all three, then lay the mutton grease down the inside of the fold so the cloth on either side of the holes was sealed before air could reach the thread. It was slow. My fingers cramped. The needle went under the nail of my left thumb once and I bled all over a quarter-libra of linen I could not afford to waste and used it anyway, a brown smear up one panel like the bag had its own old wound.

The folded seam held. I want to be clear about how small a victory that was. The bag still leaked everywhere a seam crossed another seam, at the nose and at the throat, because three folded edges meeting at a point make a knot of cloth no grease can drown. But the long runs stopped failing, and a thing that had given me ten breaths in the autumn gave me, on a gray morning in what I think was April, something past a hundred. I counted them out loud like an idiot, in Latin, because counting was the one thing my Latin did cleanly.

Heras came out near the end of it, picking his way around the drying blood with the hem of his tunic pinched up in two fingers.

"You are talking to it," he said.

"I'm counting."

"You are counting at it. There is a difference." He stopped a careful distance from the brazier. Since the autumn he had stopped coming close to the fire, after watching me beat out a burning bag with my forearm and wear the shiny red line up the inside of it for a week. He did not trust the project not to do that again. He was right not to.

The bag sat fat above the green-wood cone, straining, lit yellow from underneath. A hundred and forty breaths. It was beautiful, and it was going to come down the second I let the fire die, and we both knew it, and that was the whole next problem, which I had no idea how to solve and had been not-thinking about for a month.

"How long can a man hold a fire under it," Heras said. Not a question. He had a way of saying the thing I was avoiding as though he had just thought of it himself.

"As long as there's a man and a fire."

"And the man stands in the smoke holding a cone of green wood, and the cloth above him is grease, and the fire is open and below all of it." He let that sit. "You burned your arm putting one of these out. You have, I think, two arms."

I let the fire die. The bag came down over the cone like a slow gray ghost lying down to sleep, and I caught it before it touched the coals.

---

The heat was the second war, and it was worse than the seams, because a seam I could see.

Here is the thing nobody tells you about fire, or that everybody knows and I had never needed to know: there is no dial. I kept reaching, in my head, for the knob on my mom's gas range, the little click and the blue ring you could turn down to a whisper or up to a roar, and there was nothing under my hand but coals and air and the choice between more and less in the crudest possible terms. More meant flame, and flame meant the open mouth of a greased linen bag hanging directly over it, which is a sentence that should end with somebody getting hurt and usually did.

What I wanted was to put the heat inside something. Carry the fire in a box, so the box could be hot and the bag above it could be merely warm. I built three versions of that box and I am not proud of any of them.

The first was a cracked cooking pot Stichus smuggled out with the bottom already gone, that I packed with coals and held up into the throat of the bag on a forked stick. It worked, a little, for about as long as it took the bottom of the bag to char where the pot kissed it. A contained fire is still a fire, and clay throws its heat in every direction including up into the cloth eight inches above it. I scorched a brown ring into a panel I had folded and greased for two days, and said a word in English Heras asked me to translate, and I told him it meant the same thing in Thule that it did here.

The second version had a lid, a tin of pierced bronze that an old soldier sold me for half the coppers I had, which were Heras's coppers, which I will get to. The holes let the heat up and were supposed to keep the sparks down. They did neither well. A spark is small and a hole is a hole, and one evening a single ember went up through the bronze and into the throat of the bag and I watched a glowing orange dot crawl up the inside of the cloth like it was reading the seam I had sewn, looking for the way in.

That was the fire I did not put out in time.

It was not a big fire, as fires go. The bag went fast, the way they all went once they caught, a fat gray belly turned to a falling sheet of flame in the time it takes to stand up. I had been working closer to the shed that evening because the wind off the river was bad and I wanted the wall to break it, and a sheet of flame does not care about your plans for it. It came down across the lean-to where the women stacked the cut faggots for the fleece fires, the dry brushwood took, and for about thirty seconds I thought I was going to burn down a piece of the household I was a prisoner in.

I beat at it with the wet fleece I had soaking for exactly this and screamed for Stichus and Stichus screamed for everyone, and it was the kitchen woman who put it out, arriving at a dead run with a tub of fleece-water and throwing it in one motion like she had been waiting all spring for the excuse. The brushwood hissed and went black. A corner of the lean-to roof was gone. The back of my right hand had a fresh burn where I had grabbed at something I should not have, two fingers' worth, blistered up white by morning, and I cleaned it with cooled boiled water out of pure stubborn habit while it throbbed.

The kitchen woman stood over me in the wet dark and did not throw the spoon this time. She looked at the black corner of the roof, and then at me, and said something low and fast that I caught maybe half of. The half I caught was that the next fire I made near her buildings, she would put me on it.

I believed her. I moved everything out past the midden to the far rail of the slaughter-lot, built the fire on bare dirt inside a ring of river stones like a kid at camp, and did not work when the wind was up. The third firepot, the one I am least ashamed of, was a double thing, a small bronze cup of coals nested inside a larger clay pot with a hand's width of air between them, so the clay stayed cool enough to hold and the heat came off the top in a column instead of a cloud. It still could not lift a bag from the ground, too heavy to hang and too weak to fill. But I learned something carrying it around the lot in the evenings, watching the column of shimmer rise off it dead straight in still air and lean and break up the second a breath of wind crossed it.

The trick was not going to be carrying the fire up with the bag. It was going to be filling the bag on the ground, full and fat and hot, off a fire I could make as big and as ugly as I wanted because nothing hung over it, and then sealing it and getting it away before it cooled. Fill, then release. It would not stay up long. It did not have to. It had to go up high, in front of people, on its own, with no man and no fire under it, so nobody could say there were strings.

I tested it small first, with the threadbare see-through sack from the autumn that Heras had called very pretty. I filled it off a stupid big greedy fire I built in the stone ring with no cone, the open mouth of the sack held down over the flames on two green sticks until it fought me, until it was a hot round thing trying to leave. Then I pinched the throat shut with a twist of wet cord and let go.

It went up. No string. It went up over the rail and over the midden and a good way into the gray evening sky, turning slowly, the bottom still glowing faint where the air inside was hottest, and it kept going up for a while after I was sure it should have stopped, and then it slowed and leveled and began, gently, to come down a long way off, sinking into someone else's field beyond the lot where I never found it.

I had not breathed in a while. I started again.

Heras was next to me. I had not heard him come out. He was looking at the empty sky where the thing had been with an expression I had never seen on him, and he is a man who works hard at having no expressions.

"There were no strings," he said quietly.

"No."

"I watched for them. There were none." He was not asking me to confirm it. He was telling himself the thing he had just seen, out loud, because he did not yet believe his own eyes. He had spent a year refusing to be impressed by me. He had taken apart the numbers and the invisible creatures and the maps, found the seam in each one and pulled. There was no seam in this. A thing had risen into the sky with nobody holding it.

He pulled his eyes down at last and put the dry irony back on like a coat. "It does not stay up."

"No."

"It goes up beautifully and then it wanders off and falls in a field and a peasant beats out a fire in his barley and curses your gods." He had recovered all the way now. "You have made a thing that flies away from you. Congratulations. It is the most useless miracle I have ever seen."

"It's a year of work," I said, "to throw a sack at the sky."

"Yes." He looked at me sideways. "And men have paid more for less. You think you have failed to make a thing that stays. You have made a thing no one in this city has ever seen, that needs no explaining and cannot be argued with, that any drunk in the Subura would walk a mile and pay a copper to watch." He turned back toward the buildings. "The trouble is you cannot show it to anyone, because you belong to a household that would prefer you stop setting it on fire."

---

The household I belonged to had an owner I had still never properly met. I knew there was a man. Coins came down through the kitchen woman to Stichus to me in amounts that made it clear someone above her had decided I was worth feeding and not worth much more. Heras dealt with him, or with a steward of his, in rooms I never saw. The linen I sewed and the grease I burned and the half-libra I bled on had all been bought on credit against the idea that the strange northern man in the loft would one day be worth the outlay, and I had spent a year proving only that he could turn good cloth into ash.

That spring the city changed, and even out past the midden I could feel it change.

The cart traffic that ran all night grew until I could not sleep for the groan of axles, and in the mornings the foot traffic ran the other way, toward Rome, whole families in clean tunics with the look of people walking toward a thing they did not want to miss. The emperor was coming, Stichus told me in his fast gutter Latin I had finally gotten good enough to mostly follow. He had won his wars on some river at the edge of the world and was coming home at last, and the city was going up the road to see him.

I had been in this world a year and never once been inside Rome. I had seen it from the field the first morning, the whole vast pale sprawl down the valley with the Colosseum gleaming new on its edge, and then I had been marched the other way, into a town, into a cell, into a loft, and never gone down.

Heras took me in. He said it was so I would stop asking. I think he wanted to watch my face.

We went on foot with the crowd, down out of the hills and along the river road, and the city came up around us the way a wave comes up over your head, suddenly all the way. I had done the math from the field, a million people, the largest thing humans had ever built, and been quietly proud of the math. The math did not survive the gate.

It was the smell first, a wall of it, smoke and bread and shit and fish and ten thousand cooking fires and something sweet and rotten under all of it, and then the sound, which had no bottom to it, a roar made of a hundred thousand smaller roars that never resolved into anything. The buildings went up five and six floors of brick and wood, leaning over the lanes until the sky was a crooked river of light overhead, laundry strung between them, faces in every window. People packed and shoving and loud, slaves with litters shouting a path, a chained line of other men walked somewhere with placards hung on them and nobody looking twice, a fountain roaring in a square with a crowd six deep around it. I had thought I understood this place. I had drawn it. I had a number for it. I stood inside it and the number meant nothing, and for the first time since the field I felt the full size of how far down at the bottom I was, one foreigner with burned hands in the largest city that had ever existed, and nobody in it knew my name or my mother's name or the word for the thing I had thrown at the sky.

The emperor came in modestly, which is the thing everyone could not stop saying, modestly, modestly, passed down the crowd like the best joke. I expected a god on a chariot. I got a big plain soldier in a clean toga walking on his own two feet through the gate like any man, stopping to take the hands of the senators who came to meet him as though they were equals and he was glad to see them. The crowd around me went up in a noise I felt in my chest more than heard. A man near me was weeping. I did not understand what it cost a city to have an emperor who walked, after whatever the last one had been, and I did not need to understand it to feel the want in the crowd, the enormous shared hunger to be ruled by someone who came in on foot.

And in the press near a temple front, where men in togas were doing something slow and formal over a low altar, I saw the one who saw me.

He was an old man, gray, with the look of someone important standing very still while younger men did the work, and he had something dark and wet laid open on a board in front of him, an animal's insides, and he was reading them the way Heras read a wound. A diviner. His back was half to the crowd and his attention down on the board, and then for no reason at all he lifted his head and looked across two hundred people directly at me, at my burned hands, or my barbarian clothes that I still wore under a borrowed cloak because I had nothing else. He looked at me the way you look at a thing that has appeared in the entrails that should not be in the entrails. He did not smile. He did not frown. He held it for a count of three, and I felt every hair on my arms stand up, and then a younger priest stepped between us and the old man's head went back down to his board.

"Who is that," I asked Heras. My voice came out wrong.

Heras followed my eyes and his face shut like a door. "A haruspex. A reader of the liver. An old and powerful one." He took my elbow and turned me into the crowd, away. "Aulus Vibenius. Do not point at him. Do not let him point at you." We went four streets before he said anything else, and when he did it was lighter, on purpose. "You wished to see Rome. You have seen it. It is loud and it stinks and a great many people have come to watch one man walk through a gate. Are you satisfied."

I was not. I kept feeling the old man's eyes on the back of my neck the whole way out of the city, and I have never entirely stopped.

---

It was Heras who said it on the road home, in the dusk, with the crowd thinning behind us and the river going copper.

"The whole city is in the streets," he said, "for the next month at least. Every man in Rome wants a thing to talk about over his wine that no other man saw first. Games, processions, a hundred fools swallowing fire in every square." He let it hang in the dark. "And there is a thing that flies up into the sky on its own, with no strings, owned by a household that would like very much to be talked about."

"It doesn't stay up," I said, because I had to be the one to say it now.

"It does not have to. It has to go up, once, high, where a crowd can see it and a man with money can stand near the man who owns it and say, that one, that one is mine." He was quiet a moment. "I have spoken to him about you. The man whose roof you sleep under. I told him you waste his cloth and burn his sheds and that he should sell you to the mines."

"Thanks."

"And then I told him what I watched go up over the midden this evening with no string on it, and that I would swear to it, a Greek physician of Pergamon, before any man he named." I could hear, in the dark, that he was pleased with himself, and under the pleasure something more careful. "He is not a clever man. But he can smell when a thing is worth money before he understands why, the way a dog smells the kitchen. He wants to see it himself. And if he sees what I saw, he will want every man of standing in Rome to see it standing next to him."

So that was the shape of it. A year of burned cloth and two burned hands and a thing that flew away and fell in a field, and now a city full of people with nothing to do but be impressed, and a man I had never met who owned me and was about to decide that the Thulean and his sky-sack might be the most valuable thing he had ever bought on credit. He would put me in front of a crowd to prove it, because a crowd that gasped was worth more to him than any number I could say.

I did not sleep that night. I lay in the loft with my burned hand throbbing and the dead phone, the cold useless glass of it, in my fist where I keep it when I cannot sleep. One bag. One fill. One throw at the sky with the whole of Rome watching and no string and no second try. If it went up, I was worth something. If it caught on the fire I had to fill it from, in front of all of them, I was a barbarian who had set himself alight for their amusement, and the man who owned me would remember the cloth and the sheds and the mines.

I got up before light and went out past the midden to the cold fire ring and started sewing the best bag I had ever made, folding every edge twice, by feel in the dark, because the demonstration was coming whether the bag was ready or not, and I wanted it to be the seams that held this time.

---

# Chapter 8: Fire That Flies

The bag was the size of a man hung head-down.

I want that to be the first true thing, because everything else that day got bigger in the telling and I have spent years now hearing the bag described as wide as a house. It was not. Folded, it was an armful. Open and full, it stood maybe eleven feet from the mouth to the round of the top, a fat teardrop on its small end, pale gray linen gone darker where the mutton grease had soaked the seams, and it smelled like the inside of a cold lamp. I had stitched it in nine days. The seams were folded twice and run twice and greased over the run, the way I had finally taught my own clumsy fingers to do them, and the mouth was a hoop of green ash-wood I had bent and lashed so the cloth would gape open and hold its shape over the heat instead of sucking shut. There was no string. That was the whole point and the whole terror. There would be no string.

They had given me the corner of a square for it. Not a good square. Heras told me on the walk in that the good squares had been spoken for since the emperor came, fire-eaters and a man with a trained bear and a Syrian who could throw eleven knives, and that ours was a hard packed open lot off the lower end of the Subura where two lanes ran together, a place that filled with whoever the better squares spilled. There was a butcher's block at one edge with the flies on it and a fountain at the other, roaring, the same bottomless fountain roar I had heard at the gate a few weeks before, and overhead the insulae leaned in until the sky was the usual crooked bright river between them. I stood in our corner at the wrong hour of the morning with my heart going like a fist on a door and thought, in plain English, in my own head where no one could hear it, this is the dumbest place in the world to light a fire.

I had two helpers and neither was mine. Stichus, the water-boy, ten years old and gray with fear, holding the coal-pot against his chest like it might run. And a big slow household slave the steward had sent, a man named Davus, to do the lifting and, I understood without being told, to make sure the household's barbarian did not bolt into the crowd. Heras stood off to one side in his good cloak with his arms folded, being a Greek physician of Pergamon, a man whose word a buyer might trust. And somewhere behind me, in the press, was the man.

I had still never seen him. I want that on the record too. A year I had burned his cloth and slept under his roof and never once laid eyes on the equestrian who owned the air I breathed, and I did not lay eyes on him that morning either, not knowing it. The steward had come at dawn, a dry narrow man with a tablet, looked at the bag the way you look at a dog you have been told bites, and said the master would watch from a place where he could be seen watching, and that I was to make it go up and not make a fool of the household, in that order. Then he went and stood at the edge with a knot of other men in good tunics, and one of them was the man, and I did not know which, and I had a fire to build.

---

You cannot fill a balloon quietly.

I had learned that over the midden, where there was no one to perform for. On the ground the thing is dead cloth and you are a man crouched over a fire feeding a sack, and it takes time, real time, more time than a crowd will give you before it decides you are a fraud and goes to see the bear. I had counted it out at home. From cold bag to lift was something past two hundred breaths if the fire was right, and the fire is never right at first.

So I did the cheap trick first, the one I had worked out lying awake. I had Davus and Stichus hold the mouth-hoop open between them, low, and I built the fire under it small and fast, dry split kindling I had begged and hoarded for a week, and the moment the bag had a belly of warm air in it and started to want to stand on its own, before it could lift, I had them walk it up. Just up. Two men standing, lifting the hoop to head height with the half-warm bag swaying above it like a drunk getting to his feet, so the crowd could see the shape of the thing come up out of nothing, see it want the sky before it had the sky. It is a magician's move. It buys you the two hundred breaths.

It worked better than it had any right to. The bag came up gray and shivering and huge over our heads, eleven feet of it nodding in the air off the fountain, and the corner of the square that had been ignoring the foreign idiot with the kindling went quiet in a patch and then less quiet, and people stopped. I heard them stop. There is a sound a crowd makes when it decides to look at you and it is the worst sound in the world if you are not ready and the best if you are, and I was somewhere in between, sweating through the borrowed tunic they had finally given me so I would not stand there in my witch-clothes.

Then the long part. The fire under the open mouth, fed and fed, the heat going up into the bag, Stichus white and rigid with the coal-pot, Davus holding the hoop and turning his face from the heat, and me crouched in the smoke with my burned hand, the old shiny line up my right forearm and the newer one across the back of the same hand, both of them screaming in the warmth, feeding the fire and watching the cloth and counting. Sixty breaths. The bag fattened. A hundred. It pulled up against Davus's hands and he had to lean down to hold the hoop on the ground. A man in the crowd shouted something I did not catch and others laughed and the laugh had an edge that could go either way. A hundred and forty. The grease-smell of the seams. I had a sick clear thought, the one I always had now over an open fire with greased linen above it, the lip, watch the lip, the lip is what kills you, and I made Davus tip the hoop a half-inch off true so no part of the cloth sat in the actual flame.

A hundred and eighty. The bag stood straight up off the hoop, straining, the whole pale body of it lit warm from inside by the fire under its mouth, and the crowd noise changed. It went up the scale. Two men do not hold a thing like that down for long once it truly wants up, and I felt the moment coming the way you feel a sneeze, and I shouted the one Latin word I had drilled into Davus and Stichus until they hated me.

Now.

They let go.

---

I have tried for years to say the next part right and I always fail, so here is the failure.

It went up. It went up the way nothing in their world went up, no smoke, no bird, no thrown thing that has to come down, it went up and kept going, a man-sized gray shape climbing straight off the fire into the river of sky between the insulae with no hand on it and no string, and it turned slowly as it rose the way they all do, showing its fat side and then its small end, and the whole square made one sound.

I cannot write the sound. It was not a cheer. A cheer has words in it somewhere. This had no words. It came up out of a few hundred people at once and it was the sound you would make if the dead sat up, a kind of open vowel of the body, and under it, fast, before the gasp was even finished, it began to come apart into the other things. Delight, yes. A woman near the fountain was laughing and pointing and shaking the man next to her. Children screaming the good scream. But other voices already coming up through it, harder, and I knew enough Latin by then to catch the shapes of them as they went past my ears like thrown stones.

What god. Whose is it. What man does this. And the one Heras had warned me about, the one that can get a foreigner torn apart in a Subura lot before the household guards can reach him, the word that turns a marvel into a working, a witch's working, said sharp and afraid, and then said again, picked up.

The bag rose maybe sixty, seventy feet, clear of the rooflines, out into the open bright. And then it did what it always did, what I had not been able to fix in a year and was not going to fix that morning, because the truth under all the wonder was that I had no way to keep the heat in it once it left the fire. It stopped climbing. It hung. The warm air in it was already cooling and the cloth was already losing the fight, and the thing that had gone up like a soul going up began, slowly, in front of all of them, to come back.

It did not come back the way it went up. Going up it had been straight and certain. Coming down it was a drunk again, sagging on one side, tipping its mouth, and the little morning wind that runs down those lanes off the river took it and pushed it sideways, downhill, toward the leaning fronts of the insulae, and the gasp turned in an instant into a different noise, a scatter and shriek, because a thing that flies is a wonder and a thing that flies at your face is a fire coming at your house.

It clipped a rooftop. The small end of it dragged across the clay tiles of a five-story front, caught for half a breath on a line of strung washing, tore a hanging tunic loose and dropped it, and the bag, half collapsed now, slumped over the roof edge and down the front of the building in slow folds. There was still fire-warmed air and grease in it. A woman two floors up was screaming and hauling in her washing, and a man on the street below was beating at a tumble of cloth that had come down half-alight, a corner of my seam smoldering where it had finally, after a year of trying to kill me, touched something it could burn. People scattered from under it. Someone went down and was hauled up. The smoldering corner caught a fold of the dropped washing and there was real flame for a moment, low, on the cobbles, and a dozen men stamping at it and shouting, and the bag, my best bag, nine days of folded seams, lay over the street in a gray heap with smoke coming off one edge of it.

No one had died. I knew that even then, watching, with my heart somewhere up in the place the bag had been. No one had died, a tunic was burned, a woman was screaming about her roof, a man had a scorch on his arm from the washing. By the standard of the thing it was nothing. By the standard of the square, where a few hundred people had just watched fire fly off a foreigner's hands and come down on a house, it was the worst possible nothing, because now the question hanging over the lot was not is it a wonder but what do we do with the man who made it, and that question, in a Roman crowd that has just been frightened, has an old and ugly answer.

I felt Davus's hand close on my arm. Not to comfort me. To hold me where I was, so I could not run, because the steward had told him to, and because running is the one thing that turns what god into kill it.

---

Then the crowd parted from the temple side, the way a crowd parts for someone it is afraid of in the other direction, and the old man came through it.

I knew him before I could have known him. Gray, still, important, walking slow while younger men cleared the way ahead, and the same flat reading look he had put on me across two hundred people at the gate weeks before. Aulus Vibenius. He had been here the whole time, I understood, standing at some good vantage with his college around him, watching the household's curiosity the way he had watched the entrails on his board, and now he came out into the open lot toward the heap of smoking cloth and toward me, and the noise of the square fell off around him until I could hear the fountain again and a baby crying somewhere and the man still stamping out the last of the washing.

He stopped at the fallen bag. He did not look at me yet. He crouched, an old man's careful crouch, and he put two fingers on a fold of the gray linen the way Heras put two fingers on a pulse, and he rubbed the grease of the seam between them, and he smelled it. He looked up the front of the building where it had dragged across the roof. He looked at the bit of sky it had come down out of. He took, I swear, more time than the crowd had given me to fill the thing, and the crowd gave him all of it and more, because he was the one man in the lot whose verdict could not be argued with, and every frightened person in that square was waiting to be told which thing they had just seen.

When he stood and turned to me he spoke loud enough to carry, in formal Latin, slow, so that I caught most of it and the crowd caught all of it, and I have turned it over so many nights that I no longer fully trust my own memory of the words. But the shape of it was this.

He said the sky had been shown a thing today. He said the gods send signs in many ways, in the liver and in the flight of birds, and that a fire that rose of itself and turned in the air and came down upon the city was a sign, and that he had read it. He let that sit. He looked at me, the burned hands, the wrong clothes under the borrowed tunic, the foreign face, the whole of what I was, and he said the foreigner from the cold edge of the world had sent fire up to the gods and the gods had let it rise, which no god does for a thing the gods hate. And then, in the same breath, in the same grave even voice, with no change in his face at all, he said that the gods had also turned the fire back down upon the rooftops of Rome, and that a wise city would consider, carefully, whether a gift had been shown to it or a warning.

That was all. He did not say bless it. He did not say burn it. He gave the crowd both doors and stood in the middle of them, an old man who had spent fifty years learning exactly how much to say and not one word past it, and he let four hundred frightened people decide for themselves which one they had walked through. And the genius of it, which I only understood later and hated, was that whichever way it went now, he had read it true. If they made me a wonder, he had blessed it first. If they tore me apart, he had warned them. The haruspex is never wrong. That is the whole craft.

The crowd took the gift door. Just. I felt it tip the way I had felt the bag tip, slow and then all at once, the laughing woman by the fountain louder now, men calling out, someone near me reaching to touch the hem of the borrowed tunic as though that did anything, the word that had been witch a minute ago turning over in mouths into something nearer to marvel, the gods let it rise, the gods let it rise, you cannot argue with the sky and you cannot argue with Aulus Vibenius. But he had left the other door open and I had felt the draft from it, and I stood there with Davus's hand on my arm and the smoke still going up off my best bag and understood, in a way I had not understood the night before sewing it, that going up had only made me visible. A man can be visible and dead in the same hour. I had spent a year wanting to be worth something and I had got my wish in a Subura lot with a priest reading my fire like a liver, and worth something, here, meant a thing other men decide what to do with.

Vibenius looked at me one more time before he went. The same flat three-count he had given me at the gate. Then a younger priest was at his elbow and he turned and the crowd opened for him and closed behind him and he was gone.

---

I did not get to gather up my bag.

I had crouched down over it, the smoke gone to nothing now, to fold the wet smoldered corner away from the good cloth, because nine days, because I would need the green hoop again whatever happened, because crouching over the work was the only thing in the lot that still made sense to my hands. Stichus was crying without sound, the coal-pot still clutched to him. Heras had come up beside me and was saying something low and dry, satisfied and wary at once, the same two things he was always both of. I do not remember what. I remember the grease and ash on my fingers and the way the crowd noise was sliding back toward festival, on to the next square, the bear, the knives, a thing seen and survived and now a thing to tell over wine.

Then there were sandals in front of me, planted, two pair, good leather, and I looked up the length of two clean tunics to two men I did not know, household men, broad, with the steward's dry narrow face appearing behind them. The steward did not look at the bag. He looked at me the way you look at a sum that has just come out larger than you wrote it.

He said the master would see me. He said it the way you would say a wall had decided to move. Not the steward, not Heras, not a messenger with a tablet at the workshop, none of the ways the household had spoken to me for a year through every mouth but the one. The master. The man whose cloth, the man whose roof, the man I had never seen and could not have picked out of the knot of good tunics at the edge of the lot. He would see me. Now. Today.

The two broad men did not touch me. They did not have to. They stood and waited while I closed my fist around the green hoop and the folded gray cloth and got up off my knees in the ash, with Heras already at my shoulder saying he would come, he would come as far as the door, and the fountain roaring, and the last of the smoke of my best bag thin and blue going up between the leaning houses into the same bright crooked river of sky I had just thrown it through, where it had hung for a moment with no string and no hand on it, in front of all of Rome, before it came down.

---

# Chapter 9: Macer

They walked me across the city with the bag still folded under my arm.

I want to say I noticed the route, the way a useful man notices things, so I could find it again. I noticed that the two broad men kept exactly one pace behind me and a half-pace out to either side, so that I walked at the point of a little triangle that was pretending not to be a little triangle. Heras walked at my shoulder and said nothing, which from Heras was a whole speech. The steward went ahead, and I followed the back of his neck up out of the poor lanes and along a street that climbed, where the fronts got quieter and the doors got heavier and the gutters stopped stinking quite so personally, and somewhere in there the festival noise fell off behind us like a thing I had dropped.

The door was oak, studded, set back under a little roof. A slave opened it before the steward touched it, which meant a slave had been standing behind it with his ear to the wood, waiting, all morning, for the sound of us. In this house people stood behind doors so that other people would never have to wait at them.

Inside it was cool and dim and smelled of beeswax and lamp soot and, under that, leather. A lot of leather. The smell of the place a saddle comes from. A floor of small black and white stones laid in a pattern, a square of hard noon light falling from an opening in the roof into a shallow basin of water in the middle of the room, and then the steward stopped and half turned and put his eyes on me with a meaning I could read in any language. Stand here. Do not speak first.

The room was full of business. I had pictured, on the walk, some kind of throne thing, a man waiting to look at me. There was no waiting. Along one side two clerks sat on stools with tablets on their knees, styluses going, a man in a traveling cloak reciting numbers that another man wrote down, a slave crossing the bright square of floor with a tray and crossing back without it, and all of it moving around one chair the way water moves around a stone.

The man in the chair was not looking at me. He was looking at the tablet the slave had brought him, holding it out at arm's length the way men do when their eyes have started to go and they will die before they admit it. He was big through the shoulders and going soft over it, gray cropped close, a face that had been out in weather for a lot of years and had the broken veins to show it, a jaw like the end of a beam. He wore a plain tunic, good wool, no stripe on it, because an equestrian wears a narrow stripe and this one could not be bothered, or wanted you to notice that he could not be bothered. A ring on his hand, gold, heavy, and a smear of dried something on the side of his thumb that I think was ink and might have been blood from a butchered animal, and either way he had not washed it off.

He read the tablet. He did not hurry. He let me stand in the middle of his floor with my burned hands and my ash and my folded ruined balloon while the clerks scratched, and I understood that this was the first thing he was going to teach me, before a word: that his time was worth money and mine was worth whatever he decided to pay for it, and that the gap between those two facts was the entire room.

Then he handed the tablet back without looking at the slave who took it, and he looked at me.

It was not a kind look and it was not a cruel one. It was the look the steward had given the bag in the lot, only older at it, and wider, taking in the whole sum at once. He looked at the cloth under my arm. He looked at my boots, the last unmistakable thing on me, machine-stitched, soled in a black stuff no cobbler in the world could make, and his eyes stopped on them the way everyone's did. He looked at my face. Then he said something to the steward, sideways, not bothering to lower his voice because I was furniture, and the only word I caught clean was a number, and it was a big one.

The steward answered low. Macer grunted.

"You," he said, and now it was to me, and his Latin came slow and loud and a little too clearly, the way you talk to a child or a dog or a foreigner you suspect of being stupid. "You are the one who burned down a quarter of the Subura this morning."

I knew enough to know what he was doing and not enough to do anything clever back. "No one burned," I said. My Latin came out the way it always did under pressure, the cases sliding, the v turning to w in my mouth before I could stop it. "One tunic. One roof. No man."

"One roof." He turned a hand over. The ring caught the light. "Do you know what a roof costs, in the Subura, when a foreigner sets fire to it in front of four hundred witnesses and a priest?"

"No."

"No." He seemed to like that. Something moved at the corner of his mouth that was not a smile, a man tasting a sum he was going to enjoy. "Neither do they. That is the beauty of it. By tomorrow the roof will have been a whole house, and the day after that it will have been three, and a child in it, and the foreigner will have called the fire down with a word. You have made me famous before noon. I have had two men at my door already this morning wanting to know what I keep in my workshop, and one of them was a senator's freedman, which is worse than a senator." He shifted his weight in the chair. The wood complained. "I bought you for less than I pay for a season's mules. Did you know that?"

"No," I said again, because it was true.

"A scholar from the edge of the world," he said, and he said the words the way you'd hold up a piece of cloth a man had sold you as silk to see if the light went through it. "Draws maps. Knows the new figures. Heras here told my steward there was a madman in a country jail who could divide numbers in his head and had drawn the whole world on a tablet, and that for the price of a mule I could find out whether he was a marvel or a lunatic, and either way it would be cheap." He glanced at Heras, and there was a whole long history in the glance, money owed in both directions. "He did not mention you would cost me a roof."

Heras spoke for the first time, dry, careful, in the register of a man who has done this before. "He did not mention it because it had not happened. I sold you the sum, Macer. The sum was: a strange foreigner, cheap, possibly worth nothing, possibly worth a great deal, and a year to find out which. The year is up." He let that sit the exact way the haruspex had let things sit, and I thought, they all do this, the whole city talks in pauses. "You found out which this morning. In front of four hundred witnesses and a priest."

The clerks had stopped scratching. The man with the numbers had gone quiet. I understood, slowly, with the back of my neck, that the room had been arranged so that the room could hear this.

Macer looked at me for a while.

"Sit him down," he said to no one, and a stool appeared under me, pushed by a girl I had not seen come in, and I sat because my knees were glad of it. "Not you," he said to Heras. "You I trust about as far as I can throw a Greek, which is exactly as far as you have been useful and no further, and you have been useful, so stand there and be useful." Heras inclined his head a fraction, a man accepting a compliment that had a knife folded inside it. Macer turned back to me.

"Here is where we are, Thulean." First time he used it. He liked it; I could hear that he liked it, a word he could say with his whole mean mouth. "This morning you stopped being a thing I forgot I owned. That is bad for you. When you were a madman who drew maps, you cost me a mule and nobody knew your name, and that was the safest you will ever be in your life, and it is over. Now you are a thing that throws fire at the sky in front of a crowd and a priest reads it. A senator's man was at my door." He let me feel the weight of that, watching to see if I knew enough to be afraid, and I knew exactly enough. "Do you know what happens to a cheap oddity that a senator wants and an equestrian owns?"

I did not.

"Nothing good," he said, "for the oddity." And he laughed, one short bark of it, genuinely amused, at me, and it was the first thing in the room that had felt like a real thing a person did rather than a move on a board, and I hated that some animal part of me, after a year of being a sum, was glad to be laughed at like a person.

Then he stopped laughing and got to the point, and the point was a knife dressed as a gift.

"So." He leaned forward, forearms on his knees, and the chair eased. "You are going to stop costing me roofs and start making me money, and I am going to keep you alive while you do it, which this morning became a thing that requires keeping. You follow?"

"I follow some," I said.

"Good. Then follow this." He held up the heavy hand and folded down a thick finger at a time, the way a contractor counts a tally, and it occurred to me that everything this man had ever loved he had loved in the form of a list. "You want cloth. You will have cloth, by the bolt, the close-woven, as much as you ruin, and you ruin a great deal, Heras tells me, you are a careless animal with fire." A finger down. "You want hands. Two slaves to start, mine, to fetch and stitch and not to run, and if you teach them anything that costs me money I will sell the cleverer of them, so teach them what you like."
