A finger down, and he said it the same flat way he had said the cloth and the hands, a man pricing labor, and the floor of the room tilted very slightly under me and I kept my face still because Heras was watching me keep my face still. "You want a place. Not the loft. There is a yard I hold off the timber wharf, a real yard, with a wall and a gate I can lock and a man on the gate, where you can burn what you like and the burning is mine and the wall is between you and the next senator's freedman. You want metal, wood, a forge, you put it on a tablet and the tablet comes to him" - the steward - "and he decides if you are lying about the price, and you will be, everyone lies about the price, I built a fortune on it." A finger down. "And you want money." He stopped on that one, the one finger still standing. "A man works better with a little of his own in a jar where he can see it. I am not a fool and I am not generous, those are the same sentence. There will be a fund. A peculium." He said the word slowly so I would have it, watching me get it. "It is not yours. Understand that before you love it. It is a thing I let you use as if it were yours, and a clever master lets a clever slave keep a little jar because a man guarding his own little jar guards the master's whole house and never notices he is doing it. I am telling you this to your face. You will take it anyway, because the other thing on the table is a senator and a priest and a crowd that this morning said the word witch out loud, and you have, Thulean, no other door in this whole city but mine."

It was the longest thing he had said and the truest, and he had said it kindly almost, the way you tell a man the actual size of the hole he is standing in so he stops thrashing.

I sat there with the bag in my lap and did the only thing I had, which was think.

A year of being a sum had taught me one thing about a sum, which is that it has two sides. He had spent the whole morning telling me what I cost him, the mule, the roof, the senator, the careful generous awful list, and the hardware-store part of me that knew what a thing was worth by who was angry about it had started keeping its own tally on the other side of the line. Men at his door before noon. A senator's freedman. He had not had to walk me here under guard like a parcel for a thing he could throw away. You do not put a wall around a mule.

So I played the one card. I tried to play it small.

"The wall," I said. My Latin came slow and I let it; slow made me sound careful instead of cornered. "The wall is good. The hands are good." I looked at the folded gray cloth in my lap, because looking at a man while you do this is a mistake even I knew. "The thing this morning. It went up. You saw. Everyone saw. It came down because I cannot keep the heat in it. I know why. I have known for a year." I made myself stop there, on the edge, the way they all stopped. "No one else in Rome knows why it came down. No one else in Rome can make it go up at all."

The room was very quiet.

Macer looked at me, and his eyes had changed. Not warmer. Sharper. A man who has just heard the mule talk and is recalculating the mule.

"There it is," he said softly, almost to himself, and then to the steward, in the sideways money-voice, not bothering again that I could hear: "He knows what he is. That is the dangerous kind." Back to me, louder, and the meanness came back up into his face but there was something working under it now, a kind of pleasure, the pleasure of a man who has bought a horse cheap and watched it clear a wall. "So that is your little knife. The only one who knows how. You think I have not bought a hundred men who were the only ones who knew how? The only one who knows how dies of a fever and takes the how with him, Thulean, that is what the only one who knows how does." He let that land, and it landed, cold, in my still-blistered hands. "Which is exactly why I am going to give you the hands. So that one fever does not cost me my balloon."

He used the word like he owned it, which he did. It went through me anyway, balloon, the first time anyone but me had named the thing, in his mean mouth, as a possession.

"You will teach the hands," he said. "Some of it. Enough that a fever does not ruin me, not so much that I do not need you, and you will guess wrong about which is which and lose either way, and that, boy, is the actual deal, the one under the cloth and the wall and the little jar. You keep me needing you. I keep you alive. The day you teach too little, your balloon dies with you and I have lost a roof for nothing, and I do not forgive a roof." He sat back. The chair took him. "I find I like you. Do not let that comfort you. I have liked mules."

I did not understand all the words. I understood the shape, and the shape was a cage so well made that it looked from the inside like a workshop.

"Free," I said. I should not have. It came out of the part of me that was still eighteen and stupid and a year homesick. "If it makes you money. If I teach the hands and the balloon flies and I make you money. Then. Free."

The room did something. The steward's stylus stopped. Heras, beside me, went very still in a way I felt more than saw, a man watching another man step toward an edge.

Macer did not get angry. That was the worst of it. An angry man you can read. He looked at me with something almost like fondness, the fondness you feel for a thing that has done exactly what you expected it to do, on cue, like a dog that begs.

"Free," he said, and tasted it, and let it go. "Listen to him. One morning a marvel and already he wants the cap." He looked at Heras. "You taught him the word?" Heras said nothing, which was an answer. Macer turned the heavy ring on his finger with his thumb, the only restless thing about him. "Here is a true thing, Thulean. It is not mine to give. Freedom that is worth anything in this city is a citizen's freedom, and a citizen is made by men a great deal higher than me, men who were at this morning's festival in better seats than yours, men who own me the way I own you, near enough." The thumb went around the ring. "I can keep you. I can feed you and wall you and put a man on your gate and let you keep your little jar. I cannot make you a Roman. No more than you can fly that thing higher than a roof." He almost smiled. "We are both reaching past our hands this morning, you and I. The difference is I know exactly how far mine reach. Learn that and you might live a while."

He looked at me a moment longer to be sure it had gone in. It had gone in.

"Now get him out of my floor," he said to the steward, already reaching for the next tablet, the room closing over the place where I had been the way the crowd had closed over the place where the priest had been. "And feed him. He looks like a thing I bought cheap."

That was the meeting.

---

The girl led me out.

She had been at the edge of the room the whole time, the one who had pushed the stool under me, and I had not looked at her because you learn fast in that house not to look at the furniture, which was the lesson and I had already half learned it and that was the part that made my stomach turn. She was small, fourteen maybe, in a plain undyed tunic, her hair cut off short and uneven the way you cut a slave girl's hair so it is one less thing to keep, and she moved the way the slave behind the door had moved, quiet, ready, present before you needed her and gone before you could thank her, except that her eyes were not quiet at all. She had watched the whole thing. She had stood there fetching stools and taken in every move on the board, the mule, the roof, the wall, the little jar, free, and filed all of it behind a face that gave nothing back, and when she caught me seeing her see it, she dropped her eyes the way you drop something hot, fast, automatic, a year of practice in it.

"Tyche," the steward said, the way you name a tool. "Yours. Fetch and carry. She does not run." He said it to me, but he was looking at her when he said the last part, and she was looking at the floor, and I understood the sentence was a leash with two ends and I was holding one of them now whether I had asked for it or not.

She brought me water in the corridor, in a plain cup, and bread, because the master had said feed him and in this house when the master said a thing it became true at once in the body of a girl. My hands were shaking, I noticed, now that the room was behind me, and she watched them shake the way she had watched everything, and said nothing, two pieces of this man's property standing in a cool corridor that smelled of leather, and I drank, watered wine, faintly sour, the best thing I had tasted in a year, and I hated that too.

"Thank you," I said. In Latin. It came out wrong, the case wrong, the way it always did.

She looked at me then, full on, one quick measuring look, the same look the master had given me and the steward had given the bag and the priest had given the smoldering cloth, the look this whole city gave a thing to find out what it was worth, except she was fourteen and a slave and had no money to back it with, so it was just the looking, naked, the only thing she owned. Whatever she decided about me she kept. She took the empty cup back out of my shaking hands before I dropped it, turned it once to check the rim, and tucked it against her side.

"The yard is by the wharf," she said. Her Latin was quick and low and clean, a city child's, and she did not slow it down for me the way the master had, which I took, stupidly, as the only respect I had been paid all day. "Long walk. You should eat the bread while you walk. He won't give you time to eat it there."

Then she went ahead of me toward the studded oak door with a slave behind it, carrying the cup that was not hers in a house that was not hers, and I followed her out into the noon light with the bread in one hand and my burned balloon folded under the other arm, into the loud bright city that had just decided I was worth something, which here, by the standard of that cool dim room and that gold ring turning and turning, only ever meant a thing somebody locks behind a better wall.

---

# Chapter 10: The Workshop

The yard came with a smell before it came with anything else.

Macer's man walked me down to the wharf on a wet gray morning at the back end of the year, the river running fat and brown past the timber rafts, and the smell got into the back of my throat from fifty paces off and stayed there for the next year of my life. Wet pine. River mud. The cold tar they swabbed on the rafts to keep them from drinking the Tiber. Under that the tannery two lots up, which is a smell I will not put on paper because it would ruin the paper, and under that the general perfume of a city of a million people who all use the same river to drink from and to throw things into. I learned to stop noticing it inside a week. That is its own kind of horror, when I think about it.

The yard itself was better than I had any right to expect, which I have learned is exactly how you should feel right before you understand the price. A wall of mortared rubble higher than my head on three sides, the river side closed with a plank fence and a gate that barred from inside, a long open-fronted shed roofed in old tile with the gaps stuffed with rag, a smaller lockable room at the back that had been a tally-clerk's office and still had the pigeonholes, a yard of beaten earth that turned to soup when it rained, and a well. My own well. I stood and looked at the well for a while like an idiot. After a year of begging coals and water off a kitchen woman who threw spoons at me, a well I was allowed to draw from felt like being handed a country.

There was a man at the gate when we came. Not a porter. A guard, leaning on a stick that was a stick the way a dog is a friendly animal, with a short blade on his hip that he wore like he'd used it. He looked at me the way the broad men looked at me. Macer's man said a name at him and he stood aside, and I understood that the wall I had been glad of went both ways, and that I was now the most expensive thing inside it, which is not the same as being a guest.

Tyche came down the next morning with the other one.

I had asked the steward, on the tablet, in my careful broken way, for the two hands I'd been promised, and I had spent the night lying on a straw pallet in the tally-room imagining a couple of strong patient apprentices, the way you'd want, the way it works in the version of this where I am a clever man building a clever thing. What came through the gate was a girl of fourteen with her hair hacked off short and a face that had already decided not to be surprised by anything I did, and behind her a man.

The man's name was Pamphilus. He was maybe thirty, maybe forty, you couldn't tell, with the heavy stooped shoulders of someone who had carried things for a living since he could walk, and an iron collar.

I want to be careful here, because this is the part I have lied about to myself.

I had seen collars. You cannot walk a hundred paces in Rome without seeing collars, the thin ones riveted shut with a little bronze plate that says, in letters, hold me I have run, return me to my master for a reward. By the back end of that year I had stopped flinching at them the way I'd stopped smelling the river. I had filed it under things-I-cannot-do-anything-about, which is a very large file in Rome and the only way to stay sane. But Pamphilus stood in my yard with his collar and his hands hanging and waited to be told what to do with his day and his back and his life, and the steward's note said, in effect, here are your tools, and I stood there with my own neck bare only because nobody had bothered to put metal on it, and I could not file it.

"Take that off him," I said.

Tyche looked at me. The guard at the gate looked at me. Pamphilus did not look at me, because looking at me was not his to do, and I watched him decide whether I was the kind of madman who got slaves beaten with talk like that.

"I can't," Tyche said. Flat. City-quick, no slowing it for the foreigner, which I'd already learned was how she told you she respected you and how she told you she didn't, both at once. "It's not yours to take off. It's the master's collar. He had it riveted." She let that land. "You take it off him, you've broken the master's thing. They'll think he ran and you helped."

She was right and I knew she was right and that was the whole education of the thing, delivered in four sentences by a fourteen-year-old before breakfast. The collar was a piece of Macer's property fastened around another piece of Macer's property, and I was a third piece, and pieces do not unfasten one another. I had asked for free hands. I had been clear about it, as clear as my Latin let me be, the day of the deal: I'd rather pay men. Macer had looked at me the way you look at a child who wants the moon and said slaves don't cost wages and slaves don't quit and slaves don't talk, and he'd sent me Pamphilus, who cost nothing and would not quit and could not talk, to make the point in the cheapest available way.

So I did the only thing the situation left a man like me, which was nothing, which was small.

"Then sit," I said to Pamphilus, and pointed at a bench, and made my voice ordinary. "You'll cut cloth. You eat when I eat." I said the last part loud enough for the gate-guard to carry it back. It was not a kindness anyone would write down. It was the only lever I had and I pulled it, and Pamphilus sat on the bench and looked at his hands like a man waiting for the joke.

---

The work, at least, I could do.

Or rather, the work I could fail at on a much grander scale than before, which is what money buys you. I had cloth now, real close-woven linen by the bolt, as much as I could ruin, and I ruined a lot of it. The first show bag for Macer, the one he wanted to wave at senators' freedmen, taught me what I already knew and had been hoping was a lie: that everything I'd solved at small scale came undone at big scale. A bag twice as tall holds eight times the air and weighs four times as much and leaks along eight times the seam, and the heat to fill it wants a fire that wants to eat the bag. I burned one. Not badly, a corner, a brown blossom up one panel before Tyche beat it out with a wet sack, but I stood there afterward with my heart going and the old burn-scars tight on my right arm and understood I was going to be fighting fire for the rest of my life.

What changed was the hands. I'd never had hands before. I'd been the hands.

Pamphilus, it turned out, could cut cloth like a tailor once you drew him the line, slow and dead straight, never wasting a thumb's width, the patience of a man who has nothing waiting for him at the end of the day worth hurrying toward. And the stitching I gave to free women.

That was the one piece of the thing I could buy. The peculium, the money-jar that was mine the way the well was mine, which is to say not at all and entirely depending on the day, had coins in it now, Macer's coins that I was permitted to spend as if my hand were his hand. And Macer permitted piecework. He permitted it because it was cheaper than I expected, because freedwomen in the wharf insulae would stitch a seam for a few asses and feed their kids on it and ask no questions about why a foreigner wanted forty feet of doubled grease-sealed seam, and because a sewn seam from a woman who'd sewn sails was worth three of mine. I told myself I preferred free hands for the quality. That was half of it. The other half I am putting down here honestly: I liked walking up the lane on a pay-morning with the jar and counting coins into the palms of women who could leave when they wanted to, because for the length of that transaction the world ran the way a world should, and I needed that the way you need a window in a small room.

It was not a system. It was four women and a wharf and a slave on a collar cutting cloth, and the slave was free of charge and the women were not, and every accountant Macer owned could have told me which way that arithmetic bent in the long run. It bent the wrong way. It bends the wrong way still. That is the whole of it and I am not going to dress it up.

---

The numbers were the thing that spread, and even the numbers spread like cold honey.

Tyche I noticed by accident, which is how you notice the things that matter. I had her keeping the cloth tally, because someone had to, and because I'd shown her the new figures in an idle hour the way you show a kid a card trick. Ten marks, a sign for nothing, and the place a mark stood in told you what it was worth. I'd done it slowly, with my bad Latin, expecting to do it four times.

I did it once.

I came back from the lane the third pay-morning to find she'd taken the day's piecework, four women, different seam-lengths, different rates, an advance to one of them against next week, a bolt and a third of linen drawn against the steward's tablet, and she'd laid it all out down a wax tablet in a column, every figure in the new hand, carried clean, the asses summing to a denarius and the denarius carried up into its own column, and a remainder set off to the side with the little r I'd shown her once, once, in passing. She'd got the long division. The thing I'd flubbed in front of Heras and had to grind out by hand with my ears burning. She'd watched me do it slow and wrong and slow and right, exactly the one time, and she'd kept it.

"You checked it?" I said, because I am a coward and could not say the true thing, which was something closer to fear.

"Twice," she said. She did not look up. "The Helena one's short. She'll say you owe her two more asses on the long seam and she'll be lying, it's here." She turned the tablet so the column faced me. The two asses sat in their place, accounted, judged. "She does it to the new clerks."

I stood there in my own yard and looked at a fourteen-year-old slave who had taught herself in a week the thing that took the whole Roman accounting profession a generation, and I felt two things at exactly the same time, stacked, both real.

The first was the thing you'd feel. Look at this. Look at what's in there.

The second was Macer's voice from the day of the deal, dry as a ledger: teach the clever one anything costly and I'll sell her, because a skill is worth money and money walks. He'd said it the way you mention a fact about the weather. And I understood, standing there, that the surest way to get Tyche sold down the river was to do exactly the thing I most wanted to do, which was hand her the whole ladder, every rung, and watch her go up it. A bright mind in a body that the law called a thing. The value I could put on her by teaching her was a price somebody could pay.

So I took the tablet back and said, "Good. Do the next one alone," and I kept my face shut, and that was the first lie I told to protect her instead of myself, and not the last.

---

Macer's accountants did not learn it in a week. Macer's accountants did not want to learn it at all.

He sent me to them, or sent them to me, in the cold weeks after the new year, because the new figures were the part of me he could sell to other men without anyone catching fire. There were three of them, freedmen, gray men who ran his contracts, and the senior one was named Onesimus and he had been adding sums on an abacus since before I was a smudge in the future, and he sat across the steward's old table from me with his counting-board in front of him like a shield.

I showed them the trick I'd shown Tyche. Place value. The zero. A long multiplication done down a column in a quarter of the strokes the board took, no beads, no sliding, the working left on the wax for anyone to check after, which the board never left, because the board forgets the instant your thumb moves.

Onesimus did the sum on his board faster than I did it on the wax. That was the first thing that happened, and I should have seen it coming. He'd been doing it for forty years. His thumb was faster than my pen. He sat back and looked at the two young clerks with him as if to say, see.

But the working stayed on my wax. And one of the young ones, a thin nervous boy, leaned over it after, when Onesimus had gone to relieve himself, and put his finger on the carried figures and traced them up the column, and I watched him see the thing Onesimus would not: that the board gives you an answer and the new hand gives you an answer and a witness. You can find where you went wrong. You can hand the sheet to another man and he can find where you went wrong. In a contracting house that lives and dies on whether the grain tally matches the wagon tally, a number you can audit is worth more than a number you got fast.

The boy took to it. The other young one took to it slower, when he saw the first one getting trusted with the checking. Onesimus never did. Onesimus kept his board till he died, I heard later, and trained two more boys on it on his own time out of spite, and the house ran both systems side by side for years, the new figures creeping in at the bottom where the young men were and the old numerals keeping the top of every contract because a Roman document with Thule-marks on its face looked like a foreigner's joke and no senator would sign it. Patchy. The honest word is patchy. I planted a seed in a field of men who'd been planting the old crop their whole lives, and some of it came up, and a lot of it didn't, and the part that came up came up crooked.

The plow went out to Macer's land the same winter and I never saw it work. I gave the smith a drawing, a moldboard to turn the soil over instead of just scratching it, and a scheme for resting fields in a rotation with beans to put the heart back in the dirt, and Macer's bailiff took it up-country on a mule and I got, two months later, a single line back through the steward: the soil there was thin, the new plow pulled heavy, and the beans were beans, they already grew beans. Maybe it helped. Maybe in three years a field somewhere yielded a little more and no one thought of me at all, which is the best a thing like that can hope for. You don't get to watch the field.

---

The blocks I started in the dark months, and the blocks half-worked, which felt, after the plow, like a triumph.

I had the idea whole, which was the rare thing. I had seen presses. The whole city was presses, olive and wine, a great screw biting a flat bed down onto a thing to squeeze it. And I had, in the wharf lanes, men who carved, sign-cutters and seal-cutters and a Greek who cut letters into stone for tombs, men whose whole craft was making a shape backward in a hard surface. Put the two together and you have the thing the West would not get for thirteen more centuries and I knew it and I could not quite get it to work, which is the story of my entire life here in one sentence.

The first block was a disaster. We cut a line of Greek, a single line, a tag of Homer the letter-cutter picked because he could do it in his sleep, into a flat of close-grained pear-wood. Inked it with the soot-and-gum ink they write with. Pressed a sheet of papyrus down by hand. Got a smear. The ink was too thin and ran in the grooves and the high parts and the low parts all printed the same gray fog. We cut it deeper. Mixed the ink thicker, more gum, less water, more soot, until it sat on the raised letters and not in the troughs. Pressed again, harder, with a board and my whole weight. Got, on the fourth try, the worst-looking line of Homer in the history of the world, gray and crooked, one letter doubled where the block had skidded, the left end fat and the right end starved because the bed wasn't true.

I have it still. I kept it. It is genuinely ugly.

It is also the same line, twice, three times, ten times, off one carved block, in the time it would take a copyist to write it once and a tenth of the cost. A copyist makes his own errors, different ones each time. This made the same error every time, which a copyist cannot do, which means you can fix it in the block and then it is fixed forever. Heras came down to the wharf to see it and held a damp gray crooked sheet up to the light and was quiet a long time, which from Heras is a whole oration, and then he said the most Heras thing he has ever said: "Now make it not ugly, and you will have done something, and not before." He was right, and it stayed ugly for years.

---

The salon was Macer's idea, which is to say it was Macer showing off the mule that talked.

A man named Mettius, an equestrian with money and a wife with ambitions, kept a house on the good side of the hill where men of letters came to recite and be admired, and Macer got me an evening there the way you'd lend a friend a remarkable horse, except the horse was me and the favor was owed to Macer afterward. He had Tyche scrub me and put me in a clean tunic that was almost the right length, and he came himself, in his good wool with no stripe, to stand at the back with his arms folded and watch his investment perform.

I want to set down how it felt to walk into that room, because it is the loneliest I have been in Rome and I have been very lonely here. Warm. Lamp-lit, the good oil that doesn't smoke, couches, a floor with a sea-creature done in little stones, men reclining with watered wine in cups worth more than my yard, the talk going in two languages over my head like birds I could see but not catch. My Latin had gotten good enough to order cloth and pay women and curse at a fire. It was nowhere near good enough for this, for the allusions, the half-lines of poetry everyone finished in their heads, the Greek tags dropped in to show they could. I stood in a warm bright room full of the cleverest men in the world and had never in my life had less to say.

Then a man unfolded himself off a couch to look at me, and I met Lucius Vettius Crispus, and the loneliness got worse and also, for the first time that evening, interesting.

He was around forty, beautifully kept, the kind of trim that takes a slave an hour every morning, his hair oiled into careful waves, his tunic the soft white of cloth that has never carried anything. He held his wine without drinking it. He looked at me the way a collector looks at a piece another collector has bragged about, already deciding it's a fake.

"So this is the Hyperborean," he said, to the room, not to me. He gave the word a little spin, the old poets' word for the people beyond the north wind, and got a small laugh, because of course they'd all read the poets and of course I hadn't. "Macer's marvel. The scholar from Thule." He said Thule the way you'd hold something with two fingers. "Tell me, friend. You'll forgive an old reader his pedantry." He smiled, and it was a good smile, warm, practiced, the smile of a man who has never once meant it. "Pytheas of Massalia claimed to have sailed to Thule. Six days' sail north of Britain, he said, where the sea and the land and the air hang together in a kind of pulp, like a sea-lung, neither walkable nor sailable. You'll know the passage."

"I don't read Greek," I said. True, and the wrong thing, and I watched it please him.

"Ah." He turned a little to the couches, bringing them in, a man warming to his instrument. "He doesn't read Greek. The scholar from the land of scholars. Well. Strabo read Greek, and Strabo, who is no fool, who measured the world, calls our friend Pytheas the greatest liar who ever lived." A ripple of pleasure through the room; he rode it. "He says the man invented his Thule out of the northern fog to sell a story to the gullible at Massalia. A far country no one can reach, that conveniently no one can check." He let his eyes come back to me, soft, delighted, lethal. "And here you stand. From a country no one can reach. With marvels no one quite saw fly. Drawing seas on the far side of the world, where, how fortunate, no ship will ever go to find you out. I do admire the symmetry."

I had nothing. That is the truth of the scene and I'm not going to invent a comeback I didn't have. My Latin failed me at exactly the speed I needed it and I stood there feeling the heat come up my neck while the cleverest men in Rome enjoyed it, and Crispus watched me have nothing and his smile got wider and easier. I had just given him a gift. He'd come in ready to be threatened by Macer's famous foreigner and found a tongue-tied boy in a too-short tunic, and the relief of that turned, even as I watched, into contempt, and the contempt was already growing teeth, because a man like Crispus cannot leave a thing alone once he's decided it's beneath him and getting attention anyway.

"He flew a bag of hot air over the Subura," I said. My voice came out flat and accented and I hated it. "Four hundred men saw. A priest saw."

"I heard," Crispus said gently. "I heard it caught fire and fell on a washerwoman. The gods, perhaps, expressing an opinion of the new philosophy." More laughter, the warm easy kind, and he turned his shoulder to me, which in that room was an execution, and went back to his couch and his cup and a line of Callimachus he and another man finished together in Greek, and I was furniture again.

Across the room Macer was watching, arms folded, and his face had not moved, and I could not tell, then or after, whether he was angry that his marvel had wilted or pleased that a sharp-tongued man of letters had just spent his whole performance proving Macer owned something worth attacking. With Macer it was usually both.

Tyche was waiting at the gate when they walked me back down to the wharf in the dark, awake, the lamp lit, the cloth tally squared away in the new figures in a clean column on the table. She looked at my face.

"Bad?" she said.

I didn't answer her. I went to the back room and got the ugly gray line of Homer off the pigeonhole where I kept it, the crooked one, the doubled letter, the starved right end, and I sat down at the table under the lamp and started cutting a second block.

---

# Chapter 11: The Army's Eye

The soldier came on a dry blue morning when I had finally gotten a bag to behave, which is exactly the kind of timing the gods seem to enjoy.

I had the show bag full and tethered on three lines over the slaughter-lot, fat and trembling, holding its shape the way I wanted it to, and Pamphilus was feeding the fire under the mouth-hoop with the slow steady patience of a man who had learned that I would yell if he hurried, and Tyche stood at the line counting breaths under her breath the way I'd taught her to keep time, and for about a hundred of those breaths everything in my life was working at once. I remember thinking, with the specific stupidity of a teenager, that maybe I had this. Maybe the worst was the seams and I had beaten the seams.

Then there were two men at the gate, and the guard let them in without saying a name at them, which the guard never did, and I knew before I turned around that whoever this was, he was over Macer's head.

The first man through was an aide, you could tell by the way he held a leather case like it might bite. The second man came in behind him and stopped just inside the wall and looked at my balloon for the length of about ten breaths, and he did not say anything during those ten breaths, which is how I knew he was the dangerous one.

He was maybe thirty-five. Not tall, not short. Built like something you'd have trouble moving off a doorway. He had on a plain military tunic, undyed wool gone the color of dust, with a wide belt and good boots that had walked a lot of road, and a face that had been out in weather for fifteen years and had stopped pretending otherwise. Cropped hair going gray at the sides. A nose that had been broken and set by someone in a hurry. He stood with his weight even on both feet like a man who expected the ground to do something.

He watched the bag. He watched the lines. He watched Pamphilus feed the fire, and then he tracked the heat up off the fire to the mouth of the bag, and I watched him do the arithmetic that everyone did when they saw it for the first time, the hot-air-rises arithmetic, and arrive at the bottom of it faster than anyone I'd shown.

"It's the fire that holds it up," he said. Not to me. To the air, confirming a thing he'd already decided. His Latin was clipped flat, frontier Latin, no music in it. "Stop the fire, it comes down."

"Yes," I said.

He looked at me for the first time. Took in the accent, the bare neck, the burn-scars on my forearm, the whole of me, in about a second and a half, and filed it.

"How far down the river could that be seen," he said.

Everyone else who saw the thing asked how high it went, or how it worked, or whether it was magic. This man asked how far it could be seen, which is a question you ask about a watchtower. I felt something cold go down my spine.

---

Macer arrived before I'd finished answering, which meant Macer had known the soldier was coming and had wanted to make an entrance, which meant Macer had set the whole thing up. He came through the gate turning his gold ring with his thumb and wearing his good wool and his most expensive smell, and he was happy. I had not seen Macer happy before. It was worse than his other moods.

"Tribune," Macer said, and clasped the soldier's arm, and the soldier let his arm be clasped without enthusiasm. "You see I did not waste your morning."

"Not yet," the soldier said.

"This is the Thulean." Macer said it to the tribune the way you'd point out a horse with a good record. "Quintus Marcius Celer. You will be polite to him, boy, he matters."

So that was the name. Quintus Marcius Celer. He gave me a short nod, the kind you give an instrument you intend to use, and turned straight back to the bag, and I understood the social order of the morning in that one motion. Macer wanted the contract. Celer wanted the thing. I was the thing.

"Bring it down," Celer said. "I want to see how long it takes a man to do it and how many hands it needs and what it weighs in the basket."

"There's no basket," I said.

He looked at me again.

"It doesn't carry anything," I said. "It can't. It lifts the cloth and the air inside it and that's the whole budget. Add a coal-pot to keep the fire going and it won't lift the coal-pot. That one" - I pointed up at the bag, swaying fat on its lines - "would carry maybe the weight of a full water jar before it stopped rising, and it can't carry its own fire, so once it's up, it's cooling, and once it's cooling, it's coming down."

I was telling him the truth because the truth was my friend here. The truth was the wall between me and the thing I could feel coming.

Celer listened to all of it with his head tipped slightly, and at the end of it he said, "So make it bigger."

---

That night I lay on the pallet in the tally-room and did the math I did not want to do.

You can do it on your fingers if you're scared enough, and I was. Hot air lifts because it's lighter than the cold air it pushes out of the way, and the difference is small, a fraction of the weight of the air, and the air doesn't weigh much to begin with. To lift a grown man, call it a hundred and fifty pounds of Roman with his sandals on, plus the cloth, plus some kind of basket, plus a fire and the fuel to feed it, you need a bag so big I could barely hold the number in my head. Not twice my show bag. Not four times. The lift scales with the air you heat, the volume, and the volume goes up with the cube, so to double your lift you don't double the bag, you build something that swallows the slaughter-lot. And the cloth to make it weighs more the bigger it gets, and the bigger it gets the more seam there is to leak, and the leaking is the part I had never beaten and was starting to understand I might never beat, because the cloth was linen and linen is a sieve and the grease that sealed it added the very weight I was fighting.

And the fire. That was the part that woke me up at night even before any of this. To keep a man up you don't light it and release it like the show bag. You have to carry the fire with him. You have to put an open flame in a basket under a person, under a bag made of greased cloth, and feed it the whole time he's up there, and the whole reason the thing rises is that the flame is making the air hot enough to lift, which means the flame is never going to be polite about it.

I had burned my own arm putting out a smaller version of this. I still had the shiny line up the inside of my forearm to prove it. I knew, the way you know a thing in your stomach and not your head, what greased linen does when fire walks up a seam. I had watched it. I had watched it eat a bag in the time it takes to shout.

I lay there and I thought, in English, where nobody could hear me: this is how I kill somebody.

And then I thought the other thing, the colder thing, which was that I was property, and Macer wanted an army contract, and Celer wanted to see over the next hill, and nobody in this entire city was going to ask me whether I wanted to build it.

---

Celer came back. He came back a lot, which I learned was how the man worked. He didn't summon me up to some office and have things explained to him. He came down to the wharf in the dry middle of the day, sent his aide off to do whatever aides do, and stood in my yard with his arms folded and made me show him things and made me tell him why they didn't work, and after the third or fourth visit I noticed I had stopped being afraid of him in the ordinary way and started being afraid of him in a more complicated way, which is the way you're afraid of someone you've started to like.

Because he was good. That was the trap of him. He listened. When I told him a thing was impossible he didn't tell me I was lying, the way Crispus did, and he didn't get a faraway look and start talking about omens, the way half of Rome did. He'd ask me one hard practical question, and then another, and the questions were good questions, the questions I'd have asked, and somewhere in the third one I'd realize he wasn't trying to flatter the thing into working, he was trying to find the exact edge of what it could really do so he could use that edge and not an inch more.

"You're afraid of it," he said once. We were watching Pamphilus and two of the wharf-women fold a bag along its panels. He said it the way you'd note the weather.

"Yes," I said.

"Good. The men I bury are the ones who weren't." He picked a thread off the folded cloth and rubbed it between his fingers, feeling the grease, the weight, the way it gave. "I've watched a siege tower go up in the morning and be a smear of charcoal and screaming by midday because the man who built it loved it. Don't love it." He let the thread drop. "Tell me again why it can't carry its own fire."

So I told him again. And the maddening thing, the thing that kept me up, was that telling Quintus Marcius Celer the truth about why my invention would kill a man did not make him want to stop building it. It made him respect me more, and want the thing more, because now he trusted my numbers, and his numbers said a man who could see over the next hill before the enemy knew he was there was worth almost any price you cared to name. A hurt man here was cheaper than a thousand dead men there. He never said that. He didn't have to. I'd done his arithmetic for him.

"I can warn you," I said. "I can tell you everything that's going to go wrong. But I'm telling you it's going to go wrong."

"I heard you," Celer said. "Build it tethered. We'll see how wrong."

---

I bought what time I could with the word tethered, and I bought a little more with the things I made them do to be safe, and I want to set those down, because they were the only useful thing I did in the whole stretch and they were not enough.

I made the basket out of green willow soaked in the river so it wouldn't catch easy. I built the fire-bowl as a clay-lined thing sitting on a wet plank instead of an open pot, so that if it tipped it wouldn't just spill coals into a man's lap, and I made Pamphilus and the wharf-women run the lines so that three people could haul the bag down fast from three directions. I made them practice the haul-down empty until they hated me. I had a man stand by with a soaked hide to throw, and a barrel of river water, and I made the rule, which I shouted until I was hoarse, that the first sight of flame anywhere it shouldn't be, you cut the fire and haul, you don't wait, you don't look at me, you haul.

And I made the bag too big and too heavy on purpose, which sounds backward, but a bag that can barely lift a man rises slow, and slow was the only friend I had. I did not want a thing that leapt. I wanted a thing that grudged its way up to the end of a short tether and hung there like a sulking ox while a soldier looked at the river and then we hauled it down.

I did all of that. I want that written down. I did all of that, and a man still went up in it, and I knew the whole time that doing all of that was the same as agreeing to it.

---

They didn't even use one of the wharf-women, who could at least swim and who weighed less than a soldier and who I'd half-built the thing around. Celer brought his own man, because of course he did, because the army doesn't borrow a slave girl when it has its own.

The man's name was Naso. Titus Aelius Naso. He was younger than me, maybe eighteen, a recruit Celer was breaking in, lean and grinning and stupid with the particular fearlessness of a boy who has not yet been hurt by anything. He thought it was an honor. He kept saying so. He climbed into the green willow basket on the morning of the trial with the whole wharf watching from the wall and the timber rafts, and Macer up on the wall with his arms folded looking like a man counting money he hadn't been paid yet, and Celer at the head line, and me with my throat full of ash before we'd even lit the fire.

I checked the lines myself. I checked them twice. I had the soaked hide laid over the lip of the basket so the willow nearest the fire-bowl wouldn't dry and crack. I told Naso the rule. I told him: anything goes wrong, you don't try to fix it, you sit down in the basket and put your arms over your head and we bring you down. He grinned at me and said something to his mate about the Thulean being more of a mother than his mother, and the wharf laughed, and we lit it.

It went the way I built it to go, at first. It went up slow. It grudged. It hated lifting Naso and it told us so, swaying and sulking, and the fire-bowl threw heat up into the mouth and the bag filled and stiffened and Naso came up off the ground a hand's breadth, then a forearm, then a man's height, with the three lines paying out hand over hand and singing tight, and the wharf made the noise crowds make, that low rising aaah, and Naso whooped and stood up in the basket to wave.

He should not have stood up.

When he stood the basket swung and his weight came off center and the bag heeled over, just a little, just enough, and the mouth tipped and the flame in the bowl leaned with it and licked sideways up out of the bowl toward the cloth, and I was already shouting haul before my mind had finished the thought, and the three of them hauled, and the bag came heeling down fast and crooked, and the leaning flame found the grease.

It is so fast. That's the thing no one believes until they see it. There is no moment where you might still put it out. There is a seam, and then there is fire along the whole seam, a brown line opening into a bright one, and the bag that was lifting a man is suddenly a bag pouring fire down onto the man it was lifting.

Naso did the worst possible thing, which was the human thing, which was that he tried to climb out of the basket while it was still a man's height up and falling. He got a leg over the willow with fire coming down over his shoulders and his tunic catching and he half-jumped, half-fell, and the basket came down on top of him with the burning cloth folding down over all of it like a wave, and then there were six people in the smoke including me and the soaked hide was on him and the river water was going over him in barrels and I had my own hands in it beating at the cloth on his back with the old burn-scars on my arm screaming where the fresh heat hit them and I did not feel it at all.

We got him out. We got the fire off him. The bag burned itself to a rag of ash on the wet ground in about the time it takes to tell it and stank of mutton grease and scorched hair, which is a smell I will not get out of the back of my throat as long as I live.

Naso was alive. He was screaming, which I had to tell myself was good, because the ones who go quiet are worse. His back and the backs of his arms and one whole side of his neck were burned down past the red into the gray-white that Heras would later, very quietly, tell me was the bad kind, the kind that doesn't hurt at the center because the part that feels is already gone. His left hand had taken the fire-bowl when he fell on it and would never be a hand again, not really, not the way it had been when he climbed in grinning an hour before. His ankle was broken from the fall. His grin was gone and would be gone for months and some of it would never come back.

But he was alive. I keep saying that. I said it that day too, out loud, to nobody, while Heras worked on him in the shade of the shed with the cleanest hands in Rome and a face that did not look at me, and I stood there with my arm finally starting to scream at me and held the words alive, he's alive in front of me like the soaked hide, like something I could put between myself and what was on the ground.

---

Macer came down off the wall.

I thought, in the clean white space where I wasn't thinking anything useful, that he was going to be angry. That a burned soldier was a ruined contract, a dead deal, a thing gone wrong in front of an army officer he'd been courting. I almost wanted it. I wanted somebody to be as horrified as I was so I wouldn't have to carry the whole weight of it alone.

Macer looked at Naso on the ground, and at the rag of ash that had been four months of my work, and at me, and he turned his gold ring once with his thumb.

"Smaller fire next time," he said. "Or a longer basket. Work it out."

And Celer, who had hauled the line until his palms bled and who had been the first one into the smoke after me, Celer crouched by his recruit and said something low and soldier's-rough to him that I didn't catch and that made the boy stop screaming for a breath. Then Celer stood up and wiped his bleeding hands on his dust-colored tunic and looked at the burning rag and the broken willow and the boy, and I saw him doing it again, the arithmetic, the cold clean adding-up, and I saw him arrive at the bottom of it.

"He got up to a man's height," Celer said. "I saw the far bank over the wall. I saw the rafts on the far bank, Thulean, I counted them." He looked at me, and there was no cruelty in it, which was the unbearable part. "It works. It works and it's a butcher and we'll do it again with a smaller fire and a longer basket like the man says." He glanced down at Naso, who Heras was binding now in wet linen, and something moved in his face that he didn't let stay. "And next time the fool sits down when he's told."

He walked back up to the gate to send his aide for a litter. I stood in the wet ash with my arm on fire and the smell in my throat and watched Heras's clean hands move over the boy, and I picked up the broken end of one of the willow lines, the line I had checked twice that morning, and I held it, and I did not let go of it for a long time.

---

# Chapter 12: To the Danube

They gave me three days to pack a workshop into a wagon, and I spent the first one arguing with Tyche about whether she was coming.

She was not coming. That had been decided over my head, the way most things were, by men who put marks on tablets and never saw the people the marks moved. Pamphilus was coming because Pamphilus was strong and could cut cloth and did not run, which were the three qualities the army wanted in a man and the only three it asked about. Tyche was staying to keep the yard and the cloth-tally. I told her this was because the work in Rome mattered. She looked at me the way she looked at a sum that didn't balance, and went back to counting bolts, and we both knew the truth of it, which was that a war camp was no place for a fourteen-year-old girl who belonged to Macer, and that I could not protect her there, and that I could barely protect myself.

So it was Pamphilus and me and four bolts of the close-woven linen and two spare mouth-hoops of seasoned ash and a chest of needles and grease and waxed thread, loaded onto a mule cart in the gray before dawn, Celer's aide ticking each item off a list, and the dead phone in a wrap of rag in my tunic where it had ridden against my ribs for three years.

I had never been north of Rome. I had barely been out of the yard.

---

You think you know what a road is.

I had spent my whole life on roads, on the interstate with my mother's hands at ten and two, on the cracked asphalt between the hardware store and home. The Via Flaminia changed my mind about that inside the first morning.

It ran north out of the city straight as a drawn line, paved in fitted stone worn smooth and slick down the middle where ten generations of wheels had polished it, crowned so the rain ran off, with stone gutters at the edges, and it did not bend for hills. That was the thing that got into me first, the arrogance of it. A modern road bends. It respects the land, takes the easy way because the easy way is cheaper. This road went over. It cut the hill or bridged the valley or ran a causeway across the marsh on piers, and it did this for the better part of a hundred and fifty miles before we even reached the mountains, and then it kept doing it through the mountains.

Every mile there was a stone, waist-high, telling you the miles to Rome and the emperor who'd had that stretch repaired. I read them out of boredom and then couldn't stop, because they were a clock, counting down the distance from the only place in this world I half understood toward a river I had only ever seen as a blue line on a map I'd drawn myself, badly, from memory.

And the road was full.

That is the part I can't make anybody who hasn't seen it believe. I had pictured an army as a crowd of men with spears, a mass of helmets. What came up and down the Via Flaminia was a machine, and I was a part going into it, and it ran day and night.

Grain carts, mostly. More than I could count, ox-drawn, axle-deep and slow, hauling wheat north to feed men I hadn't met yet. Mule trains of leather and iron and tent-hide. A drove of remounts, hundreds of horses, the smell coming downwind a quarter mile ahead of them. A wagon of nothing but nails. A wagon, I swear to God, of nothing but the lead pipe and bronze fittings to plumb a bath house, going to war. Pay chests under guard. Messengers on the imperial post who did not slow for anyone. And soldiers, marching in their centuries with the easy rolling pace of men who would do twenty miles and then build a town.

I'd spent three years telling myself a quiet story where I was the clever one, the man from the future with the secret edge. And then I rode north for two weeks inside the supply train of Trajan's army on its way to take a kingdom, and the story got very small.

These people could not make a lightbulb. They thought sickness was bad air. They had no zero until I gave them one and they mostly still didn't want it. And they had organized the feeding and moving and arming of tens of thousands of men across the spine of a continent with a precision I could not have begun to design with a pencil and a year to do it. It ran on tallies and stages and depots and men who knew their jobs and a road that did not bend for hills. It ran on the assumption, baked into every stone, that Rome would still be here in two hundred years to use the road, and Rome was right about that, and I knew it, and it made the back of my neck cold.

I had been thinking of myself as the future arriving. Riding that road, I started to understand I was a tool being carried to a job, and the hand that held me had been holding tools like me for a long time and would keep holding them after I broke.

---

The first night out of the depot at the river crossing I learned the thing about the camp, and the camp was where the machine stopped being abstract and started being terrible.

I'd assumed an army on the march slept where it stopped. Threw down a blanket, posted a watch, slept. That is what tired people do.

This army dug a town. Every single night.

They came off the road in the late afternoon while there was still light, and a man rode ahead and paced out the ground, and then the whole column turned into surveyors and ditch-diggers. They squared off a rectangle the size of a small city, dug a ditch around the whole of it, threw the spoil up into a rampart, and topped it with the sharpened stakes that every legionary carried two of, lashed to his pack, all day, for exactly this. Inside, the streets ran in the same grid every night, the same gate names, the commander's tent in the same place, so that a man could be dropped blind into any marching camp from Britain to Syria and find the latrine in the dark.

And in the morning they filled the ditch back in, pulled the stakes, and marched, and did it all again at the next stop. Twenty miles and a fort, twenty miles and a fort, the length of the world.

I stood at the edge of the ditch the first evening, useless, holding a mule's lead, watching eight thousand men build a fortified town in about three hours as a routine chore they were too bored by to talk much during, and I thought about my four months of work to make a bag of hot air stay up for two hundred breaths.

But that was the night I learned the other thing, the one I filed away and could not stop turning over.

The latrines.

They dug them, of course, in the same place in the grid every night, downstream, downhill, a long trench with a rail. Roman soldiers, by and large, are cleaner than the people back in the city. They wash. They bathe when they can. The camp had rules about where you went and where you didn't, and the rules were enforced.

And the camp was sick anyway.

I noticed it the way you notice a sound that's been going a while. The flux. The bloody flux, they called it, or just the camp-sickness, the thing that emptied a man out from the inside until he was a husk and then, often enough, until he was a body. I started counting, because counting is what I do when I'm scared, and the count was bad. We hadn't seen a Dacian yet. And men were dying every few days, carried out in the morning of nothing but their own guts turning to water, and the soldiers spoke of it the way you'd speak of weather, an annoyance, a tax, the cost of being on campaign.

An optio I shared a fire with one night, a leathery man named Saturninus who had been on three campaigns, told me without my asking, when I asked why the man in the next tent had been carried out, that the flux took more of them than the enemy ever did. Said it the way you'd say the river was high. Every campaign. You expected to lose a man to a sword if you were unlucky and ten to your own bowels if you were not.

I sat there with my posca going sour in a wooden cup and reached for what I knew and came up holding most of nothing. It was the water. I was nearly sure it was the water, below all those latrines and all those animals and all those men, drawn because it was there and it was wet. I knew, cold and certain and useless, that if you boiled every cup that went into every man in that camp you would empty those morning carts. And here was an army of tens of thousands drinking from a fouled stream because no one had ever made them stop, and I had no standing to make anyone do anything, because I was a slave on loan, a foreign witch-cloth curiosity who made the fire fly, and the day I stood up in a war camp and told centurions their men were dying because tiny invisible creatures were swimming in the water and climbing inside them, I would be either laughed at or flogged, probably both.

So I did the modern thing, the only thing I had. I filed it. Later. When you matter more. When someone has to listen. You will come back to the water.

I boiled my own water and Pamphilus's, and we did not get sick, and I told myself that proved nothing, because I'd taught myself that it proved nothing, and I drank my clean water next to men drinking the bad, and I went on north.

---

We came down to the Danube in the third week, and the first thing I saw was not the river.

It was the bridge, the beginning of it, and the man building it.

The Danube there was wide and gray-brown and fast, a real river, the kind that has drowned armies, the kind the Romans had crossed before only on boats lashed into a bobbing road that the spring floods could tear apart. And someone had decided to put a permanent thing across it, stone piers standing up out of moving water, and over them a roadway, and over that an army and its grain carts and its bath-house pipes, and the someone was standing on a raft of timber in the middle of the current with his sleeves pushed up, shouting at a crew working a thing I recognized and had never seen.

It was a cofferdam. I didn't have the word for it then in any language, but I knew what it was the second I saw it, because I'd watched a video once of how the Romans built bridges in rivers, and here was the actual thing, men driving a ring of timber piles into the riverbed with a pile driver, a big iron-shod weight hauled up on ropes through a frame and dropped, hauled up and dropped, a slow brutal hammering, to make a wall, to pump the river out of the inside of the wall, to lay stone on the dry ground at the bottom of a river.

I stood on the bank and forgot, for a while, that I had a job. Pamphilus had to say my name twice.

---

His name was Apollodorus, and I'd heard it on the march without it meaning anything, the way you hear the names of important men. Apollodorus of Damascus. The emperor's engineer.

I knew there was a man in the river building the impossible with his hands and a thousand other hands, and I knew, the way you know a thing in your chest before your head agrees, that he was the first person I had met in three years who lived where I lived. Who saw a problem and saw the shape of the answer and then ground his life up making the shape real against the resistance of the actual world. I had been so lonely in that specific way that I hadn't had a word for the loneliness, and then I saw him drop a pile into a river and I almost wanted to cry, which would have been humiliating, so I didn't.

Celer took me to him the next day, because Celer was practical and the engineer outranked everyone in the matter of the river and a tribune does not waste a tool's first meeting on ceremony.

Apollodorus was older than I'd thought from the bank, fifties maybe, gone gray and burned dark, with the hands of a man who still picked things up himself, scarred and thick across the knuckles. He had a Greek's quickness in the eyes and a builder's stillness in the body, and when Celer brought me up he did not stop what he was doing, which was reading off a set of figures from a tablet to a clerk, until he'd finished the column. Then he looked at me.

"This is the one with the fire bag," he said. Not a question. His Latin was better than mine, fluent, Greek-accented, fast. "The army's eye."

"Yes."

He looked at me for a while. Not the way Macer looked at me, which was the way you look at a horse. He looked at me the way I looked at a problem, taking it apart, and I made myself stand still and be taken apart.

"Hot air," he said. "You heat the air inside a bag, it weighs less than the air outside, it rises. Like a cup pushed mouth-down into water wants to rise."

"Yes." I was almost dizzy with it. I had explained the principle to a hundred people who heard the words and understood the spectacle and missed the thing entirely, and he had it whole and right in two sentences and had clearly had it before I opened my mouth.

"And it falls when the air cools, which is always, because you cannot carry enough fire." He set the tablet down. "So it is tethered. A rope and a high point. You look down on the enemy from your high point and count his men and his rafts." He said it flatly, the way you describe a known machine. Then, and this is the part I keep: "It is a clever toy that does one thing, and the one thing is worth doing, which is more than most clever toys."

I didn't know whether I'd been praised or filed under toy. I think he meant both, exactly and without contradiction, and that was when I understood I was going to like him and that he was not going to make it easy.

"Yours doesn't move," I said, before I could stop myself, nodding at the river. "Mine goes where the wind goes. That's the whole problem with it."

Something shifted at the corner of his mouth. Not a smile. The ghost of where a smile would be on a man who didn't spend them.

"Mine moves," he said. "Constantly. The river takes a foot of my work every day I don't beat it back. You think a thing in the ground is still." He turned and looked at his cofferdam, at the river shouldering against the timber. "Nothing is still. The water wants in through every gap in the wall faster than the men can bail it, and the wall wants to spread, and the piles want to walk in the mud. I am holding back the whole weight of a river with sticks and the time it takes to lay a stone, every hour, and the day I stop holding, the river has it back." He looked at me again. "You and your wind. At least your enemy is honest. The wind doesn't pretend to be your friend the morning before it takes everything."

I have met a few people in my life who made me feel I'd walked into a larger room than the one I thought I was in. He was the first one in this world.

---

We did not become friends. I told myself a hopeful story on the bank and the man corrected it within a week.

He was proud the way a wall is plumb, all the way down, no give in it. He knew exactly how much he was worth and was not interested in being modest about it for the comfort of a tribune's pet foreigner. When I tried, the third day, to suggest a thing about his pile driver, a counterweight notion I half-remembered, he heard me out with a patience that was worse than rudeness and then explained, precisely, why what I'd described would not work in moving water with the timber and rope he actually had, and he was right, and I had been guessing, and he let me see that he knew. I went away with my face hot.

But the next morning he came and found me at the yard where we were setting up the balloons, which he had no reason to do, and stood watching Pamphilus stitch a seam, and asked me, abruptly, with no warm-up, what the bag did in wind. Not to be polite. Because he wanted the number.

I told him the truth, which was that I didn't have a good number, that in a real wind the tethered bag leaned and pulled and the rope sang, and that what looked clean over a slaughter-lot in Rome on a still day was a meaner animal here on the river, where the wind came down the water in shoves and the cold thinned my fire's bite and the bag came down faster and fought me the whole time. He listened to all of it. Then he said the truest thing anyone had said to me about my own work.

"A demonstration is a lie you tell on a good day," he said. "The job is the thing you can do on a bad one." And he went back to his river.

I wrote it down that night, in the books, in English, where no one can read it but the people I teach. A Greek bridge-builder gave it to me on a cold bank of the Danube while men died of bad water two hundred yards upstream, and he never knew he'd given me anything, and I never told him.

---

The balloons, on the bad day, were everything he'd said and worse.

Celer wanted observation, and observation was the safe use, the use that didn't put a Naso in a basket over a fire, and even the safe use was a fight. We picked a rise back from the river where the army's outriders had pushed the Dacian scouts off, a bald cold hill with a view north into the broken green country where Decebalus's people were, somewhere, watching us watch them.

The first morning the wind was wrong and we got nothing up at all. The bag filled half-heartedly in the cold, leaned, and lay down on the grass like a sick thing, and the soldiers detailed to help me looked at the witch-cloth and at each other, and I heard the word for fraud and pretended I hadn't.

The second morning was still, and it worked, and that was almost worse, because it taught the army to expect it.

We got the big observation bag up on a single heavy tether to maybe a hundred and twenty feet, no basket, no man, just the bag and a long line and, lashed below the mouth on a light frame, a set of bright-dyed cloth strips I could haul on a second line to signal down, since no one could shout that high. From up there on a clear cold morning a man with good eyes could see over the first ridge into the dead ground the Dacians used, count fires, see a column moving on a track we couldn't see from the hill. Celer stood at the base of the tether with his head back and his recruit reading the strips and a clerk taking it down, and for an hour the thing I had made did the one thing it could do, and Celer got his count, and a patrol went out that afternoon to a place it would not otherwise have known to go.

It worked, on a still morning, on a good day, exactly as Apollodorus had warned me, and the days here were not still, and the men I'd taught to haul the line were not Pamphilus, and the cold ate my fire, and the wind when it came took the bag sideways so hard one afternoon that the tether stake pulled and we lost forty feet of altitude in a breath and very nearly lost the bag entirely over the lines of the camp, where a flying fire-bag coming down on the tents would have been the end of me, contract or no contract.

Pamphilus and three swearing soldiers got it down, and we staked it triple after that, and I stood in the cold mud with my hands shaking and understood that I had built a tool that worked on Tuesdays and that an army does not get to choose to fight only on Tuesdays.

And underneath all of it, every hour, in Celer's eyes and the aide's tablet and the way Macer's last letter had been read to me, was the other pressure.

They had their eye in the sky now, tethered and safe. And it was not enough. The whole machine of the army had decided that a man who could see down from a hundred and twenty feet was worth a fortune, and a man who could be carried up and over and look down into the king's own hills was worth a kingdom. Naso's ruined hand was three weeks behind me on the road and already, I could feel it, being counted as a price and not a verdict.

---

On the last clear evening before the weather turned, I went up to the bald hill alone while there was still light, and stood at the staked-down tether, and looked north at the country we were going to take.

It was beautiful, which I hadn't expected. Forested ridges going blue in the distance, woodsmoke from villages of people who did not yet know how thoroughly the machine on this side of the river was going to arrive in their valleys. Somewhere out there a king named Decebalus was deciding how many of his people to spend, and on this side a road crawled north a fort at a time, and in the river to my left a Greek held back the whole weight of the water with sticks and time, and I stood between them in borrowed cloth, a slave on a string, with the dead glass of my old life against my ribs and a bag of hot air leaning in the wind above me.

Down at the camp they were carrying out the evening's dead, two of them, both from the flux, neither from a sword.

I put my hand on the tether rope. It was cold and stiff and singing faintly where the wind worked it, and the bag above me strained toward the dark and the river and the war, and I held the rope, and I did not let go of it, and below me on the path I heard Celer's boots coming up the hill to tell me what they wanted me to fly next.

---

# Chapter 13: The Fall

The hill had a name in Dacian that nobody in our camp could say, so the men called it the Loaf, because it sat on the far side of the valley with a round brown back like a loaf left too long in the oven. The Dacians were behind it. That was the whole problem, the whole reason any of this happened. They were behind the Loaf and Trajan's scouts could not get a man up high enough to count them without the man being seen and killed, and somebody up the chain had remembered that the tribune Celer had a Thulean and a balloon.

So they sent for me to put a man over the Loaf.

I want to be fair to them, the way you have to be fair to a thing that is going to crush you, because pretending they were stupid would be a comfort and a lie. They were not stupid. They were in a hurry, and a hurry is a different animal. There was a column that was going to move up the valley in three days whether anyone had counted the Dacians or not, and the men in that column were going to walk into whatever was behind the Loaf, and the difference between knowing and not knowing was going to be measured in how many of them came back down. Celer laid it out to me flat in the command tent with a wax tablet and his thumbnail for a stylus, the valley, the Loaf, the column, the three days, and he did not raise his voice once, and at the bottom of it he looked at me the way he looked at everything, weighing exactly what it could do and not an inch more.

"Tell me what it can't do," he said. "All of it. Now, while there's time to hear it."

"It can't clear that hill on a tether," I said. "Not from this side. The valley's too wide. To see over the Loaf from where we can stand a man you'd need him up past three hundred feet, and the tether to hold him there weighs more than the bag can lift, and the wind off the river will lay him sideways before he gets half that high. You saw it lie down in the wind last week. That was at a hundred and twenty with no one in it."

"So not a tether," Celer said.

I had known he was going to say it. I had heard it coming the way you hear a cart in the dark before you can see it. "No," I said. "Don't."

"Free, then. Cut him loose, let him go up clean, let him count, bring him down on the far side or wherever the wind drops him."

"Down where," I said. "On whose side of the Loaf."

That landed. I watched it land. He didn't like it, and a man who didn't like things the way Celer didn't like them was a man already doing the arithmetic of how to do it anyway.

---

Apollodorus was in the camp because the bridge was in the camp's reach and Trajan kept his engineer close, and somebody, I never found out who, thought the great man should look at the flying business before the army bet a column on it. He came down to my patch of trampled ground with his thick scarred hands behind his back and looked at the bag laid out on the hides and the fire-bowl and the willow and he looked at it for a long time the way he looked at everything, which was as if it had personally claimed it could do his job.

"You want to put a soldier on a fire under a sack of greased cloth," he said, "and let the wind have him."

"I don't want to," I said. "They want to."

"They always want to." He crouched, with the ease of a man whose knees had carried him up scaffolds for thirty years, and he picked up a fold of the linen and rubbed it between finger and thumb the way Celer had once, feeling the grease, the weight, the give of it, and his mouth did something sour. "This holds heat the way a sieve holds water. You light a fire under it and you climb up away from the ground, which is the only thing that ever puts a fire out, the ground, the sand, the water. Up there you have none of those. You have a man and a flame and the wind, in a basket." He let the cloth drop and stood and brushed his palm on his tunic. "A clever toy that does one thing. I told you that at the bridge. The one thing is not this."

"I know," I said.

He looked at me then, and for a moment there was something in it that wasn't rivalry, that was just one man who builds things looking at another man who builds things across a thing that was going to go wrong. "Then say so to the man who can stop it," he said, "if such a man exists in this camp, which I doubt." And he went back to his bridge, where the things he built held, because he built them out of stone and his own stubbornness and never once out of greased linen, and I have hated him and envied him in the same breath more times than I can count and that morning I did both at once and wished he'd stayed.

There was no man who could stop it. I was a slave on loan. Celer could be talked to, and Celer had already heard me, and hearing me was not the same as obeying me, and obeying a slave was not a thing a tribune did in front of an army three days from a fight.

---

I built it as safe as a thing like that gets, which is to say I built it to kill more slowly. I am going to set down what I did, the same as last time, because it is the only part of this I get to keep.

I made the envelope bigger than anything I'd flown, so it would rise slow and grudging and carry its man up at a walk and not a leap. I built a longer basket, the way Macer had said back in Rome, the way you'd say it about a thing you'd never have to climb into, so the man could sit low and keep his weight under the bag and not have to stand. I lined the fire-bowl in wet clay and set it on a wet plank and rigged a lid he could drop on it by pulling one cord, so that the instant anything went wrong he could choke the fire from where he sat without standing, without reaching, one pull. I soaked the willow. I soaked the hides. I made him a hood and gloves of wet leather. I tied a long light line to the basket anyway. It would not hold him; the wind would have that line for breakfast. It was so that when he came down we would have something to run to, a thread to follow into the brush.

And I made the rule again. One thing goes wrong, you don't fix it, you pull the cord, you drop on the fire, you sit, you come down. I made him say it back to me. I made him say it back to me twice.

His name was Sabinus. Marcus Aurelius Sabinus. He was a real soldier, not a recruit, maybe twenty-five, with a farmer's forearms and a scar through one eyebrow that pulled the eyebrow up so he always looked faintly amused, and he volunteered, which I have never forgiven the army for letting a man do. He volunteered because there was extra pay in it and because the man who went up and counted the Dacians and saved the column would have his name said by the legate, and a soldier's whole life is his name being said by the right man. He had a wife in a town in Pannonia and two children he told me about while I was fitting the wet hood on him, a boy and a girl, the boy old enough to mind the goats, and he told me their names and I made myself not hear them and I heard them anyway and I have them still.

Naso was there. They'd brought him up north with the baggage because a half-burned recruit who can't grip a shield with his ruined hand is no use in a line but he can still tend mules, and he came and stood at the edge of my trampled patch with his left hand curled against his chest the way it had healed, a claw of shiny skin, and his ankle still putting a hitch in his walk. He watched me fit the hood on Sabinus. He didn't say anything for a while. Then he said, to me, low, "Make him keep his seat." His face when he said it was not a young face anymore. "Tell him I said. The standing up. Tell him."

I told Sabinus. I told him a man who'd been up in one of these and burned told him to keep his seat. Sabinus looked over at Naso's hand, at the claw of shiny skin curled against the chest, and something went out of his amusement, and he said he'd keep his seat, and that was the closest thing to a good omen I got that morning.

---

The wind was wrong from the start and I knew it was wrong and I said it was wrong and the legate's man said the column moved in three days, and that was the end of the conversation, because three days is a fact and my fear was just a slave talking.

We filled it in the lee of a baggage cart to keep the worst of the wind off the mouth while it took its heat, and it filled slow and sullen and good, the way I'd built it to, swelling up off the hides into the tall lopsided teardrop, and the men hauled it upright with poles in the wet leather mitts I'd made them, and Sabinus climbed down into the long basket and sat, and I checked the cord to the fire-lid myself, put it in his right hand, closed his fingers on it. His eyes over the wet hood were steady. The scar held his eyebrow up. The wind came across the valley in a long cold shove and the whole bag leaned and pulled and the men dug in.

"On your word," Celer said. He was at the line himself. He was always at the line.

I did not have a word. I had every reason in the world and no power and a fact about a column, and the thing about being property is that your no is a sound you make, not a thing that happens. I looked at Sabinus and Sabinus looked back at me with the cord in his fist and said, "Let it go, Thulean, before I lose my nerve and you lose your morning," and the men laughed because soldiers laugh, and I lifted my hand, and they cut it loose.
