It went up clean. That is the cruelty of it. The first part went exactly the way the whole idea promised, the way it looked in my head all those nights on the pallet, the bag rising slow and huge and Sabinus going up over the camp sitting low and small in the long basket with one hand on the cord and one on the rim, up past the cart-tops, up past the standards, up past the height a man falls from and lives, and the camp went quiet the way crowds do, that held breath, and for a count of maybe forty he was a man flying and it was the most beautiful thing I have ever built and I knew with my whole body what was coming.

The wind had him at a hundred feet. I'd told them. The river wind came down the valley in a sheet and it caught the bag broadside and pushed, and the bag, which wanted to go where the wind went, leaned and ran with it, and Sabinus and the basket swung out under it on the long swing of a thing hung from a single point, out and up, like the seat of a swing at the top of its arc. And the fire-bowl, wet clay and wet plank and all my careful weight, leaned with the swing, because clay and plank don't care how careful you were, they care about which way is down, and which way is down had just changed.

I saw the flame come out of the bowl. From the ground it was a small bright tongue, no bigger than a candle at that distance, leaning off the lip of the bowl and laid sideways by the same wind that was laying the bag, reaching, and it touched the cloth.

He pulled the cord. I have to believe he pulled the cord. The lid was for a fire sitting straight in its bowl and the fire was not in its bowl anymore, it was a sheet of flame being pressed flat against the side of the bag by the wind, and a lid does nothing to a fire that has already left the dish and found the grease. I watched a brown line open up the side of the bag into a bright one, the seam going, the same seam, always the same seam, and the bag that was holding him up stopped being a bag.

It came apart in fire. The top of it, the part still full of hot air, that part went on rising for a breath even as the rest of it burned, so that for one moment Sabinus hung under a crown of fire with the basket trailing flame, and then the air went out of the top through the burning and there was nothing holding him up at all.

He fell with the burning cloth coming down around him and over him, a hundred feet, into the trampled ground at the edge of the camp, and the sound of it reached me a moment after I saw it, a sound I am not going to write down, the sound of all of him arriving at the ground at once. The cloth came down after him, still burning, and laid itself over him like something being tucked in.

---

The camp was silent for about three breaths and then it was not. Men ran. Somebody was already throwing dirt, the right thing, the only thing, dirt and a wet hide, and the burning cloth was pulled off, and there was no question to ask of what was under it, no alive, he's alive to hold up in front of myself this time, nothing to bind in wet linen, nothing for Heras's clean hands even if Heras had been within fifty miles. The fall had done it. The fire was just the fire.

I do not remember crossing the ground to him. I remember being there, on my knees in the dirt that the men were still throwing, with my hands out and nothing for them to do, and somebody, an optio, taking me by the shoulder and moving me back the way you move a child away from a thing, not unkindly, just out of the way of the work, because there was work, there is always work, a body to carry and a fire to make sure of and a column that still moved in three days.

I stayed on my knees. I picked up a fistful of the burned cloth, a corner of it, scorched stiff and still hot enough to hurt, and I held it, and I could not have told you why. My old burn-scars were screaming where I'd grabbed it and I held it tighter. I was looking at the grease blackened into the weave, the mutton grease, my grease, the thing I had used to seal the seam so it would hold the heat that lifted the man so the man could fly so the army could see over the Loaf. I had built every link of that chain with my own hands and my own cleverness and there was a man at the end of it in the dirt and his children's names were in my head, the boy who minded the goats, and I knelt there holding the rag and I did not make a sound because there was no sound that was the right size.

Naso came and stood over me. I felt him before I looked up, the hitch in his step. He looked down at me a long time with his ruined hand against his chest. He didn't say I told you to make him keep his seat. He didn't say anything. He reached down with his good hand and closed it over the rag in my fist, not to take it, just to put his hand over mine on it, the one hand he had left, and then he took it away and limped off toward the baggage where there were mules to tend, because a man with a ruined hand can still tend mules and the work does not stop for anybody.

---

Celer found me still kneeling there when the body was gone and the ground had been turned over and dirt thrown until the burned smell was mostly under the smell of churned earth.

He crouched in front of me, the way Apollodorus had crouched by the cloth that morning, easy on his haunches. His palms had rope-burn across them, fresh. He looked at the rag still in my hand and he looked at my face and he did not do the arithmetic, or if he did it I didn't see it, and I had wanted to see it. I had wanted him to be cold so I could hate him and hating him would have been somewhere to put it.

"You said it," he said. His voice was rough and low and steady. "You said the wind. You said all of it. I heard you and I let it go anyway. That's mine." He didn't say it like a confession. He said it like a fact he was entering into a ledger, the way you'd note a wagon lost in a river crossing. "He volunteered. They'll write it down that he volunteered. It won't be a lie."

He stood up. His knees cracked. He looked out across the valley at the Loaf sitting there brown and round with the Dacians still uncounted behind it, and I watched the soldier come back into his face, because it had to, because a column moved in three days and Sabinus had died for nothing if nobody learned the one thing the army wanted to learn, and Sabinus had not even gotten high enough to count them.

"No more men go up in it," Celer said. "Not from me. I'll tell the legate it can't be done from this side and I'll be telling him the truth, because you told me first." He almost left it there. Then he looked back down at me, at the rag, and something crossed his face that he didn't let stay, the same thing that had crossed it over Naso in the wharf yard a world ago. "It's a good thing you made," he said, "and it's a butcher, and now it's killed a man, and you're going to have to decide what kind of man builds a thing like that. I can't decide it for you. I've decided my own." He went to send the litter and write the letter.

---

I did not go back to the trampled patch for the rest of that day. They cleared it without me, the willow and the wet planks and the long line I'd tied so we'd have a thread to follow, all of it. Pamphilus found me near the river after dark, where I'd gone to be where the noise of water could be louder than the inside of my head, and he didn't say anything, because Pamphilus had a riveted collar around his own neck that I could not take off and he knew better than anyone in that camp what it was to build the thing your owner told you to build. He just sat down near me on the bank with his cut-cloth hands hanging between his knees, and after a while he started the small fire I'd taught him to start for boiling water, because the flux was still in the camp and we still boiled our water, because that was a thing that worked, that I knew worked, that did not lie on a good day.

I watched him feed the little fire. The same orange. The same lick of it up off the coals. I had spent two years teaching myself not to be afraid of fire so I could make it lift a man into the sky, and I sat on the bank of a river whose name I couldn't say and watched a small clean fire boil water that would keep men alive, and I held the scorched corner of cloth that I had carried all day and not been able to set down, and I put it, finally, into the fire, and watched the grease in it catch and the bright line run up the weave one more time until there was nothing left of it but the smell, and that smell I will not get out of the back of my throat as long as I live.

---

# Chapter 14: What It Costs

The war did not end the way I had pictured wars ending. I had a teenager's idea of it, gates thrown open, a king kneeling, a single afternoon when the thing was over and you could feel it stop. What happened instead was that the camp got quieter by degrees, the way a fever breaks, in sweat and small mercies, so that you only knew it afterward.

It came down through the ranks as rumor first, which is how everything came down. Decebalus had sent men to talk. Then it was that Decebalus had sent men to talk and Trajan had let them stand there long enough to learn the difference between a king and a man asking for terms. Then it was a name for it, the king would keep his head and most of his hills and give back the engineers and the standards and the deserters and tear down the forts and call himself a friend of Rome, which everyone in the camp understood to mean a man being let live on a leash until it suited Rome to take the leash off. Nobody cheered. A century down the line from us had been told three times over four days that they were going home and then not going home, and the third time a man threw his cup at the tent pole and got flogged for it, so by the time it was real the men received it the way you receive a thing you have stopped trusting.

I remember the morning I knew it for certain because Saturninus came to where I was packing the bag down into its crate and stood there picking at the splinter in his thumb he'd had for a week and said, "They're saying we winter south of the river." He did not say we won. He had buried too many to the flux to think in those terms. He said it the way you'd report that a long rain had finally let off, with a kind of suspicion that it might start again the moment you said so out loud.

So that was the war. The great machine I had ridden north inside of, the carts and the grain and the marching forts thrown up new every night, all of it slowed and turned and began, hugely, to drain back south. I had thought I would feel something at the end of it. What I felt was tired in a way that sleep did not touch, and a low steady relief that I was ashamed of, because relief meant I was getting to go home and Sabinus was getting carried home in a different kind of box, and I could not make the two facts sit in the same chest without one of them choking the other.

---

The bag went into its crate and I nailed the lid down myself.

I want to be precise about this, because it mattered, and because I did it cold and on purpose and not in the storm of the day Sabinus died. That night I had burned a corner of cloth at the river and that was grief, the kind that wants to destroy a thing with its hands. This was after. This was a clear morning with a hard white sky and my hands steady, and I crated the observation bag, the tethered one, the one that had only ever lifted rope and cloth strips and never a man, because Celer's mark was on a tablet somewhere that said the army had use of it still. The tethered bag could stand. It saw the far bank in still air and it killed nobody doing it, and a thing that does one small honest job and kills nobody is a thing I could nail a lid over and let live.

The other one I did not crate. There was no other one, by then, only the makings of one, the basket willow and the long planks and the patterns I'd cut for a wider envelope, all the careful improvements I'd been turning over for the next attempt before there was no next attempt. I gathered the patterns, the cut paper shapes of a bag built to carry a man higher and safer than the last bag had carried a man, and I did not burn them. Burning would have been a feeling. I folded them flat and I put them at the bottom of the crate under the tethered bag, where they would not be found and would not be used, and I nailed the lid down over both, the thing that worked and the thing that had wanted to, and that was the closest I came to a ceremony. Apollodorus would have called it sentiment. Maybe it was. I had built a thing to put a man in the sky and a man had come down out of the sky in pieces, and I was not going to put another one up there, and folding the patterns flat under the lid was me saying that to nobody, in a language with no words in it, the only kind of vow I trusted myself to keep.

Naso watched me do it. He had drifted over from the mules, the way he did now, slow, the ankle never having set quite true, and he stood with his ruined hand against his chest and his good one hooked in his belt and watched me hammer the lid down. When I straightened up he looked at the crate and then at me and there was a question in it that I answered before he asked, because I knew the shape of him by then.

"That one's done," I said. "The one that goes up with a man in it. That's done."

He worked his jaw. Naso had been a talker, before, by the small accounts I'd heard, a recruit who'd put his hand up for the high job because the high job got noticed. The fire had taken some of that out of him along with the use of the hand. He thought about it a while.

"Good," he said. And then, because he was eighteen and could not quite leave a thing alone any more than I could, "You'll build it again." He said it without heat, the way you'd tell a man he'd cough again in winter.

"No," I said.

He looked at the hand against his chest, the fingers curled in on themselves where they'd healed wrong, and he looked at me with something that wasn't unkind and wasn't soft either, the look of a man who has paid for his own opinion and feels entitled to it. "Everybody says no," he said. He went back to the mules.

He came south with the baggage. I made sure of it, which was a small thing and the largest thing I had the power to do, a word to Saturninus and a word that Saturninus carried to someone with a tablet, that the maimed one was useful with the mules and should be kept on the strength and not turned loose at the river to beg his way home on a ruined ankle. It was the kind of mercy that costs a slave nothing and weighs nothing in the ledger of what I owed and I did it anyway. A man with one hand can still mind mules. The work does not stop for anybody, and as long as there was work he had a place, and a place was the only thing I could buy him.

---

Celer found me the day before the column I traveled with broke camp.

He came on me at the horse-lines at a grey hour, in a plain tunic with no marks of rank on it, which I had learned to read as a man going off duty into himself for an hour. He had a wineskin and he did not offer it, which was Celer, and he stood next to me looking at the horses the way men look at horses when they have something to say and would rather look at horses.

"South tomorrow," he said.

"South tomorrow."

"You'll be glad of the city." He said city the way frontier men said it, half a sneer and half a hunger.

"I'll be glad of a roof that doesn't get struck every night," I said, "and bread that isn't more grit than bread, and a day where nobody asks me to put a man over a hill."

He let that sit. A horse down the line stamped and blew. Celer had rope-burn scars across both palms now, healed pale, from the day he'd hauled on the lines that didn't save anyone, and he stood there with those hands flat on the rail and he did not look at me and he said, in the flat ledger voice, "I wrote the letter. The legate has it. Can't be done from that side." A pause. "I left your name out of the part that mattered and put it in the part that didn't. You scouted, you signaled, the bag served. The rest of it's a soldier's death and soldiers die." He turned his head a fraction. "You understand me."

I understood him. He was telling me he had taken it onto his own back where the army could see, the whole weight of it, so that the thing would not be hung on a Thulean slave who could be sold or whipped or worse for an officer's mistake. He was telling me I was protected and he was telling me he had decided the thing he'd said he'd decided, the kind of man he was, and the kind of man he was had cost him in the writing of it. He was not going to say any of that. He was a man who would rather be flogged than caught caring out loud.

"Thank you," I said, which was the wrong size, which was always going to be the wrong size, and he heard the wrongness of it and was relieved by it, because a thing the right size would have made us both ridiculous.

"Don't thank me," he said. "I let it go up." He drank from the skin then, finally, one long pull, and handed it to me, and I drank, and it was bad wine, and we stood at the horse-lines drinking bad wine in the grey, two men who had stood at the edge of a trampled patch of ground together, and we did not say his name, Sabinus, we did not say it, and the not-saying of it was the realest thing that passed between us. After a while Celer pushed off the rail.

"Build me something that doesn't need a man hung off it," he said, which from him was nearly tender. "Or build me nothing. Either one." He went off toward the command tents and the war that was ending around us, and I did not see him again before the column moved, and I was glad of that, because I would only have got the size of it wrong a second time.

---

The road south was the road north run backward and it was nothing like the road north. North I had ridden inside the machine half-drunk on the scale of it, the genius of the supply, the marching forts, a teenager's awe at how cleverly Rome could put twenty thousand men anywhere it wanted them. South I rode in the same machine and saw what the cleverness was for. We passed the markers of the road we had built going up. We passed graves. The flux had not signed any treaty with anybody and it came south with us in the men's guts, and at one camp a man two fires over from mine was loud in the night and quiet by morning, and they wrapped him and we moved on, and I boiled my water and Pamphilus boiled his and I gave a cup of the boiled stuff to a muleteer who would not take it twice because it tasted of nothing and a man wants his water to taste of the river, and that was the whole of my power against the thing that was killing the army, one cup of flat water that a grown man wouldn't drink because it bored him.

I thought about the flux a great deal on that road. I had filed it away going north, a thing I was powerless against, and powerless I still was, but somewhere between the Loaf and the Alps the filing-away curdled into something with a harder edge. I had spent two years and a man's life teaching fire to lift a basket, and the basket killed, and the cup of boiled water that bored a muleteer would have saved him and a hundred like him if I could only make a hundred like him drink it. I had been building the wrong thing. Not wrong because it failed. Wrong because of what it was for. The balloon had only ever had one honest customer and his name was war, and war had taken my best work and put a man in it and burned him, and I had let it, because letting it was the price of being worth keeping.

I did not have a word for what I decided on that road. I was a slave. I would build what I was told to build or I would be a slave who built nothing, which is a slave with a shorter life. But inside that, in the inch of choosing a man owns even in a cage, I could choose which of the things they wanted from me I gave eagerly and which I gave slow, and which I pretended I could not do at all. I could put my hands toward the things that kept men out of the dirt and drag my feet toward the things that put them in it. It was a small freedom and it was the only one I had and I took it.

---

Rome came up out of the haze on a hot afternoon and I cried, which surprised me, a few hard ugly tears I scrubbed off before Pamphilus could see, because the city was loud and filthy and reeked of garum and dung and wet wool and it was the first thing in two years that smelled like being alive instead of dying, and because Sabinus would never smell it, and the two things came up my throat at once again the way they had in the camp, and I let them, walking, with my eyes streaming and my face turned away, in the safest privacy a slave gets, which is the privacy of being beneath notice in a crowd.

Heras was at the wharf yard when the steward's man brought me to it. I do not know how he knew the day. He was a freedman with no business at a timber wharf and he was there anyway, sitting on an upturned crate in the shade of the shed with a scroll he was not reading, and when I came through the gate he looked up and looked at me for a long moment, the whole length of me, the way he had looked at me the first day when he was deciding whether I was mad. He had heard. Of course he had heard. A man had died in my machine on the Danube and that kind of thing travels faster than the man who survives it.

He did not ask what happened. That was Heras. A lesser friend would have asked, would have wanted the story, would have offered the thing people offer, that it was not my fault, that I had warned them, that the army overruled me, all of it true and all of it useless. Heras knew it was useless. He was a physician. He had stood over more dead men than I would ever count and had long ago run out of things to say that helped.

"You've gotten thin," he said. "And you stink. The frontier agrees with no one." He stood, his knees doing the thing Celer's did, the thing every man's knees over forty did, and he picked up his scroll. "There is a bath two streets over that I have been told does not have a corpse in the cistern this month. We are going to it. You are going to be quiet and I am going to talk about Epicurus, who has the great advantage of being dead and therefore unable to put men in baskets, and you are going to listen or pretend to, and tomorrow we will look at the water."

"The water," I said.

"You wrote me about the water." He had stopped at the gate. "From the road north. A bad letter, half of it about a bridge. But there was a thing in it about the flux and the boiling and how the army drinks from wherever it stops and dies of it. I kept that part." He looked at me with those dry clever eyes that had punctured every grand thing I had ever brought him and had, every time, quietly kept the part that worked. "You came back wanting to build a different thing than you left building. I can hear it in you like a cough. Don't tell me what it is yet. Tell me at the bath, after you don't stink." He went out into the lane and waited for me to follow, an old Greek who had decided to be my friend on no evidence and had never once let me make a speech, and I followed him, and we did not say Sabinus either, Heras and I, not that day. We did not need to. He had set the man down between us by naming the water, and we both knew it, and we left him there, present and unspoken, and went to wash the frontier off me.

---

The yard was Tyche's now, in the way that a place belongs to whoever has run it while you were gone.

I saw it the next morning, properly, in the early light before the wharf got loud. The well, the shed, the tally-room, all of it standing, all of it run. There were cloth bolts stacked where I had left chaos. There was a tally on the wall in the new figures, my figures, in a small fierce hand that was hers, columns of receipts and outlays that balanced, that she had kept balanced for a year and more with nobody to check her and Onesimus the old accountant surely sneering at every numeral. She had not let it slide. She had not let them take it from her. A girl of sixteen with a slave's collar of caution still around everything she did had held my whole small enterprise together by sheer stubborn competence, and the wall of figures was her saying so, in the only language that was safe for her to say it in, which was the language of work done right.

She came out of the tally-room when she heard me and stopped in the doorway. She had grown. Two years. She had the look on her she'd had the first day, the watching look, the slave's quick read of a man's mood before she committed to a single word, and she read mine, and whatever she read made her not say the thing people say.

"You ate on the road," she said instead. Not a question. An accusation, almost, that I had managed to feed myself without her arranging it. The first thing she had ever said to me had been about bread.

"Badly," I said. "There was grit in everything. I think I ate a denarius' worth of the actual road."

The corner of her mouth moved, which from Tyche was a great deal. Then her eyes went past me to the gate, counting, the way she always counted, who had come, who had not.

"Pamphilus," I said, before she could ask. "He came south. He's well. As well as he is." I did not say the other names because there were no other names, because the man who had not come back had never been mine to bring, and she knew that too, and she let it go by, and went back to the tally as if the conversation were over, which with her it was.

I stood in the yard a while after she'd gone in. It was a workbench under a lean-to, a well, a shed, a wall I was kept behind, the finest cage I had. The river smelled like a river. Somewhere out past the wall the wharf was waking up, men shouting timber tallies, a cart, a dog. On the bench were the tools I had left, a knife, a needle, the awl, the things I had used to teach fire to lift a man, all of them just as good for other work, all of them waiting to be told what they were for now.

I picked up a scrap of wax tablet and the bronze stylus and I sat down on the upturned crate Heras had warmed and I started, not with the balloon, not with anything that flew, not with the powder I knew the army would want from me the moment somebody clever asked the right question, the white salt and the charcoal and the third thing I had written ??? for in a cell two years ago and had never let myself chase, because I had seen now what fire did to a man and I was not going to be the one who taught fire to do it on purpose, not yet, maybe not ever, not while I had a say in the order I built things in and I meant to fight for that say. I started with the water.

I drew the well. Then I drew, badly, from a half-memory of a thing I'd seen in a textbook diagram once and could not fully reconstruct, the cut-open guts of a slow filter, the layers, sand over charcoal over gravel, the way Heras and I had played at it years ago before the balloon ate everything. I got the layers in the wrong order and knew it and turned the stylus and scraped them flat and tried again, and got it wrong a second way, and that was honest, that was the right kind of failure, the kind you can grind your way out of with a man who won't be impressed and a girl who won't let the figures slide and enough mornings. I would get it wrong a hundred more times. Sand does not sterilize. I knew that much, I'd told Heras that much. You still had to boil. But you could make the water clear enough that a man would drink it, clear enough that it didn't taste of the river he was afraid to leave behind, and a man who would drink it was a man you could keep out of the dirt, and there were a great many men.

Tyche came back out with bread and set it on the bench beside the tablet without a word, because I had said I'd eaten badly and that was the same to her as not at all. I scraped the filter flat and started it a third time. The light came up over the wall. The stylus dragged in the wax, sand over charcoal over gravel, and I leaned into the work, and behind me the wharf got loud, and the day started whether I was ready for it or not, the way days do.

---

## PART THREE — How Far to Go

---

# Chapter 15: The Chokepoint

The filter worked, more or less, by the spring of the year I turned twenty-two.

I should be honest about what "worked" means. It made river water clear. You poured the brown wharf water in at the top of a barrel I had cut down and packed with gravel and char and clean river sand in the order I had finally gotten right after a humiliating month of getting it wrong, and what came out the spigot at the bottom was a water you could see the cup through, a water that did not taste of the eels and the offal and whatever the dyers two wharves up were sending down. It did not make the water safe. I knew that, and Heras knew that, and we never once let the wharf men hear us say it, because they would have drunk the clear water and skipped the boiling and died grateful. So we framed it the way I framed everything that touched the little ones, the invisible ones the gods send to climb inside a man and eat him from the gut out. Clear the water through the barrel, then boil it, then it is clean. Two acts, not one. Heras pretended the second one was a Greek physician's old wisdom about cooked water and bad air, which made it respectable, which made the wharf men do it, which was the whole game.

Heras came down most mornings that spring to look at the sick. He had a sailor with a leg gone wrong, a long red heat climbing it from a rope-burn, and he cleaned it with the boiled water and the posca and he saved the leg and probably the man, and he did it in front of me on purpose, the way he did everything in front of me on purpose, so that I would see a thing get better instead of worse for once in my life. I had spent two years watching my work hurt people. He was a patient man about correcting that.

"You see," he said, winding the clean linen, "no fire in the sky. No basket. A leg." He had a way of saying basket that I had asked him twice to stop and he had not stopped.

I watched him work and I should have felt the thing he wanted me to feel, the simple cleanness of it, a man fixing a man with his hands and a pot of boiled water. What I felt instead, watching, was the knife.

It was his knife I kept looking at. A small bronze blade he used for the dressings, and it would not hold an edge worth a damn, so he had a little oilstone in the satchel and he touched the blade to it between patients the way other men breathe. Bronze. The good Roman scalpels were bronze because bronze does not rust in a wound, but bronze is soft, and a soft edge tears where a hard edge cuts, and a tearing edge in a leg is the little ones' open door. The hard steel knives the butchers used took an edge and held it and went orange with rust in a week and could not go near a wound. He had to choose, every day, between an edge that lasted and an edge that would not poison the man, and the choosing was a fault line running straight down through the middle of everything he did.

And it was not just the knife. Once I had seen it I could not stop seeing it. I had wanted, that whole winter, to build Heras a finer thing for measuring out his drugs, a balance with a true thin beam that would not bend under its own weight, and I could not, because the beam wanted spring in it and Roman iron has no spring, it bends and stays bent like wet clay. I had thought, idly, about the clock I was never going to build, and the clock died on a spring too. I had drawn a plow share that would not blunt after one field and could not make the steel for it. I had imagined a hundred fine small honest things, none of them weapons, all of them saving, instruments and edges and springs and gears, and every single one of them ran headlong into the same wall, which was that I had no metal worth a damn to build them out of.

You cannot go around it. That is the thing I understood that spring, sitting on the crate watching Heras strop a knife that would not stay sharp. There is no clever path that gets you to the saving instruments without first getting you the metal, because the instruments are made of the metal. The metal is the floor under all of it. Better tools to make the better tools. Better steel, or nothing finer than bronze and bog iron for the rest of my life, which was the same as saying nothing finer than bronze and bog iron for the rest of theirs.

"You've stopped listening," Heras said.

"I need a smith," I said.

---

Macer found me the smith, because Macer found me everything, in the way a man stocks a horse he intends to race.

I had learned by then how to want a thing in front of Macer. You did not ask. You let him discover that the thing was money. I told the steward, who told Macer, that I could make iron goods harder and finer than what came off the wharf smiths, harder edges, fittings that did not bend, and that men who sold to the army paid for hard and fine. That last part was the only part Macer heard. He sold leather and timber and grain to the legions, and a legion buys iron the way it buys everything, by the ton and forever, and a contractor who could put his stamp on a better grade of it could name a better price. He turned the gold ring on his thumb the way he did when he was watching a number get bigger somewhere behind his eyes.

"You don't know iron," he said. "You know sky."

"I know a thing about iron the smiths don't," I said, which was a gamble and a lie sized to it. "I need a man who knows the rest."

"You need a man who'll suffer you," Macer said. "That's the rarer thing." But he sent word, because the number had gotten big enough behind his eyes, and three days later the steward walked me up off the wharf and away from the river into the lanes behind the cattle market where the smiths were, where the air goes from river-stink to coal-stink in the space of a street, and left me at a forge under a brick arch with a freedman named Gaius Julius Hermes.

The first thing about Hermes was the noise, which stopped when I came in, so that I walked into a sudden quiet and four men looking at me. Then the size of him. He was not tall. He was built the way the anvil was built, low and squared and meant to take a blow, with forearms like the hams that hung in the market and a chest like a wall, and burn scars up both arms gone shiny and white the way mine had on the inside of my right forearm, a constellation of old spatter. He was past forty and his hair and the stubble on his jaw had gone iron-gray, which I would have found funny on a smith if his face had left any room for finding things funny. It had not. He looked at my hands first. Everyone in Rome looked at my hands at some point, because my hands were soft for a man who claimed to make things, scribe's hands, and Hermes looked at them the way the magistrate two years gone had looked at my drawing of Italy, finding it both impossible and beneath him.

"You're the flying one," he said. His Latin was city Latin, fast, with the consonants bitten off short. "The one who burned the soldier."

"That was the army," I said.

"It was your bag." He turned back to the fire. A boy worked the bellows, a great wood-and-leather lung wheezing the coals up to a heat I could feel from the door, an orange that shaded toward yellow at the heart of it. Hermes drew an iron bar out with the tongs, a glowing length of it, laid it on the horn of the anvil, and the hammer came down, and the quiet broke, and he talked to me between blows without looking at me, which I came to understand was the only way Hermes ever talked, with his eyes on the metal and his words coming out in the spaces the hammer left.

"Macer says." Blow. "You'll make my iron harder." Blow. "Than I make it." Blow, and a turn of the bar. "I've been at this fire thirty years." The hammer paused, hung. "He was at it before me." He tipped his chin at an old man sitting against the wall, doing nothing, watching, a smith too old to swing. "His father had the arch before him. You've been at it how long, Thulean?"

"At iron? Not at all," I said.

The hammer came down hard, a finishing blow, and he sank the bar back in the coals. "At least you don't lie about that one," he said.

---

I am going to tell the steel honestly, because it is the truest thing in this whole account, truer than the balloon, and the truth of it is that I could not deliver it.

Here is what I had, the whole of what I had, the seed I carried in from the modern world that I thought would crack iron open like an egg. It was this: that the difference between soft iron and hard steel is a small amount of something from the coal, the black part of the coal, mixed into the metal. Too little and you have soft iron that bends. The right amount and you have steel that takes an edge. Too much and you have a thing that is hard and shatters like glass. Carbon, we called it where I came from, though I never used that word to Hermes, it would have meant nothing. And the second thing: that you could make a finished blade harder by heating it red and plunging it into water, and then take some of the shatter back out of it by warming it again gentle, to a color, and that the color told you how much shatter you had left. Hardness from the black of the coal. The bite-and-bend balance set by water and a gentle second heat. That was my whole gift to metallurgy, and it was real, every word of it true, and it was also nothing, because I could not tell him how much. I could point at the dial. I could not read it. I did not know the number and there was no number anyway, no scale, no measure, only a man's eye on a glowing thing in a dark shed.

Hermes already knew most of it in his hands without the words for it. That was the humbling part and I should have expected it and did not. The smiths knew that steel from certain hearths was better, that you could pack iron in charcoal and bake it and draw something harder off it, that you quenched a blade and it got hard and brittle and you "let it down" in the fire to take the brittle off. They had no theory. They had thirty years of hands. When I told Hermes that the hardness came from the coal soaking into the metal, he stopped the hammer and looked at me for the first time as if I had said a thing instead of made a noise, because it fit something he had felt and never been told. And then he said the thing that ended that small triumph.

"How much," he said.

"That's the work," I said. "That's what we'd find out."

"You don't know."

"No."

He went back to the fire. "Thirty years," he said, "I've not known either. So we're the same, you and me, except you came in here in a soft tunic to tell me we're the same."

---

We tried it anyway, because Macer was paying for the coal and the iron, and a man will try a great many things on another man's coal.

The path I pushed him toward was the one I half-remembered as the good one, the one the books had called the secret of the finest blades, which was to melt the steel. To get the iron hotter than baking it in charcoal ever did, hot enough to go liquid, in a sealed clay pot, with the iron and the black of the coal and some ash of bone shut in together away from the air, and hold it there long, a long furious heat, so the carbon went all through the melt even and true instead of skinned over the surface like the case-hardened stuff. Crucible steel. I did not have the word for them either but I drew the pots, fat little clay jars with lids luted on with more clay, and Hermes looked at the drawing and at me and made the pots, because he was a craftsman and the making of a clean clay pot that would not crack in a fire was a thing his hands knew how to argue with even when his face was arguing with me.

The first batch we got too cold. We knew it was too cold because when the pots came out and cooled and Hermes broke one open with the hammer there was a lump in the bottom that was not steel, a grainy sponge, iron that had soaked up the black and gotten hard at the edges and never melted, never gone liquid, never mixed. He turned it in his scarred fingers. "Sponge," he said, and dropped it in the scrap, and that was the whole of his comment, and the boy banked the fire higher for the next try.

Because the trouble, the whole trouble, the trouble that beat us that spring and was going to beat us for years, was that we could not hold the heat and we could not read it. There was no way to know how hot the fire was. None. We judged it by color, by the orange going to yellow going to a white that hurt the eyes, and a man's eye is a liar that changes its mind by the hour, brighter at dusk, dimmer at noon with the arch-mouth full of daylight, different from Hermes's eye to mine to the boy's. We judged it by how long, counting, and the counting drifted. We fed it with charcoal because charcoal burns hotter and cleaner than the wood I'd grown up taking for granted you could just turn a dial and have, and good charcoal cost, and we burned through a frightening amount of Macer's coal getting one pot hot enough while the pot beside it sat too cold because the fire was not even across its floor, because no fire is even, because we were two men and a boy and a leather lung blowing air at a clay box and calling it a furnace.

The second batch melted. I knew it had melted before Hermes broke the pot, because the pot when it cooled had a different ring to the tap of his finger, and when he cracked it there was a button of steel in the bottom, gray and dense and whole, a little risen dome of it, and even Hermes went quiet over it. He took it to the anvil and worked it down and folded it and worked it again, frowning the whole time in a new way, not the contempt frown, a different one, and he drew it out into a blade the length of my forearm and ground it and we quenched it in the trough, the long hiss, the steam, and he let it down to a color, a straw color creeping along the steel as it warmed, the gold edge of brittle burning off the way I had told him it would, and when it was cool he set its edge to a soft iron rod and it bit, it sheared a curl off the rod and held its edge, and he ran his thumb along the flat of it and looked at it the way I imagine a man looks at a child he was not expecting to love.

"That," he said, "is a blade."

It was. It was the best thing either of us had ever made. I want that written down, because of what came after. For one afternoon in a coal-smelling shed behind the cattle market, two men who had no business succeeding made a piece of steel that a king would have worn, and we did not know why, and that was the poison in it, that we did not know why.

---

The third batch we did everything the same. That is the part I cannot make anyone who has not done it understand. Everything the same. Same pots, Hermes's hands. Same iron from the same bar. Same charcoal, same ash of bone, measured by the same horn scoop. The boy on the same bellows. We brought the fire up to the same color, or to the color we both swore was the same, in the same shed, and we held it for the same count, and Hermes had gotten superstitious about it by then and made the boy keep the count out loud and made me keep it too so there were two counts to disagree and we'd take the longer, and we pulled the pots and let them cool and he tapped one with his finger and his face changed.

The ring was wrong. He knew before he broke it. He broke it anyway, and the button in the bottom was there, gray and dense and whole, it had melted, it looked like the good one, it looked exactly like the good one, and he worked it down and folded it and drew it into a blade and ground it and we quenched it, the long hiss, the steam, and he started to let it down to the straw color.

It cracked in the trough. Not even at the tempering. It cracked in the quench, a sound like a green stick stepped on, and when he lifted it out of the water it came up in two pieces, the break face bright and crystalline and grainy in the light, sugar, not metal, and Hermes stood there with half a blade in the tongs and water running off his wrist and said nothing for a long time.

"Too much black in it," I said. I did not know that. I was guessing. It could have been too much carbon, gone past steel into the brittle cast stuff. It could have been the heat. It could have been a flaw in the iron I'd never see. I was a boy who had read the principle and could not read the metal, standing next to a man who could read the metal and had no principle, and between the two of us we still could not tell why one Tuesday gave us a king's blade and the next Tuesday gave us sugar.

"You said." Hermes set the two pieces on the anvil, gently, side by side, fitting the break back together so it looked whole, a blade with a thin bright line through it. "You said the black makes it hard. The water makes it hard. The second fire takes the hard back out. You said it like a man reading off the back of a coin." He looked up at me and there was no contempt in it now, which was worse, because what was there was a workman's flat tired anger at being made a fool of by something. "Tell me how much black. Tell me how hot. Tell me how long. Tell me one number, Thulean, one I can do twice."

"I can't," I said.

"Then you've told me nothing." He swept the two halves of the blade off the anvil into the scrap with the back of his hand, the king's-blade twin gone in among the sponge and the broken pots, and the sound it made going in was the only verdict he gave it. "You've told me what I already knew with prettier words. The fire is the master. Always was. You don't measure the fire. You stand in front of it your whole life and it lets you make a good blade when it feels like it." He pulled his apron straight and turned back toward the arch where the coals were still going, the heat coming off them in a wall, the orange shading toward yellow that meant nothing, that told us nothing, that we would chase tomorrow and the day after and the year after with our eyes and our counting and our luck.

"We try again," I said.

"We try again," Hermes said, not as agreement, the way you'd say a thing was raining.

I stood in the doorway of the forge a while after, the way I'd stood in the yard the morning I came back from the frontier, looking at the tools of a thing I could not yet make do what I needed it to do. The good blade was somewhere in Macer's house by now, set aside to show the steward, proof of a thing we could not prove again. The bad one was in the scrap with its bright line through it. Between them was every spring I was never going to forge, every fine balance and true beam, every share and gear and barrel of a thing the army was eventually going to want me to make burst, all of them waiting on a number nobody in the world could give me, a heat nobody could hold, sitting on the far side of a wall I had finally walked all the way up to and put my soft scribe's hands flat against.

The boy banked the fire for the night. The coals settled and dimmed and went the deep red of a thing cooling, which was the one color in that shed I could read for certain, because it meant the work was over and we still did not know why.

---

# Chapter 16: The New Figures

The thing about a good idea is that it walks off without you.

By that summer I had stopped being able to find the new figures. For two years they'd lived in one place I could see, the column Tyche kept on the wax tablet in the wharf yard, and a second I could half see, the practice scratchings of Macer's younger clerks, who used them on the sly the way boys use a curse word, with one ear cocked for Onesimus. They'd been a secret the house kept from itself. Now they were loose in the city and I could not say how far.

I found them first on a fish-seller's slate down by the Aemilian bridge, the morning's prices chalked in the figures, clean, a little 4 and a 6 and the wide-open 0 that Romans had never had and could not stop staring at. I asked her, in my w-for-v Latin that still made people decide I was simple, where she'd learned them. From her son, she said, like I'd asked where she'd learned to breathe. He kept the book for a grain factor across the river, and the factor had them off another factor, back up a chain nobody could see the top of, because the top of it was a barrel in a yard by the timber wharf and nobody was looking there.

It had nothing to do with anyone admiring me. Heras had it right years ago when I tried to sell the figures as a wonder and he laughed at me. Nobody adopts a wonder, he said. They adopt a thing that saves them an afternoon. The figures saved everybody an afternoon. You could add a column of them straight down the way you add nothing in Roman numerals, where adding is its own small ordeal of counters and a board and a man who's done it twenty years. More than that, the part the factors loved with a love close to fear, you could not cheat them as easily. A Roman ledger in the old numerals is a forest, and a clever steward can lose a herd in it. Lay the same accounts in the figures, places lined up, the running sum at the foot, and the forest turns into a field where a man skimming his master shows up plain. The merchants who took up the figures fastest were the suspicious ones, robbed by their own bookkeepers and wanting a writing that robbed harder to do.

So it spread the way water spreads, downhill, into the low cheap places, the slates and the day-books and the back of a tile a porter scratched a tally on. The high places stayed dry. The figures were not on a single contract that mattered, not on a tombstone, not on a milestone, not on anything a man wanted to last or look dignified. Onesimus had a phrase for them that went around the counting houses: shopkeeper's marks. He meant it to bury them and instead named them true. Rome ran on shopkeepers, and the shopkeepers were teaching their sons.

I went home that day and was not happy, because the slate had reminded me how little of me was actually loose in the world, and how much of what was loose was the one cheap thing I'd given away free in a cell four years ago, while everything since, the flying and the burning and the dead men, had stayed locked in a yard.

---

The books were slower, and they were mine in a way the figures had stopped being.

We had blocks now. Not many. The first one, the ugly gray line of Homer I'd cut on pearwood and printed crooked, with the doubled letter and the starved right edge, still sat in the pigeonhole where I kept my failures, and I'd cut a hundred after it, each a little less crooked. A Greek-letterer worked three days in five in a back corner of the yard, a freedman named Eros with a steady hand and a deep boredom, carving my blocks for a piecework wage out of my peculium, and I borrowed a pressman off an oil house when their screw stood idle. We could do a page at a time, the same page fifty times in a morning, sixty if the ink behaved, and the ink mostly did not behave, too thin and crept or too thick and filled the letters to blots, and we threw away one sheet in four and called that a good day.

What we made were not new books, and I want to be clear about that, because the dream had a way of getting ahead of the press. We made cheap copies of books that already existed. A page of Aesop. A schoolmaster's list of maxims. A grammar of the kind a boy is beaten through. The first thing that ever sold, that a stranger put a coin down for without Macer arranging it, was a single printed sheet of the multiplication a child needs, the times-tables a Roman boy spends a year crying over, laid out in the new figures, fifty copies sold to a Subura schoolmaster for less than the cost of copying one out by hand. He did not care that a machine had made it or that I had made the machine. He cared that it cost two asses.

Two asses. A scroll copied by a scribe is days of a man's life and is priced like it. Our sheet was cheap because the cutting was done once and the printing nearly free after, and that gap, between the scribe's days and the press's morning, was the only revolution I ever started that I could watch turning. A schoolmaster could own fifty sheets, lend them out, get them back grubby, and not weep over the loss. Reading had been a wall with a narrow gate and the gate was money. I had not knocked the wall down, nowhere near. I'd cut one extra notch in it, low down, where the small people could get a hand through.

Heras came to look at the press the way he came to look at everything, ready to be unimpressed and prepared to be honest if he failed at it. He picked up a fresh-pulled sheet of the Aesop, held it at arm's length and then close, turned it over to look at the back where the ghost of the letters pushed through, and was quiet for longer than was like him.

"It's gray," he said finally. "And the line wanders."

"It's the same gray fifty times," I said. "That's the trick. The wander I'll fix. The fifty I've already got."

He set the sheet down and wiped his fingers, although there was nothing on them. "When I was a boy in Pergamon," he said, "the great library was the wealth of the city. A king's wealth, scrolls a man could not buy in a lifetime, kept behind a door, lent to scholars by favor." He looked at the fables drying on the line, common as washing. "You're proposing to drown that. To make the king's wealth common as fish."

"Yes," I said.

"Some men will hate you for it more than for the fire in the sky," Heras said, and he was not wrong.

---

The idea I could not build came to me at the press, with my hands black, talking to Tyche.

She was eighteen that summer and had stopped being a child I was hiding and become a problem I was hiding, which is a different and worse thing. She could do arithmetic faster than any man in Macer's counting house, and we both knew that if Macer heard the full size of it he'd sell her to a factor for a price that would make him grin, because a slave who can keep a great house's books is worth a small farm, and I could not stop it, being property myself. So we did the work quietly and let the yard think she was a girl who kept a cloth tally.

She was setting the figures of a child's times-table for Eros to cut, copying them out large on a board, and she said, not looking up, "Who teaches the children that machines can do the work of a hundred men."

I thought I'd misheard her. "What?"

"This." She tapped the board, the bored neat sevens. "You make the page cheap so the children learn the figures. The figures are a tool. A child learns the tool." She set the chalk down and looked at me, and Tyche looking at you straight was a thing you felt in your sternum. "But he learns them the way he learns to carry water. Because his father did. Nobody learns to carry water and then dreams of the sea. Who teaches them the sea."

She had a way of taking a thing I'd half said and handing it back as a question I couldn't answer. I stood there with ink on my hands and the idea arrived whole, the way the bad ones and the good ones both do, with no warning and no humility.

A contest. A prize. Money out of a strongbox, every year on a fixed day, given for a story. Rome already had a thousand poets choking the salons with gods and wars. This was for a story of a machine that does the work of a hundred men. A cart that runs faster than a horse with no horse in it. A glass that shows you the little ones crawling, the invisible made visible. Let men write lies, I thought, beautiful detailed plausible lies about things that did not exist, and compete to lie the best, and read the best of it aloud to a crowd, and hand the best liar a purse of silver. Do it once and it's a curiosity. Do it every year for forty years and you've raised a generation who will not, when one of those machines turns out to be possible, find it strange. They'll have been waiting for it. The thing you cannot build in iron you can build in a boy's head, years early, the way a tree grows around a stake.

I said some of this out loud to Tyche, fast, too fast, my Latin tripping. She listened with her chin down and her eyes up, the way she did when I was being either very clever or very stupid and she hadn't decided which.

"It needs money," she said when I ran down.

"A strongbox of it. Every year."

"You have a peculium Macer lets you touch." She said it without unkindness, which was worse than if she'd been cruel. "And it needs a man to stand up in front of the room and say, I, this man, give this prize. They won't take a prize from a slave. They won't take a fable about machines from the Thulean who burned a soldier and a washerwoman." She picked the chalk back up. "You'd need to be free and respectable and rich, and you're none of those, and you're talking about it like it's tomorrow."

"It's not tomorrow," I admitted.

"No," she said, and bent back to her sevens. But she'd stopped, for one breath, and looked at the board not seeing it, the way you look at a door you've decided to walk through someday, and the idea hadn't died, it had only gone somewhere to wait, in her as much as in me.

I told Heras that evening too, walking him to the gate. "You want to make men homesick for a country that doesn't exist yet," he said, and pulled his cloak up. "That's either the wisest thing you've said to me or the saddest, and I genuinely cannot tell which. Mind it doesn't make you new enemies. The poets," he said, with the small precise distaste of a Greek physician for the Latin literary trade, "do not like to be told the future belongs to plumbers."

---

The future, as it happened, was waiting at Mettius's house with a cup of watered Falernian and four years of resentment, and it had read its Strabo again.

Macer sent me back to the salon, and I went more willingly than I once would have. Two years had put Latin enough in my mouth that I no longer stood there mute, and I'd let myself imagine I might walk in and be, if not feared, at least not furniture. Macer stood at the back in his good wool with no stripe, arms folded over the gold ring, watching his investment, which had appreciated.

Crispus had not appreciated. He had concentrated. He was oiled and easy on his couch the way he'd been before, and when Mettius made his little flourish of presenting me, the Thulean, the man whose marks were on every fishwife's slate in the city now, ha, Crispus did not unfold and stroll over to play with me. He stayed put and let me come to the middle of the room, and he smiled the warm smile that had never once meant anything, and waited until the talk had quieted enough that he would not have to raise his voice, which is its own kind of power, and then he addressed the room.

"I've been thinking about our friend," he said, "since last we had him. I confess he's troubled my sleep. Not as a marvel. As a question. Bear with an old reader." He had a scroll by him on the couch, half open, and he touched it without picking it up, two fingers laid on it, so we'd know he'd done his work. "Four years ago I made a small joke about Thule. About Pytheas, the Massaliot, the great liar, and his far country no ship can reach. You'll remember." He looked at me, soft. "I treated it as wit. I've since decided that was a failure of seriousness on my part, and I have let it go too long, as I think have all of us, charmed."

The room had gone still in the way a room goes still when it scents blood and is ashamed of itself for liking the smell. I felt the heat start up my neck the old way and I made myself breathe and stand square and not look at Macer.

"Consider what we have here," Crispus said, and now he did rise, unfolding from the couch, and he was good, I'll give him that, he moved like a man who had spent his life being watched and decided to enjoy it. "A foreigner. No city, none we can write to, none that can send for him or vouch for him or claim him. He comes from a place that Strabo, the gravest geographer of the age, who measured the bones of the world, names a fiction invented to gull fools." He paced, easy, the scroll left behind, his audience caught. "He draws us seas on the far side of the earth where no keel will go to check his hand. He teaches foreign figures to fishwives and slaves and the keepers of grain-factors' books, the senators and scholars passed over, so that quietly, cheaply, beneath our notice, the accounts of Rome are kept in a script no honest Roman elder can read at a glance. He prints copies of our books fifty at a stroke, gray and crooked, cheap as fish, so any porter's brat may own a page of letters and any letter be multiplied past all controlling." He stopped. He had them. "I ask only this. By what name do we call a stateless man with no city and no patron-by-blood, who arrives from a country proven not to exist, who unsettles our commerce, who cheapens our letters, who flew a fire that the gods, in the hearing of this whole city, struck back down upon our roofs? In Greek there is a clean word. *Goēs.* A charlatan, a worker of false wonders. And there is a graver word, which I will not be the one to say first in a friend's house, except to note that the law knows it, and that the law has a long memory for foreigners who trouble the order of things and call down fire."

That last was the knife, and he slid it in gently and stepped back from it so the room could look. He had not said magic. He had said the law knows a graver word, naming impiety without owning it, the way you point at a man's house and say nothing about the man. And the worst of it, the thing that closed my throat, was that every separate fact he'd laid out was true. He had built the whole charge out of true bricks, and that is what makes a clever man dangerous, that he does not invent the bricks, he only chooses which ones to stack and in what order, and Crispus was a master of the order.

I could not win it as an argument, and I knew that standing there with the heat in my face. He had Strabo and Greek and forty years of standing in rooms like this, and I had broken Latin and a true country I could not name and a dead soldier I could not explain. If I argued Thule he'd quote me Pytheas line by line. If I argued the gods I'd be a foreigner lecturing Romans on their own gods, the shortest road to the graver word he'd been too careful to say.

So I didn't argue. I asked Mettius for a cup of water.

It threw the rhythm, which was the only thing I had. Mettius blinked and a slave brought a cup and I held it up to the lamps so the room could see it, plain water, clear, drank half of it, and then said, in my w-for-v Latin, slow because slow was all I had and slow at least sounds like a man choosing his words instead of a man drowning, "Lucius Vettius Crispus has not had the flux."

It wasn't clever. It landed in the room like a dropped plate. He stared at me.

"The bloody flux," I said. "The camp sickness. It killed more of Caesar's soldiers on the Danube last year than the Dacians did, and some man here, I'd wager, has buried a son or a brother of it, of bad water." I drank the rest of the cup. "I cannot make you read my figures. I cannot make you believe my country. You'll beat me on Strabo every time we meet, and I'm not going to lose to you in Greek. But the water in that cup came clear out of a barrel I packed, off the wharf, the foulest water in Rome, and I drank it in front of you, and I am not going to die of it. Any man here with a household who wants to stop burying his slaves and his children of the summer flux, I'm in the yard by the timber wharf, and the barrel costs less than this man's dinner." I set the cup down. My hand wasn't quite steady and I set it down anyway. "Call me a charlatan when the children stop dying. I'll answer the graver word the day my barrel kills somebody. It hasn't yet. His Strabo never saved a single child from anything. Mine's a worse book and it works."

It was not a victory, and I want to be exact, because it would be easy to write it as one. The cleverest men in the room did not suddenly love me. But I'd done the one thing his box had no door for, which was to put a true result on the table next to his true bricks and let the room weigh them with their own dead children in the scale, and you cannot beat that with Strabo, and Crispus knew it before I'd finished. He recovered beautifully, said something light about the Thulean preferring the company of fishwives to philosophers, got a laugh, a smaller one, a divided one, and turned his shoulder to me the old way. The easy contempt was gone out of him, though, and what replaced it was worse, and I'd put it there myself. He left early, and did not finish anyone's line of Callimachus on the way out.

---

There was one man in that room who had not laughed at either of us.

He'd come in late and taken a couch in the shadow near the door, and I hadn't marked him until the water was done, until the room was breaking up into its knots of talk and a slave was relighting a guttered lamp. An old man, past fifty, heavy in the shoulders, with the still, weighing face of a man whose work is to read a sign in a slick of blood and tell a consul whether to march. I knew him before Mettius murmured the name. I'd seen that face once before, four years ago, across a festival crowd, looking up from a steaming gut on an altar to lock his eyes on the boy whose burning linen bag had just come down on Rome's roofs.

Aulus Vibenius did not come over. He did not send a slave. He sat in the shadow with a cup he wasn't drinking and looked at me across the emptying room the way he'd looked across the crowd, without warmth and without hate, the long patient look of a man adding a column he was in no hurry to total. He had heard Crispus name the graver word and not own it, and heard me name the dead children and the clear water and the gods I'd been careful not to lecture. He was the only man in Rome who could turn either of those into a charge the law would have to hear, or into a blessing that put the whole thing out of Crispus's reach forever, and his face gave away which way he was leaning the way the steel gave away why it cracked, which is to say not at all.

When I looked again the couch was empty.

Macer walked me down to the wharf himself that night, which he never did, and said nothing the whole way, turning the gold ring on his thumb, and at the gate where Tyche had the lamp lit he stopped and looked at me with the broken-veined face that never moved.

"You made an enemy in there worth keeping," he said, "and the wrong man saw it." He meant the priest. "Get the barrels into a senator's house before that crow makes up his mind. A man with the flux out of his halls won't let them burn you. Move." And he went off up the dark wharf, and Tyche held the lamp up to my face and read whatever was there and didn't ask.

I went into the back room and took the ugly first block of Homer off its pigeonhole, the crooked one, the doubled letter, the starved right edge, the failure I kept, and I sat under the lamp with the chalk and a clean board and started laying out, large and plain in the new figures, the rules a contest would need. A day. A prize. A subject. A machine that does the work of a hundred men. I couldn't build it. I couldn't fund it. I couldn't stand up free in any room in Rome and put my name to it. I wrote it out anyway, the whole shape of it, the way a man writes a letter to a country he means to go home to, and Tyche came and stood at my shoulder and read it over in the firelight, both of us looking at the door we hadn't walked through yet.

---

# Chapter 17: Citizenship

The summons came on a wax tablet, which I thought was a joke until I saw the seal.

It was midwinter, the wharf cold and the river the color of pewter, and a man came down to the yard in a clean tunic with a satchel and two others behind him who stood the way Macer's men stood, hands easy, eyes on mine. He gave the tablet to me and not to Pamphilus, which was the first wrong note, a stranger handing a thing to the slave instead of asking for the master. I broke the cord and read it slow, the way I read everything, the case-endings swimming and then settling, and the gist of it was that I was to present myself in eight days at a house on the Palatine, with my apparatus, and that the request was made in a name I had to read three times because I kept assuming I'd parsed it wrong.

I had not parsed it wrong.

Tyche read it over my shoulder, which she was not supposed to do in front of strangers and did anyway, and her breath went out of her in a small sound. The man with the satchel watched us both and said nothing about the slave girl reading Latin. He had bigger things on the tablet than that.

"Whose house," I said, because I wanted him to say it, because I did not believe it.

"You read," he said. "Eight days. Bring the flying thing and the water and the new figures. Bring the man who keeps your accounts." He looked at Tyche when he said the last, which meant someone had already told him a great deal about my yard, more than I'd told anyone, and that was the second wrong note and the worse one. Someone had been counting my barrels for me.

He left. I stood in the cold holding the tablet, and Tyche stood next to me holding the lamp though it was day, the way she'd held it that night at the gate, and after a while she said it.

"Macer didn't send this."

"No," I said.

"Macer doesn't even know about this."

"No," I said. "He's going to, though. About to."

***

Macer knew within the hour, because of course he had a man on the wharf, and he came down himself before the noon meal with his cloak thrown on crooked and the gold ring going on his thumb like a man working a problem in his hands.

He read the tablet the way he read a bill of lading he suspected of cheating him, twice, holding it to the light, and when he was done his broken-veined face had not moved at all, which with Macer meant it had moved a great deal.

"You wrote to somebody," he said.

"I didn't write to anybody. Who would I write to? I can barely write."

"Somebody wrote about you, then." He turned the tablet over as if the back would tell him who. "Celer," he said, almost to himself. "Celer's gone back up to the army, the second war's coming, they're stockpiling already, leather and grain, I've got the contracts. Celer talks. The flux talks louder." He looked at me, and there was a thing in his face I had not seen on it before, fear, and not of the emperor, of me, of what I'd quietly become while he was counting it as mules. "You understand what this is," he said. It was not a question, and I answered it like one.

"I understand he wants to see the balloon."

"He does not want to see the balloon." Macer set the tablet down on the workbench, flat, with one finger, the way Crispus had touched his scroll, and I hated that I noticed. "Men want to see the balloon. He's a soldier. Soldiers don't pay for marvels, they pay for things that come home alive. He wants the water. He's taking an army back into those hills in the spring, and he buried more men in the last war to the bloody flux than to the Dacians, I sold the leather for the tents they died in, and somebody up there has told him there's a Thulean by the timber wharf who drinks the worst water in Rome out of a barrel and doesn't die." He picked the ring around once. "He wants the water and the figures for the men who count the grain, and he wants to look at the man and decide if he's a fraud or a tool. And you're going up there, boy, to be a tool, because the other thing he does with frauds I am not going to describe to you in my own yard."

"Macer," I said.

"Bring me into none of it," he said, sharp, and that was the third thing I understood, slower than the others, that the man was protecting himself, that an emperor's interest in a piece of your property is weather you cannot insure against, that I had stopped being entirely his and he'd felt the deck shift under him. "You're mine, a thing I bought, that's the truth on any tablet, and if he wants to be told so I'll tell him on my knees and glad to. But you go up there and talk about water. Not Thule. Not the gods. Not the figures past what a quartermaster needs. Water, and the flying eye, and how many men it brings home. That's the whole of you for eight days. Can you be that small."

"I've had practice," I said, "being small," and he almost smiled, the mean half of a smile, and went away up the wharf with his cloak still crooked, and did not tell me good luck, because Macer did not believe in luck, he believed in leather and in the flux and in the price of a man.

***

I will not pretend I slept those eight days.

I packed the show bag, the good one, the pale gray greased linen that went up clean, and checked every seam twice by lamp and made Tyche check them after me, because a demonstration is a lie you tell on a good day, Apollodorus had given me that and I'd paid for it in a man's life, and I was not going to have the lie come apart in the air the way it came apart over the Loaf. Two barrels of foul wharf water, two of the filter sand and charcoal and gravel, a clean cup. A sheaf of the figures, a sum laid out the long Roman way beside the same sum in the new marks, the one a wall to climb and the other a stair. The abacus too, because the room is never convinced by the thing alone, it wants to see the thing beat the thing they already have.

The morning of, Tyche came with me, because the tablet had asked for the man who kept my accounts and the man who kept my accounts was a slave girl of nineteen, and I'd thought a long time about substituting Onesimus and decided against it, because if I was to be weighed I'd be weighed honest, and the honest truth of my yard was that the cleverest set of hands in it was hers. She wore her good tunic, the undyed one without the patch. She did not look afraid. She had got very good at not looking afraid, and I knew what it cost because I was wearing the same face.

The Palatine is up. That sounds stupid written down and it's the truest thing I can say about that walk. You climb up out of the noise of the city, the carts and the garum and the cookshops, and the higher you go the quieter it gets and the bigger the stone gets, and the men at the doors stop wearing tunics and start wearing the look. By the time we'd been passed from one set of them to the next four times and stood in a colonnade with a floor so polished it gave back the winter sky, I'd stopped being able to feel my hands. There was no incense. I'd expected marble and incense and gold, and instead it smelled of cold stone and lamp-oil and, faintly, of leather and horse, a soldier's smell, and that told me the truth about the man before I'd seen him.

They took the bag and the barrels from us and set them up in a smaller room than I'd pictured, a working room, a long table scarred from use, a brazier going, tablets stacked at one end the way they stacked at my own bench. A clerk was there, an old freedman with ink to the second knuckle, two officers in the dust-colored tunics, and a thin sharp man in his thirties standing apart who watched everything and said nothing, the kind of man an emperor keeps to remember things for him. Then a soldier came in from a side door wiping his hands on a cloth as if he'd come from some other work, broad, gray at the temples, with a square plain face that had been out in weather for thirty years, and everyone came to attention in a small way, a settling, and I understood that this unremarkable thick-necked man drying his hands was the most powerful human being on the surface of the earth.

He did not look like a god. That was the thing I kept failing to get over. He looked like a centurion who'd done well, like a man you'd ask to look at your cart axle. There was a scar on the back of one hand, old, white. He looked at me the way you look at a tool a man's recommended, hefting it with his eyes.

"You're the Thulean," he said. His Latin was plain and quick, the army's, no salon in it. "They tell me you fly."

"I make a thing fly," I said. My voice came out steadier than I'd any right to. "I don't go up in it. Caesar." I'd practiced the word a hundred times and it still felt like a stone in my mouth.

"Wise," he said, "if the stories are true," and that was the war already in the room, the dead at the Loaf, somebody had told him everything, and I felt the cold go down my back. He said it dry, not cruel, the way Celer said things, and I understood these were men of the same trade and the trade was killing and they spoke of it the way I'd spoken of deck screws. "Show me the flying eye first," he said. "Then the water. I care about the water." He sat on the edge of the table, the edge of a working table, and folded his arms over the scarred hand and waited to be either impressed or wasted.

I did not fill the bag. There was no room and I wasn't risking fire under that roof, so I laid the packed bag out on the table, the whole eleven feet of it folded to an armful, and unrolled it and showed him the seams and the mouth-hoop and talked, fast and plain, about what it did and only what it did. Tethered, in still air, a man's height of rope at a time up to a hundred and twenty feet. A man with good eyes in a basket, or no man and a glass if the glass were any good, which it wasn't, I told him the glass was a blurry toy. The far bank of a river. The smoke of a camp behind a hill. Signals down a line in colored cloth, a code, a thing seen from the height that the men on the ground cannot see. It failed in wind. It failed in cold. I had put two men up against my own judgment and burned them both, and one of them had died, and I would build him the tethered eye and the signals and never put another man under a free balloon as long as I drew breath, and he could have the eye or he could have nothing, those were the terms, and I heard myself give an emperor terms and stopped, and the room was very quiet.

He looked at me for a while.

"The one who died," he said. "Whose man."

"A volunteer. Marcus Aurelius Sabinus." I had not said the name out loud since the Danube. It came out wrong in that polished room, too small. "He had a boy who minds goats."

"You remember his name," the emperor said. Not a question. He looked at the officer nearest him, some small soldier's calculus passing between them I couldn't read, then back at me. "A man who'll give me the truth about what his own thing can't do is rarer than a man who flies," he said. "Crispus says you're a fraud." He dropped it on the table flat, watching what I'd do with it. So the salon reached even up here. Everything reached up here.

"Crispus is right about almost everything," I said. "I am foreign. My figures are foreign. My country can't send you a letter. He's stacked it all true. He's only wrong about whether the work works, and that one I can settle with a cup of water instead of an argument, and I'd rather, because he beats me at arguments." That got something out of the thin sharp man by the wall, not a laugh, a breath. The emperor didn't smile but the weather of his face changed.

"Settle it, then," he said.

So I did the thing I'd done in the salon, except it mattered more and my hands knew it. I drew a cup of the foul water and held it to the light, brown, a thread of something turning in it, set it down, then poured the rest of that barrel slow through the filter into a clean jar, the sand taking the worst of it, and it came out the far side near clear.
