I held that up too, and told him the truth Heras made me always tell, that clear is not safe, that you must still boil it, that the sand cleans the eye and the fire kills what's in it, the two together the whole of it, no marvel in it, any quartermaster taught it in a morning. And then because the cup was the thing they remembered, I poured a fresh one off the day-old boiled-and-filtered barrel I'd carried up cold, and drank it down in front of him, the worst water in Rome, and set the cup on his scarred table and looked at him.

"How many men," he said.

"Caesar?"

"To the flux. On the Danube. The last war." He was leaning forward now, off the edge of the table, and the affable weather was gone out of him and underneath it was the thing Macer had tried to warn me about, the thing that was the state, the cold doing-of-sums about the lives of men. "I lost more to the latrines than the line. You're telling me a barrel of sand and a fire saves them."

"Some of them," I said. "Not all. I won't lie and say all. I've got no way to count the dead I'd have saved against the dead I didn't. But fewer. Camps sited above the fouling, not below. Water boiled by century, not by whim. Latrines downstream and limed. The men who keep clean and drink clean don't go down with it the way the rest do. I watched it on the road north, I boiled my own and didn't take sick when men around me did, and that's one man, no proof, but you've buried whole cohorts of the other thing and you know in your gut I'm telling you true." I'd run out of careful and kept going. "You want to take Dacia in the spring. You'll take it. But you'll lose a legion's worth of men squatting in their own filth in those valleys before you ever see a Dacian, the way you did last time, and I can give you back a piece of that legion for the price of fuel and digging. The most boring thing I make and the only thing I make that's killed nobody."

He was quiet a long time. The brazier hissed. Somewhere down the hill the city went on roaring at itself, faint, under the stone.

"The figures," he said at last. "The ones my grain-counters fight you over."

I'd meant to show them myself, the long sum and the short sum side by side, the wall and the stair, the marks that catch a man cheating because you can't quietly add a stroke to them the way you can to a Roman tally. Then I looked at Tyche standing there in her good undyed tunic with her face very still, and I thought, if I'm to be weighed honest, and I said, "She's faster than I am." The room looked at the slave girl. She went to the table and ran a column of provisioning sums in the new figures faster than the old freedman could do half of it on the abacus, and when she finished she stepped back to exactly where she'd stood and folded her hands and looked at nothing, which was the most frightened thing I'd ever seen her do.

The emperor looked at her a moment, then at me, and there was something almost like amusement in it, and almost like the other thing, the cold sum.

"You teach slaves to keep an empire's accounts," he said.

"I teach whoever learns," I said. "She learned in a week. I didn't make her clever. I just gave her the marks." It was the closest I came, in that room, to saying the thing I was not allowed to say, and I think he heard the shape of it under the words, because his plain face went still for a beat I couldn't read, and then he stood up off the table and the audience was over, no warning, the way a soldier ends a thing when he's decided it.

"Wait outside," he said.

***

They put us in the cold colonnade with the polished floor and we waited a long time, long enough that the winter light moved on the stone, and neither of us said anything, because there was nothing to say that the walls shouldn't hear. I counted the columns. I counted them four times and got a different number twice, which would have made me laugh on any other day.

When they brought us back in the emperor wasn't there. The thin sharp man was, with the old inky freedman, a stack of tablets gone over, and a document on real papyrus that the freedman was waiting to read. And Macer was there. They'd sent for him while I waited, fetched him up the hill, and he stood by the door in his good wool with no stripe and his face like a wall, summoned to the Palatine to be told about his own property by men who didn't ask. He did not look at me. He looked at the freedman with the papyrus the way you look at a man about to read your bill.

The freedman read it. It was in the careful dead Latin of law and I caught maybe two words in five and got the rest from the faces, and the two words were enough. The first was a price, a fair sum, more than mules and less than a marvel, what Caesar's household had agreed to pay Titus Flavius Macer for the foreign craftsman in his keeping. The second came at the end, and I'd been waiting two and a half years to hear it, and when it came I didn't feel what I'd thought I'd feel.

Bought. Then freed. I stood there understanding it one phrase behind, that the imperial household had purchased me from Macer and then manumitted me, that I was, as of the reading of this papyrus on a winter morning, a freedman, *libertus*, of the household of the emperor, an imperial freedman, not a citizen the way a senator is a citizen and more powerful than half of them, a man whose patron is the state itself.

And then the freedman read the name, and that is the part I will carry to whatever grave they finally put me in, far from the one I was born to.

A freed man takes his patron's name, the praenomen and the nomen of the man who freed you, your own name kept on at the end as the cognomen, the little that's left of who you were. And my patron was Marcus Ulpius Traianus. So the freedman read it out slow and clear, the way you read a thing into being. Marcus, the emperor's first name, given to me. Ulpius, the emperor's family name, the gens, mine now, mine and every other freed slave of his house, a name that told anyone in the empire whose man I was. And then, last, smallest, the tail, my own name, the one my mother gave me, which the freedman could not pronounce and rendered into the dead law-Latin as Danihel, two careful syllables, a stranger's word.

Marcus Ulpius Danihel. The man who hated this whole machine, the buying and selling of people, the empire that ran on it, had just been bought and freed and named for the machine itself, the way you'd burn a man's brand off and burn your own on in the same hour.

I'd dreamed of being free. I want to be exact, because it would be easy to write that I felt joy. I'd lain in the loft and on the road and in the cold yard and ached to be free, to make a will, to own myself, to free Tyche, to free Pamphilus, to stop being a thing on a tablet. And I was, all at once, with a stroke of a freedman's reed, and what I felt on that polished floor with the winter sky in it was that a collar had come off and a finer one had gone on warm, a chain with the emperor on the end of it, and I was the emperor's man now, expected in the spring to go back into the valleys where Sabinus had died and keep the next men alive, with my boring water and my flying eye, in the service of the war I'd dragged my feet from for two years, wearing his name.

Tyche, beside me, had gone white, because she'd heard the part I'd missed in the law-Latin, the freedman still reading, a list of what a freedman of the emperor's house could lawfully do that a slave and a peregrinus could not. He could hold property in his own name. He could go to law. He could make a will, and his will would stand, and his writings be his own to leave. And he could marry, lawfully, as a man with a name.

I heard the will part and something that had been clenched in my chest two and a half years let go, and it was relief shaped like grief, because the first clear thought I had as a free man, standing in the emperor's house with the emperor's name fresh on me, was: now I can write it down and leave it where it won't die with me. Now there can be the books. Now if a cart takes me on the wharf tomorrow the ladder doesn't go in the ground with the body. I'd been writing notes for whoever found my corpse since the second night in the cell, and now I could write them into a thing the law would let me leave behind.

Macer crossed the room. He came and stood in front of me, and for a moment I thought he might do something I had no shape for, and then he put out his hand, the one with the gold ring, and gripped my forearm the way men grip when a deal closes, hard, businesslike, his thumb over the old burn scar from the first big bag, and his broken-veined face had still not moved.

"Marcus Ulpius," he said, and I heard him taste it, the emperor's name on the thing he'd bought for less than a season's mules. Macer did not stock sentiment. Under his voice ran arithmetic, the swift remaking of a sum. He'd lost the asset, the property, the thing on the tablet that he owned, and gained in its place a client in the emperor's own household, a freedman of the Ulpii who'd stand at his door at the salutatio, a connection an army-contractor stockpiling leather for a Dacian spring could spend for years, and he was already, I could see it, spending it. "Caesar paid me fair," he said, which was Macer for it cost me, and "you'll come to my door of a morning like a proper client and we'll see what the war's worth to us both," which was Macer for I'm glad, you bastard, in the only tongue he owned. He let go my arm. "Get a toga," he said, looking me up and down. "You can wear the thing now, God help us. You'll look like a barbarian in a bedsheet, but it's the law." And then, at the door, half turned, because he could not leave without it: "Thulean."

"Macer," I said.

"The crow won't touch you now," he said, low, and he meant Vibenius, he meant Crispus and his graver word the law knew, he meant the priest weighing me in the shadow at the salon. "No haruspex reads a bad sign over a man Caesar's just named for his own house. Crispus can call you a fraud till his teeth fall out. He bought you for the water. Give him the water, and don't go up the hill of yourself in the spring." And he went out, down the polished stone, an old hard man who'd just lost a fortune and made one in the same breath and couldn't have told you which, the gold ring going on his thumb.

***

They gave me a tablet to carry down, a thing that said who I was now, in case the men on the doors didn't believe the barbarian in work boots was an Ulpius. Tyche carried the empty cup. We went down off the Palatine into the noise, the carts and the garum coming back up to meet us, the city not knowing or caring that a thing had been bought and freed and renamed in the quiet stone above it, and somewhere on the steps Tyche stopped and looked at me with the lamp-look and asked the only question that mattered to her, low, in her quick clean Latin she'd never once slowed for me.

"Can you free us now," she said. Us. Not her. She'd thought of Pamphilus first, the collar she'd watched me fail to take off, and only then, maybe, herself.

A freedman of the emperor's house could hold property in his own name. He could free his own slaves. I had no slaves yet, I owned no one, the two at the yard were Macer's still and I'd have to buy them, and I had a peculium that was mine now in law and not in sufferance, and a patron who was the state, and a war in the spring with my name on the orders.

"Yes," I said. "When I can buy them. Pamphilus first." I didn't say her name and she didn't ask me to, and we went down the hill, the cup empty in her hands, into the city I belonged to now, that owned the name they'd put on me.

At the bottom of the steps I looked back up at the stone, all of it, the colonnades and the door-men and the cold smell of leather and horse, and I tried to feel like a free man and felt instead like a tool that had been picked up off the table and found worth keeping, and I thought, in English, where no one could hear it, the word my mother used to use, and it didn't fit my mouth anymore either.

Then I tucked the tablet that said Marcus Ulpius Danihel inside my barbarian tunic, against the dead glass of the phone I still carried, the two relics of two countries that couldn't write to each other, and went to find a place that would sell a freedman a toga he'd look like a fool in, because it was the law, and because I was, God help me, the emperor's man.

---

# Chapter 18: The Siege

The price of Pamphilus was four thousand sesterces, which Macer named to me across his own atrium with the gold ring going on his thumb, and which was, he wanted me to understand, robbery in my favor.

"He's a strong back going gray," Macer said. "Ten years of carry left in him if his knees hold. I'd get more at the block off a man who didn't know his joints. You I'll bleed slow because you'll come back for the girl and I want you to remember I was kind." He had a steward bring the tablet, the bill of sale, and he had me read it through, the careful law-words, *Pamphilus, servus,* a collared porter of the household of Titus Flavius Macer, sold for the named sum to Marcus Ulpius Danihel, freedman of the deified house, and there it was on the wax, a man bought and a man buying, both of us his once, and I counted out the coin from my own peculium that was mine now in law and not in sufferance, heavy bitten silver, and Macer's clerk weighed it and bit two of the aurei and wrote the receipt, and Pamphilus belonged to me.

That was the morning. The freeing I did myself in the yard, badly, because I had never done it and there is a way to do it and I did not know the way.

You are supposed to go to a magistrate. The full thing, a rod laid on the slave's head, a citizen made in a morning by a man with the power to do it. I did not have eight days and a praetor's patience, I had a war to march to, so I did the household thing, the cheap door, which makes a free man but a lesser-free man, only the start of citizenship. I sat Pamphilus down on the bench by the well and said the words wrong twice and got Heras, who was there because Heras was always there when a thing mattered, to feed them to me, and I declared before him and before Tyche and a wharf-clerk I'd dragged over for a witness that this man was free.

The collar was the trouble.

It was riveted. It had been riveted for the eleven years Pamphilus had worn it and the iron had thinned where it rode his collarbone and gone green-black in the creases of his neck, and you do not unlock a thing like that, you cut it, and I had not thought about that part, the way I had not thought about a great many parts, because in my head freeing a man was a sentence said and a tablet signed and the man stood up different. The man did not stand up different. He sat on the bench with his hands on his knees while Hermes set a cold chisel against the rivet at the back of his neck and looked at me to ask was I sure, because a slip there opens the big vessel and a freed man bleeds out on his first free morning, and Pamphilus held still, stiller than I have ever seen a living man hold, and Hermes struck the chisel twice and the rivet sheared and the collar came away in two halves and left a band of paler skin, soft, never weathered, like the print of a thing lifted off a sun-bleached table.

Pamphilus put his hand up to his own neck and felt the bare skin there and did not say anything for a while.

I had imagined, God help me, that he would weep, or grip my arm, or do some version of the thing that happens in the story when the chains come off, and I had imagined it because I wanted it, because I wanted to feel the clean thing once, the one clean thing, after Sabinus and the boring water that keeps men healthy enough to be killed and all the rest of the ledger I was carrying. I wanted Pamphilus to make me feel like I had done a good thing.

What he did was ask, in his slow careful Latin, whether he was still to cut the cloth.

"You don't have to cut anything," I said. "You're free. You can go."

"Go where," Pamphilus said.

It was not bitter. He was asking. He was a man past forty with a band of pale skin on his neck and no family that had survived being sold twice and no trade but carrying and lifting and cutting bolt-cloth, and the question was real, *go where,* and I did not have an answer that was not the answer the world gives every freed man, which is: nowhere, you go nowhere, you stay near the man who freed you because he is your patron now and you owe him the duties a freedman owes and outside his door is a city that eats men with no door, and the chain you can't see is the better fitted of the two. I had freed him into being my client. I had moved him one rung and called it the sky.

"You stay," I said. "On wages. Real ones, into your own hand, your own peculium that's yours now, not sufferance. You cut the cloth because I'll pay you to cut the cloth, and when you don't want to cut it you tell me and we'll find the thing you do want, but Pamphilus, you eat at this yard and you sleep dry and nobody, " and here my Latin failed me and I said it too plain, "nobody owns you. That's the whole of it. It's smaller than I wanted it to be."

He looked at the two halves of the collar lying in the dirt by the well. Tyche had gone very still by the shed, watching, because she had asked *can you free us,* us before her, and she was watching the shape of what free turned out to be, and I could not look at her.

"It's not smaller," Pamphilus said, after a while, and rubbed the pale band with two fingers like a man testing a scar. "It only weighs less." Then he picked up the shears and went to the cloth, because that was the morning's work and he was a man who did the morning's work, and I stood there holding a freedom that had cost four thousand sesterces and felt like a man who'd paid full price for half a thing.

Heras took the collar halves away. He said he wanted them for something. He never said what, and I never asked, and I think he simply did not want them in the yard where I'd keep looking at them.

***

I marched north in the spring with my name on the orders.

That is the difference I cannot make small enough, between the first war and this one. The first time I went up the Via Flaminia I went as a thing on loan, Macer's property lent to Celer's command, a slave with a clever toy, and I rode in the baggage and boiled my own water and watched the machine of the army move and was moved by it like a parcel. This time I went as Marcus Ulpius Danihel, freedman of the deified house, with a warrant that requisitioned timber and lime and digging-men in my name, and a mule of my own, and Pamphilus and two hired wharf-men I paid wages, and a sheaf of the new figures the quartermasters were now ordered to suffer, and I went because the emperor's man does not get asked whether he is going.

The army was bigger. I keep wanting to say the war was bigger and that is the historian's word and not the true one. The war was the same hills. The army was bigger, doubled, more than doubled, legions pulled off the Rhine and out of the East, a flood of men and mules and the smell of them going north all spring, the road never empty, the dust never settling, and I understood walking in it that the client-king peace I'd watched them make four years before, the relief and the anticlimax, Decebalus keeping his head and his hills, had been a thing Trajan meant to come back and finish, that it had been an intermission, that the whole vast machine had been quietly oiling itself for the second act the entire time I'd been in Rome teaching shopkeepers to count. Macer had known. Macer had been stockpiling leather for tents the whole while. The flux talks, he'd said, and the flux had talked, and here was the answer to it, going up the road in a column you could not see the end of.

And there was the bridge.

I'd seen Apollodorus build the first bridges, the wood on the cofferdams, the river fought back a span at a time, and I'd thought that was the great thing. The great thing was at Drobeta. He'd put a bridge of stone across the Danube. Twenty piers of dressed stone standing in the river that had drowned his cofferdam-men in the first war, the timber arches riding up off them, the thing running bank to bank wide enough for the army to cross it marching, and the legions tramping over it in their thousands as if crossing a river that size were a thing a man simply did on a Tuesday.

I found him on the Dacian bank, plumb in hand, watching a pier the way a man watches a child he doesn't trust near water.

"You came back," he said, not turning. "They freed you. I heard." He said *freed* the way you'd say a man had been promoted into a worse job, which was about right. "Marcus Ulpius now. The emperor collects useful Greeks and useful barbarians the way other men collect bronzes."

"It's a good bridge," I said, because it was, and because I wanted to see what he'd do with a true thing said plainly.

He turned then. He'd gone grayer and the sun had worked his face harder and his hands were the same scarred thick instruments. He looked at the bridge, his bridge, and there was no false modesty in him and no boasting either, just a man looking at a sum that had come out.

"It will outlast the province," he said. "They'll take the country and lose it in two lifetimes, three, the way they lose everything past the edge, and the bridge will still stand when there's no Rome on either bank to use it." He said it flat, a fact, the cold long view of a man who built in stone for a state that thought in seasons. Then he looked at me. "Your flying toy. Still does the one thing."

"Still does the one thing."

"Good. Do the one thing." He turned back to his pier. "Don't put a man under it. I heard about that too." And that was Apollodorus, who did not forgive a botched demonstration and did not pretend the dead man hadn't been my fault, and who I respected more than men who'd have been kinder, and I left him to his bridge and went up into the hills to do the one thing.

***

What I did in that war, set down plainly, so that whoever finds this knows what the work was:

I sited camps. That is the dullest sentence I will ever write and it kept more men alive than anything I have built. I walked ground ahead of the engineers and I said put the latrines here, downstream, downslope, lime them, dig them deep, and the water-point here, upstream, above the horse-lines, above the fouling, and I made them mean it, because the emperor's warrant meant I could make a centurion move a latrine, and a centurion does not forget being made to move a latrine by a barbarian in work boots, and I paid for that in small ways all summer. I set the filters at the water-points, the sand and the charcoal and the gravel, big ones now, barrel-batteries, and I made the water boiled by century and not by whim. Where the men did it the flux thinned and where the men were lazy the flux did what the flux does, and I watched both happen in the same army in the same month and had no way to count it, only the latrine-pits, fuller in one camp than another, and the carts of the sick, lighter where I'd been listened to.

I sent up the tethered eye. Just the eye, the bag on its hundred feet of rope, no man, a code of colored cloth and a soldier with good distance-sight in a basket no higher than a man could survive falling from, and it saw what it saw on the still mornings and was useless the rest of the time, and Celer's successors used it and cursed it and used it again.

Celer was there. He'd come up with the second war the way he'd gone down from the first, clipped and dust-colored and gallows-humored, the broken-set nose, and when he saw the warrant with my name on it he read it twice and looked at me and said, "They made you real," and there was something under the dryness I won't name because he'd have hated me naming it. We never said Sabinus. He checked, the first day, that the bag I'd brought went up empty, ran his eye over the basket and the rope, and when he found no man's harness in it he let out a breath and said, "Good. The boring stuff, then." So we did the boring stuff, the water and the eye, and he stayed alive all that war, which I am setting down because I spent the whole of it braced for the other thing, and the other thing did not come, not in Dacia.

And I did the figures. The grain and the fodder and the days of march and the men to feed, columns of it, the new marks racing ahead of the old freedmen and their abacuses, and a quartermaster who'd fought me in the spring came to me by autumn with a sheaf of his own done in the marks and a column that wouldn't balance, *find my error,* not *teach me,* a man too proud to learn and too pressed not to, and I found his error, a transposed pair, the kind the marks catch because you can't quietly thicken a stroke the way you can a tally, and he took the sheaf back without thanks and used the marks the rest of the war. That is how it spreads. Not gratitude. Need.

***

Sarmizegetusa sat up in the mountains the way a thing sits that has never been taken.

I am not going to pretend I understood the strategy of it. I understood what I was shown and what I was told to solve, and what I was told to solve was the water.

It was a hill fort, a high place, walls of that murus dacicus the engineers admired and hated, stone faces packed with rubble and tied with squared timber so the rams bit and the wall ate the bite, and behind it the Dacians and their king and their gold and their gods, and around it the army, dug in, the lines of circumvallation going up, the ballistae hauled into the heights piece by cursing piece. A siege is not a battle. A siege is patience and engineering and starving a place, and I had not understood that the way I understood it that autumn, standing in the cold thin mountain air watching men dig lines around other men to kill them slowly.

The water came into the place from springs, channeled, the way water is always channeled, in pipes and covered runs along the contours of the hills, ceramic and lead and hollowed stone, the same craft I'd spent four years admiring in Rome, the aqueducts I'd gawked at, the Roman genius for moving water that I had loved without reservation, because water moving is life moving, because the thing I knew best in the world and was best at was water. And an engineer of Apollodorus's, a young hard Greek with a survey-staff, found the lines on the slope below the walls, traced them, and brought the question up the chain, and the question came to me, because I was the emperor's water-man and the warrant said so, *the Thulean knows water,* and the question was simple.

Could it be cut.

I want to be exact about my own hands here, because it would be easy to write it so I was only asked, only consulted, a man who pointed and others dug. That is not the truth and I will not let it be the truth. I read the slope. I knew, looking at where the springs had to be and how the runs would have to fall to feed a fort that high, roughly where the channels ran, and I said so. I said cut here, and here, where the contour forces the run, and where you can't reach the buried part, foul it, put the camp's filth above their intake, do to their water what I spend my life stopping us from doing to ours, and I heard myself say it in my plain accented Latin to a man with a survey-staff, the exact inversion of every word I'd said to the emperor in the cold room, and the young Greek wrote it on his tablet and it was good engineering and it was the cleverest thing I did in that war and it broke a city.

It did not break it cleanly. Nothing works cleanly. They had cisterns, they had rain, they had the discipline of a people who had held that hill against Rome for a generation, and the cut water did not bring the walls down in a day the way the cup of clean water had brought the emperor over to me in an afternoon. It worked the way the flux works. Slowly, and on the weakest first, and out of sight. I did not see them die of it. That is the thing I keep coming back to, that I did the water and never saw the water do its work, the same as I never saw the boiled water save a man, no number either way, only the result that came down the mountain in the end, that the place broke, that the fight went out of it, that a city full of people, the fighters and behind them the others, the ones who are always behind them, ran dry on a high cold hill because a man from a country that didn't exist had read the slope right.

I did not refuse. I want that on the wax too. There was an inch where I could have been slow, vague, wrong on purpose, the inch I'd used for two years to drag my feet on the powder, *not yet, maybe not ever,* and I did not use it, because I was the emperor's man and the warrant had my name and a freedman of the deified house who is slow and vague at a siege is a freedman who has discovered what the state does with tools that lie to it, the thing Macer would not describe to me in his own yard. I could have refused and I would have died for it, or worse, and I am not going to dress up the fact that I chose to live as a moral act. I read the slope. I said cut here.

Apollodorus came to see the cut once, professionally, the way he came to see any clever piece of work, and he stood looking at the fouled run with his plumb-line still in his hand out of habit and said, "Neat," and nothing else for a while, and then, because he was the only man in that army who knew what the water work was supposed to be, who'd watched me at the bridge keeping legions from the flux, he said, "It's the same knowledge." Not a question. Not comfort. He said it the way he'd said the bridge would outlast Rome, a fact about the world that he had no intention of softening. "The same hand that keeps your men off the flux fouls theirs. You didn't think it would stay on the one side."

"No," I said.

"Then don't make the face," he said, and walked off down the slope, and I have wondered since whether that was the cruelest thing anyone said to me in that war or the kindest, and I have never decided.

***

The end of it I saw the way you see the end of anything in an army, which is sideways and late and mostly as noise.

The place fell. The fight went out of it and the lines went in and the city that had never been taken was taken, and the king, Decebalus, who I never saw, who was a name to me and a reason for all of it, did not let himself be led down that mountain in a chain to walk in front of a cart in Rome the way the Dacians on the platforms walked. He cut his own throat. A cavalry scout found him doing it, or just done, the story changed in the telling the way every soldier's story changes, but the throat was the constant of it, the king who would not be a placard, and they cut off his head and his right hand to carry south as proof, because the state needs proof, the state does cold sums and a head is a sum that balances.

And then the gold.

I had heard there was gold. Everyone had heard there was gold, Dacia's gold, the king's treasure, and there were stories all summer, the river dammed and the riverbed dug, the hoard buried under a stream-bed by slaves the king then killed so they couldn't tell, who knows, soldiers' stories. What I know is what came down the mountain and down the bridge and down the long road south in the autumn, cart after cart under guard, more guard than I'd ever seen put on anything, and the men knew what was in the carts the way men always know, and the mood of the army changed around it, a greed coming off the columns like heat, because every man in that machine knew that a haul like that meant a donative, coin in the hand, and meant games, and meant that the war had paid, that the dying in the valleys had been an investment and the investment had come good, and you could feel the whole vast thing turn from a war into a triumph while it was still standing on the conquered ground.

I did the figures on some of it. Of course I did. They put the emperor's water-man and his quick marks onto the reckoning of part of the haul, and I sat in a tent counting Dacian gold by weight into the columns of the new figures I'd carried up that road to keep men from dying of bad water. The two things sat in my hand at once, the column of grain that had kept a cohort off the flux and the column of looted gold that the same cohort had died and killed to take, the same marks, the same hand, and I balanced the column, because it balanced, because the marks always balance, that is the whole virtue of them.

Dacia was a province now. The country with the bridge across the great river was Rome's, the gold was Rome's, the king's head was going to Rome, and Rome was going to spend the gold on the longest games anyone could remember, and I would hear about those games from the city when I got back down, the days and days of them, the killing made into a holiday, paid for out of the carts I'd helped count.

I came down off that mountain a different man than the one who'd gone up, and I do not entirely have the shape of the difference even now, writing it. I had freed one man in the spring and found that freeing was smaller than I'd dreamed. I had spent the summer keeping men alive with water and the autumn killing a city with water, the same knowledge, the one hand. And somewhere in the middle of it, doing the cut, a thought came that I have never been able to put back in its box. The first war had ended in a client-king peace, an intermission. This one ended in annexation and a dead king and the gold, finished, the way I half-remembered from a history class two thousand years off that it had ended. Had it ended that way. Was I remembering it or watching it. Had any of it gone otherwise than it would have without a barbarian by the timber wharf who read a slope, and I had no way to know, no number, no second world to lay beside this one and check the column.

We crossed back over Apollodorus's stone bridge in the cold, the army and the carts and the head and the gold and me, twenty piers standing in the river that had drowned the men who built them. I stopped at the rail a moment in the press of the column and looked down at the gray water going under, fast, indifferent, the water I knew better than anything in the world, and I put my hand flat on the cold stone. Then a mule behind me balked and the column shoved and I went on across to the Roman bank, where my warrant was still good and my name was still the emperor's, and there was a winter of figures waiting, and a city full of gold, and a girl at the wharf I had promised, next, to buy.

---

# Chapter 19: The Contest

Rome in that winter was drunk on a scale I had no frame for.

The gold I'd counted in a tent on a mountain had come down the road ahead of me and turned into spectacle, games announced that went on for a span of days I didn't believe when somebody first said the number, money given away to the citizens, bread and oil and meat. The wine shops had their amphorae stacked in the street, and a man could not walk to the wharf without somebody pressing a cup on him, because the dead king's gold was paying for it and you'd be a fool to refuse free wine bought with somebody else's war. I drank it in a crowd that loved the emperor and loved that the war had paid, and I thought about the slope, and I drank another.

So when I tell you that this was the winter I finally did the thing I'd been drafting by lamplight for two years, understand that part of why I did it then was that I needed, badly, to make something that wasn't that. I had spent a summer keeping men alive with water and an autumn killing a city with water, the same hand, and I wanted to use the hand on something that just made people imagine. That's not a noble reason to found an institution. It's the true one.

***

The thing I wanted was simple to say and a nightmare to arrange. A prize. Real money, every year, on a fixed day, for the best tale of a machine that never was. A cart that ran with no horse and no slave to push it. A glass that showed the little ones I'd spent five years trying to talk Romans into believing in. A ship that crossed the sea with no wind and no men chained to the oars. An engine that did the work of a hundred men.

I had a head full of machines I could describe and not build, and I'd understood somewhere on that road that the machines weren't the gift. I was never going to live to see a horseless cart. The gift was getting a few thousand people to want one, to think it ordinary to want one, so the wanting would still be there in the heads of people not yet born when the steel finally came good.

I tried to explain this to Heras and got about three sentences in before he held up a hand.

"You want to pay people," he said, "to lie."

"To imagine."

"In Rome," Heras said, "those are close cousins, and the family is not respectable." He was grayer than when I'd met him, and I was selfishly glad of one face that hadn't been on a mountain. "A man may write of gods doing impossible things, because the gods did them. A man who writes of a cart doing impossible things has written a fable, and a fable is for children and Greeks." He paused. "I am a Greek. I am allowed to say it."

He was right, which was his entire function in my life. A foreigner, the man some salon-going people were still being told was a fraud and a possible blasphemer, could not simply announce that he was paying silver for the best made-up story about a machine. That's a foreign oddity buying attention and lying about machines, which is approximately the sentence I could hear Crispus composing before I'd written a word of the rules.

The shape of it came from the city itself. Rich men endowed things, games and feasts and a fountain with their name on it, *so-and-so paid for this, remember so-and-so,* because in a city with a short memory, your name carved on a thing was how you went on existing. The prize wasn't the problem. The prize was normal. The problem was whose name was on it.

So it couldn't be mine.

***

I went to Macer.

This will not surprise anyone who has been paying attention. He was my patron now in the proper Roman sense, which meant I attended his door in the gray morning with his other clients, an indignity he enjoyed visibly and I endured, because the emperor's freedman queuing in Macer's atrium was a thing Macer could show people, and showing people things was half of what wealth was for. I made the pitch in his study, the room that still smelled of the leather he sold the legions, with him turning the heavy gold ring on his finger the way he did when he was deciding whether a thing would make money or make him a fool, which to Macer were the only two outcomes worth considering.

"You want me," he said, "to put my name on a prize for stories."

"I want your name on a benefaction," I said. "The Macer prize. Given by Titus Flavius Macer, every year, for the best Latin telling of a marvel."

"What marvel."

"A machine. A device. A thing of craft that does something men can't do." Macer did not care about a civilization dreaming forward. Macer cared about leather contracts and his own immortality and being seen to be a man of taste, which he was not, and knew he was not, and minded. "Apollodorus builds bridges across rivers nobody could bridge, and Caesar gives him glory for it. You can't build a bridge. But you can be the man whose name is on the contest where Rome dreams up the bridges nobody's built yet. You'll have your name on the new thing."

"You want my name," he said, "because nobody'll come to a prize given by a barbarian freedman who'll be telling them to wash their hands at it."

"Yes. And the silver's mine. I pay, you're seen to pay. It costs you nothing but lending the name and letting it be read out under your roof once a year."

Macer turned the ring. I have wondered since whether he saw all of what I was doing, the long game, the generation I was trying to plant a wanting in, and I think the honest answer is that he saw a low-cost way to be a literary patron without having to read anything, plus the pleasure of the emperor's clever Thulean funding glory for the name Flavius Macer, and that those two things were sufficient.

"What do I have to do," he said.

"Host the reading. Sit at the front. Look like a man of taste."

"I can't look like a man of taste," Macer said. "I look like a man who sells the army boots."

"Then look like a man who sells the army its boots and also funds poetry. It's a better look. Half the senate would kill for it and can't afford the boots."

He laughed at that, the short ugly bark he had, and I knew I had him, because the one flattery that always worked on Macer was the one that was also a little insulting and also true.

"Write me the rules," he said. "Short. And keep your name off the front of it. You're the judge if you want, the Thulean who knows the foreign marvels, that's fine, that's a flavor, people like a tame barbarian to weigh the entries. But the prize is mine."

"It's yours," I said, and meant it, and that was the price and I paid it gladly. I have noticed that almost everything I built in Rome, I built by handing the credit to a Roman. I stopped minding it years before this. You learn, as the foreigner, that your name on a thing is a weight the thing has to carry, and most things fly better if you take the weight off.

***

We set the day at the *Quinquatrus*, the festival of Minerva in the spring, which Heras suggested and which was perfect, because Minerva is the goddess of craftsmen and the schools, and a prize for marvels of craft given on her festival had the gods covered, which mattered more than I can say with Vibenius still out there somewhere weighing me. You did not, in Rome, launch a strange new thing about impossible machines without nailing it firmly to a god first.

The silver was a hundred denarii to the winner, a third of a legionary's yearly pay, enough that a poor man with a quick imagination and somebody to write his words down would walk a long way to try for it. I could have made it more, the Dacian gold had everybody throwing money around, but I didn't want senators' sons entering for sport. I wanted the schoolmaster, the surveyor, the freedman clerk, the kid in the Subura who'd never owned anything. A hundred denarii to them was the moon. To a senator it was a tip. I aimed it the way you aim anything, by deciding who I wanted to bend down and pick it up.

The entries came in on whatever people had, the part I loved most once it started. Most people in Rome can't write, and most who can can't afford clean papyrus, so the marvels came on wax tablets and potsherds and the backs of old accounts and once on a strip of board that had been part of a wine crate, the grain showing through the soot-ink. A man brought his on a borrowed tablet and stood over me while I read it because he had to take it back. I kept a clerk just to copy entries off tablets people needed returned, and the clerk was overwhelmed inside a week, and I understood that I had not built a small thing.

***

The first marvels.

I have kept some of them. I'll put a few down exactly, because somebody finding this should know what the inside of an ordinary Roman head looked like when you gave it permission to dream.

A man who drove a cart for a builder wrote about a chariot pulled by trained eagles, in little harnesses. There was the problem of how you steered them, which he solved: a man sat behind the driver holding pieces of meat on long poles, one to each side, so you guided the team by where you dangled lunch. He'd done a sum, in Roman numerals, of how many eagles a loaded cart would need, and the sum was wrong, but he'd done it. At the end he'd written that the trouble was getting the eagles to come down once they were up, that he hadn't solved that part, and that maybe a wiser man could. I read that and laughed out loud and then sat with the *maybe a wiser man could* for a while, because that little sentence was the entire thing I was farming for.

A baker's wife sent in a bronze man who ground grain. Life-sized, wound up somehow, she was vague on the winding, and he turned the quern all day and never tired and never had to be fed. She'd thought hardest about the part that was hers, that the bronze man would never steal flour, never get sick, never need watching, and could grind through the night so the bread was ready at dawn. There was nothing in it about how the bronze man worked, because the how wasn't her dream. The not-having-to-own-the-grinder was the dream. I gave her tablet to Tyche, who read it twice and said nothing and gave it back, and I think about that one more than is comfortable.

There was a boy, maybe twelve, who'd had a grammarian's slave write his out in exchange for errands, and his marvel was a glass that let you see the moon as a country, with mountains, because he'd reasoned, the way a sharp kid reasons, that if it threw back light the way a hill throws back light then it had hillsides, and a thing with hillsides is a country. I had a single-lens magnifier in a drawer that would have done nothing for the moon and a head full of a telescope I couldn't build because Roman glass was full of bubbles, and this child had walked in and asked for the one marvel I knew was actually coming, someday, for somebody who wasn't either of us. I asked how he'd thought of the mountains. He looked at me like I was slow and said the moon wasn't smooth, anybody could see it wasn't smooth, what did I think the dark parts were. I told him it was a good marvel, which it was.

And there was the ship.

***

The ship's tale came in on the back of a grain-factor's discarded day-sheet, in a hand that was careful and a little cramped, the hand of somebody who'd learned letters late and was proud of them. It was signed with one name, the way slaves and the very poor sign, and the name was Felix.

Felix's marvel was a ship that needed no wind and no oars and no rowers. So far, the same dream as a dozen others, the no-rowers dream, which I'd noticed came from people who had reason to think about rowers and never from people who'd never been one. But Felix didn't wave his hand at how the ship moved. He wrote that everyone knew steam, the breath that comes off a boiling pot and lifts the lid and rattles it, anyone who'd watched a pot knew the lid wanted to fly. And if the breath off a pot could rattle a heavy lid, then a very great pot, fire under it always, its breath caught and aimed, could surely turn a wheel, and a wheel turned in the water would drive a ship, and the ship would carry its own fire and its own pot and its own sea, and go where it liked with no wind, against the wind, in a dead calm, forever, as long as there was fire.

I read it twice, then sat very still with the wharf noise coming through the door, and read it a third time, because I had shown Heras, years before, a toy I'd had a coppersmith make, Hero of Alexandria's spinning ball, the *aeolipile,* the hollow ball that whirls when you boil water under it and the steam jets out, and I'd said, *imagine this strong enough to pull a hundred carts,* and Heras had been completely unmoved, because it was a toy, it did one thing, it spun. And here was a man called Felix who'd never seen my toy, who'd watched a pot lid rattle in some kitchen and reasoned his way, on the back of a grain-sheet, to the engine I knew was the door to everything.

He'd gotten the shape of it. Not the working, the shape. And he'd hit the real problem without naming it, because he wrote, near the end, that no pot he knew could be made strong enough and large enough and not burst, that you'd need a metal nobody had, a metal that didn't yet exist.

The kid wanted the moon-glass I knew was coming. Felix wanted the steel I knew was the wall, the thing Hermes and I cracked our heads on in the forge behind the cattle market batch after ruined batch, gorgeous one firing and crystalline garbage the next. They were both standing at the same locked door I was standing at, the difference being that they thought it was a story and I knew it wasn't.

I gave Felix the prize.

***

It caused exactly the trouble I should have expected and somehow hadn't.

Felix turned out to be a slave, the property of a man who imported African grain and kept a counting-house of literate slaves, where Felix had learned his careful late letters doing his master's sums. So the prize money, by every law in Rome, belonged to a grain importer who had not written a word about a ship and did not, when I went to see him, have the first idea what a steam-driven hull might be.

I'd built a prize to bend down to the man at the bottom and found the law would not let the man at the bottom pick it up.

I am not going to pretend I solved this cleanly. I bought the result with maneuvering and Macer's name and some of the silver going where I'd rather it hadn't. The grain importer was happy to be flattered as the master of a slave clever enough to win the Macer prize, and happy to take his owner's cut. In the end Felix got to keep, with his master's gracious permission, a portion toward his own *peculium,* toward, someday, if the master stayed gracious, buying himself. I'd turned a hundred denarii for the best marvel into a down payment on a man's own freedom, filtered through a master's vanity and a master's cut, and it was a win, and it was also the bronze grinder run backward, a man who'd dreamed the way out of being owned and got, for it, a faster road to buying the rest of himself from the man who owned him.

I read his tale out at Macer's, on the Quinquatrus, with Macer at the front looking like a man who sells the army its boots and also funds poetry, the room fuller than the salon had ever been. People laughed in the good places, and went quiet at the metal that didn't yet exist, and a man near the back said, not unkindly, "Well, and when the gods give us that metal," and got a laugh, and I thought, *a wiser man could,* and that Felix was at that moment in a counting-house adding up another man's grain, and I said none of it.

***

Crispus came.

Of course he came, oiled and elegant and cited-up, my old friend from the salons who had spent years insinuating that Thule was a lie and the Thulean a fraud and who now had a fresh, delicious target. He'd learned the last time that I'd drink my own foul wharf water to win a fraud argument and that a demonstration beat his Strabo, so he didn't argue facts. He argued taste, the one ground I couldn't drink my way out of. He stood up when Macer invited the men of letters to remark, and he remarked.

He said it was charming. The eagles were charming and the bronze grinder was charming and the boy's moon was charming, genuinely, he said it warmly, and I felt the thing coming the way you feel weather. A delight to see the common people of Rome at play with their imaginations, one was reminded of children and their wooden horses. And he wondered, only wondered, with the greatest respect to our host, whether it was quite the thing, on the festival of the goddess of *true* learning, to crown lies. A barbarian's notion dressed as a marvel, the dream that a ship might one day need no rowers, told to men who row, by, he gestured at me with great courtesy, a man who had built us to date a clever bag that flies until it burns and a way to write numbers like a Syrian shopkeeper. He sat down to a very good laugh.

I'll give him this. It was well done, and not entirely wrong, which was always the trouble with Crispus. He'd seen the shape of what I was doing more clearly than Macer, more clearly than the crowd, that I was a foreigner teaching Rome to dream away from the gods and the dead myths and toward machines, and he didn't like it, and his not liking it had a sharp end on it, because a man who can make the goddess of craft sound offended is two steps from a man who can make her sound blasphemed, and I'd been there before, with the little ones, with the graver word the law knows.

I didn't out-argue him. I'd given up out-arguing Crispus. I stood and thanked him for his learning, because the room liked it when I was gracious to him and it cost me nothing, and I granted he was right, these were not poetry, they were a kind of fable. And I said that the goddess of craft, as I understood her, was the patron of the man at the forge and the woman at the quern and the boy who looks up at the moon and wonders what the dark parts are, and that if she ever minded being honored by a slave who dreamed a ship and a baker's wife who dreamed a free pair of hands, then she was a smaller goddess than the men of Rome deserved, and I, a foreigner, would not presume to think so little of her. It got a better laugh than his, and Macer enjoyed it enormously, and Crispus smiled the smile that meant he was filing it away, and I knew walking home that the contest catching on had just made me bigger and a bigger thing is an easier thing to throw stones at.

***

Then we printed them.

A prize read out once at a rich man's dinner and then forgotten is a dinner, not an institution. The dream had to spread and outlast me.

So Eros, my letterer, carved the winners onto pearwood. Not all of them, the press was crude and gray and we wasted a sheet in four. But the ship and the eagles and the bronze grinder and the boy's moon, inked and pressed fifty crooked impressions at a time, sold for an as or two to schoolmasters and whoever wanted them, because a marvel that won the Macer prize was a thing people wanted to own, to read to a class of boys, to pin up in a tavern. The press that had started its life making times-tables for a Subura schoolmaster now made marvels, and they went out on cheap gray sheets, and got copied by hand by people who couldn't buy even the cheap sheet, and started turning up, Tyche told me, in the mouths of children playing at the wharf, who'd decided among themselves that the bronze grinder was real and lived somewhere and could be visited.

A generation playing at machines that didn't exist. That was the crop. I knew when I planted it that the field was a hundred years long and I'd be in the ground for most of it, and I planted it anyway, on a borrowed name, on Minerva's festival, with a slave's tale of a ship in the front row. There are worse ways to spend stolen gold's leftover good weather.

***

Felix sent word, near the end of that first year, through the grain importer's steward. He'd been allowed paper for his next, and he wanted me to know he was already working on it, that with proper paper and time, he thought he could fix the pot. The metal that didn't yet exist. He thought he could fix it.

I sat in the tally-room with the steward's tablet in my hand, the wharf going on outside, Tyche at the other table running the day's figures in the new marks, Pamphilus down on the dock bellowing at a crane crew with the collar long gone off his neck, and I read the slave's message that he thought he could fix the pot, the one thing in all of Rome I knew for certain could not be fixed, not by him, not by me, not by Hermes and every ruined batch behind the cattle market.

I took up the stylus and wrote back, in my plain accented Latin, that I would very much like to read it, that he should take all the time he needed with the pot, and that the metal would come. Then I smoothed the wax flat with my thumb where I'd pressed too hard, and gave it back, and went out onto the dock to see what Pamphilus was shouting about.

---

# Chapter 20: Tyche

The trouble with buying a person you care about is that you have to do it the way you'd buy a mule, in front of a man who knows you can't walk away.

I went to Macer's about it in the spring after the contest, on a clear cold morning with the river smell coming up the streets, and I made the mistake of dressing well. I had a toga now, a citizen's toga, which I still couldn't drape without Pamphilus pinning the back fold and which I'd worn maybe six times, and I put it on because I thought it would make me look like a man Macer had to treat as an equal. He took one look at me in his atrium, with the leather smell thick around us and a clerk waiting at the side with a tablet, and he laughed the way he laughed when I'd done something he found genuinely funny, which was rare and which I'd learned to dread.

"You came to buy a girl in your wedding sheet," he said. "Sit down, Thulean, before you trip on it."

I sat. He turned the gold ring on his thumb and looked at me for a while, pleasant, taking his time, the way he took his time when he was about to do business he was going to enjoy.

I had rehearsed this. I'd told myself I would be light about it, that I would make it sound like tidying up, the freedman buying back the household furniture, of no more weight than the cloth tally she kept. I'd told myself not to let him see it mattered. I had been telling myself that for two years, since I'd said *Pamphilus first* and watched her take it without a flicker, and I had failed to think hard enough about what it meant that I'd needed to tell myself.

"The girl Tyche," I said. "She keeps my tally. I'd like to buy her, the way I bought Pamphilus. Name a price and I'll have the silver to you."

"Pamphilus," Macer said, "is a porter with a strong back and a collar I'd riveted on him because he'd run twice. I sold you a man who was worth four thousand because his legs were good and his head was empty and I was tired of feeding a thing that wanted to be elsewhere." He let that sit. "You paid over the odds for him because you wanted him free that week and I knew it. Four thousand for a porter. I told my wife and she didn't believe me."

"And Tyche."

"Tyche," he said, and smiled, "keeps your tally."

So he knew. I had spent five years hiding it, hiding her, telling Onesimus she was slow and let her do the donkey-work of the cloth count because she was reliable and dull, keeping her sums off any tablet that left the yard, teaching her in the back of the tally-room with the door shut and a story ready about why the door was shut, because of the thing he'd said the day he gave me the workshop, that he would sell the cleverer slave to a factor if I made his hands worth more than he could keep. I had built the whole architecture of her invisibility on that one sentence, and I sat there in my stupid toga and understood that he had seen through all of it, possibly from the start, and had simply been letting me think I was clever.

"How long have you known," I said.

He shrugged the big shoulders, the wool moving. "You hid a girl who does in her head what my counting-house does with an abacus and a freedman and an afternoon. You hid her by telling Onesimus she was slow. Onesimus has been keeping books since before you were born on whatever rock you crawled off, and he came to me three years ago and said your slow girl had caught two errors of his that the abacus missed and that you'd told her to stop. He thought you were protecting your own importance." Macer's mouth did something dry. "I let you keep your secret because the secret was working for me. A clever beast that nobody knows is clever stays cheap and stays put. I've owned a hundred slaves, Thulean. You think I can't smell the one that's worth money."

"Then name it."

"Forty thousand," Macer said.

I had brought, in a strongbox on a cart with two of Macer's old yard men I'd hired to guard it because I trusted them more than strangers, eight thousand sesterces, which was twice what I'd paid for Pamphilus and which had felt, loading it, like an obscene amount of metal to set against one person. Forty thousand was most of everything I had. It was the contest fund and the press and the next two years of the yard. It was a senator's furniture, a small farm, a frontier officer's pay for more years than Celer had served.

"She's a tally-keeper," I said, and heard my own voice climb and hated it. "She's a girl of twenty-two who can do sums. Forty thousand buys a Greek tutor and a cook and a litter team and you know it."

"It buys what a man will pay for it." He wasn't enjoying it now, or he was enjoying it the way you enjoy winning a thing you'd rather not have had to win. "A Greek who can read Homer to your sons is worth what a Greek's worth, there are shiploads of Greeks. A girl who can audit a grain factor's books faster than the factor and won't run because she's got nowhere better, sold to the right house, to a man with shipping and contracts and a need to know which of his stewards is stealing, that girl is worth what you cannot afford to find out. I've had two offers I didn't go looking for. I told you. A clever beast doesn't stay secret in a city this size. People come into your yard. People watch a girl reckon."

"You said you'd sell her to a factor."

"I said I would if you made her worth selling. You did. I didn't. I held her for you, you ungrateful barbarian, against two men who'd have given me what I'm asking, because I knew you'd come for her eventually with your sheet on and your sad face, and I'd rather sell her to you and keep you sweet than sell her to Vibius Maximus and lose the man who flies." He leaned back. "Forty thousand. I'm not gouging you. I'm telling you what you let her become."

There it was. He had put the price on her, and the price was true, and the truth of it was the worst thing in the room, worse than if he'd been cheating me, because if he'd been cheating me I could have been angry at Macer. He'd made me look at the number she was, and the number was high because of everything I'd taught her, and so the bill, when it came, was a bill for her mind, which I had loved like the one piece of home I'd managed to grow in this place, and which I had now to buy back at full market rate from the man who happened to own the body it lived in.

I am not going to dress up what I felt sitting there. I wanted to break his face with the gold ring still on it. I wanted to tell him exactly what I thought of a world where a person was a line in a counting-house, and I had the words, I'd had the words for years, the whole speech, and I knew with total clarity that the speech would cost me the deal and her the freedom and would change precisely nothing about the law, and so I sat in my wedding sheet and did sums in my head about what I could sell.

"Thirty," I said, because you don't take the first number, even when you've already lost.

"Thirty-eight," Macer said, "and you bring your water-barrels and your foreign filter into my country house at Tibur, free, and you teach my steward's boys your shopkeeper's numbers, so that when you're dead or grand and gone I'm not the last man in Rome doing his accounts the slow way. Thirty-eight and a string attached. I'd rather have the string than the two thousand."

"Done," I said, before I could think about it, because I'd have given him fifty.

He had the clerk write it. A *bill of sale*, a tablet, one slave, female, called Tyche, of his household, sold to Marcus Ulpius Danihel, freedman of the deified, for thirty-eight thousand sesterces, and the clerk's stylus going through the wax and the figure written in Roman letters because Macer would not have his own sale recorded in my marks, and the leather smell, and Macer turning the ring, and that was how I came to own another human being for the length of an afternoon.

He saw my face when the clerk turned the tablet for my mark. "Don't," he said, almost kindly. "You're going to free her the moment you're out my door, I know you are, and you'll feel clean about it, and that's your business. But you bought her, boy. Whatever you do after. Sign your grand name."

I signed my grand name.

***

He couldn't let me leave without the joke. He walked me to the atrium door himself, which he didn't do for clients, his hand heavy on my shoulder over the toga, and at the threshold where his porter could hear and the morning's last few hangers-on could hear he said, loud and good-humored, "Thirty-eight thousand for a girl of twenty-two. Marcus Ulpius Danihel, the emperor's own freedman, who doesn't keep a woman, who's never been seen to keep a woman, and pays a small farm for one with short hair and a quick tongue." He patted my shoulder. "I'd have sold her cheaper if you'd told me what you wanted her for. Buy her a comb."

The hangers-on laughed. The porter kept his face still the way porters do.

I had known it was coming. I'd known since I started planning this that there is exactly one story Rome tells about a man who buys a young woman and keeps her in his house, and that the story does not change because the man is a freedman with a yard and a strange reputation, and does not change because the truth is that I thought of her as the closest thing I had to a sister in a thousand miles of strangers, because Rome did not have a slot for *the closest thing I had to a sister* and did have a very well-worn slot for the other thing. I'd thought I'd weathered it in advance. I hadn't. Standing in the door with men laughing about what they assumed I'd been doing to a girl I'd watched grow up, I felt the particular sick heat of being talked about in a way that was filthy and that I couldn't correct without sounding like exactly the kind of man who'd protest too much.

"For her sums," I said, which was true and which got another laugh, because of course it did, it was the thing the man in the story always said.

Macer grinned, not unkind, just a man who found the world funny because he'd given up being shocked by it decades ago. "Tell yourself stories, Thulean," he said. "Just don't put her name on a contract next to yours where a magistrate can see it, or the story tells itself."

That stopped me, on the step, and I think he meant it to. Because under the dirty joke there was a real warning, the kind Macer gave for free when he could afford to, and the warning was this. The gossip wasn't a danger to me. I was a man, and the worst Rome would think of me was the ordinary thing it thought of every man with money and a household. The danger was hers. A freedwoman living unmarried in the house of the man who'd bought and freed her, doing his books, going where he sent her, was a woman whose respectability was a thing other people got to decide, and the deciding had already started, in Macer's doorway, with men I'd never see again. I had been about to make her free, and I had not once, in two years of planning the silver, thought hard about what I was making her free *into.*

***

I freed her in the yard, that afternoon, badly.

I'd meant to do it properly, the full ceremony, *manumissio* before a magistrate, the rod laid on the shoulder, the turning, the whole legal machinery that makes a freedwoman a citizen of limited kind and not a Junian half-thing the way Pamphilus had come out, lesser-free, his children's status still a question. I'd looked into it. I had standing now to do it right, and I wanted it right for her, the real kind, the kind with the name and the cap and the law behind it. But the magistrate's court took days to get into and I had a tablet in my hand that said I owned her and I could not stand to own her for days, so I did the informal thing first, in the tally-room, in front of Pamphilus and Heras, who I'd asked to come, and I said the words, that she was free, that I freed her, *inter amicos,* among friends, witnesses present, and I told her we'd do the court part as soon as I could get the date, for her citizenship, for the proper kind.

I had imagined this moment for two years. In the version in my head she wept, or she didn't weep, or she said something I could carry around afterward, and in all the versions she looked at me a particular way, and I am ashamed of how much I'd wanted the way she'd look at me.

She stood by her table with the day's tally still open on it and listened to the whole thing with her arms folded, and when I finished she said, "How much."

"What?"

"How much did he take you for." Her voice was flat and clean and city-quick, the way it had always been, the way she'd never slowed it for me even when my Latin was broken and she was fourteen. "I keep your books. I'll find it in a month when I balance the year. You can tell me now or I can find it and know you didn't tell me."

"Thirty-eight thousand," I said. "And the filter at Tibur, and teaching the steward's boys the figures."

Something moved in her face and was gone, fast, like a fish turning under water. She looked at the open tally. "Thirty-eight," she said. "More than nine of Pamphilus." She was doing it in her head, I could see her doing it, the arithmetic of herself, and I understood I had told her the one number I should have lied about and that lying would have been worse. "You priced low on the porter and they laughed at you. You priced high on me and they laughed at you. There's no number where they don't laugh."

"Tyche."

"I'm not thanking you," she said.

It landed exactly where she'd aimed it. I'd told myself I didn't want her gratitude, and that had been a comfortable lie, because some part of me had absolutely been waiting for it, had bought it in advance with the silver, and she'd seen that, the way she saw the errors the abacus missed, and she'd refused to pay.

"I'm not asking you to," I said, which was the second lie of the afternoon.

"You hid me." She wasn't loud. Heras had gone very still by the door, and Pamphilus was looking at the floor. "Five years. You taught me the figures and then you told the old man I was slow and you told *me* to stop catching his mistakes so I'd stay cheap and stay in the yard. You did it to keep me from being sold to a factor, I know, I'm not stupid, I heard the threat the same day you did, I was carrying the bread. I know you were saving me." She put her hand flat on the tally, on her own neat columns. "But what it was, every day, was you deciding how much of me Rome got to see. You. A girl who can do this" - the hand on the columns - "had to spend five years pretending she couldn't, so that the man who'd been good to her could keep being able to be good to her. You don't know what it is to make yourself small on purpose every single day so that nobody clever buys you. You decided I'd be slow. For my own good. And now you've decided I'm free. For my own good. And the comb." Her chin went up. "I heard about the comb. The wharf heard about the comb before I did."

The Tibur joke had beaten the cart back to the yard. Of course it had.

"That's the part I got wrong," I said. "Macer's right that it's a danger and he's wrong about who it's a danger to. It's not on me. It's on you. A woman in my house, freed by me, unwed, keeping my books and walking where I send her. They've already started telling the story. I should have thought about that two years ago and I didn't, because the only thing I thought about was the silver, and I'm sorry."

She looked at me for a long moment. It was not the look from the versions in my head. It was the look she gave a column that didn't sum, working out where the error was.

"You think you freed me into freedom," she said. "You freed me into being a freedwoman, which is a citizen who can't hold office and owes you duties and whose word a magistrate weighs at a freedwoman's weight, and you freed me into being a woman, which Rome owns a different way, papers or no papers. I'm twenty-two. With no man and no family and your bought name on me. You know what a free poor woman alone is in this city? She's a thing waiting to be married off by whoever takes an interest, or worse, and the worse is most of it." She said it without self-pity, like a sum. "Freeing me was the easy part. It was thirty-eight thousand and an afternoon. Now there's the rest of my life, in a city that has a story about me already, and I get to live it, not you."

Heras spoke for the first time, dry, from the door. "She's not wrong, you know," he said, to me, in Greek, which she half-followed now and pretended not to. "You solved a problem and bought yourself a harder one. Congratulations. It's what you always do."

***

So I let her set the terms, because she was right that they were hers to set, and because I had finally understood, two years too late, that the whole point was that they were hers to set.

She would stay at the yard, she said, because she had nowhere better and the work was real and she liked the work, which was as close to a kindness as she'd give me that day. She would be paid, a real freedwoman's wage, on a real reckoning, no peculium-fiction, no allowance, money she could keep and bank and that I could not take back. Her name would go on nothing of mine, no contract, no shared lease, nothing a magistrate or a wagging tongue could read as the other story. She would have her own room with a door that barred from the inside, away from mine, in the front building with Pamphilus and his wife between, so the geography of the yard itself argued for her. And the court date, the proper *manumissio*, the citizenship, I would get it within the month or she would know I'd lied about that too.

"And one more," she said.

"Anything."

"Don't say *anything* to me like a man in a story. You don't have anything. You have a yard and a borrowed name and more enemies than you count." She almost smiled. It was the first almost-smile of the afternoon and it was at my expense. "Teach me the thing you won't teach anyone. The real one. Not the figures, everyone has the figures now, the fish-sellers have the figures. The other thing. The one you do at night when you think we're asleep, the marks that aren't Latin and aren't Greek and aren't the figures, the ones in the little book you keep against the dead glass."

I had thought no one had seen me writing English.

***

I'd kept it back from everyone for nine years. Heras had glimpsed a line of it once and called it a homeland script and I'd let him, because that was the cover I'd built for it, that the people of Thule wrote this way in their cold north, and it was useful precisely because no one in the world could read it but me, which made it the only truly private thing I owned, the only place I could put the things I couldn't say to anyone.

The encyclopedia was going to be in it. I'd known that for a while. Everything I carried that mattered, the ladder I wanted to leave so that someone, someday, could climb past the steel wall and the flux and the rest, all of it had to go down in a script that the priests couldn't read for blasphemy and the rivals couldn't steal and the magistrates couldn't subpoena, in a hand that was mine and dead and unfalsifiable, the script of a country no one could check. And it could not stay only mine, because I was one bad cut from dead and then it would all die behind my teeth, which was the fear that had grown, over the years, into the biggest one I had.

I got the little book and the dead phone fell out of the cloth onto the table, the black glass, and she looked at it the way she looked at everything, once, hard, and let it go without a question, which was its own kind of mercy.

I made a line of letters. *A B C D.* I said them. I made her name in it, the Roman way it sounded, T-Y-C-H-E, and turned the book so she could see, and watched her face do the thing I'd seen it do over the figures five years before, the stillness that meant the machine inside her had started turning and would not stop.

"It goes left to right," I said. "Like Latin. There are only these, look, twenty-six marks, the same twenty-six, and they make every word there is, you don't learn a new mark for a new word, you spell the sound. The figures were the sounds of numbers. These are the sounds of everything else."

She put her finger on her own name. "*Tyche.*"

"That's the *y* sound. We argue about that one even at home."

"At home," she said, and looked at me, because I never said *at home,* I had spent nine years not saying *at home,* and she filed it, the way she filed everything, and didn't ask.

She copied the four letters under mine, slowly, getting the *D* backward, which everyone does, and then again, right, and then her name, and then, unprompted, the way she'd taught herself long division in a week with no one watching, she made a mark I hadn't shown her, a fifth letter, *E,* having reasoned that the sound at the end of her own name probably existed and probably had a mark, and held it up.

"Is there one like this," she said. "I think there's one like this."

There was. I told her there was. I was handing her the only thing in the world that was entirely mine, the country I couldn't get back to written in the marks no one else could read, and she'd already, in the first lesson, started reaching past where I'd shown her toward the marks I hadn't, the way she always had, the way I'd spent five years hiding from a man who'd seen it anyway.

"Show me the rest," she said. It wasn't a thank you. It was an order, given by a free woman to the man who worked for her now in the one currency she'd decided to accept.

Outside, Pamphilus was getting the cranes quiet for the evening, and Heras had gone, and the lamp needed trimming, and Tyche bent over the little book in the bad light and made the letter *E* again, larger, until she had it the way she wanted it.

---

# Chapter 21: The Cannon That Bursts

I want to put down clearly that I knew what I was doing when I started keeping the manure.

I had nailed the lid on the manned balloon and meant it. I had told an emperor to his face that I would never hang another man under a bag of fire, and I had told myself a smaller version of the same thing about the other thing, the thing I had written "???" beside on the second night I was ever in Rome, when I couldn't remember the white powder. I had said it to myself on the bridge coming back over the Danube with my own water-knowledge fresh on my hands and a city dead of thirst behind me. Not yet. Maybe not ever. I'd been pleased with how grown-up it sounded.

What I had not understood about a vow is that you don't break it in one piece. You sell it off in slivers, each one cheap enough that you can pretend you haven't spent it.

The first sliver was the steel.

Hermes and I had been at the crucibles for going on two years and we were exactly where we'd been at the start, which is to say we could make a blade that would shave the hair off your arm and we could make, with the identical method on the identical day, a thing that came out of the quench in pieces like a dropped cup. I would stand in his forge under the brick arch behind the cattle market with the heat coming off the fire in a wall and watch him pull a glowing billet and judge it by a color, and I would have nothing to give him. No number. The whole problem was a number neither of us could see. There is a temperature, and we could not measure the temperature, and so we were two men trying to bake by the smell of the oven, and one of us at least should have known better.

"The fire is the master," Hermes said, which he had said before and which had stopped being a complaint and become a kind of prayer he muttered while he worked. He was past forty, gray as iron filings, and his forearms had a written history of every spark that had ever found him.

I told him, more than once, that if we could only get the powerful men of Rome to care about steel the way they cared about marble and frontiers and their own faces in bronze, we would have the money and the men to chase the number until we caught it. He didn't disagree. He also didn't see how a man got a senator to lie awake at night over the carbon in a crucible, and neither did I, for a long time.
