That was the second sliver. The realization, which arrived slowly and which I welcomed because it was useful to me, that the steel chokepoint gated everything. Not just blades. Springs, which I'd wrecked a small fortune of soft Roman copper trying to make. Fine instruments. A clock that would keep time, which I could not build and was beginning to suspect I would never build. The whole ladder I told myself I was climbing for the saving things, the clean water and the medicine and the books, all of it ran up against the same wall, which was that nobody with money believed metal was a problem worth solving, because their metal held their swords and roofed their temples and did everything they had ever asked of it.

I needed to ask it to do something it couldn't.

The third sliver I am least proud of, because it was a lie I told myself on purpose to make the rest go down. I told myself the secret wasn't mine to keep. The Seres had it, I was fairly sure, the silk people at the eastern edge of the maps, China, they'd had fire-powder for centuries, I'd read it somewhere or seen it somewhere, in the half-life of facts a high-schooler carries, and if a whole civilization already had a thing then a man holding his breath in Rome was not the cork in the bottle. He was just a coward pretending to be a wall.

I believed that. I want to be honest that I believed it, standing in my own yard at the wharf with money in my peculium for the first time in my life, money I'd earned and not been allowed to keep until a year ago. I believed it the way you believe the thing that lets you do what you'd already decided to do.

I have wondered since whether the Seres had it then at all. I think now that I was wrong, that I'd done the thing I always did, slid a fact two or three hundred years out of its place because the timeline in my head was a smear. But I didn't know that then, and a man can build a great deal on a fact he can't check.

---

So. Niter.

I had three of the words. Charcoal I had by the cartload, the same charcoal Hermes burned. Sulfur came up from the volcano country, yellow and stinking, a curing-house and dyer's good that nobody thought twice about my buying, and I bought it through Pamphilus so my name stayed off it. The white salt I had nothing of. *Sal petrae*, Heras called it when I asked him sideways, the salt of the stones, the bloom you scrape off old cellar walls and the underside of stable floors, a curiosity that some cooks used to redden meat and otherwise good for nothing.

I sent men to scrape walls. It was the obvious move and it was wrong, and it was wrong in a way that cost me the better part of a year before I understood why. We boiled the scrapings and leached them and crystallized them and got a salt that would not work. It sucked water out of the air until the powder was a paste; it fizzled and smoked and would not bang. I didn't have the chemistry to know the word for what I'd made, but I know now it was the wrong nitrate, the calcium one, the lime in the old walls poisoning it. I only knew then that the white salt off a wall was no good, and that the white salt I needed came from a different and far worse place.

Dung.

You make a niter bed the way you make the worst compost heap in the history of the world and then you tend it like a vineyard. Manure, and the bedding straw, and ash from the wood fires, and lime, and over all of it, urine, as much as you can collect, which means you become a man who buys other men's piss. We built the beds in a leased shed two streets back from the wharf, far from anything I cared about burning, and we turned them and watered them and waited, and the smell got into the timber and the timber held it and the whole street learned to walk on the other side. You wait months. You wait through a summer that bakes the stink into a thing with weight. Then you leach the rotted beds through ash, wood-ash and water dripping slow through a barrel with a false bottom, and you boil the brown liquor down, and skim it, and let it crystallize, and you get, if the gods and the bacteria you don't have a word for are kind, a handful of long white needles that taste cold on the tongue and burn the right way.

It took most of two years to get a quantity worth the name. Two years, for a thing I'd half-promised a contest of children that Rome would have in a generation.

I told the men it was for medicine. For Heras, for a cooling salt, which was not entirely a lie because I was learning everything from Heras's books and Heras's patience and Heras knew exactly what I was doing and hated it and helped me anyway, in the careful way he did things he disapproved of, which was to make sure I did them less stupidly than I would have alone.

"You've decided," he said one evening, not a question. We were in the shed and he had a cloth over his nose and mouth and his eyes were watering above it.

"I've decided to be able to," I said. "Deciding to use it is a different thing."

"Is it." He looked at the beds, at the white forming at the edges where the wet liquor had splashed and dried on a board. "You're a man who built a wall and now spends his evenings learning where the gate is. Tell yourself the gate is still shut if you like. I've watched you find this many gates."

I didn't answer him. There wasn't an answer that wasn't the lie about the Seres, and I didn't want to say the lie out loud to Heras, who would have known it for one.

---

The ratios I had to find by killing powder.

I knew there were three parts and that the white salt was most of it and the other two were small, and that is genuinely all I knew, a shape with no numbers in it. I had a memory of a number, three-quarters, and I clung to it, and I was lucky that the memory was close. I made small batches in a stone mortar, ground fine, and burned a pinch on a tile, and most of it whooshed and stank and did nothing, and slowly, over a winter, the pinches started to crack instead of hiss.

I had a man on the grinding. His name was Geta. He was one of the wharf hands, a freedman like Pamphilus now, a wages man, broad and slow-spoken and good with a pestle, and I had told him the powder was dangerous and I had not told him enough, because I didn't know enough. I thought dry powder ground gently was safe to grind. I had read, somewhere in the smear, that you ground it wet or you ground the parts apart, and I had a memory of the warning and not of the reason, and I let Geta grind a dried cake fine in a stone mortar in a closed shed in February because I wanted a finer powder and I was impatient and I had begun, by then, to think of the thing as nearly solved.

I was across the yard when it went. There was a sound I felt in my chest before I heard it, a flat hard slap of the air, and then the shed door came off and a gout of dirty smoke stood up over the roof, and I was running before I'd decided to.

Geta was alive. I will say that first because it's the thing I most needed to be true while I was crossing that yard. He was alive and he was on his back in the spilled char screaming in a voice I'd never heard a grown man use, and his hands were wrong. The pestle had still been in the mortar and the mortar had still been mostly whole and the flash had gone up and out, and it had taken his face on one side and it had taken his hands, the left one worse, the fingers of it gone or going, the right one burned to the bone across the back. His eyebrows were gone. His beard was gone in a patch and smoking. The smell was the balloon smell, the burned-grease-and-hair smell I knew from the Loaf, the one I will not describe again because I described it once and that was enough for one life.

Heras came. Heras was always close in those years, thank God, and he packed the hands and the face and gave him poppy and worked through the night, and Geta did not die, which I had no right to and took anyway. He lost the left hand at the wrist in the end and three fingers of the right and the sight in one eye. He could not grind anymore. He could not do most things anymore. I put him on wages for life out of my own coin and I built him a room and I have paid it every month I've had a month to pay it in, and none of that is a thing I get to feel good about. I bought a man his hands and then I burned them off teaching myself a number I could have learned from a book if the book had survived to the century I needed it in.

When Geta could talk, days later, gray and shrunken in the cot, the bandaged stumps held up off the blanket on a roll of cloth, he asked me if the powder had worked.

I told him it had.

He said good, in the cracked voice, and shut his one eye, and I sat with him until he slept, and that is the part of this that I would undo if I could undo any of it, and I can't undo any of it. You can't un-know a thing for the world and you can't un-burn a man's hands. I had wanted, when I started keeping the manure, to make the powerful men of Rome feel something true about metal. The first person my powder made feel anything was Geta, and what he felt was his own hands going.

---

I went on. That is the worst sentence in this whole account and I'm going to leave it short.

I went on, because the powder had worked, and because a thing that has cost that much demands to be made worth it, which is a lie people tell themselves to keep from admitting the cost was paid for nothing. I ground the rest of it wet, with the parts kept apart until the last, the way I should have from the start, and I corned it, pressed it damp into cakes and broke the cakes back to grains so the powder breathed fire instead of smothering itself, which I'd remembered too late to do Geta any good.

And I built the cannon, knowing it would burst. That was the entire point of it. I want that understood, because what I staged was a kind of theater and the audience was not supposed to know the ending and I did.

Hermes cast it. We did it in bronze, the good gun-bronze the Romans used for the best of everything, because the argument I was making was about exactly that bronze. A short fat tube, closed at one end, the bore a hand's-width across, the walls thick as I could make Hermes pour them, mounted in an oak cradle so it couldn't leap. Hermes hated it. He'd cast ballista parts and temple doors and he knew metal in his hands the way Heras knew a fever, and he kept running his thumb along the cooling bronze and telling me the wall was too thin for what I was telling him would happen inside it, and I kept telling him that was the point and watching him fail to believe me, because no craftsman wants to build a thing to break.

"You're paying me to make a flawed casting," he said.

"I'm paying you to make a perfect casting of a thing that bronze can't do," I said. "When it fails, it fails the metal. Not you."

He didn't like that either. But he poured it true, beautifully true, a clean tube without a flaw in it, and that was important, because a man could look at it afterward and not say it broke because the smith was a fool.

---

I had Celer get the army men there, and Celer's word and my own name as the emperor's freedman got me people who mattered, officers of the Guard and an engineer or two and a man from the imperial household whose job I never learned, standing under an awning on a stretch of bank well downriver of everything, on a gray morning with the cold coming off the water. I'd had the cradle staked into the mud sixty paces out, and the line where the spectators stood I'd set myself, and then I'd moved it back twenty paces farther because I was the only one there who knew what was coming and I wasn't going to kill an officer of the Guard to win an argument about steel.

Celer stood by me. He looked at the squat bronze thing in the mud and then at me.

"This is the powder," he said. Not a question. He'd been on the Loaf. He knew the look of me building toward a cost.

"This is the powder."

"You swore off it."

"I swore off hanging men under fire," I said. "I didn't keep the rest of it." He let that sit, the way he let things sit, and I added, because he'd earned it, "There's no man on this one, Celer. Just the metal."

I loaded it heavy. I want to be honest: I loaded it heavier than the argument strictly needed, because I wanted no chance of a fizzle in front of those faces, a stone ball seated on a charge I'd packed to do exactly what it did. We touched it off with a slow-match on a long pole and everyone flinched back and the morning came apart.

I have tried to find the words for the sound and the closest I've got is that it wasn't a sound, it was a thing that happened to your body, a flat enormous blow that hit you in the chest and the teeth at the same instant, and the bronze tube was simply not there anymore, it had opened like a flower made of knives and gone in every direction, and a piece of it the size of a fist went over our heads with a noise like a torn sky and into the river, and the stake and the cradle were gone, and the stone ball, the stone ball had gone first and gone true, out over the water, and we heard later it had crossed the whole width of the river and struck the far bank hard enough to throw mud.

There was a long quiet after, the kind of quiet that comes when a lot of frightened men are deciding not to have screamed.

Then they all started talking at once, and I let them, and I waited, and when they'd worked their way around to the question themselves, which was the whole craft of it, I gave them the answer they'd already arrived at. Here is a force that will throw a stone through a wall. Here is the best bronze in the world, poured without a flaw by the best smith I could find, and it could not hold the force for the space of a heartbeat. It tore like wet bread. So if you want the thing that throws the stone, and you have just seen with your own eyes that you want it, then you do not need a smith and you do not need bronze. You need a metal that does not yet exist, and the only place in Rome that anyone is trying to make it is a forge behind the cattle market run by a freedman named Hermes, who is out of money.

I had the wrecked pieces gathered up and laid out on a cloth so they could handle them. A Guard officer picked up the fist-sized chunk that had gone over our heads and turned it in his hands and went pale around the mouth, and I knew I had them, and I knew exactly what I had cost to get them, and the two facts sat in me side by side and did not cancel.

---

It is loose now. That is the thing I can't take back and the thing I keep coming around to in the dark.

Before that morning, the powder was a secret in a stinking shed and a number in my head and a man with no hands. After it, it was a thing that powerful men had seen with their own eyes throw a stone across a river, and powerful men do not unsee a weapon. They went away from that bank knowing it could be done, and knowing roughly what it ate, charcoal and brimstone and a white salt that comes from a slow filthy patience, and that knowledge was no longer in one frightened freedman's keeping. I'd told myself the Seres already had it so it wasn't mine to hold. Whether or not that was ever true, it is true now that Rome has it, and Rome got it from me, and I did the arithmetic on that the way I do arithmetic on everything and the sum did not come out the way I'd wanted.

The steel question is theirs now too, which was the whole point and which has turned out to be a heavier thing than I bargained for. Men who would not have crossed the street for a crucible are sending to ask about Hermes's forge. The household man whose job I never learned came back twice. I had wanted them to care about metal and now they care about metal the way an army cares, which is the way that ends with the metal pointed at someone, and I have made myself the man who can be asked about it, which means I am more useful and more watched than I was, and being either of those things in Rome is a way of being closer to the edge of a high place.

Hermes was glad, in his way. The money is coming and the money buys fuel and the fuel buys attempts and the attempts might, someday, with men and luck I didn't have before, catch the number. He has not caught it. I want to be clear that the steel is exactly as unsolved this morning as it was the morning we cast the cannon to argue for it; I have spent a man's hands and a great deal of my own peace to buy the right to keep failing at it with better funding. That is the honest shape of what I did.

I went to see Geta after, the evening of the demonstration, because I didn't want to drink wine with officers and be congratulated. He was sitting up. The room I'd built him had a window and he had it open to the cold and the river smell, and the bandages were off the worse arm finally, the wrist healed over smooth and wrong. He heard me come in and turned the good side of his face to me.

I sat down on the stool by the cot and didn't say anything for a while, and neither did he, and the wharf made its evening noises down the lane, the carts starting up now that the day-ban was lifting, the river slapping the pilings. After a while he asked me whether the powerful men had been frightened.

I told him they had.

He was quiet, working at something. Then he lifted the smooth healed stump a little off the blanket, the way you'd lift a hand to make a point, out of an old habit the body hadn't lost, and he said that was good, then, and he hoped they stayed frightened, because frightened men are careful, and I should make sure I stayed frightened too.

I sat with him until the light went. There was a wax tablet in my lap that I'd carried from the bank without remembering picking it up, and on it, in my own English that no one in the world but Tyche could read, I'd written the ratio I'd killed his hands to learn, the three numbers, finally right, and I sat in the dark with my thumb over the figures and did not rub them out.

---

# Chapter 22: Marcia

The thing nobody tells you about getting what you wanted is that it arrives as paperwork.

By the autumn after the cannon I had more money than I knew how to hold and more obligations than I could carry in my head, and the two were strangling each other. There was the contest, on my coin through Macer's name. The press, Eros, the pressman I now paid by the month. The yard, the wharf rent, Pamphilus and his wife and the new hand, Geta on his lifetime wages in his room off the well, the niter beds I had to pretend belonged to a man in volcano country, the sulfur that came up the river under another name, Hermes's forge swallowing money on steel that worked one batch in four. Army drafts to honor and salon invitations I couldn't refuse without insulting somebody who could end me, and a patron, Macer, whose dawn door I still stood at like a good client, and a higher patron, the emperor's house, whose man from the cannon demo kept turning up to look at things and say little.

And there were the contracts. That was the part that was killing me. A freedman doing business in Rome is doing it inside a thicket of law and custom that was grown over centuries to favor the people who grew it, and I was a foreigner with a bought name who could not always tell when he was being robbed. I had been cheated on cloth. I had been cheated on cartage. I had signed a lease for the second shed that, when Heras read it back to me slowly, turned out to let the owner take the shed and everything in it if I was late on a payment by a single day, and I had not been able to see it because the Latin of contracts is its own language, dense and barbed, and I read at the speed of a clever child.

Tyche kept the books beautifully. She could tell me to the as where the money was and where it had gone. What she could not do, what neither of us could do, was the other half, the part that wasn't arithmetic. She was a freedwoman of twenty-three, her word weighed at a freedwoman's weight, and she could no more walk into a shipping factor's counting-house and make him respect a clause than I could. We were two people who could see the trap and not get out of it.

So I went looking for a man to run the legal and commercial side, and I found a woman instead.

***

Heras sent me to her. He'd been treating her household for years, a daughter's fevers and an old slave's bad chest, and he said it the way he said things he'd already decided about, dry, over the top of a cup.

"There's a widow on the Aventine side keeps her dead husband's wharf business better than he did," he said. "Caepio's. You'll know the warehouses, the ones with the good roof, the only roof on that stretch that doesn't leak. She's a freedwoman. She buys and lets storage, lends short against cargo, factors for men who can't be bothered to factor for themselves, and she has not been cheated in eleven years that anyone can name. If you want someone who can read a Roman contract and tell you where the knife is, that's her. She'll cost you. She doesn't need you."

"Why would she take it on, then."

"Ask her," Heras said. "I expect she has reasons. People who don't need you and take you on always have reasons. That's the ones worth having." He set the cup down. "Her name is Marcia. Don't be charming at her. She's had charming men in her warehouse her whole life and she counts the spoons after."

***

The warehouse smelled of pepper and damp rope and the particular sourness of stored hides, and it was the driest, best-kept building I'd been in on that wharf, the floor swept, the cargo tallied on slates hung from nails by lot, the slaves moving like men who knew that idleness would be noticed. She had a room off the loading floor with a high window, a table, a strongbox, and a stool for her and no stool for anyone else, which I understood the moment I was standing in front of the table with nowhere to put myself.

Marcia was a head shorter than me and built solid, a woman in her middle thirties in a good plain dark stola with her hair up and pinned hard, no curls, no paint, a ring of keys at her waist and ink on the side of her right hand. Her face was square and unhurried and her eyes did to me what I'd watched Tyche's do to a column of figures, ran down me once, took the measure, and moved on to whether the measure was worth her morning.

"You're the Thulean," she said. "The one who flies."

"I don't fly anymore."

"I know. You stopped after the soldier." She said it flatly, a fact she'd filed, and I felt the floor tilt a little, because that was not the public story. The public story was that the balloon was the emperor's eye and observed from a tether. The soldier who died had been written down as a volunteer and the manner of it kept off the rolls. She watched me find out that she knew and she let me find out, the way Macer let me find out he'd seen through Tyche, and I understood I was dealing with someone who collected the true version of things and kept it in a strongbox with the rest of her assets.

"Heras tells me you're drowning," she said. "He used a kinder word. He's a kind man. You've outgrown your own counting. You sign things you can't read and you pay men who laugh about it on the way home. You want someone to hold the business end so you can go on making your toys."

"They're not toys."

"Everything is a toy until it makes money," Marcia said. "Then it's a business, and a business has a hundred ways to die that have nothing to do with whether the thing works. You know the thing works. You don't know the hundred ways. That's why you're in my warehouse instead of me in your yard." She picked up a slate, glanced at it, set it down. "What do you make in a year, clear, after the silver goes out the door for cloth and coal and wages and the rent and the press and the prize you pretend belongs to Titus Flavius Macer?"

I opened my mouth to give her a number and realized I didn't have one. I had Tyche's number, which was real, but it was the gross, the river of coin in and out, and the question she'd asked, what's left, what's actually yours when the year is done, I could not answer, and she watched me not be able to answer it.

"You don't know," she said. "Of course you don't. You know what came in. A child knows what came in. Sit down."

There was no stool. I stood.

"That was a test," she said. "You failed it. Now I know what I'd be dealing with."

***

I will not pretend I walked out of that first meeting with a partner. I walked out of it stung, and went back to the yard, and told Tyche about the woman who'd asked me a question I couldn't answer, and Tyche put down her stylus and said, "She's right, though," and then we spent the rest of the afternoon working out the answer to the question, what was actually mine when the year was done, and it took us both until the lamp, and the number, when we had it, was smaller than I'd have guessed and the smallness of it was the most useful thing anyone had done for me in months.

I went back with the number written down, put the tablet on her table, and said, "There. That's what's left. Now tell me what you'd do that I'm not doing."

Marcia read it the way Heras read a contract, slow, going back. Then she told me, without softening any of it, exactly where I was bleeding. The lease that could take the shed. The cloth contract where I bought by the bolt and paid by weight and the bolts had been getting heavier with damp on delivery and I'd never thought to check. The cartage I paid by the trip when the volume said I should pay a man by the month and own the carts. The prize money that went out as a clean gift and could have gone out as a thing that bought me obligation. She had the whole architecture of my enterprise mapped in the time it took me to drink a cup of her watered wine, which was good wine, better than I served, and I noticed that and filed it the way she filed things.

"You're frightened of the law," she said, "because you don't have it the way a citizen has it from the cradle. So you sign to make the fright stop. Don't. The law is a tool. It's a worse tool for a freedman than for a senator, but it's still a tool, and a freedwoman's learned to use a worse tool than yours and eat well." She tapped the tablet. "I'll take it on. The books, the contracts, the suppliers, the side of it you handle badly because you handle people who can hurt you badly. I'll be a fool for any man with a stripe on his tunic so they go on underestimating me, and I'll take them apart at the warehouse where there's no one to perform for. You'll go on making your steel and your powder and whatever else, and you'll show me everything, because I won't manage a business whose insides I'm not allowed to see."

"And your price."

"A share," she said. "A wage means I work for you and you can stop me. A share means we sink together and I'll fight for the ship. You don't want a manager you can fire. You want one who can't afford to let you fail. That's a partner. That costs more and it's worth more and you can't get it cheap, so don't try, I'll know."

I had come looking to hire a clerk. I stood in her dry warehouse and understood I was being offered something I hadn't known to want, and that the offering was on her terms, which she'd worked out before I came in the door, because Heras had told her about me days ago and she'd had time to decide what I was worth to her.

"Why," I said. "Heras said you'd have reasons. You don't need me. Caepio's runs. You eat well. Why take on a foreigner with a strange reputation and more enemies than friends."

She was quiet a moment, and it was the first time her face did anything that wasn't deliberate.

"Because Caepio's is mine until I die and then it's a freedwoman's estate with no man to it and the law and a dozen cousins of my dead husband come for it like dogs," she said. "Because I'm thirty-four and I've spent eleven years making a thing that the moment I close my eyes goes to people who never swept that floor. Because a widow's standing is a held breath. And because you," she looked at me, "are building something with no name yet. You're building something nobody has the shape of, past a wharf and a roof, the numbers and the printed sheets and the prize and whatever the powder turns into, and a thing nobody has the shape of yet is a thing a person can get a real stake in, an early one, a deep one, before the men with stripes notice it's worth fighting over." She picked up her slate again. "I'm not doing you a kindness. I want in while in is cheap. Now we understand each other."

We did. I have been understood by very few people in this place and most of them have been wrong about the part that mattered. She was right about all of it and didn't know the one thing that wasn't her business, and I found, standing in the pepper smell, that I trusted that combination more than I'd trusted anyone since Heras.

***

Tyche and Marcia were always going to be a problem, and they were, for about a month, and then they weren't, and I had almost nothing to do with the resolving of it, which is the only reason it worked.

Marcia came to the yard to see the books, the real ones, and Tyche put them in front of her with the look of a craftsman handing work to a rival craftsman, daring her to find the flaw. Marcia read them. She read for a long time, the new figures and all, which she didn't know and didn't pretend to, asking short questions, and Tyche answered short, and the two of them circled the table in their separate silences while I tried to be useful and was told twice, once by each of them, to leave it.

What broke it was an error. Not Tyche's. Marcia found a figure in a cartage account that didn't sit, a sum that the carter had been shaving for a year, small enough that it had slid under even Tyche's eye because it was hidden in a tariff Tyche had taken on trust, a wharf custom she'd had no reason to question. Marcia laid her finger on it the way Macer had laid his on Tyche's whole secret, and Tyche went still, and I braced for the worst, and Tyche said, "Show me how you saw that."

So Marcia showed her. The arithmetic Tyche had cold. The thing Marcia had that Tyche didn't, the eleven years of knowing which customs were robbery dressed as custom, which a person only learns by being robbed and surviving it, that she laid out plainly, and Tyche took it in the way she took in a new letter, reaching past it to the next one before Marcia had finished, and by the end of the afternoon they had stopped circling and were both bent over the cartage accounts hunting for the rest of it, and they found four more, and I made myself scarce and let them.

They were never going to be friends in the soft sense. They were two women who'd each made themselves out of nothing in a city that owned women in different ways, Tyche out of a slave's hidden cleverness, Marcia out of a widow's held breath, and each of them recognized in the other the thing she'd had to do to survive and respected it and did not need it cooed over. What they built was an alliance, the kind that's worth ten friendships, and the terms of it they set between themselves and never told me, which was correct, because it was theirs. I learned later that Marcia paid Tyche, out of her own share, a fee for the figures Tyche taught her, because Marcia did not take instruction for free any more than Tyche took freedom with thanks, and the two of them had worked out a coin price for it across that table without my knowing, and when I found out I had the sense to say nothing.

Tyche stayed family, apprentice, the keeper of the cipher that was mine. Marcia was the partner, the one who held the gates. The women drew the line between those two things themselves, cleaner than I could have, and kept to their own sides of it, and the enterprise stopped bleeding inside of a season.

***

We were married a little over a year after the day I failed her test, in the spring, by which point we had been running the business together long enough that the marriage was the smaller change of the two.

I want to be honest about how it came about, because it was not the way the songs have it. There was no moment. There was a long sequence of ordinary things, of working late at her warehouse and mine, of arguing over the cartage and the lease and which men we could trust and which we'd let go on thinking they'd fooled us. She came to the yard often enough that the wharf stopped remarking on it. She told me I was wrong about things in a flat way that didn't try to spare me and didn't try to wound me, and after a life of people who flattered me because I was a curiosity or feared me because I was the emperor's, the flatness of her was like cold water on a burn. I was lonely in a way I had stopped admitting to myself, the specific loneliness of the man who can't tell anyone the true thing, and she could not fix that, no one could, but she sat across the true thing without prying at it, and that turned out to be the next best thing to being known.

It was Marcia who said the word first, of course it was, because she said the hard words first about everything.

"We should marry," she said. We were at her table, the strongbox open between us, doing the year's reckoning. "Hear me before you make a face. You're a citizen. You can marry. I'm a freedwoman and so it's a marriage the law allows, freedman to freedwoman, no stain either of us can give the other we don't already carry. We've been partners a year. Married, your enterprise and my warehouse are one estate, and the law that comes for a widow's goods can't come the same way for a wife's, and if there are children they're freeborn, both of us free, and freeborn is a different thing in this city than what either of us started as. Their word weighs full. They can hold office, theirs can, if they're clever and we're careful. I've watched you go strange in the face every time someone calls you the emperor's man and I know you're alone in a way I can't put my finger on, and I'm not going to put my finger on it, that's yours. But I'd rather be alone next to you than alone in this warehouse waiting for my husband's cousins. I've thought it through from every side. It's a good arrangement for both of us and there's worse reasons to marry and I like you, which is more than most people who marry can say. Well?"

I have been asked to do a great many things in Rome by a great many people who had power over me. This was the first time in years someone offered me a thing and let it be mine to take or not, laid out plain, no leash on it. I sat in her warehouse with the pepper smell and the open strongbox and a woman who'd told me she liked me as the fourth or fifth reason on a list of better ones, and I understood that the better ones were why I could believe the last one.

"Yes," I said.

"Don't," she said, "look at me like that. We're doing the year's reckoning. We'll be sentimental on our own time, if we turn out to have any in us, which I doubt and don't mind." But she put her hand flat on the table near mine for a moment, near, not on, the ink-stained right hand, and left it there while she found the next slate, and that was the whole of it, and it was enough.

***

We married without much ceremony, which suited us both. There is a form for it, the words, the joining of right hands, a few witnesses, and we did the form because the form is what makes the law hold. Heras stood as witness and looked insufferably satisfied with himself the entire time. Pamphilus and his wife came. Tyche stood at the back with her arms folded and afterward said to Marcia, not to me, "He's careless with money but he's not a liar, watch the first and trust the second," which Marcia told me later was the most useful wedding gift she'd ever been given.

Marcia kept Caepio's. She did not fold it into me and vanish into a wife, which I would not have wanted and which she would not have done if I had. She kept her own warehouse, her own keys, her own slaves whom I noticed she worked hard and freed promptly and who stayed, the way good people's freedmen stay. She ran her end and I ran mine and we ran the joined middle together, and she went on being a fool for any man with a stripe and counting the spoons after. She had her own designs on the thing we were building, designs that ran out past me, and she let me see the edges of them. She wanted the printed sheets sold by the thousand. She wanted the prize to grow teeth, to bind the men who won it, to become a thing the enterprise owned rather than gave away. She wanted, she told me once, plainly, doing sums, to be sure that when I was dead, which I would be before her by the odds, the thing did not die with me or fall to the emperor's house or to whichever cousin or rival came for it, but went on, with a shape and a strongbox and people who couldn't afford to let it fail, the way I'd taught the figures to spread on their own because a thing that spreads on its own can't be killed by killing one man.

She was, in other words, already building the part that came after me, and she told me so, doing the year's reckoning, the way she told me everything. A partner planning the survival of the work past my death. It was the most romantic thing anyone had ever said to me and neither of us called it that.

***

The night after the wedding she came to the yard, because the yard was where the year's work was and she wanted to see the powder figures before she'd let her name be joined to a man who'd lost one hand off a workman already.

I showed her the corned powder and the cannon drawings and Hermes's failed steel, the brittle batch beside the good one, indistinguishable to the eye, the whole stubborn wall of it. She asked the cost of each thing and the chance it would ever pay and whether the imperial house could take it from us if it worked, and I answered all of it, even the parts that frightened me, because she'd married into the frightening parts and had the right.

At the end she picked up the little book, the one in the cipher, with the dead glass folded in the cloth, and she looked at the black glass once, hard, the way Tyche had, and at the marks that were not Latin or Greek or the figures, and she did not ask. She set it back down exactly where it had been, squared to the edge of the table the way she squared everything.

"Tyche reads that," she said. It wasn't a question. She'd worked it out.

"Tyche reads that."

"Good," Marcia said. "Then if you die with your head full there's one who can read it down and one who can sell it, and between us the thing lives." She blew out the second lamp, the one we didn't need, because she did not waste oil any more than she wasted words or trust, and in the dark of the tally-room with the river going by outside and the cranes quiet she put her ink-stained hand over mine on the closed book, flat, the whole hand this time, and left it there, and we stood like that for a while, two people who had chosen each other for good reasons in a hard place, and then she said it was late and there was the cartage to settle in the morning, and we went in.

---

# Chapter 23: The Things That Don't Work

There is a particular silence a workshop makes when a thing is supposed to move and doesn't. The men stop talking. The fire ticks. Somebody scratches under his tunic. And the device sits there on the bench being exactly as dead as a brick, while everyone waits for me to explain why I lied to them.

I got to know that silence well the winter Marcia took over my books.

It started, like most of my worst ideas, with me being smug. I had money now, real money, the kind that buys bronze by the ingot and a man's whole month of skill without flinching. I had Hermes, who could cut a gear so clean you could shave with the teeth. I had a memory full of clocks. Every house I had ever lived in had clocks in it, on walls, on stoves, on the little square of glass I still carried dead against my chest. I had watched a documentary once about a man in a leather apron building a tall clock for a museum, and I remembered the brass guts of it swinging away behind a glass door, patient as breathing.

So I told Hermes I was going to build a machine that told the hours by itself, with no water, no sun, no slave to watch it. A thing of wheels. He looked at me the way he looked at everything I said before I proved it, which was the look of a man pricing your funeral.

The wheels were easy. That was the trap. I drew a train of them, big driving wheel down to small, and I knew without thinking too hard that a falling weight on a cord would turn the big one, and the train would step the motion down, fast wheels off slow. Hermes cut them over three weeks. They were beautiful. I hung the weight, a lead cylinder Pamphilus cast in sand, and let it go.

The whole train spun up like a dropped top and the weight slammed the floor in about the time it takes to say a prayer.

That was correct. That was the problem doing exactly what falling weights do. A clock is a thing that is stopped, over and over, a precise number of times, by something that lets the wheels forward one little bite at a time and no more. The moving was the easy part, the part any falling weight would do for free. I knew that. I knew it the way you know the shape of a word you can't spell. There was a piece, a small ugly piece, that caught the wheel and released it and caught it again, and it ticked, and the tick was the whole machine. Everything else was just gears. The tick was the clock.

I could not remember the piece.

I want to be honest about what that was like, because it is the truest thing in this chapter and the hardest to make anyone here understand. I sat on a stool in front of a pile of perfect bronze and I closed my eyes and I went looking in my own head for a thing I had seen a hundred times, and it was not there. I could see the documentary. I could see the man's hands, the apron, the brass swinging, a sort of anchor-shaped bit rocking on a pin, two little points dipping into a toothed wheel, click, click, click. I could see it and I could not build it, because seeing a thing swing is not knowing how its faces are angled, how deep the points bite, how the rocking gets fed by the wheel it's holding back. The angles were the secret and the angles had never gone into my head. I had watched it the way you watch a fire. Pleasantly. Learning nothing.

I tried anyway. God, I tried. I bent a strip of bronze into the anchor I could see and pinned it over the toothed wheel and rocked it with my finger. It would catch, sometimes, one tooth, and stick. Or it would slip the whole wheel at once and the weight would go to the floor. I filed the points. I changed the angle. I made Hermes cut three different toothed wheels, coarse and fine. I rigged a crossbar to swing the anchor with a weighted arm, thinking the swing itself would set the rhythm, and the swing just died, every time, like a child on a swing nobody pushes. There was a thing that fed the swing and I did not have it. The shape that took the kick from the wheel and gave it back to the swing was the shape I couldn't remember, and round it went, the dog chasing the tail it had already lost.

Hermes watched me ruin a week of his work and finally said, between hammer-blows on something useful, "It wants to go or it wants to stop. You are asking it to do both."

"Yes," I said. "That's exactly what I'm asking it to do."

"Then you are asking for a quarrel, not a machine." He set the hammer down. "What does it look like, in your country, the part that quarrels?"

And there it was. I opened my mouth to tell him and I had nothing. Nothing. I, the man from the land of clocks, the wizard who drew the whole world and made fire fly, could not describe to an honest smith the one small bronze argument that every clock for the next two thousand years would be built around. It existed. It would exist. Men not yet born would file it in their sleep. It was maybe a hand's-breadth of metal and it was as far beyond my reach as the moon, and I had told these people I came from a place where such things hung on every wall.

I sat with that. It is a specific flavor of shame, knowing a true thing and being unable to use it. Worse than not knowing. When you don't know, you can go find out. There was no finding out here. The book was my own head and the page was torn out before I arrived.

So I did what a coward does, which is, I built the thing I could build and called it the thing I'd promised.

I built a water clock.

The Romans already had water clocks. *Clepsydra*, Heras called it, the water-thief, and they used them to time speeches in court so a windbag couldn't talk all day. A pot, a hole, water dripping out, marks on the inside to read the falling level. Crude, and everyone knew it was crude, and the problem with it was simple and ancient: a full pot pushes the water out fast and an empty pot dribbles, so your hours at the start of the pot are not the same length as your hours at the bottom. The water lies as it leaves.

I knew the fix for that, or half of it. A second vessel, kept always brim-full and overflowing, feeding the first through a fixed jet, so the pressure never changed. And here my gears were not wasted, because I floated a cork in the catch-vessel, ran a cord from the cork over a little wheel as the water rose, and made the wheel turn a drum, and on the drum a painted pointer that crept around a dial of the hours like the face of a real clock. A toothed rack, a pinion, a slow proud finger going round.

It was gorgeous. I won't pretend it wasn't. We mounted it on the wall of the tally-room in a frame of oiled cedar, the catch-vessel glinting, the float-cord whispering over its wheel, the pointer ticking up the painted hours in a hand Tyche lettered. Men came off the wharf to look at it. Macer came, and stood in front of it with his thumb working his ring, and said it was the prettiest piece of foolery on the river and asked what it was for, and I said it told the hours, and he said so did the sun, for free, and walked out, but he came back twice more to look at it, which from Macer is a hymn.

It did not keep time.

It kept time the way a drunk keeps a promise. Across an afternoon it would wander a fistful of marks off the sun, and the next day it would wander the other way. The overflow vessel helped, but the jet silted, and the water was thicker on a cold morning than a warm noon and crept slower, and the float stuck a hair on its cord and then jumped, so the pointer hitched. Heras, who is the only man I let measure me honestly, took to setting it against a sundial at noon and writing down its lie, and the lies were never the same lie twice. A clock that is wrong the same amount every day is a clock you can correct. A clock that is wrong a different amount every day is a wet decoration.

"It is very beautiful," Heras said, which is what he says about my failures, the same words he used for the first balloon that wouldn't lift, "and I would not plan a funeral by it."

I kept it on the wall. I keep it there still. It is honest about one thing, at least. It shows everyone who walks in that the Thulean, the man from the country of wheels, can make water crawl around a circle and cannot make the wheels argue properly with a weight. Tyche reads the sun and corrects the pointer twice a day with her thumb. I watch her do it sometimes. I built a machine to free a person from watching the hours, and the machine needs a person to watch it. There's a word for that in my old language and the word is most of my life here.

---

The glass was a longer disappointment, and a more expensive one, and the only one I brought entirely on myself with my own big mouth.

It was the contest's fault, partly. Three years of the prize and the stories had gotten ambitious in a way I had not planned and could not stop. The first year it was eagles pulling chariots. By the third year half the entries were about glasses. Glasses to read a letter on a far hill. A glass to see the moon as a country, mountains and seas and the little cities of moon-people. And one, the one that haunted me, a glass so strong it showed the little invisible ones I had told everyone climb inside you when you sicken, the tiny creatures swimming in a drop of pond-water, finally caught and counted. Somebody had listened to me talk about the invisible world and reasoned, correctly, that if it was there, a strong enough glass would show it. The boy who wrote it asked, in the last line, when I would build the glass that proved my own story.

I had been telling that lie for ten years. The invisible ones. I believed in them. I had no way on God's earth to show them to anyone, and now a child had written a tale where I did, and the tale was being printed by Eros and sold for an as in the Subura, and people were waiting.

I had made a single lens before, years back, a fat little disc of glass that made small writing big if you held it just right, a thing for an old man's failing eyes. That worked. A magnifier is easy. You polish a lump, you get a bigger blurry thing. What the stories wanted was a tube with a glass at each end, the far one to gather and the near one to spread, and the two together to drag a far thing close. I knew the shape of it. I had held one at a baseball game once, somebody's father's binoculars, the green field jumping suddenly huge and crisp into my eye. I knew it could be done. The Romans had glass. Beautiful glass, glass cups you could read a coin through, glass beads, glass for windows in the rich houses.

I bought the best glass on the river and it betrayed me at every step.

Roman glass is clear, and Roman glass is full of ghosts. Hold a good blown cup to the light and you see them, threads in the body of it, faint snakes of denser and thinner glass twisting through where it cooled unevenly, *striae* the Greek glass-cutter called them, and little round bubbles caught like held breath, some a pinprick, some big as a lentil. For a cup it doesn't matter. For a window it doesn't matter. For a lens it is the difference between an eye and a wound, because every bubble bends the light its own wrong way and every thread smears it, and a lens is nothing but a thing for bending light all one right way at once.

I hired the Greek, Demetrios, a glass-cutter with hands like a spider and no patience for me. I had him grind discs against a dished iron block with wet sand, working the curve I drew, and the work was murder. Grinding a sphere by eye, with no way to test the curve but to stop and look through it and guess, is a thing that takes a man a lifetime to learn and I was asking him to learn it in a season for a barbarian's whim. He'd grind for a day and we'd hold the disc up and the curve was lumpy, too deep on one edge, and there was no fixing it, you ground it down and started the shape again and lost the day. Out of a dozen blanks we got two that were near enough to round to bother mounting, and both of them had ghosts in them I couldn't grind out because the ghosts went all the way through.

We made the tube. Leather over a wooden form, a glass at each end, the far one barely curved, the near one a deep little dish, the spacing slid back and forth until a far thing swam into a kind of focus.

I looked through it at the far bank of the river, at a man unloading a boat, and here is exactly what I saw, because I owe the truth to the page if to nothing else. The man was closer. Two times closer, maybe a little more, near enough that I could have told you he wore a brown tunic if I hadn't already known. He was also dim, the way the world is dim through a dirty pane. He was soft at the edges, never quite sharp no matter how I slid the tube. And he wore a rainbow. A thin skin of color clung to every edge of him, blue down one side and a sick yellow-red down the other, so the man and the boat and the far roofs all looked painted by someone whose hand shook and whose colors ran. The ghosts in the glass laid a faint smear over all of it, like looking through a window someone had breathed on.

It was, to be precise, a slightly closer, dimmer, blurrier, rainbow-edged view of a thing I could already see fine with my own eye.

I stood there on the wharf holding it and I felt the whole bright tower of what I'd promised lean and not quite fall. The war-glass that lets a general count an enemy three valleys off. The moon as a country. The drop of pond-water teeming with the proof of every cure I'd ever pushed. All of it, the whole future, supposed to start in this leather tube, and what came out the other end was a fuzzy man in a brown tunic wearing a rainbow he didn't ask for.

Marcia tried it. She slid the tube the way I showed her, found the boat, lowered it, and asked me what it cost to make.

I told her, glass and Demetrios and the iron block and the weeks. Her face did the thing it does, the small private adding-up. "And a child can see the far bank by walking to it," she said, "for nothing." But she didn't make me throw it out. She had me make three more, ground a little better each time, and she sold them, God help me, to rich men who wanted the newest toy and didn't care that the toy was bad. A senator's wife paid more for a blurry rainbow tube than Demetrios earned in a season. "Everything is a toy until it makes money," Marcia said, wrapping it in wool, "and a bad toy that no one else has is still the only one. Make me ten."

So there are perhaps a dozen of my spyglasses in Rome now, in the houses of the very rich, and every one of them shows a dim two-times rainbow, and the men who own them tell each other it is the wonder of the age, and not one of them has seen a single invisible creature or one mountain on the moon. The boy who wrote the story is still waiting. He will wait until he dies. I will not build the glass that shows the little ones. There is no such glass on this side of a hundred patient lifetimes of clearer glass than Rome can pour, and I told a true thing I cannot prove and dressed it up as a marvel I cannot make, and the dressing-up is the part I'll answer for.

I kept the first tube too. It and the water clock sit near each other in the tally-room, the two prettiest things I have made that don't do what I said they would.

---

The rails I want to tell last, because the rails worked, and that was the worst of the three by a long way.

This one I did not get from a documentary. I got it from being a kid who liked trains, and from the simplest physics there is, the kind even I can't garble. A wheel rolling on a smooth hard road takes a fraction of the muscle that dragging a sledge does, because rolling beats sliding, and if you keep the wheel from wandering off the smooth part by giving it a lip that rides against a rail, you can run a heavy load down a fixed line with almost nothing pushing it. No new metal worth speaking of. No chokepoint. Wood and a little iron for the wheel-rims and the flanges. The most certain thing I had tried to build since the numerals.

There was a stone-cutter's yard up past the brickfields that hauled cut blocks down a long slope to a barge landing, and the hauling was done by men. Slaves, chained at the ankle in a coffle when they weren't under the load, dragging the blocks on greased rollers and timber sleds down a churned mud track, twenty and thirty of them to a big block, in the heat, all day. I had walked past it a hundred times on the way to the brick I needed and made myself not look, the way you learn to here, the way you have to or you go mad in a week.

I went to the owner, a hard dry equestrian named Maximus, with Marcia, because by now I knew better than to talk money without her in the room. I told him I could move his stone with a quarter of the men. I laid out wooden rails, squared oak baulks pinned to sleepers, set the gauge to his cart, built four cars with flanged wheels Hermes turned, and ran a line down one edge of his slope at my own cost, to show him, because Apollodorus once told me a demonstration is a lie you tell on a good day and I wanted this to be a true thing on a true day.

It was. We loaded a block that had taken twenty-six men to drag, a real monster, onto a car on the rails, and four men walked it down the slope at a walk, leaning back on ropes to hold it from running, and at the bottom two men pushed the empty car back up. Four men and a slope did the work of twenty-six men and a whip. Maximus stood at the top and watched the block go down smooth and quiet where it used to scream on the rollers, and I watched his face, and I let myself have one clean breath of the thing I almost never get here, which is the feeling of a true machine doing a true job in front of a man who can use it.

Then we sat down to the arithmetic, and Marcia did it, because she does it better than I do and because I think she already knew where it went.

The rails cost so much in oak and iron and the cutting and laying, she said, naming the figure. The wheels and the cars, this much. To rail his whole yard and the long haul to the landing, this much, a sum that made Maximus's mouth go flat. Against that, she said, set what he'd save. And then she said the thing I had been not-thinking for a month, in the flat voice she uses for the hard word.

"You own the men, Maximus."

He did. That was the whole sum, right there, and the rest was just writing it down. He owned the men already. He'd bought them years ago, the price long since paid and forgotten, and a slave who hauls stone costs him a bowl of grain and a wall to chain him behind and nothing else, and if one dies under a block there are always more off the boats from Dacia cheaper this year than last, because we had just dragged a whole nation south in chains and I had counted some of that haul myself in the new figures. The men were a cost he had already paid and would pay again in bodies, not coin. The rails were a new cost, in money he had now and iron he could sell, to replace a thing he already owned and didn't have to buy twice.

"So," Marcia said, and tapped the tablet where the two columns sat, "you would spend good iron and good silver to stop using men you have already bought and cannot sell for much. The rail saves labor. You have labor to burn, Maximus, bought and fed and going nowhere. What you are short of is iron and silver." She let it sit. "It is a fine machine. It is a machine for a man who has to pay his haulers. You do not have to pay your haulers."

Maximus looked at the rails, and at the block sitting at the bottom of the slope where four men had set it down easy, and he was not a stupid man and he was not even, I think, a cruel one past the ordinary cruelty of the whole arrangement we all stood inside. He saw it work. He never once said it didn't work. He said it was clever. He said the Thulean was always clever. And he said he would think on it, which is what a man says when he has already thought, and he sent us home, and he kept his coffle and his rollers and his mud track and his twenty-six men, because his twenty-six men were free to him now in the only sense his ledger understood, and my rails were not.

I tore out the demonstration line a month later when he wouldn't buy the oak it sat on. The wheels are in the shed. Hermes turned them so true.

I have built things that hurt people. I put a man up in a balloon and watched him burn out of the sky. I made a powder that took Geta's hand and his eye in a shed I built. Those are heavy and I carry them and I have written them down where I write the heavy things. The rails were a different weight, and lighter, and somehow it has worn a deeper groove. Nobody died. Nothing burned. I built a clean, true, simple machine that took the killing labor off men's backs, and it lost, fair and square, in the open court of arithmetic, to the plain fact that a man already owned the backs and a back costs less than a wheel. The machine was right. The world had done its sum a long time ago, and the sum came out the other way, and no clever wheel of mine was going to make it come out different in the life I have left.

I had been telling myself the books were a vanity, a wizard's hoard, a way of not dying all the way. They are that. They are also the only move I have left after a day like the one with Maximus, because I cannot win here. One fallible head full of torn-out pages, one short life, against a sum the whole world has already settled. I can't beat it. I can write the rungs down, plain and in order, so that when the sum finally changes, and it will, the men standing where Maximus stood will have the wheel turned true and the lens ground right and, God willing, the small bronze argument that makes a clock tick, drawn by a steadier hand than mine.

That evening I went into the tally-room where the water clock was lying about the hour and the dead spyglass sat beside it, and Tyche was at the table with the wax and the English letters, working down the cipher I'd given her, building it letter by letter the way I should have built the clock. I sat down across from her and pulled a fresh tablet over and started to write out, in the small flat hand I save for the books, everything I knew about a wheel that rolls on a rail and why four men can do the work of twenty-six. I got to the flange and stopped and drew it twice before I got the lip on the right side. Tyche watched me get it wrong and then right, and she didn't say anything, and she went back to her letters, and outside on the wharf somebody was still hauling something heavy the old way, the rope creaking, the men counting the pull in a language I dream in now, and I wrote it down.

---

# Chapter 24: High Ground

There is a sound a yard makes when it is working and nobody is fighting about anything, and I learned that summer how rare it is and how little I had ever stopped to hear it.

It was the evening sound. The pressmen had gone, Eros last, with ink to the elbow and a sheet of the Minerva-prize tales held away from his tunic like a man carrying a baby he isn't sure of. The river was going from pewter to nothing. Somewhere down the wharf a man was loading grain by torchlight and cursing the man above him in the easy way of people who do it every night. In the shed the water clock ticked its lie, off by a quarter of an hour and gaining, and Tyche had already corrected it by the sun and would correct it again at dawn, and the correcting had become a thing she did with one hand while reading something held in the other.

I stood by the well with a cup of my own filtered water, which still tasted of charcoal and of being right, and I counted.

Not money. I had stopped being able to feel the money as a number a while back; Marcia held the money now and the money held still for her the way nothing held still for me. I counted the things. Eleven men and women on the wages, not counting the wharf-women who stitched piecework and the carters. A press that still ate a sheet in four and still threw off more printed paper in a week than the whole city of Rome had owned in writing the year I fell into it. A forge across town that great men sent money to because of a bronze tube I had made burst on purpose in front of them. A filter in two of the public fountains, where it would do something. A spyglass that did not work, sold to people who did not know it did not work. A contest that put a baker's dream of a self-grinding mill into the hands of children who now drew, in the dust of the Subura, carts with no mules and ships with no oars.

I had counted myself once, on a wax tablet in a cell, for whoever found my body, a list of what I knew with half of it wrong. This was the other list. Twelve years long, and I stood in the dark over my own well and could not make it feel like mine.

That was the trouble with the high ground. You could see everything from up there. You just couldn't tell who was up there with you.

---

I had walked through the markets that morning, which I did sometimes the way other men go to the baths, to be among the thing without being asked anything by it.

You came up from the wharf through the cattle market and the new figures were everywhere, the way water is everywhere once it has found the low places. A fish-seller had them chalked on a slate, the morning's count and the price, in the quick hooked hand the slate-writers had developed that I would not have recognized as my own digits if I hadn't known to look. A boy ran them off on his fingers for a woman buying eels and got it right faster than the woman's own slave with his counters, and the slave gave the boy a look I knew from the inside, the look of a man watching a thing he was trained for be done better by a child for free. Nobody had taught that boy from a book. The marks had simply gotten into the city the way a tune gets into a city, by being easier to carry than the thing it replaced.

In a colonnade off the cattle market a man was selling small painted clay figures off a board, the kind they sell for the festivals, gods and gladiators and lucky phalluses, and among them, between a Hercules and a little Venus, were two of mine. A balloon, fat and gray-painted, with a stick man hung under it in a basket the size of a thumbnail. And a thing with a long body and many little wheels under it, painted with a smoking stack at the front, that I had never built and never would and that the potter had copied off a printed prize-tale because it sold. A horseless cart. A train, in everything but the rails, which the potter had left off because rails would have looked like nothing to a buyer and a smoking stack looked like power.

I stood and looked at it for too long. The man selling them took me for a buyer and started his patter, told me the smoking one was a Thulean engine, a wonder from the far north where the cold makes men clever, that it would one day drag the grain of all Egypt up the Tiber with no men and no beasts, that his sister's boy had seen the drawings. He had a whole story. It was a good story, and it had drifted a long way from mine, picked up a sister's boy and the cleverage of all Egypt and a smoking stack that meant it had been near some fire I'd set, the cannon or the contest or the general rumor of the man who flew, and come out the far end as a two-as toy a man could buy his child.

I bought the train. I gave him more than he asked and told him to keep the patter, it was a good patter, and he thought I was a soft fool and I let him.

I have it still. It sits where I can see it. A clay engine that pulls nothing, made by a man who copied a dream printed by my press from a tale entered in my contest by a boy who will never know my name, sold to me as a marvel from a country that does not exist by a man who will go to his grave certain that somewhere in the cold north it is true.

That is what a rung looks like, I think, when you are standing on the one below it. It looks like a toy somebody made up.

---

The contest reading that year was held, as always, in Macer's house, under Macer's name, the Macer Prize, paid out of my coin and crowned with my judgment, with Macer at the head of the room taking the credit for a thing he privately thought was a waste of a perfectly good dinner. I had stopped minding. Letting Macer own it was the price of it being allowed to exist, the way letting the gods own the water filter was the price of the filter, the way letting Heras call the boiling "Greek wisdom" was the price of the boiling. I had a lot of children, and not one of them had my name on it, and most days I told myself that was the design and not the wound.

A girl read that year. Eleven, twelve, freeborn, the daughter of a wool-factor who had brought her along because the wife was sick and the man didn't know what else to do with her, and she'd heard them reading and she'd written something on the back of one of her father's tally-sheets, in the new figures where she'd run out of words, and somebody had put it in the basket as a joke.

It was about a ladder.

Not a metal ladder. A ladder of knowing. She'd written that a man could not jump to the moon but he could build a step, and on the step his son could build a step, and she had drawn it, a stair going up into a sky full of the little painted stars children draw, and a stick figure on the bottom step holding up a torch for the ones above him who weren't born yet. It was a child's drawing. The perspective was wrong. The stick man's torch was bigger than his head.

I had to go and stand by the wine for a while.

Crispus was there, of course, oiled and elegant and citing somebody, and he made a remark about the spectacle of a barbarian teaching Roman children to dream of staircases to heaven, the impiety of it, the bad taste, and the room laughed the way a room laughs at a clever man, and I parried it the way I had learned to parry him, gracefully, with a joke against myself that let everyone feel generous. I won the room. I always won the room now. That was another thing the high ground gave you, the wind at your back in any room Crispus tried to take from you, because Caesar had named me and no man of letters wants to be the one who turns out to have been mocking the emperor's wonder.

But I had drawn that ladder myself, in the dark, in a script no one in that room could read, years before that girl was old enough to hold a stylus. I had drawn it as the whole point of everything. And a child I would never meet had reached up out of her own head and grabbed the exact shape of it and drawn the stick man holding the torch for the ones not born yet, and she did not know, she could not know, that the man clapping for her by the wine was the one who'd lit the torch and that he was standing in a room full of people and there was no one in it, not one, he could turn to and say: she got it. She got the whole thing. Look.

I gave her the prize. A child's tale beat a baker's machine and a senator's son's war-engine, and I made the case for it cleanly and Macer grumbled that there was no profit in stairs and paid out the hundred denarii into the wool-factor's astonished hand. The girl looked at me like I was a wall that had spoken. I told her the torch was too big. She told me, with the flat certainty of the young, that it had to be, so the ones above could see it.

I think about that more than I should.

---

I keep the phone.

I should say what it is, because by now even I have to reach for what it is. It is a flat piece of black glass and dark metal, the size of my palm, heavier than it looks, with a single eye of glass at the back and rounded corners worn smooth where my thumb used to ride. There is a thing Romans do with a thumb on a worn coin or a god's bronze foot, a rubbing, and I have done it to the phone for twelve years without meaning to and the metal there is brighter than the rest.

It does nothing. I want that understood, because there is a part of me that has never fully understood it. It is not broken the way a thing here breaks, a spring snapped, a gear stripped, something Hermes could open and curse and maybe mend. It is whole. There is not a scratch on the glass that matters. It is simply asleep and there is no waking it, no fire I can build, no wheel I can turn, no clever Roman trick and no clever Thulean one either, because the thing it needs is a current I cannot make and could not explain and have given up grieving for the way you give up grieving for a country.

That night, after the well and the counting, I took it out.

I do that. Not often. There is danger in it; a man who held that up in a market would be a witch by sundown and a dead witch by the next festival, and I keep it wrapped in oiled wool in a box under a board only Tyche knows about, next to a wax tablet that says Marcus Ulpius Danihel and made me a citizen, two relics of two countries that cannot write to each other, and I had thought that line was clever the day I thought it and now I just think it is true.

I held it and I rubbed the bright place with my thumb and I did the thing I do, which is I tried to remember a song.

There was a song. There were a thousand songs; the glass was full of them once, I had carried a whole shouting world of music in my pocket and never once thought it was a wonder. And now I cannot keep one. I can keep the shape of one, the rise and fall of it, the feeling in my chest where the chorus went, and the words come up in pieces and the pieces are wrong, I know they are wrong, I am misremembering them the way I misremembered the clock and the third powder and the boiling-point of water on a mountain, and there is no one, there is no glass, there is nothing in this entire world that can write the true words back to me. The song is in a place that cannot send to here. Same as the current. Same as my mother's voice, which I cannot any longer be sure I am remembering and not inventing.

I hummed a few bars of the wrong song over a dead piece of glass in the dark of my own rich house with my freeborn wife asleep down the hall and eleven people on my wages and the emperor's favor on my name, and I have never in my life been so completely alone, and I am not going to write any more about it than that, because the rule of this whole place is that you do not get to say the loud true thing out loud, you make a toy and a filter and a contest and let the gods take the credit, and the loud true thing stays in a box under a board.

I put the phone back. I put the citizenship tablet on top of it. Then I took the tablet off again, because it felt wrong, the new country lying on top of the old one like dirt over a grave, and I put the phone on top instead, and that felt worse, so I left them side by side, and that was the only honest way to keep them, two flat things that knew nothing of each other, in the dark, in a box, in Rome.

---

Heras came to the yard a few days after, on no errand, which was itself the errand.

He was an old man now and let himself be one, which had taken him a while; he had carried himself young for years and then somewhere past sixty had set it down. He sat on the bench by the dead forge-corner where we sat, and he had brought a flask of something Greek and resinous, and we drank it and watched Pamphilus's wife hang washing in the last light and said nothing for a good while, which between Heras and me was most of the conversation. We had earned that. You can sit in silence with a man for thirty years or you can sit in it for one good evening; we'd done the one good evening so many times it added up to the thirty.

"The little wool-factor's girl," he said eventually. "With the stairs."

"You heard about that."

"Everyone heard about it. Crispus has been dining out on the impiety of it for a week. He has a phrase now. 'The barbarian's heaven by ladder.'" Heras tasted his wine. "It's not bad, as phrases go. He'll wear it out."

"He always does."

"Mm." He looked at the river. "You gave a child a hundred denarii for a drawing of a staircase. Macer is telling people it proves you've gone soft and he's keeping a closer eye on the accounts."

"Macer thinks crop rotation is a soft idea."

"Macer," said Heras, "is sixty-two and has buried two wives and made four fortunes and he is not, whatever else, a stupid man, and I want you to hear me when I say I'm about to repeat something stupid that a not-stupid man asked me." He turned the flask in his hands. He had the cleanest hands I had ever seen, still, age-spotted now, and they never quite stopped being a doctor's. "He asked me what you're for."

"For."

"What you are for. He's owned you, he's sold you, he's dined off you for twelve years, and he sat in his own house watching you crown a child's drawing of a staircase to heaven and he leaned over to me and asked, in earnest, with money on his mind, what the man is for. What is the thing under all the things." Heras drank. "I told him I didn't know. Which is the truest thing I've said all year and I've said a great many true things, I'm a doctor."

I should have made a joke. I had a joke. I felt it come up the way they do and I felt it be wrong and I let it go past.

"You've made the figures," Heras said, mild, looking at the washing and not at me, which was how he did it when it mattered. "I'll grant you the figures, the figures are clean and they're loose in the city and no power on earth puts them back in the box now, the fish-sellers have them. The boiling, the water, the washing of wounds, that's saving people, I've stood over the people it's saving, I'll grant you that and I'll thank you for it as long as I draw breath. Those are gifts. A man could give those and the gods would smile and Crispus could choke." He set the flask down. "And then there's the rest of it. The tube that bursts. The white salt that took two years and Geta's hand. The drawings of carts that carry the grain of Egypt with no men, which means no men working and no men eating, have you thought that through, or the ships with no oars, which a child draws now because you taught a city to draw them. You light a torch and you say it's for the ones not born yet. I'm a man who has watched a great many people die, Daniel, and I have learned to be suspicious of any gift addressed to people who can't yet say no to it."

The river went the rest of the way dark while he talked. I let it.

"You think it's pride," I said. Not a question.

"I think," Heras said carefully, "that I cannot tell the difference, from where I sit, between a man doing a duty no one asked him to do and a man who has decided he knows better than the whole world that made him, and I have known you twelve years and I cannot tell the difference, and I don't think you can either, and I think that's the most interesting thing about you and the most frightening, and I am too old to be frightened of much." He picked the flask back up. "Macer wants to know what you're for so he can price it. I want to know because I'm fond of you and I'd like to be sure, before I die, that I helped a man and not a fire."

I could have told him. That was the thing of it, sitting there in the dark with the one man alive who came closest. I could have said: I'm from a place where the staircase is built, all of it, up past the moon, and I saw it, and I fell off it into your stubble field, and every gift and every tube and every loose number is me trying to lay one more step under your people's feet because I cannot get home and the only thing worse than being stuck at the bottom of the staircase is being the only one who knows the staircase is there. I could have said it. He would not have believed the from-a-place part. He would have believed the rest, the part about the fire and the fear, because that part is true in any country.

"I'm not for anything," I said. "I'm a guy who got lucky with a map and never stopped running."

Heras looked at me for a long moment with those clean old eyes that had punctured my germs and my Thule and my every comfortable lie for twelve years, and he did not puncture this one, which was how I knew he'd heard the size of it.

"All right," he said, and drank, and gave it back to me. "A guy who got lucky."

He left when the wine was gone. He patted the bench twice when he stood, the wood not me, the way he did, and told me to drink less of his country's wine and more of my own boiled water, and went up the wharf into the torchlight, an old man walking carefully on stones, and I sat there with the question he'd left sitting in my lap like a cat that won't be moved.

What are you for. Hubris or duty. He hadn't said either word. He was too good to say either word; he'd just opened the door and let me stand in front of it, and I was still standing in front of it, and I have a feeling now, writing this, that I will be standing in front of it the day I die.

---
