I want to end this part of it on the yard, because the yard is the true thing and the rest is weather.

I stayed out after Heras left, until the washing was in and Pamphilus's wife had banked the kitchen fire and the only light was the watchman's brazier at the gate and the city's dirty glow over the rooftops, which was Rome the way Rome is at night, a million small fires pretending to be sleeping.

The high ground. I had it. A man who came into this world with a dead phone and a head full of half-remembered drawings now had a wife, a household, freed people who chose to stay, money he couldn't feel, the emperor's name laid over his own, and a city that drew his dreams in the dust without knowing they were dreams or whose. From here you could see a long way. I could see the press running tomorrow and the figures spreading and the books I was building in secret, the ladder, rung by rung, in a script only one freedwoman could read. I could see all of it laid out below me like the lamps of the wharf.

And the thing about high ground, the thing they teach you if you spend any time near soldiers, is that high ground is where they come for you. It is worth taking and it is worth holding and it is the most dangerous place on the field to be standing, because everyone below can see exactly where you are, and the higher you stand the more men decide they need you, and want you, and cannot afford to leave you to the other man. I was too useful now to be safe. I had made myself too valuable to a world that had already shown me, with Geta's hand and with a soldier I will not name falling a hundred feet in burning cloth, exactly what it did with the things it found useful.

I knew all that, leaning on my own well in the dark, the way you know a draft in a good room. Not a thing you can point to. A coolness on the back of the neck. The ground was too good. Ground that good does not last; somebody always wants it.

Stichus, who was a man now and not the boy who'd worked the coal-pot, came across the yard from the gate to ask if I was staying out, and whether to leave the brazier, and I told him to bank it and go to bed, and he did, and I stood a while longer.

Then I went in. I checked the board over the box without lifting it, just pressed my foot on it once in the dark to feel it sit true, the dead glass and the citizen's tablet under my own floor, the two countries that couldn't write to each other lying quiet under the house I'd built between them. Marcia was asleep, her breathing slow and certain, a freeborn child of ours already, she said, on the way. I lay down beside her on the high ground I'd spent twelve years climbing to, in the loudest, fullest, emptiest moment of my whole strange life, and listened to the city's small fires, and waited, the way you wait on high ground, for whatever was coming up the slope.

---

## PART FOUR — Too Valuable

---

# Chapter 25: Three Faces

The boy was eight days old when the senator's litter first stopped at my gate, and I have always held those two things in the same hand, because I learned that winter that a man comes for you most gently in the season when you have most to lose.

We had named him nothing yet. That is the Roman way and I had not fought it; you wait, you let the early days do their winnowing, and on the ninth day you give the name, before which a child is not quite a person you are allowed to have. Marcia ran the books from her bed with the boy at her breast and a tablet on her knee, and worked sums one-handed while he fed, and once, in the dark, I came in and found her looking at him with an expression I had no word for in either language, something between accountancy and terror. She caught me looking. "He eats and he sleeps and he does the other," she said. "A sound investment so far." Then she put her hand flat on his back the way you steady a cup you've set too near an edge, and did not move it for a long time.

So that was the house the litter stopped at.

---

It came with eight bearers, which I learned later was the precise number, the senatorial number, the number that says I could afford ten and have chosen restraint. The man who climbed out of it did not look like a knife. That was the first thing, and I should have known by then that nothing dangerous in Rome looks like the thing it is.

Publius Cornelius Scaeva was a tall old man gone soft in the way of men who had been hard once and let the hardness sink under good living without quite losing the frame of it. Fifty-seven, fifty-eight. A long face, deeply lined down from the nose, the lines of a man who smiles often and means it about a third of the time. His hair was iron and brushed forward in the old fashion, and he wore the broad purple stripe without the smallest fuss about it, the way Macer wore no stripe at all, two men telling you the same thing from opposite ends. His voice, when it came, was low and unhurried and beautifully placed, an orator's instrument kept oiled, and he used it the way a rich man uses a quiet room, certain it would be listened into.

"The Thulean," he said, and took both my hands in both of his, which a senator does not do, which is itself a gift, a deliberate spending of dignity. His hands were dry and warm. "I have wanted to stand in this yard for two years and have been too much a coward to come. A man hates to be one more face wanting something from the man everyone wants something from."

"You honor the yard," I said, which was the safe Latin, the toga-Latin I had drilled into myself, and he heard the drill in it and smiled wider, forgiving me it.

He would not sit in the tally-room. He wanted to see the press. He stood over Eros's shoulder while a sheet came off, the times-tables of all the dull things, and watched the gray digits bloom on the wet paper and said, softly, as if to himself, "There it is. There is the thing they don't understand." He turned to me. "They think you are about flying. Or fire. The Senate, when your name comes up, and it comes up, thinks fire." He touched the edge of the drying sheet with one finger, not smearing it, a man who knew where ink was. "But this is the engine. A boy in Lugdunum and a boy in Antioch can be made to think the same number on the same day. That is an army that needs no feeding. I have read your contest tales, all of them. My guests thought I'd gone simple." The smile again, the third-of-the-time one. "I had not."

I have been flattered by experts. Romans flatter the way they cook, richly and at length and with a clear sense of who is paying. This was not that. He had read the tales. He had found the one true thing in my whole strange enterprise, the figures, the quiet thing under the loud things, and named it back to me in my own yard, and I felt the warmth of being seen go up my spine before my head could catch it and call it what it was, which was a man showing me he had done his reading on me. The way you show a horse the bridle before you show it the bit.

We walked. He talked, and the talk was a country in itself, broad and pleasant and watered everywhere with names, this consul who was a cousin, that legate who owed him, and woven through it, never once landing, the soft repeating word: useful. How few men were useful. How a useful man in Rome was a coin every great house wanted in its purse, and how a coin in too many purses got worn thin and clipped, and how a wise man chose the one purse. He did not say be in my purse. He said it for an hour without saying it, and at the end of the hour I understood that I had been proposed to, and had not been able to find the sentence I could have used to say no, because there had been no sentence, only an hour of warm water you could not push back against any single part of.

At the gate he took my hands again. "Macer is a fine man," he said, the way you'd say a dog was a fine dog. "A contractor. He thinks in seasons, leather and grain, this year's army. I think in"-and here he let it hang, a craftsman's pause-"longer things. You think in longer things. I knew it from the staircase tale, the child's, the one your enemy calls impiety." He watched me when he said the word, lightly, to see it land. "We should be friends, you and I. Friends do small things for each other, and the small things make the large ones possible. I will start. Tell me a small thing you need."

I should have said nothing. A slave's no and a freedman's no are the same animal wearing different collars, I knew that, I had known it on a hill in Dacia watching a man burn for the lack of my no being a thing that happened. But I had a household now and a boy eight days unnamed and a yard full of people on my wages whose safety was the emperor's favor and nothing else, and a senator with eight bearers was at my gate offering to do me a service, and you do not refuse a great man's gift, because the refusal is the only message it carries and it is a loud one.

So I maneuvered, which is to say I lied a little with the truth. "There's a customs man at the river gate," I said, "who holds my sulfur three days every load and takes his bite each day. It costs me a denarius and a week's temper."

It was true and it was nothing and it was, I thought, cheap, the kind of favor that obligates a man only by a denarius. Scaeva's face did something I have turned over many times since. A flick of pleasure, quickly housed. I had not understood, standing there, that by naming the customs man I had told him I import sulfur, how much, and through which gate, and that I had a temper that could be reached through my purse. I had thought I was buying time with a coin. I had handed him a map of where my walls were thin and called it small talk.

"Consider it done," he said. "There. We are friends." He stepped up into the litter, and the curtain was half across when he leaned back out, easy, an afterthought that was not one. "The powder," he said. "When the steel comes. Hermes will solve the steel, your great men are pouring enough into his fire. And when there is a tube that holds, the man who has the tube and the powder together is going to be asked, by someone, to point it somewhere." His eyes were very kind. "Better it be a friend who does the asking. Macer would sell it to the highest bidder and not lose a night. I would use it well, and remember who gave it." The curtain came across. "Kiss your son for me. Choose him a strong name."

The bearers took the weight up onto their shoulders all together, a small grunt and a lurch, and the box of him went off down the wharf, and I stood at my own gate and felt the specific coolness of a particular man having decided he needed me, and meaning to have me, and being patient, and rich, and old in the trade of having people.

---

Crispus I met by accident, which is how Crispus liked best to meet me, so that it would look like the gods arranging his triumphs.

It was at the baths, the good ones, where I went now because a man of my standing was expected to be seen sweating among his betters and where I never once relaxed. He was in the warm room holding a small court, four or five younger men of letters who laughed where the laughs went, and I saw him see me and saw him decide, and there was no avoiding it without making the avoidance the story, so I came over and we did the dance.

He had aged into himself well; vanity keeps a man, I'll give it that. Fifty-two and oiled and lean, the gray at his temples arranged. He greeted me with enormous warmth, which is how you know with Crispus that the knives are out, and asked after my son with the tender solicitude of a man who would have been delighted to hear the child had died in the night.

"And the work," he said. "Always the work. They tell me Caesar's Thulean has a senator courting him now. Cornelius Scaeva. A great honor." He let the younger men murmur at the name. "Though one does wonder. Scaeva is a man of, shall we say, appetite for the field. The triumph he was not given. One imagines what he sees when he looks at your little tubes." He smiled at me, the warm knife. "But that is politics, and I am only a poor reader of old books. I concern myself with truer things."

Here was the change, and I felt it before I could name it, the way you feel a stair that isn't where your foot expected it. The old Crispus would have gone for the lie of Thule, for Strabo and Pytheas and the geography of my fraud, and we'd have fenced over coastlines and I'd have won the room by being charming about my own dubious north. He had spent years there. He had worn that ground bare.

He did not go there.

"The staircase," he said. "Your prize this year. To the stars." He said it to his young men, performing it for them, but his eyes stayed on me. "I was unkind about it in the moment, and a friend chid me, and I have reconsidered, and I find I was not unkind enough." A few of them laughed. He raised a hand, mock-modest. "No, hear me. A ladder of knowing, the child wrote, each son building a step. Charming. And what is at the top of the ladder?" He spread his hands at the warm wet air. "Heaven. The seats of the gods. The Thulean teaches Rome's children that a man may build himself to the gods' own doorstep, step by step, by his own cleverness, with no rite, no priest, no offering. The barbarian's heaven by ladder." The phrase landed soft and sure; he had thrown it a hundred times now and it flew straight. "I called it impiety as a jest. I find I meant it. There is an old word for a man who would scale the heavens by his own works and put no god at the foot of it. The Greeks had a long one. We have a shorter."

The warm room was very quiet under its dripping. One of the young men had stopped smiling and was watching me carefully, the way you watch a man to see how he takes a wound, which told me the wound had been aimed and the young men had been brought to witness it.

The thing Crispus had always lacked was teeth. He had wit and bile and the geography of my lie, and none of it could touch me while the emperor's name sat on mine, because a man of letters who proves the emperor's wonder a fraud has merely embarrassed the emperor, and emperors do not love men who embarrass them, and Crispus knew it and it had caged him for years. Impiety was a different animal. It was a charge, a thing with a courtroom at the end of it and priests who could be consulted and a crowd that could be turned, and it touched the gods, who outranked the emperor, who was careful with the gods and would not spend a denarius of his own favor shielding a foreigner the priestly colleges had decided was an offense to heaven. Crispus had stopped trying to prove I was a liar. He had found that he did not need me to be one. He only needed me to be a man who had taught a city to dream of climbing past the gods, which I had, on the page, in front of witnesses, and which was true.

"It's a child's drawing of a staircase, Crispus," I said, and made it light, and made it land, because the room was still mine if I was fast enough. "Her torch was bigger than her head. If the gods are troubled by a wool-factor's daughter, they're more nervous than I was raised to think them." A laugh, a small one, mine for the moment. "Where I come from we build steps because the gods put the mountain there. A man climbing toward heaven praises the one who made the mountain worth climbing. Call it the longest prayer there is."

It was good. I had gotten good. He saw it work the room and let it, gracious in the small loss, because he was no longer playing for the room. He had said the word in front of witnesses and watched me answer it, watched me admit in the answering that I knew exactly what charge I was answering.

"A prayer," he said, pleased, tasting it. "I shall use that. The Thulean calls his ladder to heaven a prayer." He pressed my shoulder in parting, warm and dry and brief. "We must dine. You are far better company now that you understand what we are talking about." And he went off to the cold water, and I sat in the heat I no longer felt, having won the room and lost the afternoon, because he had what he had come for, which was me on record knowing the word.

---

I did not seek Vibenius. You do not seek a haruspex; that itself is a thing the colleges remark on, a man going to the priests with a question on his face. He came to me, and he came on the worst possible afternoon, and whether that was chance I could never decide, the same way I could never decide anything about that old man except that he saw further than I did and let me know it without once raising his voice.

It was the day before the naming. The house was full of the small frenzy of it, Marcia's women and the offerings to lay out and the bulla to hang round the boy's neck, the little gold case with the charm in it against exactly the kind of attention I had been collecting all winter. I had stepped into the yard to be away from it for a breath, and the watchman came to say a man was asking for me, an old man, no litter, on foot, and would give no name but said I would know the face from a ceremony in the year the present Caesar walked into Rome.

I knew the face. I had carried it eleven years. He had locked eyes with me across the crowd the year I first saw the city, as if he already had a question filed against my name, and he had stood at the bottom of the Subura lot the day my balloon burned in the street and called it an omen with two edges, and he had sat silent at Mettius's salon weighing me while Crispus circled. Each time he had looked and not spoken. Now he was at my gate, on foot, an old priest of sixty-seven with his face folded down into itself like a thing kept too long in a drawer, and the timing of it, the day before I named my freeborn son, sat in my chest like a swallowed stone.

He would not come in past the gate. He stood just inside it where the watchman's brazier could reach him, and he looked at the yard, the press-shed, the well, the washing line, Stichus crossing with a load, the whole small kingdom, for a long time, the way a man reads a liver, finding the lobes of it, the marks.

"You have a son tomorrow," he said. No question.

"Tomorrow, gods willing."

"Gods willing." He turned the phrase over as if checking it for false coin. His Latin was old and slow and very clear. "Men say that the way they say the weather. You said it as if you had thought about whether they were willing." He looked at me then, full on, the first time we had ever stood close enough for me to see that one of his eyes had gone milk at the edge. "Most men do not wonder whether the gods will a thing. They assume it, or they fear it, but they do not wonder. You wonder. I have watched you wonder for eleven years, from the day the fire went up over the Subura and the gods let it rise and then put it back down on the roofs. I told them then I did not know what it meant. I am old, and I have read a great many livers, and I will tell you the secret of my craft, which is that most days I do not know what it means either, and the art is in knowing which days that is allowed and which days it is not."

I said nothing. I had learned, with Heras, what silence buys you, and Vibenius was an older and deeper silence than Heras and I held still in front of it.

"A man came to the college last month," he said, "of standing, with a complaint dressed as a question. About a foreigner who teaches Roman children to climb to heaven by works, and whether the gods are mocked by it." He let that sit. He was telling me it had reached the colleges, which I had guessed, and that he was the one it reached, which I had not. "We have not answered him. We answer slowly, and we wait to see which way the great houses lean before we lean." He looked at the brazier. "Scaeva's house leans toward you this winter. That is known. A man wonders why a senator with old battles still in him leans toward a maker of fire. Your enemy with the phrases wonders it loudest of all, because if your protector is a man hungry for war, then the ladder to heaven is also a ladder to the field, and impiety becomes a thing with an army behind it, and that is no longer a question for slow priests. That is a question for the courts."

He had laid it out flat, all three of them, in four sentences, the way a man who reads a liver lays the marks out and lets you see they make a shape. Scaeva's hand on my shoulder, Crispus's word in the colleges, the colleges waiting on Scaeva, and somewhere at the end of the slope a courtroom. He had seen the whole field from the gate, in an afternoon, what it had taken me a winter to half-feel as a coolness on my neck.

"And you," I said, because I had to say something and that was the only thing in the field I did not understand. "Where does your house lean."

Something moved in the folded face that might once, fifty years before, have been a smile. "My house does not lean. That is the only power an old priest has left, that he can decline to lean while the great houses fall over each other. The day I lean is the day I am no longer worth consulting." He drew his cloak closer; the wind off the river had teeth that evening. "I came to look at you before you have a son, because a man with a son is a different reading than a man without one. I wished to see whether you knew what you were standing in. I see that you are beginning to. That is enough for one visit."

He turned to go, and then did the thing that bound us, the only gift and the only debt he ever gave me in one motion. He stopped at the gate, half out of the brazier's light, and said, not looking back, "When they ask me whether the gods favor the fire you bring or are offended by it, I shall have to say something. I have not decided what. I have been not deciding it for eleven years, which is a luxury, and luxuries end." He paused. "It would help me, when the day comes, to know that the man who brings the fire knew it was a fire and brought it anyway, for a reason he could say to a god's face. Have that reason ready. I may come and ask you for it on a day not of your choosing, and what I do with the answer will not be in your hands. Name your son well."

And he went out through the gate on foot, an old man on the river stones in the cold, no litter, no name given, leaving me holding a debt with no figure on it, a thing I could not pay down by a denarius the way I had thought to pay Scaeva, a question filed against my name eleven years ago and not yet answered, and now, by his own telling, soon to be called.

---

I went back into the house, where the bulla lay out on a cloth, the little gold case, gold because we could afford gold now, with the charm sealed inside it against the evil that comes for a child the world has noticed. Marcia had the boy.

I picked up the bulla and held it, the small weight of it, and thought about the three men who had come to my gate that winter, each offering to do small things for me and asking only that I let them decide where the fire pointed. The senator who saw the engine and wanted the tube. The man of letters who had stopped trying to prove me false and started, far more dangerously, agreeing I was true. The priest who would not lean, and had told me in the kindest voice I'd heard all year that he was the one who would decide in the end whether my son's father climbed to a god's doorstep as a prayer or hung from a charge as an offense, and that he had not decided, and that the deciding would not wait on my convenience.

Marcia watched me holding the charm. "You're going to drop it," she said. "It's gold."

I gave it to her. She fitted it round the boy's neck to see it sit, took it off again, set it back on the cloth for the morning, all without setting down her tablet, and the boy made the small blind sounds a thing makes when it does not yet know what is in the room with it, and I stood in the warm lamplight of the house I had built on the high ground and listened, under the women's voices, for feet on the slope.

---

# Chapter 26: The Forum

On the ninth day we named him Lucanus, and the house breathed out.

I had wanted to give him something of my own and could not. There is no Latin for the name I was born with that does not sound like a man clearing his throat, and in any case the name is the law's, not the father's. So he became Marcus Ulpius Lucanus, the praenomen mine, the nomen the emperor's, the cognomen a soft Roman word a child could grow into without it ever once weighing on him the way Thulean weighed on me. Marcia chose Lucanus. She said it sat well in the mouth of a man calling an account, which was how she chose most things. The priest-of-the-house rites I let happen around me, the salt and the purified water and the small bird, and I held my son when they put him in my hands, freeborn, citizen at his first breath, able by the bare fact of his birth to stand for offices and wear stripes that I, who had taught his city to count, would die a freedman barred from. I had built him a higher floor than I would ever stand on. That should have been the whole of what I felt. What I felt, holding him while the bulla went round his neck against the evil that comes for a noticed child, was the cold arithmetic that I had now given the slope below my house one more thing to roll downhill at.

He gripped my finger. They do that; it means nothing, it is a reflex left over from clinging to fur. I knew it meant nothing and let it mean something anyway, because there was no one I could say it to.

---

The Forum opened to Rome in the high summer, and the city went out of its mind in the particular Roman way, which is orderly and enormous at once.

I had seen Trajan's men widening the cut behind the old fora for years, the whole shoulder of the Quirinal eaten back, the spoil hauled south past my own wharf in carts I had sometimes counted out of habit. I knew the project the way you know a noise you have stopped hearing. Then they took the hoardings down, and the noise had been a building, and the building was a thing I did not have the right words for in either of my languages.

You came in under an arch and the space simply refused to end. A court paved in colored stone, wide enough to drown the Subura in, ringed in columns the color of old honey and gray storm, and at the far end the basilica with its roof of gilt bronze throwing the sun back so hard you put a hand up without deciding to. Statues stood in the upper register looking down, captured Dacians in their trousers and soft caps, carved over and over, a stone army of the defeated holding up the architrave with their patience. In the center the emperor rode a bronze horse bigger than any horse, and the horse's raised hoof hung over the heads of the crowd as if it had been stopped in the instant before it came down, and would come down, in the carver's intention, on us.

I stood in it and was, for a moment, the seventeen-year-old in the stubble field again, doing his sums to keep from screaming. Six hundred feet, I thought. More. The columns are forty if they're a foot. I did the arithmetic the way I had done the Colosseum's, and it steadied me, and then it stopped steadying me, because the number I arrived at was simply the truth, and the truth was that these people had done this on purpose, with ramps and ropes and ten thousand hands, in a few years, while I was failing to make a clock keep time.

I was not anonymous in that crowd. That was the new thing about my life that I never got used to. Men I half-knew turned and named me to men I did not know, the Thulean, Caesar's Thulean, the one with the flying bag, the one with the figures, and I learned again that I had become a kind of monument myself, a thing pointed at, smaller than the bronze horse and in the same business. A perfumer's wife wanted me to tell her whether the moon was truly a country with mountains; her husband had bought one of Demetrios's miserable spyglasses and seen nothing through it but colored fog, and she suspected, correctly, that she had been cheated, and was too well-bred to say so to a man Caesar favored. I told her the fault was in our glass and not in the moon, which was true and pleased no one. Scaeva's people found me twice in the press of bodies, not Scaeva himself, only a freedman of his who put a hand at my elbow and asked after my health and my son's and let me feel, through the warm words, the length of the leash. I had a customs man unmolested at the river gate now. I had paid for that.

---

The column they had not finished, and the column was the thing that undid me.

It stood behind the basilica in its own small court between two libraries, and it was already up, white as a peeled bone, banded top to bottom in a carved ribbon that wound round and round like the rind cut from an apple in one unbroken strip. Men on a scaffold were still working the lower turns with the libraries' colonnades half-hiding them, fine little chisels going at the stone, and the stone was the war. My war. Both of them.

You read it from the bottom and the band climbed. There was the river with its god leaning out of the bank to watch the legions cross, the boats lashed into a bridge, the wall of shields. There were the camps going up, the men cutting turf, hauling timber, the orderly violence of Roman work that I had marched inside of and learned to fear more than any battle. Higher up, where I had to crane and could only half-see past the scaffold, the war went on, the burning of villages, a Dacian woman with her hands up, the king at the last with the cavalry closing, the head being carried off on a tray.

It was all true and none of it was what happened.

I stood under it a long time. Somewhere in those turns was the Loaf, the hill with the Dacians massed behind it that a column was to advance past in three days, and somewhere in that white unbroken ribbon there was no man falling a hundred feet inside burning cloth because the wind off the river took the basket the way I had said it would, and was overruled, because the no of a slave is a sound and not an event. The carver had not put him there. There was no place for him in the rind of the apple. The army had recorded him as a volunteer and it had not been a lie, and the column recorded nothing of him at all, and both were true, and standing under it I understood for the first time what the difference was between a thing that happened and a thing that is remembered, and that the second is made by hands holding chisels, on purpose, for money, by men told what to leave out. The river god leaned from the bank, kind and enormous, watching the legions cross. He had watched a great many things from that bank and the stone had been told to show him watching this.

I do not know how long I would have stood there. A voice behind me said, in fast Greek-cornered Latin, "It's wrong, of course. The bridge. They've drawn it too narrow and left out a whole course of piling so the boats would fit the band. Tell a story long enough and someone shortens it for the wall."

---

Apollodorus had not changed in the way the truly proud do not, which is to say the world had gone on arranging itself around him and confirmed him in everything. He was past sixty now and built like a thing that had been left out in weather for a long time and not weakened by it, the gray gone white, the hands worse than I remembered, two knuckles on the right thickened wrong from some old break he had never let set. He had the emperor's whole Forum behind him as a thing he had made, and he wore it lightly, the way Macer wore no stripe, certain you would notice.

"It's still standing," I said, of the bridge. "The real one. I came back over it."

"I know you did. I built it to stand. That up there" - he tipped his white head at the column without looking, a man dismissing a likeness of his own work - "is a poem about it. They asked me to approve the marching order of the reliefs and I told them I am an engineer and not a man who arranges processions, and they were offended, and I let them be." A short hard sound that on another man would have been a laugh. "You're looking at it like it bit you."

"I served on it," I said. "Both of them."

"So did I. Mine's the part that holds water." He came and stood by me and looked up at the band with me, and for a moment we were two men reading the same lie and knowing different halves of it, which is as close to friendship as we ever came, and was not friendship. "Your bag is up there. Did you see? Two turns above the scaffold, a little round thing over the camp with strings on it. The carver didn't know what it was. He'd been told a Thulean device watched from the air, and he made a fishing-float with a man drawn small beneath it, looking up." He found it for me with a blunt finger, traced the air. "They asked me what it was meant to be. I told them a clever toy that does one thing."

He had said that to me on the Danube eleven years before, of the same toy, before it killed a man he never bothered to learn the name of. He said it now without malice and without memory of having said it, because to Apollodorus my balloon had always been the one true sentence and he had no reason to vary it.

"It does one thing," I said. "You build the one thing that holds water and I build the one thing that floats on air. We're the same trade, you just got the better element."

This pleased him. Vanity is the door into a proud man and I had learned where his hinges were. "Water is the only honest element," he said. "It finds its level and it tells no lies and if your work is wrong it shows you on the first day, not the tenth. Fire flatters you." He looked at me sidelong, the old appraisal, the one that had never once warmed past wary. "You're rich now. They tell me you're rich and courted and a senator keeps your name in his mouth. You've gotten fat in the way that matters, which is the standing-still way. When did you last fail at something with your own hands?"

It landed because it was fair. "A clock," I said. "I cannot make a clock keep true time. The thing that lets the wheel go tooth by tooth instead of running down all at once. I had it in my head and it's gone." I had never said this aloud to anyone but Hermes, who had only grunted, and Tyche, who had written it down in a script no one else could read. To say it to Apollodorus was to hand a blade to a man I knew would take it.

He took it. "Then you've reached the bottom of the well you brought with you," he said, simply, not unkindly, which made it worse. "Every man does. I reached mine years ago and have been getting clever about the same depth of water ever since. The difference is I know how deep my well goes. You keep dropping the bucket as if you'll be surprised." He turned from the column. "Build the things you can hold in your hands until the day you die and let the rest go. The men who chase the thing they can almost remember go mad, or get poor, or get killed for promising it."

---

There was a stir at the basilica end, the particular ripple a crowd makes when men of weight are moving through it, and Apollodorus's face did a thing I had not seen it do, which was close, like a hand making a fist out of itself.

A knot of men came along the colonnade, the kind of knot the Forum was full of that day, the great walking and being seen to walk. I knew few of them by sight. One I half-knew, a Spaniard of the emperor's own blood and circle, somewhere near forty, full-bearded in the Greek manner when no Roman of standing wore a beard, which told you before he opened his mouth that he intended you to find him learned. He had a draughtsman's roll under his arm in a crowd where no one carried work, and he came toward the column the way a man comes toward a thing he means to have opinions about, and he stopped, and he looked up at it, and then he looked at the libraries flanking it, and he said something to the man beside him about the proportion of the colonnade, gesturing, sketching a different cornice in the air with two fingers.

Apollodorus watched him do it the way you watch a child rearrange tools on a bench you have spent forty years learning.

"He designs," Apollodorus said to me, low, not low enough. "He draws gourds. Domes like cut melons, one swallowing another, lines no stone wants to make. He showed Caesar a temple once with the roof in segments like a squash and was very proud of it. I told him to go and paint his gourds, and leave temples to men who know what holds a thing up." The contempt in it was total and serene, the contempt of a man who has never once been wrong about water. "He has not forgiven me. Men like that keep a ledger. They put a small mark in it and carry it for thirty years and one day they are in a position to settle, and they settle the whole column at once."

The bearded man's eye traveled the crowd as men of that rank let their eyes travel, owning what they crossed, and found Apollodorus, and there was a held instant where the two of them looked at each other across the white shadow of the column, and the bearded man inclined his head a precise small distance, courteous, cold, the bow you give a man whose work you mean someday to improve upon, and Apollodorus gave him back nothing at all, not a nod, not a word, only the flat blank stare he gave a course of piling that had been laid wrong. Then the knot moved on toward the libraries and the moment closed and the bearded man laughed at something one of his circle said, easily, a man entirely at home in the world.

I felt the cold of it go up my back. I had stood in enough rooms by then to know the shape of a powerful man being despised by a prouder one in front of witnesses, and to know which of the two had the more to lose, and it was not the one with the power. Apollodorus had the truth about water and the certainty that the truth was enough, and the bearded man had the long memory and the ledger and, one day, time. I had watched a man I respected spit, gently, into a well he might one day have to drink from, and do it with the clean conscience of a man who has never imagined the well belonging to anyone but himself.

"You shouldn't have said that loud enough for him to hear," I said.

"I have been saying true things loud enough to be heard for sixty years," Apollodorus said, "and it has built me everything you see." He looked up at his Forum, at his column wrapped in its shortened poem, at the gilt roof flinging the sun, and there was no crack in him anywhere, no place the doubt could get a finger in. "A man who softens his true things to suit the listener is building on sand. You of all men should know what water does to sand."

---

He went off to inspect something about the basilica drains, which were his and which delighted him, and I stood a while longer under the column in the wheeling noise of the dedication, a rich and courted man among marvels, named and pointed at, with a senator's hand at my elbow through a freedman and a senator's knowledge of where my walls were thin, with a word in the priestly colleges that had a courtroom at the end of its slope and an old priest somewhere in the city not deciding what he would say about my fire, and with a son nine days old at home who could rise as high as Rome went and was, for exactly that reason, the largest stone I had yet set rolling.

The sun came off the bronze roof and I put my hand up. All around me Rome looked up at the white ribbon of the war and read the story it had been given, the crossing, the king run down, the head on the tray, the river god watching kindly from the bank, and none of them looked for the man who was not carved there because there had never been a place for him in the rind of the apple, and I thought that this, more than the figures or the press or the powder, was the real engine of the world, the chisel that decides what the future will be allowed to remember, and that I did not own it, and that the men who did were even now walking the colonnade ahead of me, settling their ledgers, deciding which fires were prayers and which were offenses, and that all my cleverness had bought me was a higher place to stand while they decided.

I went home along the wharf in the cooling evening. The river had the same teeth in its wind it had carried all that winter, and I drew my cloak the way I had watched an old priest draw his, and let myself in at my own gate, and the boy was awake and making his small blind sounds at a ceiling he did not yet know he could one day be carved on. Marcia had the tally open. "Well?" she said, not looking up. "Is it as big as they say."

"Bigger," I said. "And mostly true."

She made a mark and turned the tablet over. "Then it'll do," she said, which from Marcia was a benediction, and I sat down across from her in the lamplight to count the day's loads, and outside on the slope the river went on telling the wharf the same flat thing it told every stone it passed, which was nothing, which was that it had found its level and would keep it, and did not care in the least whose walls were thin.

---

# Chapter 27: The Accusation

Vibenius came for the reason on a market day in early spring, which is to say he chose a morning when the wharf was loud enough that no one would notice an old man walking through it, and quiet enough at my gate that I would have no one to hide behind.

I was at the bench with a bad casting of Hermes's in front of me, a hinge that had cooled with a flaw down the middle of it, and I was turning it over and feeling sorry for the iron when Stichus came and said the priest was here. Not the priest's name. The priest. The whole yard had learned that word over the winter without my teaching it to them, the way a household learns the word for the disease that is in the next street.

He stood inside the gate in the same place he had stood before, in the reach of the watchman's brazier though the morning did not need it, and he did not look at the yard this time. He looked at me, and he said, "Tomorrow."

"Tomorrow," I said, as if I knew what was tomorrow.

"They convene tomorrow. In the house of Manius Aemilius Scaurus, who has lent it because his is a name that leans nowhere and so can hold a thing that has not yet decided which way to fall. There will be three of the college, and there will be your accuser, and there will be such of the great as care to come and watch a foreigner answer for the gods." He said all this in his flat old Latin the way a man tells you the tide. "It is not a court. You will want it to be a court, because in a court there are rules and a man may learn the rules. It is a room. Bring nothing you cannot carry in your two hands and your mouth. And the reason." He paused. "You have it ready."

I had not had it ready. I had spent the winter not having it ready, the way you do not pack for a death you have been told is coming, because the packing makes it true. "I have it," I said.

"Say it to me now."

And there it was, the thing I had dreaded more than the room, that he would make me try it on him first, the man who read livers, the man whose whole art was knowing on which days he was allowed not to know what a thing meant. I opened my mouth and I had nothing in it. A winter of nothing.

"Tomorrow," I said, "you'll hear it tomorrow."

He looked at me a long moment with the milk-edged eye and the clear one, and I could not tell, then or ever, whether what crossed the folded face was contempt or patience or simply the next thought. "No," he said. "Tomorrow I hear what you say to the room. The room wants a performance and you will give it one and it will mean nothing to me, because I have watched men perform for forty years and the gods are not in it. I want the other thing. The thing you would say if you believed the door behind the room opened onto the actual seat of an actual god and he was leaning forward to hear you. Say that to me. Now. Or do not, and I will go to the house tomorrow with nothing of yours in my hand, and I will read whatever I read, and you will take your chances with three priests and a senator and a man who hates you, on the merits, as a foreigner and a freedman, which is to say with no chances at all."

So I gave it to him. I will set down what I said, because it cost me, and because a man should keep an account of the prices he has paid, even the ones he paid in counterfeit.

---

"The knowing isn't mine," I said.

He waited.

"That's the reason. The whole of it. A man can't steal what was never his to begin with, and he can't climb to a god's house on a ladder the god didn't first set down for him to find. Where I'm from we have a way of saying it. That the mind is a gift, and the things the mind builds are the gift opening its hand. I didn't make the air hold up a hot bag. I found that it does, because someone made it do that before there was anyone to find it. I didn't make the figures count true. They counted true before I was born and will after I'm dead. Every rung I've shown this city was already there in the world, set down, waiting, and I only had the eyes to see it was a rung and not a stone. A man who builds a staircase out of what the gods left lying isn't reaching past them. He's saying thank you in the only language that doesn't lie, which is work. The fire I bring is theirs. I'm carrying it back to them and asking them to take the offering."

I ran out. It had come out of me in a rush, half of it lines I'd turned over at night and half invented with his eyes on me, and the worst part was that it was good. It would work in the room. It had the shape of the thing they wanted, the god put back at the foot of the ladder, the cleverness made into reverence, and not one word of it did I believe. I do not believe the air was made for me to find. I believe the air is air. I had taken the truest thing I owned, that there is a real world and it can be read by anyone patient enough to look, the one thing I had carried two thousand years still holding, and I had dressed it in robes and handed it to a religion I think is a beautiful empty house, and called the dressing-up piety, because the alternative had a courtroom at the end of its slope.

Vibenius did not tell me whether it was good. He did not tell me whether it was true, or whether he believed I believed it, or whether any of that was a thing he weighed at all. He stood and let it settle in him the way he let a liver settle, and then he said, "It will do for the room." Nothing else. Not it will do for me. Not the gods are content. He drew his cloak and turned and the milk eye caught the brazier light and then he was gone out the gate on foot, and I stood holding a sentence I had built to save my life and could not say to my own face in a mirror.

---

The house of Aemilius Scaurus was on the Caelian, the kind of old house that has stopped trying to look like money because it has been money so long it has forgotten the want of it. They put me in an anteroom first, which I understood was so that everyone who mattered could arrive and settle and be a wall I would walk in to face rather than men I would meet as they came. Marcia had wanted to come. I had said no, and meant it for once. She had spent the night before with Tyche instead, going through every name we knew would be in the room, their debts and feuds and wives, building me a map of the field the way she built a map of the wharf, and at the end of it she set down the stylus and said, "It's not the kind of figures I can add," which from Marcia was as near to fear as I had heard her come.

When they brought me in, the room had the great in it.

I will not pretend I read it well. I read it the way a man reads a language he learned late and under threat, getting the nouns and missing the music. There was a long room with the spring light coming in high and cold, and a sort of arc of chairs, and in the chairs the men of the priestly college, three of them, in their bordered togas with the apex caps that make even a fool look like a judgment. Vibenius was the rightmost of the three and he did not look at me. Behind and around, on benches and standing, the watchers, a dozen, twenty, men I half-knew and men I had never seen, here because a foreigner answering for heaven is the best free entertainment a Roman of standing can buy on a spring morning. I found Macer among them with my eyes, fast, the way your tongue finds the sore tooth. He was near the back, in his good wool with no stripe, and he gave me nothing. Not a nod. He looked at me the way he looked at a load coming off a ship he had not yet decided to buy.

And Crispus stood to speak.

He had dressed for it, simply, which was clever, the man of letters with no need of purple, and he began the way the good ones begin, with the gods and not with me. He spoke of the rites his fathers had kept, the colleges that had carried Rome since before there were marble temples, and how a city is a contract with heaven, paid each year in blood and grain and the right words in the right order, and how the whole of Rome's fortune rested on that contract being kept by men who knew they were below the gods and grateful to be.

Then he turned to me, and he was kind, which is how I knew the knives were out.

"I bear this man no malice," he said, and the room understood, as it was meant to, that he bore me a great deal. "I have admired his cleverness. We all have. The flying bag, the figures the shopkeepers love, the water you may drink without dying, the tube that throws a stone across the river. Cleverness. Caesar himself has been pleased to be amused by it, and who am I to weigh my judgment against Caesar's." A small bow toward the absent emperor, a fence neatly stepped over. "But the gods are not amused by cleverness. The gods made the world and gave it to us in trust, ordered, each thing in its place, the farmer below the soldier and the soldier below the magistrate and all of them below heaven, and a man's first piety is to keep his place in the order and teach his children to keep theirs."

He let that sit. He had a way of letting a thing sit that was itself an argument.

"This man," he said, "teaches Rome's children to leave their place. I do not accuse him of the flying bag, which is a toy, or the figures, which are a convenience, or even the water, which I am told saves lives, and I am glad of saved lives. I accuse him of the dream he sells them at his yearly festival, on Minerva's own day, under the goddess of true and honest craft, in printed sheets a child buys for an as and learns by heart. He gives them prizes, with his own coin and Caesar's favor and a great house's name on it, for stories. And the stories all say one thing. That there is a ladder. That a man may climb it, rung by rung, by his own works, his sons after him, higher and higher, the cart that needs no horse, the bag that holds an army aloft, the glass that shows the creatures too small for the eye, and at the top of the ladder, the child wrote it herself, the child he crowned, at the top of the ladder is heaven. The seats of the gods. To be reached by works. By cleverness. With no rite at the foot of it and no priest on the road and no offering at the door."

A movement in the room, the small shift of men who have been handed the shape of a thing.

"He calls it a prayer." Crispus had not forgotten my line at the baths. He had been saving it. "He told me so himself, that his ladder to heaven is the longest prayer there is. A prayer is a man on his knees with his hands open, asking. This is a man on a staircase with a hammer, taking. And in the matter of the invisible creatures he says the gods send into the body, which he teaches the physicians to fight with boiling and washing as if a man might hold off the will of heaven with a clean rag, I ask whether this is the reverence of a man who knows his place, or the impiety of a man who has taught a whole city to dream of standing where the gods stand, and made them love him for it. The Greeks had a long word. We have a short one, and a knife to go with it. I do not ask for the knife. I ask only that the gods be consulted, through their proper readers, as to whether they are mocked. If they are content, I will be the first to embrace him. If they are not, then no favor of any living man can stand between him and what is owed."

He sat. It was, I have to say it, a fine piece of work. He had built me a gallows out of true planks. Every board of it was a thing I had actually done. The contest was mine. The ladder was mine. The invisible creatures were mine, and the clean rag against the will of heaven, that one I felt go in like a splinter, because it was the realest thing he said and I had been saying it for fifteen years.

---

Then it was mine to answer, and I want to be honest about how it went, because in the years since I have heard the story told back to me as a triumph, the Thulean who out-talked the priests, and it was not that. It was a man drowning who happened to come up near a rope.

I had a speech. Marcia and I had built one. It went out of my head the moment I opened my mouth, the way they do, and I stood in that cold high room with the great watching and I heard my own accent thick in my ears, the v gone to w, a foreigner's mouth, and I knew before I'd said six words that I could not win this on words. Crispus had Latin in his bones from the nursery. I had it from terror and drill. He could make the room laugh and weep in the same sentence. I could ask for bread and be understood most days. To stand against him with rhetoric was to bring a wax tablet to the cannon.

So I did the only thing I have ever been any good at, which is the thing in front of me. I did not answer the ladder. I answered the rag.

"The man is right that I teach the physicians to wash," I said. "I'll not pretend I don't. I taught it because children were dying of a thing that gets into a cut, and the washing makes fewer of them die, and I have watched it make fewer of them die with my own eyes, in this city, in the fever sheds by the river where your honors do not go." My voice was shaking and I let it shake; it was the truest thing in the room and I had nothing else. "He says it's impiety to fight the will of heaven with a clean rag. I'd ask him this. When a man's house is afire, and he carries water to it, is he fighting the will of the gods, or is he using the water the gods put in the well? When the flux is in a camp and a tribune boils the water and loses ten men instead of a hundred, has he insulted the gods, or has he buried ninety fewer sons?" I had found the floor under me now, the only floor I had. "I never told anyone the creatures aren't sent. I've always said they're sent. I say the gods send them, and I say the gods also showed us the boiling and the clean rag, the way a father who sets a hard task leaves the tool beside it. To use the tool isn't to spit on the father. It's to say I see what you left me, and I thank you, and I'll not let your gift sit in the dirt while children die for the want of me bending down to pick it up."

It was the reason. I had not meant to use Vibenius's reason in the room, I had meant to save it for him alone, but I was drowning and it was the nearest plank and I grabbed it and it held. I heard it hold. The room went the particular still that a room goes when a thing has been said that the listeners would like to be able to disagree with and find they cannot. I had given them the gods at the foot of the ladder. I had said thank you in the language that doesn't lie. And I had done it about dead children in the fever sheds, which is a thing no Roman of standing wants to be heard arguing the other side of.

Crispus rose at once to take it back. "Charmingly put," he began, "but the question is not the rag, the question is the ladder, the question is whether-"

And Scaeva, who had not yet spoken, who had been sitting in the best of the watchers' seats in his broad purple with his long lined face arranged into pleasant attention, said, into the gap before Crispus could close it, in that low oiled voice that filled the room without seeming to rise, "The question is whether Rome is better with this man's fire or without it. And I have served on three frontiers and buried more of my own men to bad water than to the Dacians, and I find I am not minded to call it impiety that a freedman of Caesar's house has handed the legions a way to keep their bowels and bring their children home. Minerva is the goddess of craft. I had always understood that craft pleased her."

He did not stand. A senator of his years does not stand for a freedman. He simply spoke from his seat, and the speaking from his seat was itself the message, that he was so much larger than the matter that he need not rise to settle it, and the room read it, all of them, faster than I did. The great houses had been waiting to see which way the great houses leaned, and one of the great houses had just leaned, openly, on a spring morning, in front of witnesses, with the emperor's name and his own purple behind it, and the lean of one such man at the right instant is worth more than an hour of any foreigner's drowning eloquence.

I watched Macer at the back while it happened. I could not help it. He had not moved through the whole of Crispus's gallows-building, and he did not move now, except that something in his face eased, the ease of a man who sees that the thing is going to be decided by someone else and that he will not, after all, have to spend himself on it. He had brought me into Rome on a gamble of mules. He had made his fortune turn on me. And in that room, with a charge over me that could have ended in a knife and a crowd taught a lesson, he had hung back at the wall and let a richer man take the weight, and kept his own safety dry, and I do not think he ever once considered doing otherwise, because Macer thinks in seasons, and a season is a thing you survive. I felt the cold of it more than I felt the relief, and filed it where I filed the rest of what that old man was, next to the day he first called me a useful mule and meant it kindly.

---

The college conferred. That is to say the three of them put their heads together and murmured, and Vibenius's was the head the other two leaned toward, the old one, the one who would not lean, leaned to by the two who would. And then they did not give a verdict in words, because that is not how it is done, the gods do not file opinions. They called for a sacrifice.

It would be public. It would be at the Quinquatrus, Minerva's own festival, the same days I held my foolish contest, and that was Vibenius's hand in it, I am sure of that much and nothing more, the neatness of it, the way he folded my offense and my expiation into the same five days of the same goddess so that the city would see the wound and the healing in one motion and remember only the healing. A white heifer to Minerva, paid for out of my purse, led by my own hand to the altar before the temple on the Aventine. And the dedication. That was the thing with teeth. I would dedicate the work, the contest and the press and the figures and the whole enterprise of it, formally, with the college's words, to Minerva, goddess of craft, so that all of it stood henceforth under her protection and within her rite, which meant within theirs.

I knelt on the Aventine steps eight days later in front of more of Rome than I had ever stood before, more than the demo, more than the cannon, the crowd packed down the slope and up onto the rooftops, and held the rope of a white heifer with gilded horns while a priest of Minerva's college made the offering and Vibenius stood by in his apex cap and watched. They put the wine on the beast's head and the meal between its horns, and the man with the hammer stunned it and the man with the knife opened its throat, and the blood came down the stone runnel cut there for exactly this, ancient and practical, a gutter for god's portion, and the smell of it went up with the incense, copper and smoke and sweet resin. A younger haruspex took the liver out steaming and read it and held it up and pronounced it sound, full, the lobes all present, the gods content. The crowd made the sound a crowd makes. And then the words were said over my work, the press and the figures and the ladder, all of it taken into the keeping of the goddess of true craft, consecrated, made Roman, made theirs.

I knelt through it. I made the responses I had been taught, the right words in the right order, the contract paid. I bowed to the smoking liver of an animal I had bought, in the name of a goddess I do not think is there, in front of a city that loved me for it, and somewhere in the crowd I knew Crispus was watching the gods declare, in blood, in public, on the goddess's own day, for the barbarian, which was the worst thing that could have happened to him and the thing I had handed him to ensure it never happened to me. I had taken the secret center of my whole life, the ladder, the real true ladder I am building in a script Tyche keeps under a floorboard, the rungs for whoever comes after, and I had let them bury it alive under incense and a knife and call it piety, and I knelt there and let them, because a man kneels, in the end, to whatever is holding the knife.

The blood ran down the runnel. The crowd loved me. I knelt in the middle of all of them with a clean rag of a soul and was about as alone as a man gets to be.

---

It bought my life and it cost me three things, and I knew the price of all three before the heifer had stopped twitching.

The colleges had their hook in now, in the press and the contest both, the right to be consulted, the rite said over every Quinquatrus by men in apex caps before the winners could be crowned. The thing I had built to teach a city to dream past its priests now belonged, on the books of heaven, to its priests, and they would never let go of it. I had handed them the leash to the one device of mine that mattered and they did not even know what they were holding.

Scaeva had me. The lean had been seen. Every man in that room and every man they told had watched a senator hungry for the field spend his weight to save me, and a man's weight spent on you is a debt, and Scaeva did not lend at small interest. I had walked into that house Caesar's Thulean and walked out, in the eyes of Rome, Scaeva's. The tube would hold one day and the powder was already loose, and a man who has saved your life on a spring morning is owed, when the asking comes, an answer he will like.

And Vibenius. Vibenius had read me sound. He had stood by while the younger man held up the good liver, and he had let it be sound, and I will go to my grave not knowing whether he believed my reason, or was bought by Scaeva's lean, or made a cold dry calculation that an old priest who would not lean had, for once, found it cheaper to bless than to brand. He never told me. He never once showed his hand. What I knew, kneeling on the Aventine and walking down off it through a crowd that wanted to touch my cloak, was that the old man had spent something of his own to make my liver sound, whatever his reason, and that a thing spent on you by Vibenius was not a thing you paid back in coin or in sulfur or in a customs man at the gate. He held a debt I could not see the figure of, and he had told me himself, that night at my gate, that what he did with my answer would not be in my hands. He had done it. It had saved me. And the not-knowing of why was the rope I was hung on now, finer than Crispus's, and I would carry it.

I walked home down the slope with the smell of the blood and the incense still in my clothes, and Marcia met me at the gate, and did not ask how it went, because she could read it off me the way she read a ledger.

"They have the contest," I said.

"I know," she said. She had known before I said it; she had been working out, all the hours I knelt, what it would cost the enterprise to have the priests' hand in it, and how to price around them, because that was her craft and her craft did not stop for the gods. "We'll work inside it. A hook in a thing is a thing two can pull on." She stood aside to let me in. Then, as I passed, she put her hand flat against my chest, over the stink of the offering, the way she steadied a cup set too near an edge, and held it there a moment, and took it away, and that was the whole of what either of us said about the fact that I had nearly died that morning for the crime of teaching children to count.

Inside, the boy was awake in his basket making his small blind sounds at the ceiling, and I stood over him with the blood-smell on me and did not pick him up, because I did not want it on him. He had his father's higher floor and his father's fresh enemy and a god he would be taught to fear who had just, on the books, adopted the family trade. He gripped the air. I let him. Out past the gate the wharf went about its loud business in the failing light, the carts, the porters, the river taking everything down to the sea, and I stood in my own house, saved, richer, lashed now to a senator and a priest by ropes I could not see the ends of, and thought that the whole of my life in this city had been built on being useful, and that a useful thing is kept only so long as someone has a use for it, and put down the moment they do not, with no more thought than I had given the bad hinge that morning, the one with the flaw cooled into the middle of it, where the iron had gone wrong all the way through and only looked sound from the outside.

---

# Chapter 28: East

The order came in two halves, the way the important things always did, and the second half was the one with the hook in it.

The first half was a tablet from the imperial freedman's office, the dry house-Latin I had learned to read past the way you read past a man clearing his throat before he says the thing. Marcus Ulpius Danihel, libertus, was to attend the eastern expeditio in the service of the army's health, water and camps and the flux that Caesar held in particular dread, and would present himself at Brundisium before the close of the sailing season. My name on it in the new figures for the tally of what I was to be issued, which someone in that office had taken to using because it added faster, so that I was being sent to a war partly by a hand I had taught to count.

The second half came in person, two days later, in the shape of a freedman of Scaeva's at my gate with a basket of late apricots and a message that had no apricots in it at all. The senator was glad, said the man, smiling, that Caesar saw fit to send so useful a person where useful persons were so badly wanted. The senator hoped I would write to him of what I saw, the supply, the country, the temper of the legions, small things, a friend's curiosity. The senator had, the man said, taken the liberty of commending me to certain officers of his acquaintance who would be in the east, so that I should not arrive among strangers.

So that was the leash, handed to me with fruit. The emperor sent me to keep his soldiers' bowels in their bellies. Scaeva sent me to be his eye, and had arranged, before I had agreed to anything, that the men I worked under would already half-belong to him. I gave the freedman a cup of wine and thanked the senator for his care and ate two of the apricots after the man had gone, standing in the yard, because they were good and because there was nothing else in the morning I had any power over.

Marcia did the figures of it before I did. She always did. That night she sat with the lamp and Caepio's old ledger habit on her, and she worked out how long the eastern thing might run by the simple measure of how much grain the office had told me to expect to oversee, which was not a number you laid in for a season. "This is a war that means to stay where it goes," she said. She did not say what that meant for how long I would be gone. She set the stylus down. "Tyche keeps the figures. I keep the rest. The contest will go on under the priests' hand whether you kneel for it or not, and the press will print, and Lucanus will grow, and you will come back to a thing that did not need you, which is the only kind of thing worth building." It was the closest she came to telling me to live.

I left her the seal and the will and the floorboard, which she already had. I left Tyche the cipher, which was hers more than mine now. I held the boy in the grey before dawn while he was still too asleep to know it, the small weight of him, the milk-and-wool smell of him, and then I put him back in the basket and got on the cart, because a man who is owned by the emperor's house does not get to stay for the waking.

---

I had gone north to a war once and thought I had seen the machine. I had seen a finger of it.

The road south to Brundisium and then the sea and then the long crawl of the coast toward Syria showed me the arm, and then the body, and I never did see the whole of it, which is the thing about it I have never been able to put plainly to anyone who was not there. You cannot stand far enough back. Rome going to war in the east was a thing you were inside of, like weather, with no front edge to it and no outside to watch it from.

At Brundisium the harbor was so full of bottoms that the late ships rode out in the roads waiting their turn at the moles, and the grain lay on the quays in hills under their leather and the men slept on the hills to keep the porters honest. I stood among barrels stacked taller than a house, my barrels, the filter batteries I had drawn the plan of years ago in a yard by the Tiber, and watched a clerk tick them off a list against a tablet, and the tablet was one of a stack, and the stack was one of a room, and somewhere there was a man whose whole task in this life was rooms of tablets that were rooms of barrels that were the drinking of an army that had not yet reached the place it would drink. The reach of it. I had thought I understood numbers. I understood a number. I did not understand the thing where you keep finding, under the number, another number, all of them pulling east.

We coasted Greece and put in for water and went on. The harbors of Asia were strung along the shore like beads on a wire and every one of them had Rome's grain in it and Rome's pay-chests under guard and Rome's sick lying in the shade of the warehouses waiting to be carried home or buried, and the wire ran on east past where I had any names for the places, and the whole length of it was tightening toward the same point, the way a net does when it is drawn. Antioch, when we reached it, was a great roaring city that had become a thing it was not built to be, an army's mouth, everything coming in and being chewed and sent on, and over all of it the dust of men and animals moving in one direction, which was the direction the maps in my head ran out of detail.

I lay awake my first nights in that dust and did the arithmetic I could not stop doing, the bushels and the miles and the mules and the men, and the answer my arithmetic kept giving was a kind of vertigo. There was no end built into any of it. The grain came east to feed the men who went east to take the land that would, when it was taken, need grain sent east to it, and the line of supply that fed the conquest would have to lengthen to feed the conquest's fruit, and lengthen again, and there was no place in the design where a Roman had drawn a wall and written here we will be content. They were pouring the whole weight of the world they had into a country to take the country next to it, so that they might pour the weight into that one, and I could not find the bottom of the want.

---

Celer found me. He always found me. He came into the camp outside Antioch where I was arguing with an engineer about the fall of a drain, an older man now, the grey gone right through the cropped hair, the broken-set nose, the same dust-colored tunic as though it were the one I had met him in, and he stood and watched me lose the argument for a while before he said, "You've not got any prettier."

"You've not got any kinder."

"No money in it." He looked at the trench, at the engineer, at the camp running off in its straight Roman lines to the haze. "They told me a freedman of Caesar's was coming to teach us to drink water. I said I knew him. I said he'd kill more of us with boiling than the Parthians would with arrows, just to make us obey him." He let that sit, the old dry way. "Walk with me. I want to show you a thing and I don't want to do it in front of your drain."

He was a prefect now, of an auxiliary cohort, and what he wanted to show me was the cohort's stretch of the supply, which was to say a road, and the road was the thing. We walked out along it past the picket and it ran east straight as a plumb line over country that had been somebody's fields before this year, and on it, both ways, the traffic never stopped. Mule trains nose to tail. Ox carts groaning under timber for engines and bridges. Remounts. Wine. A century marching out with their stakes on their shoulders to build the next camp on the next clean water I or someone like me had not yet sited. Empty carts coming back for more. And the sick, coming back, always coming back, in the empty carts, a man's foot hanging off the tail, gray-faced, the flux taking them the way it had always taken them, more of them than any battle would.

"How far does it go," I said. I meant the road. I think I meant the road.

"Armenia's ours now," Celer said, as a man tells you the price of bread. "King knelt to Caesar. They took his crown off him and made it a province between one new moon and the next, and the lads who did it are already grumbling about going on to where it's worse." He stopped and looked along the line of it east, where the dust took the road and you could no longer tell road from sky. "I've fought on the Rhine and the Danube and I'm here. My whole life's been the edge of it moving out from under me. You build the camp, you win the ground, and the next morning the camp's not the edge anymore, it's the rear, and the edge is a day's march further on and you're packing up to chase it." He spat into the dust. "There's lads in my cohort whose fathers I served with. We're further out than their fathers ever stood, and the talk in the tents is all of how much further we'll go, the rivers there are, the cities, the gold. Nobody asks where it stops. You ask a Roman where it stops and he looks at you like you've asked where the sky stops."

"And where does it stop," I said.

He turned and gave me the look he gave a recruit who had said something either very stupid or worth hearing, he had not yet decided which. "When we run out of world," he said, "or out of us. One of the two always comes first. I've a wager with myself which." He started back toward the camp. "Mind your drain. The lads die of the water out here same as everywhere, and I'd as soon you kept mine alive long enough to die somewhere useful."

I walked back behind him with that in me like the splinter Crispus had put in, the realest thing said in the worst place. Out of world or out of us. I kept my soldiers' guts in them and they marched east on the strength of it to be spent further along the line, and that was my trade now, the same trade it had been at the siege, the knot I had stopped pretending I could cut. I made them able to go on. Going on was the disease.

---

The work itself was the work. There is no glory in it and I will not invent any. I sited camps where the ground would carry the filth away from the water instead of into it, which is the whole of the art and most of the time it is enough. I set the barrel batteries and made the centurions answer for the boiling, century by century, a tedium of standing over fires telling grown men who had killed other men to wait for the bubbles, and being hated for it, and saving them. Heras was not with me, being old now and kept in Rome, but his methods were, the clean cloth and the cooled boiled water on the wound, said in my mouth as Greek wisdom because that is how it went down. I lost men anyway. You always lose men. You lose fewer, and the fewer go on east to find the place that kills them, and you tell yourself the arithmetic and the arithmetic does not love you back.

The empty basket went up when the legates wanted to see over a ridge, the tethered bag with no man in it, the cloth strips for signals, useful only in still air as it had ever been, and there is a country south and east of Antioch where the air is still enough in the morning that it earned its keep more often than it had on the Danube. They asked me, twice, both times an officer new to me and once an officer of Scaeva's commending, whether a man could be sent up in it to see the further country, the rivers, the marching order of an enemy that had not yet shown itself. I said no the way I had learned to say it, flat, total, a freedman's no that this time was a thing that happened because the emperor's letter had given me the camps and the water and the bag both, and the no came with the bag. I did not tell them why. The why was a soldier in Pannonia with a scar that made him look amused, and a sound, and cloth coming down. I had buried the why under a lid years ago and nailed it shut, and I let them think me only stubborn.

It was on one of those still mornings, watching the empty basket hang at the end of its line over country that ran flat and brown and enormous toward a horizon the army was going to cross, that the other thing came to me, and I want to set down honestly that it came to me as a sickness comes, not as a plan.

I was looking east because that was where the bag pointed and where everything pointed. And I thought, the way you think a thing you immediately wish you had not, that all of this, the road and the grain-hills and the mule trains and Celer's lads grumbling toward the next river and the carts of the gray-faced sick, all of it was a hunger that had to go somewhere, and that it had chosen to go where there were people on the ground to be ground up, because that was the only direction the maps in their heads ran. And I had drawn them other maps once. On a wax tablet, in a cell, before I could speak to them, I had drawn the world and put two badly-drawn continents across a blank western sea, lands with no king to kneel and no city to starve and no cohort grumbling that the further river was worse, empty on the page, a year of open water away, reachable by no ship Rome had yet built.

The hunger could be pointed west. The thought arrived whole and obscene and I felt it the way you feel the lip of a height. A want with no bottom, turned at a sea instead of a country, would grind up no one but the men who chose to sail it. It would need ships, which meant timber and shipwrights and the patience of years, which meant it could not happen in a week, which meant no Sabinus would fall out of it before I could stop him. It would empty Rome's appetite into the one place I knew was empty, because I was the one who had drawn it empty.

And I recoiled from it standing there with the line in my hand. Because I knew what it was. It was me deciding, again, in private, where a thing should point, the same move that had cut Sarmizegetusa's water and burst the bronze tube and put a boy in a basket over a hill, the move of a man who has noticed that he can turn the wheel and has not yet found a reason good enough to keep his hands off it.
