And because I did not know if there was anything in that western sea to land on within a year's sail or three, my drawing being a thing I had copied off a dead phone and not a thing I had measured, so that to point the hunger west might be to point it at open water and drown it, which some nights I thought would be the kindest thing I could do for the people on the eastern ground and other nights thought was the worst lie I would ever tell.

I did not have a plan. I had a wound with an idea in it. I told no one. I hauled the empty basket down and folded the bag and said nothing to the officer who asked again what a man might have seen from up there, and I went back to the drains.

---

Celer and I ate together the night before his cohort moved up, in his tent, posca and hard bread and a bit of the bad local wine he'd got hold of, and we did not talk of where it stopped again because we had already said the only true thing there was to say about it. We talked of the Danube, the cold, a mule that had bitten an optio, men we both knew who were dead now of one ordinary thing or another, the long boring catalogue two men keep who have been frightened in the same places. He asked after the boy and I told him the boy was fat and furious and Marcia ran the whole enterprise better than I ever had, and he said that was no surprise, that he'd met her once and she'd priced his loyalty to my face and got it about right. Naso's name came up, the ruined hand, the ankle; Celer had kept him on with the baggage, alive, mending mules in the rear, and I was more glad of that than I let him see.

When I got up to go he said, not looking at me, working a strap on his kit the way a man does when he wants his hands busy for a hard thing, "You'll mind yourself out here. You're a senator's man now, they tell me. That's worse than being nobody's, in a war. Nobody's man just dies. A great man's man gets used first and dies after." He pulled the strap tight. "Build them their water. Keep your no about the bag. And when this is over, if it's ever over, go home to the fat furious boy and stop saving the army. The army doesn't deserve you and I should know, I'm the army."

"You're a sentimental old bastard."

"I'm a tired one." He looked up then, the broken nose, the grey, the dry mouth that did not smile but you knew. "Go on. I've a war to lose sleep over."

I went out into the camp with the cooking fires going down all along the straight lines of it and the picket calling and beyond the picket the road still moving in the dark, the carts you could hear but not see, east, always east. Somewhere ahead of all of it the country flattened out and ran on toward rivers I did not know the names of and the army would learn them one at a time and add them to the list of things it had taken and could not let go. I stood at the edge of the firelight a while. Then I went to my tent and slept the way you sleep when there is too much to do in the morning, and in the morning Celer's cohort marched east before the light, and I went and stood over a centurion's fire and waited for the water to boil.

---

# Chapter 29: Overreach

The word came up the road the way good news always came, ahead of the proof of it, carried by men who had heard it from men, so that by the time it reached the camps behind Antioch it had been polished smooth as a coin passed through too many hands. Ctesiphon was taken. The great city on the river, the Parthian's own seat, had fallen, and Caesar had stood in the king's own hall. The legions had gone down the rivers to the place where the rivers ended and the water turned bitter and went on forever, the southern sea, the edge of the maps, and there was no more east to take.

I heard it from a wine-factor at the quay and then from a wounded optio in a returning cart and then from a tribune's slave who had it, he swore, from a man who had been there. In every telling it got bigger and emptier at once. They had reached the sea. They had loaded a ship and seen it stand out toward lands that paid no tax to Parthia or to Rome or to anyone. And the old man, they said, the emperor, had stood on the shore in the heat with the bitter water at his feet and watched a merchant ship go out toward India and said he wished he were younger, that he might cross over after Alexander, and the men around him had laughed because it was the kind of thing he said, and then had not laughed, because of the look on him.

I did not see it. I want that plain. I was a great many miles behind the shore where the old man stood wishing he were a boy, standing over a fire telling a centurion to wait for the bubbles, and everything I know of the high-water mark of the world I know the way the people in a town know about a flood that took the next valley, from the brown line it leaves and the things it floats down to you afterward. What floated down to me was the strangeness in the men who told it. They told it as a victory and their faces told it as something else, the way a man's face does when he has eaten until he is sick and is still being handed plates. They had taken everything. There was nowhere left to point. A Roman with nowhere to point is a frightening thing, like a draft horse in a stall too small to turn around in, and I stood at the bottom of the world's longest supply line and felt the whole weight of it arrive at a wall it had not known was there and begin, very slowly, to lean back on the people who had pushed it.

---

The leaning back was a smell before it was anything else.

I had learned to read a camp the way a sailor reads water, by the things under the surface, and what I read first was the grain. There was less of it coming up the road, and then it was coming up the road wrong, in guarded clumps with too many soldiers walking beside too few carts, and the soldiers walking beside it had the look of men who had been shot at. A supply line is a held breath. You do not notice it until it catches. Ours caught somewhere behind us, down toward the rivers and the great taken cities, and the catching came up to me as rumor and short rations and a quartermaster swearing over a tally that no longer balanced because the figures going out the far end were not coming back as anything but trouble.

The cities had risen. That was the shape of it as it reached me, and I will not pretend I understood it better than I did, because I did not understand it at all, and the men who told me understood it less and were more certain. The taken places would not stay taken. They had bent the knee while the legions stood in their streets, and the legions had gone on east to the bitter sea, and there were too few of them left in the rear to hold what the front had grabbed. Garrisons cut off. A column ambushed in a place that had been Roman a month before. Killing in towns whose names I heard three different ways, and a kind of killing I understood even less, a rising of peoples I had only heard of as words, somewhere south and west, whole communities turning on their neighbors and being turned on, a fire that had nothing to do with Parthia and burned hotter than Parthia ever had. I heard it as a Roman heard it, which is to say wrong, as madness and treachery and the gods withdrawing their favor. I knew only that the line I kept alive with clean water now ran through country that wanted it dead, and that the further the army had reached, the thinner the hand was that held the ground behind its back, until the ground simply closed up behind it like water behind a swimmer.

The army was a fist that had punched all the way through and now could not pull its arm back out.

---

Celer's cohort was in the rear, on the supply road, which had been a dull safe duty in the summer and was the most dangerous place in the east by autumn, because the road was the thing the rising wanted to cut, and a cohort spread thin along a road is a cohort spread thin along the exact thing everyone is trying to kill.

I knew this the way I knew most things, by arithmetic, lying awake. So I was not surprised when they brought him to me. I had been half waiting to be surprised by it for weeks, which is a different and worse way to wait.

They brought him on a cart, with four of his men around it who would not leave even when an optio told them to, in the gray before a dawn, the way the carts always came. He was awake. That was the first thing, and I let it be a good thing for the space of one breath before I made myself look properly. He had taken a thrust low on the left side, below the ribs, in a night action on the road where his men had held the carts and lost six and held them. Somebody had bound it on the road with whatever was to hand, which was the trouble, because whatever was to hand on a road in that country was filth. It had been two days and a half. The binding when I cut it away came off in a way I had smelled in other men and learned to dread, and under it the edges of the wound were the wrong color and the wrong heat, and the skin around it had gone a tight shine that ran further than it should, and I knew, with the part of me that had spent fifteen years keeping armies from dying of exactly this, what I was looking at.

"Don't," Celer said. He was watching my face the way he watched everything, weighing it. "I've seen that look on enough surgeons. Save it for someone who hasn't seen the inside of a tent before."

"It needs cleaning."

"It needs the gods," he said, "but clean it, by all means, if it gives you something to do with your hands. I always said you'd kill me with washing."

I cleaned it. I am going to set down what I did, because it is the only part of this I get to keep, and because a man should be able to say exactly how far short everything he had reached.

I had water boiled fresh and cooled, more of it than the camp could spare, and I washed the wound and the country of skin around it until the cloths came away less foul, and I cut away what my knife told me was already dead, which made him swear at me steadily in three languages and grip the cart-rail with a hand that went white, and I packed it with linen boiled and dried clean and bound it loose so it could weep instead of holding the rot in against him the way the road-binding had. I gave him posca to drink and washed it on the wound besides, the sour wine that I half believed and half only hoped did something. I built him a place off the road, by clean water I had sited myself, and I changed the dressing twice a day and boiled everything between, and I made the men who tended him scald their hands until they hated me, and I did every single thing I knew.

I knew a great deal. I knew more than any man within five hundred miles about keeping the invisible climbing things out of a body. And I knew, washing his side at noon on the first full day, watching the shine on the skin reach a thumb's width further than it had at dawn, that I had come to the wall I had spent my life pretending I could not see clearly, the one Heras had named for me a lifetime ago and I had not listened. The little creatures were already inside him. They had been put inside him on a dark road by an iron point and two days of a dirty rag, before I ever cut the binding, and there is no clean water in the world that washes out a thing that is already in the blood. Everything I had was for keeping them out. I had nothing for getting them back. I never had. I had been able to save the army from this for fifteen years only by reaching every man before the road did, and the road had reached Celer first.

---

He went down the way that wound goes down, which is to say not steadily, which is the cruelest part of it, because it leaves you room to be a fool.

The first day he was himself and angry at being on his back. He had his optio come and report and he gave orders about the cohort and the carts and where the road was weakest and he was right about all of it, his voice flat and clipped and exactly as it had always been, and I let myself think, against everything the skin was telling me, that he was a tougher animal than the rule. The second day the fever came up the way the tide had come up around the old man's feet at the bitter sea, slow, certain, with nowhere built into it for it to stop. He sweated through the bedding and I changed it and boiled it. He drank the water I gave him. The shine on his side went up past the wound and around toward his back and the smell of it came through the clean dressings no matter how often I changed them, and I changed them, by lamp and by sun, and the changing did nothing but let me look at it and know.

Naso came. They had kept him in the rear with the baggage and the mules, his ruined hand curled to his chest, his ankle still putting its hitch in his walk, and he came and sat where he could be useful, which was holding water and carrying out the foul cloths to be burned, the one good hand doing the work of the bad one's absence. He did not say much. He had buried things in his own lid years ago and he knew the weight of a man sitting with another man's dying. He kept the small fire I needed and he kept it clean and he kept his face still, and once, when I came back from being sick behind the tent where I thought no one could see me, he had a cup of the posca ready and he put it in my hand without a word, the way I had once watched him put his one hand over my fist on a burned rag, a long way north, a long time ago.

On the third day Celer stopped being angry, which frightened me worse than the fever, because a Celer who has stopped fighting the thing in front of him is a Celer who has done his arithmetic and got an answer. He was clear-eyed between the fevers. That is the thing about that death, that it leaves the mind alone for too long. He looked at the dressing when I changed it and he looked at the shine that had reached his back and he looked at my face doing the thing faces do when they are working very hard to be steady.

"You've gone gray over it," he said. "When did you go gray. You were a boy."

"You've gone grayer."

"I've earned mine." He let his head go back. "How long. And don't lie to me to be kind, I'll know, and I'll think less of you, and I've thought a great deal of you, which is a thing I'd as soon not undo at the end of it."

I did not say it for a moment. The water clock in Rome would have measured the moment; out there I had nothing to measure it with but my own pulse going in my ears. "Not long," I said. "A day. Two. The fever takes more of you each time it comes."

"Good," he said. "Not the fever. The knowing." He worked his dry mouth. "I've watched men go who didn't get told. They spend their last day on plans. It's an obscene thing to watch, a dying man making plans." He looked at me. "Tell me a thing. The bag. You never put another man up, after the boy. Did you ever want to? After. When they pressed you."

"Every time," I said. "I wanted to give it to them. To be done arguing."

"But you didn't."

"No."

"There," he said, and something eased in his ruined face that was as close to peace as I saw on it. "You did decide, then. What kind of man builds a thing like that. You decided it with your no, every time, for fifteen years, and never once told yourself that was the deciding." He closed his eyes. "Mind your drain," he said, which was a joke we had, and then he was asleep, or under, and the fever came up again that night and took more of him, and the day after that he was not clear anymore, and talked to men who were not there, a centurion long dead, his mother by the sound of it, and I sat with him and washed him and did the useless tender work that is all there is left to do, and Naso fed the fire, and the road outside went on moving in the dark.

---

He died before dawn on the fifth day, while I was holding the cup to his mouth and he was past drinking from it, the water running down his chin onto the clean cloth I had boiled, and there was no last word, because the wound that kills you that way does not leave you a word, it takes the words first and the breath after. The breath went out long and ragged and did not come back in, and I sat with the cup still at his lips for longer than made any sense, the way I had knelt by the burned cloth with my hands out and nothing for them to do, and then Naso reached over with his one good hand and pressed my arm, and I set the cup down.

I washed him. There was no need to wash a dead man and I washed him anyway, the way I had washed him living, the wound that had won and the rest of him the wound had wasted, and I closed his eyes with my thumb, and I straightened the kit-strap he had worried at the last night we ate together, the one his hands had wanted to keep busy on, and I left it lying neat across him because his hands could not do it now and somebody's should. His optio came and his men came, the four who would not leave the cart, and they stood in the gray with their helmets off, and I gave him over to them, because he was theirs more than mine. They wrote it down that the prefect Quintus Marcius Celer fell of a wound taken in a night action holding the supply road, which was true, every word of it, and which left out the two days and the dirty rag and the wall I had broken myself against, the way the stone in Rome leaves out the soldier in the fishing-float, the way the writing-down always leaves out the part that is the whole of it.

---

I went back to the drains. There is always work, and the work that morning was a centurion's century waiting for clean water on a road that wanted them dead, and I went and stood over the fire and waited for the bubbles, and my hands shook on the dipper so that the centurion noticed and looked away from it to give me the manners of not having seen, which was the kindest thing a soldier did for me that month.

I did the arithmetic, because I could not stop doing the arithmetic, it is the thing my hands do when the rest of me cannot be trusted. I had kept thousands of men from dying of the thing that killed him. I had it in figures, near enough, the men who would have gone down to the flux and the foul water in two wars and had not, because of clean cloth and cooled boiled water and a tedium of standing over fires. Strangers, every one, men whose names I never learned and whose children's names I was spared. And I could put every one of those saved strangers in one pan of a scale and the one man in the other and the scale would not balance, it would slam, because the strangers were saved before the thing got into them and Celer was brought to me after, and there was no cleverness in any head in that century of the world that could reach back across that little distance of two days and pull the climbing things out of his blood. My whole life's gift was a wall built to keep them out. I had no door in it for going in after the ones already inside. I never built one. I did not know how. No one would know how for longer than my life, longer than the lives of everyone burning a foul dressing on that road, and I stood over the fire and understood that the very thing that made me worth sending east, the saving, had a floor in it, and the floor was where my friend was, and I had been standing on it for fifteen years calling it the top of a ladder.

The army would not hold what it had taken. I knew it the way I had known the wind was wrong over the Loaf, in the body before the mind would say it, watching the grain come up the road in guarded clumps and the empty carts come back full of the gray-faced and the worse-than-gray-faced from places that had been ours a month before. A thing reaches as far as it can hold and then it reaches a little further, because reaching is the only thing it knows how to do, and the little further is the part that kills it, the part that closes up behind it like water. Someone, some day, was going to have to stand at the front of all that overreached length and do the thing no Roman in my hearing had ever once said aloud, which was to let go of ground that had been taken, to pull the arm back out of the wall, to draw a line somewhere short of everywhere and write here, and not past here. I did not know who. I did not know it was coming. I knew only that it had to come, the way I knew Celer had been right that you run out of world or out of us and one of the two always comes first, and that out here, on this road, in this autumn, we had run out of both at once.

---

I did not point the hunger west to anyone. The thought was in me harder than it had been, hardened by the smell of a clean dressing over a wound that had already won, by the brown enormous emptiness the empty basket had hung over, by the figures of grain that would not come back as anything but trouble. The east was a dead end. It was desert and rivers with foreign names and cities that would not stay knelt, and it had eaten the old man's wish to be young again and it had eaten Celer below the ribs, and every instinct I had, the saving instinct and the turning-the-wheel instinct both, said the want would have to go somewhere or it would go on grinding up the people it could reach, and I knew a place it could go that was empty, because I had drawn it empty on a tablet in a cell before I could speak. But there was no one to say it to. Celer was the one man in the east I might have tried it on, the one who would have heard the arithmetic in it and the obscenity in it both and told me which it was, and the road had reached him first.

So I kept it. I kept it the way I kept everything, in the only homeland I had left, which was the inside of my own skull, and I went on standing over fires.

Naso left with the next baggage train going back toward Antioch, mended enough, alive, his hand curled to his chest, his ankle hitching him up onto the cart. He stopped beside me before he climbed up. He looked at me a while with the face that had stopped being young a long way north. Then he took, from inside his tunic, the kit-strap I had left lying neat across the body, the one Celer's hands had worried at, that I had not noticed anyone take, and he put it into my hand and folded my fingers over it with his one good hand, the way you close a child's fist around a thing it is too tired to hold, and he climbed up onto the cart and did not say anything, because there was nothing the right size to say, and the train pulled out east to west along the road that the rising would cut again before the moon changed.

I did not put the strap in the fire. I had burned the last thing I carried, on a riverbank a world ago, and I was older now and had learned that burning a thing is only another way of keeping the smell of it. I worked it into my own kit instead, where my hands could find it in the dark and worry at it the way his had, and I went back down the line to the next century and the next fire and the next grown men who had killed other men and would now stand and wait, because I told them to, for the water to boil.

---

# Chapter 30: The Blindside

The thing I remember from the morning it came is that the trumpets were wrong.

A camp keeps time by horn. You stop hearing it the way you stop hearing your own heart, and then one day the heart does something it has never done and you hear nothing else. I was at the fires, where I was always at the fires, on a stretch of the road north of the city where the army was pulling itself back westward in long sullen lengths, more carts every week, fewer men beside each cart, all of it leaning the same direction now, away from the rivers, toward the sea and home. The dawn watch should have blown and changed. Instead the horns went up out of order, two of them at once and then a third that came in late and broke off, and the men around my fire stopped with their bowls halfway to their mouths and looked east down the road, which is the direction you look when you are afraid the enemy has done a thing in the night.

It was not the enemy.

A rider had come up the road in the dark, a dispatch rider on a blown horse, and he had gone to the legate's quarter and not stopped, and the men who carry water and empty pots carry news faster than any rider, and the news came down the line the way cold comes through a wall, from the inside out, a man going still before the man beside him knew why. I had it from a baggage clerk I had taught the new figures, a sharp Greek-tongued boy who did the grain tallies, and he did not say it as news. He said it as a question, because he could not make it sit flat enough to be a fact. He said, "The Thulean. Is it true Caesar is dead."

I told him not to repeat things he heard in the dark.

I want to be honest about the next part, because the whole of what I am is in it. For the length of several breaths I did not believe him, and the not-believing was not hope and was not grief. It was the simple animal refusal of a thing that does not fit the shape of the world. I had been in this country nineteen years. The emperor had been emperor the entire time. He had bought me out of a leather-merchant's yard with his own coin and set his own family's name on me, and in all those years the sun had come up over the marching camps and Trajan had been Caesar, and the two facts had grown together in some place under thought where I did not examine them, so that the boy might as well have told me the sun had been recalled. I knew old men died. I knew this one was old and had been sick on the road west, everyone knew that, it was the reason the whole army was crawling home instead of marching, the emperor was unwell and turning back. I had filed it the way you file a thing you are not ready to use. I had let myself believe, without ever once putting it in words where I would have laughed at it, that the old soldier was a fixed point. That he would outlast the war. That he would outlast me.

He had died at a town on the coast called Selinus, in Cilicia, with the sea on one side and the mountains shutting down to the water on the other, turning back toward Rome with the conquests souring behind him. A seizure, they said, a stroke that took one side of him and then the rest over a few days. The eighth day of the month. The news had walked up the coast and inland to us the way all news walked, so that by the time it reached my fire the man had been dead the better part of a fortnight and the world had already gone on being a world without him in it, only nobody on my stretch of road had been told yet that they were living in it.

I did the only thing my hands knew. I went on tending the fire. I told the waiting century to wait for the bubbles. And under the steady stupid work of it the bottom went out of me, not for the dead man, who I had stood in front of exactly once in my life and who had been kind to me in the cold transactional way the state is kind to a useful tool, but for me. I am not proud of how fast it turned to me. It turned to me before the water boiled.

---

Here is the arithmetic I did, standing over a fire on a road that wanted us dead, the morning I learned the emperor was a body being carried somewhere west.

Everything I was, in law, came from him.

I was not Daniel out here. Daniel was a sound men could not say and a boy who had stopped existing in a stubble field a lifetime ago. I was Marcus Ulpius Danihel, freedman of the deified house, and every word of that name was the dead man's gift. The Marcus he had hung on me like a coat. The Ulpius was his own blood, his gens, the name his sisters carried and his father had carried, lent to a stateless foreigner he had decided was worth more enfranchised than enslaved. That name was the wall I lived behind. It was the reason a haruspex with a grudge had read me a sound liver instead of a guilty one. It was the reason a grammarian who hated me to the marrow had only ever been able to talk, and never to act, because you do not move on a man the emperor has personally named unless you want to explain to the emperor why. Macer had said it to me plainly, years back, the night the citizenship tablet went inside my tunic against the dead glass. No haruspex reads a bad sign over a man Caesar's just named. The man Caesar had named was a man Caesar protected. Caesar was a body on a cart.

The wall did not fall down all at once. That was the worst of it, and I understood it standing there. A dead patron does not unmake your freedom; the law is not that crude. I was still free. The name was still mine. But a name is only worth what stands behind it, and the thing that had stood behind mine had stopped breathing on the coast of Cilicia, and the men who had feared to move on me had feared him, not the seven letters of his family. With him gone they did not have to be afraid of a sound. They had to be afraid of whoever stood behind it next, and nobody yet knew who that was, and a man nobody yet knew was a man with no debts to me and no reason on earth to keep an old emperor's pet barbarian safe.

I went down the list of them the way you check the lashings on a load before a steep road. Crispus, in Rome, who had wanted me ruined for the better part of two decades and had been beaten back every time by the same fact and would now wake up one of these mornings to a courier and a different world. The priestly colleges, who held a permanent hook in my press and my contest and my figures since the impiety business, who had been content to be consulted because consultation was as far as the wind let them reach, and who could feel a wind change as well as any haruspex alive. Vibenius, who held a debt I could never pay and had told me to my face that his house does not lean, and whose house, in a new reign, might find a new direction worth leaning. The complaint had been quieted, not killed. Nothing in this country was ever killed. It was buried in shallow ground and the ground here was about to be turned over by other men's spades.

And I was the whole width of the sea from any of it, on a dead-end campaign, on a road in revolt, carrying a dead friend's strap worked into my kit and a dead emperor's name worked into my law, and the one thing in the world I wanted, with a want that came up so fast it frightened me worse than the news had, was to be in Rome. To have my hands on the thing I had built before the men back there got their hands on it. To be standing in front of Marcia and Tyche, who were running the whole of it on a wharf a month's hard sail away and did not yet know, could not yet know, that the floor had gone out from under all of us.

---

The other leash had not died. I thought of that next, and it was a cold practical thought and I was glad to have it, because it was a thing I could hold.

Scaeva was alive.

The senator was alive in Rome and the senator had spent real weight to make me his, had leaned in across a borrowed room and tipped a college of priests and made me, in front of witnesses, the senator's man. Whatever else had fallen, that had not. The emperor's hand had loosed me; the senator's had not. And a senator with an emperor freshly dead was a man doing his own arithmetic that morning, faster and colder than mine, because senators had been doing exactly this arithmetic since before there were emperors. Scaeva would already be sorting the new reign for handholds. He knew I imported sulfur, knew how much, knew through which gate. He knew the powder worked and roughly what it ate. He had once said, with that beautiful unhurried voice, that he wanted the powder pointed somewhere by a friend once the steel was solved. A change of master at the top did not make a senator want power less. It made him want it more, and faster, and with fewer of the old restraints, because the man who had set the restraints was being carried home in a jar.

I was a tool with two handles and one of the two hands holding me had just gone slack, which does not free the tool. It only means the other hand can swing it however it likes.

---

By midday the rumor had grown its second head, the way they always do, and the second head was worse than the first because the first had at least been a single clean blow.

There was a new emperor. The army had been told there was a new emperor and the army did not believe it, and I stood in the middle of the not-believing and tried to make a shape out of it the way I made shapes out of grain reports, by what came in three different versions and what stayed the same in all three. What stayed the same was a name, Hadrian, and a place, Antioch, where the legions of Syria had hailed him. What changed in every telling was how. The governor of Syria, a kinsman of the old man's, who had been sitting in Antioch the whole while the old man died up the coast. Adopted on the deathbed, the official word ran, named son and heir in the last days, the seal still warm. And under that word, already, the same morning, in three soldiers' mouths out of four, the other word. That the deathbed adoption was a thing the wife had arranged. That the empress had been at Selinus and the prefect of the Guard had been at Selinus, and between the two of them, a wife and a soldier who held the knives, a dying man with one side of his face gone could be made to have said a great many useful things, or could be reported to have said them after he could no longer say anything at all. That a freedman of the household had announced the adoption to the troops in the right order, and that the freedman had then conveniently died.

I had no way to know. That is the thing I keep wanting to write large, that I had no way to know, none, not the smallest grain of certainty in any of it. I was a man in a field hearing about a flood in the next valley. The new emperor was a name and the manner of his making was a quarrel between people I had never seen, in a city I had passed through once, and there was no man within a hundred miles whose word on it I would have trusted with a coin. A new emperor with a name I knew the way I knew the name of any city official, which is to say not at all, and unknown priorities, and a making that half his own army called a fix in the same breath they cheered it. The men cheered, where the officers could see them, and muttered where the officers could not, and the muttering said the army's old hard core had wanted one of the marshals, the killers who had taken the cities, and had got instead a bookish kinsman the old man had kept close and never quite given the thing to, until the thing was given in a sickroom with only the wife to hear.

There was a moment, standing there, when the name and the talk of a bookish man in the dead emperor's circle caught on something at the back of my skull and would not come loose. A man I had seen. A beard, a roll of drawings under an arm, a cold exchanged bow in a crowd at the Forum a few years back, and Apollodorus's voice loud and contemptuous over the top of it. Go and paint his gourds. I had watched a proud man humiliate a powerful one that day and thought, idly, that the powerful one had the longer memory. The catch was there and then it was gone, the way a name you half-know slides off when you reach for it. I could not make it sit. There were a great many bearded men of letters around an emperor, and I had seen that one for the length of an insult, and the road in front of me was full of grain that would not come back and water that needed boiling, and I let it go. I had no room in me that morning to be sure of anything I could not boil or count.

---

I went back down the line. There is always the line.

I stood over the next fire and the next century and made grown men wait for the water the way I always made them wait, and I did the work because the work was the one thing the death of an emperor could not change in the next hour, the invisible climbing things did not care whose name was on the standard, they killed a soldier of Trajan and a soldier of Hadrian the same way and were beaten the same way, by clean cloth and cooled boiled water and the tedium of standing over a flame. The work was the floor that did not move. Everything above the floor had moved, all at once, in the night, and I had been told about it by a baggage clerk who could not make it sit flat.

The army would crawl west now whether the emperor was a god or a corpse or a quarrel between a wife and a guard, because there was nothing left east to crawl toward, and somewhere ahead of all of us, in a city on the sea, a man I could not place was being made into the thing that would decide whether the law that held my life still held it. I needed to get to Rome. I needed it the way I had once needed the bubbles to come, with a steadiness in the wanting that frightened me more than panic would have. Marcia had the books and Tyche had the cipher and between them they had every dangerous thing I had ever made or written, the powder ratios in a script only she could read, the press, the contest, the figures, the whole engine, sitting on a wharf in a city that was, this morning, no longer certain to be safe for the man whose name was on all of it. They did not know yet. The news would take weeks to reach them by sea the way it had taken weeks to reach me by road, and there was a stretch of the world's width between us in which they would go on running the enterprise of a dead emperor's freedman without knowing the emperor was dead, balancing the tally, crowning the contest, while the ground under the whole of it had already given way and only had not finished falling.

I worked the dead man's strap once between my fingers, where my hands could find it now in the dark, and made the century wait for the boil, and turned the problem over the only way I knew how to turn anything, which was to start counting the days it would take a man to get from this road to that wharf, and to find that the number was very large, and to begin, while the water came up to its bubbles, to make it smaller.

---

# Chapter 31: Vacuum

The sea did me the one kindness it had. It was fast.

I will not pretend I remember the voyage as days. I remember it as a single long held breath broken into watches. I bought passage off a grain master running empty hulls back west to load again, the trade not yet caring that an emperor had died, money being the one thing in the world that does not stop to mourn. We coasted Cilicia and stood off Crete in a wind I would have called a gift if I still believed the sky took requests, and we made Puteoli in something near five weeks, which is a thing sailors cross themselves over and which I knew better than to call luck out loud, because the men who say a crossing was easy are the men the next sea drowns. I slept on deck against the cargo and did the only arithmetic the boat would let me do, the run of the keel against the run of the days, and every morning I made the number of days remaining smaller by one and was no calmer for it.

Celer's strap I had worked into my own kit at the shoulder, where my hand found it without my asking, and on the bad nights my hand asked.

We raised the bay at dawn, the cone of the mountain above the towns with its thread of smoke standing straight up in the still air, and the whole curve of the shore already crowded with masts, and I stood at the rail and felt the want come up again, that steady frightening want to have my hands on the thing before other men did, and made myself wait for the gangway like a person who had not lost his mind.

---

I went up the road to Rome with the post traffic and a hired mule, and the city told me what had changed before any man did.

It was in the gates. The gates of Rome are never quiet, the toll men shouting, the press of carts and drovers, and the toll men were shouting still, but there were more of them, and behind the toll men were soldiers who do not stand at gates. I knew the look of the Guard the way you know a dog that has bitten before. They were not searching carts. They were watching faces, and letting you see them watch, which is the whole of the thing, a man at a gate with a sword he is not using is a sentence the city reads all day without anyone speaking it aloud. I went through with the grain wagons and was not stopped, and was sweating through my tunic by the time the arch was behind me, and there had been no reason given for the sweat that a magistrate could have written down.

The markets were loud the way a room is loud after a death in the next house. Too loud, and then a hole in the middle of the loudness where no one wanted to be the one heard. I bought bread off a stall on the cattle-market side out of old habit, the same stall, and the woman who had sold me bread for years took my coin and did not gossip, and she had always gossiped, it was half of what the as bought. She looked past my shoulder while she gave me the loaf. Everyone on that street had taken up the trick of looking past the shoulder of the man in front of them, to see who stood behind him and whether they should be seen talking to him at all. I had been five weeks at sea and the city had learned a new way of holding its face in that time and was already fluent in it.

A wall by the meat sellers had a thing scrawled fresh on it and limed half over again, somebody's name and a word, the kind of word the colleges would call impiety and the Guard would call sedition depending which got to it first, and the lime had gone on before the scrawl was even dry, by a shopkeeper who did not want that name on his wall when the wrong man walked past. I could read it now, every cursed letter, nineteen years too fluent. I did not stop. Stopping to read a thing on a wall was, that month, a way of being a man who reads such things.

There was no emperor in the city. That was the whole shape of it, under the soldiers and the limed walls and the bread woman's eyes. The emperor was a name they were cutting onto fresh stone and chanting at the right hours in the right temples, and the man it belonged to was somewhere east of the sea I had just crossed, on a road, coming, not here. Into the place where the emperor was supposed to stand, which is the place a whole city leans against without knowing it, other people had quietly put their hands. You felt the city find nothing solid and shift its weight, the way a man going downstairs in the dark puts his foot for a step that is not there.

---

The wharf yard was the wharf yard. That undid me worse than the gates had.

Pamphilus was at the gate before I was off the mule, his collar five years gone and the mark of it long faded, and he had a man's restraint about him that he had not had when I bought him, and he took the mule's head and looked at my face and said only, "You're thin," which is what you say to a man whose other news you are afraid of. The yard smelled of the river and of wet hemp and of the press oil, ink and lamp soot, the smell I had carried east in my head as the smell of the one place I was something other than a tool. Stichus had grown into the shoulders he used to trip over. The clepsydra still dripped in the tally-room and still kept its lying time. It was all exactly where I had left it, and that was the thing my chest could not hold, that it had gone on being itself for three years while I learned in a tent how a friend dies of a wound I had cleaned with my own hands.

Lucanus did not know me. That is a thing I had done arithmetic about on the boat and had thought I was ready for and was not. He had been a baby on a hip when I marched. He was a boy now, five years old, with Marcia's square set to his face and the bulla on its cord at his throat, and he came to the doorway because there was a stranger in the yard and stood behind his mother's leg and looked at me with the frank weighing look children give, deciding whether I was a thing to fear, and I understood that to him I was a man off a boat with a sea-burnt face he had never seen. Marcia put her hand flat on his head and said, "This is your father," and he considered it and was not convinced and went back inside to whatever he had been doing, and that was correct of him, and it took the legs out from under me more than the soldiers at the gate had.

Marcia did not embrace me long. She is not built for it and I was past it. She held the sides of my arms and looked at me the way she looks at a column of figures she suspects of lying, and then she sent Pamphilus for the mule and Stichus out of the room and took me into the tally-room and shut the door, which is how Marcia says she loves you, by clearing the room of everyone but the problem.

"You know," she said. Not a question. The news had beaten me by a fortnight; it had come on a faster hull. "We've known three weeks. I've had three weeks to count it. You've had how long."

"Five."

"Then you're ahead of where I thought." She sat. She had a wax tablet in front of her, not for show, she always had one, but she did not write on it. "I'll give it to you the way I'd give it to a partner who'd been at sea. You want it soft or you want it right."

"Right."

"We are worth more dead than alive to four men I can name and a college I can't sue." She let that sit, the way she lets a figure sit. "Your name is the security on every contract this house holds. Your name was good because of one thing, and that thing is being carried to the Campus in a jar to be burned. The Ulpius doesn't stop meaning what it means the day he dies. It stops meaning it the day the next man decides whose names he honors and whose he forgets, and we don't know that man, and the men who do know that man are not friends of ours."

She turned the tablet a quarter, an old habit, lining its edge to the table's edge.

"The press is fine. The press makes money and the contest makes friends and none of that is the problem. The problem is the dedication." Her voice did not change for it. "We gave the press and the figures and the contest into Minerva's keeping to get you off a charge. That was the price and I'd pay it again. But the colleges hold that now, do you understand me, they have a right of consult and a rite they say over our work every spring, and a right of consult is nothing in a reign that lets it stay nothing. In a reign that wants something to give, it's a door already standing open. We handed a man who hasn't arrived yet a way to take the whole engine and look pious doing it. Crispus has known that since the day we did it. He's been waiting for the lock to change. The lock's changing."

I sat down across from her. The clepsydra dripped. I thought of Vibenius coming on foot to this yard, telling me his house did not lean, and I thought, his house may have found a wind worth leaning into, and I did not say it, because she had got there before me and was waiting for me to catch up.

"And Scaeva," I said.

"And Scaeva," she said, and for the first time something moved behind the figures. "You're his man. That was your shield once. I'm not sure whose man Scaeva is, this month, and neither is he, and a man who isn't sure whose man he is, is a man you do not want holding your leash when the scores get settled." She put two fingers flat on the dead tablet. "I want to know what you owe him. All of it. The sulfur, the gate, the rest. Because if he goes into the fire, we are tied to him at the wrist, and I would like to know how long the rope is before I find out by being pulled."

I told her. The sulfur, the gate he had fixed, the powder he had said he wanted pointed somewhere by a friend. She listened to all of it without writing any of it, which was how I knew it was bad, because Marcia writes down the bearable things.

"Burn nothing," she said when I was done. "Not the ratios, not the cipher, nothing. A house that has burned its books in a bad week is a house that had something to burn, and the Guard can read ash." She stood. "Eat. You look like the war. The boy will come round to you. He came round to the wet-nurse and she was a horror." She put her hand once, briefly, against the side of my neck, which is the most she will do, and went to find food, leaving me with the dripping clock and the smell of ink and a sea-legs sway in a room that was not moving.

Tyche I found in the press room, and she did not turn from the form she was setting. She finished locking the line before she looked, which was its own kind of greeting, the work first, always. She was thirty-two and there was gray starting at her temple that had not been there, and her eyes went over me once, the strap at my shoulder, the burnt face, the thinness, all of it filed in a breath. She did not ask about Celer. She knew Celer. I had written her of the army in the cipher for years, the only person on earth to whom I could write a true sentence. She looked at the strap and back at my face and what she said was, "The box is where you left it. I move it when the floor needs sweeping and I put it back." Meaning the dead glass, the citizenship tablet, the powder ratios under the board only she knew. Meaning, I have kept your secrets while the world changed, and I will go on keeping them. Then she said, "Marcia tell you about the dedication," and I said yes, and she said, "Good," and went back to the form, and I stood in the ink smell and was, for one breath, less alone than I had been in three years, and could not have said so to her without ruining it.

---

I went to Macer because you do not not go to Macer, and because his fear, when I found it, told me more than the gates had.

He was at his town house and he was old. He had always been old to me and now he was old the way a wall is old, sixty-nine and big still in the shoulders but settled, gone heavy at the jaw, the broken-veined face fallen in a little. He turned the gold ring with his thumb when I came in and he kept turning it the whole while, which I had seen him do thinking, and had never seen him do without stopping. He did not stop.

"The Thulean," he said. "Off the boat. Of course you are. Caesar dies and the Thulean comes home like a cat through a window." But there was no meat on the joke. He was listening past me toward the door the way the bread woman had looked past my shoulder. His own house. He was watching his own atrium.

I had come for the bond between us, whatever it was, the cage and the shield, the man who had owned me and sold me to an emperor and called it being glad for me. In the bad business with the colleges he had stood at a wall and spent nothing to save me, and I had not forgiven it and had not broken with him over it, because you do not break with weather. I had expected, coming up his steps, to find that weather, the cold of a man calculating his own skin. I found the cold. I had not expected the fear under it.

"You contracted the legions for thirty years," he said, before I had said three sentences, and it came out of him sideways, almost to the ring. "Leather, timber, the grain. You know whose army that was. A man does not feed an emperor's wars for thirty years and come out the far side smelling of bread. I'm in the rolls. Everything I am is in some clerk's roll as the old man's man, and clerks' rolls don't burn, boy, that's the whole point of a clerk." He looked at me then, full on, and his eyes were a frightened man's eyes in a hard man's face, and I had known him eighteen years and never seen it. "They'll go through the houses. They always go through the houses, after. The only question is whose, and how high, and a man my age in those rolls has had a long time to make a tidy line for a new prefect to draw straight through."

"Who is they," I said.

"You don't know." He almost laughed and it died. "You've been at the bottom of the sea. The wife and the prefect run the city till the boy gets here." He never once that day said Hadrian's name; the emperor was the boy, with a contempt and a caution wrapped in the one word. "The wife. Plotina. And Attianus with the Guard, and the two of them between them hold every door in Rome, and they are not waiting on the boy to start putting their house in order, because by the time the boy comes the house had better look like he chose it. You understand what that means."

"It means they settle things before he can be blamed for them."

"It means," Macer said, and now he leaned in, and the ring stopped, finally, his thumb pressing it still, "that there are names being said. In the right houses, behind the right doors. That certain men of consular rank, men who took the cities in the east, men who wanted the purple and made no secret they thought they had a better claim than a bookish ward, are going to be found to have plotted. That's the word that's already loose. A plot. Against the boy. Four of them, some say. Some say three, some say it's nonsense and nobody will touch a consular without a trial, and the man who told me that last is a fool who has not been alive as long as I have." He sat back. The breath went out of him. "Palma's name. Celsus. The Moor, the cavalry one, Quietus, who Caesar broke command from already. Maybe a fourth. I don't know the fourth. Nobody knows the fourth, which is how you know there'll be a fourth, because a list isn't a list till it's longer than you'd like."

"And that touches you how," I said, and I knew how, I wanted to hear him say it, because a frightened Macer was a thing I needed to understand the shape of.

"It doesn't touch me," he said, too fast. "I'm a contractor. I sold them rope and ate at their tables because that is the trade." He turned the ring once. "But you sit close enough to a fire and your beard smells of smoke whether you lit it or not, and a man drawing a line through the rolls does not stop to ask whose beard caught honest. So. I'm tucking in my edges. I'm calling in what's owed me quiet and owing nobody loud, and I'd give you the same advice if I thought you'd take it, which I don't, because you've never in your life known when to be small." He looked at the strap at my shoulder, the soldier's strap, and his face did a thing I could not read. "You came back smelling of the army. That's worse than nothing this month. Whose was it."

"A friend's."

"Friends," Macer said, like the word cost him, and did not ask the rest. And then, because he was Macer, and because the bond was a real thing even bent the way the Caelian had bent it, he said, not looking at me, "If they come to my door for you, Thulean, I will tell them where you are. You know that. I've a wife of my own and an estate and a name to keep alive. I'm too old to be brave at someone else's funeral." He turned the ring. "But I'll tell them slow. I'll tell them at the speed of an old man's memory. That's what I've got to give you and it's more than I gave you on the Caelian, and we both know it, so don't make me say more about that than that."

It was the most honest thing he had ever said to me, and it was a coward's promise, and I took it, because it was true and because slow was a real gift in a city where the only currency left was time.

---

I walked back to the wharf in the late light and the city did the thing again, the lean against nothing, the foot for a step that was not there. Scaeva, whose man I was, did not know whose man he was. Macer would betray me slowly. The colleges held a door into everything I had built, and the lock at the top of the city was being changed by a wife and a soldier I had never seen, settling scores against names I had heard for the first time that afternoon. That was my standing. I had come five weeks across the sea to stand on it.

At the yard the lamps were lit and Lucanus, who did not know me, fell asleep in the corner over a wooden horse while the grown people talked low, and at some point Marcia carried him to bed and I watched her do it, my son carried past a father he had not chosen and would have to learn, and I thought, with the cold clear part of me the war had ground sharp, that everything in this room was a thing I could lose. The press. The boy. The woman. The figures loose in the city. All of it propped, for nineteen years, on a dead man's favor, and the favor was being burned on the Campus, and the man who would decide whether the law that held my life still held it was a name on fresh stone and a beard I half-remembered being insulted in a forum, somewhere east of here, on a road, coming.

I went up to the flat roof of the shed where I sometimes went to think, where the river wind came off the water and you could see the bend of the Tiber going dark and the city behind it putting out its lamps house by house. I stood there a long while. The smoke from the mountain at Puteoli I had left five weeks behind me, and there was no smoke here, only the ordinary haze of a city cooking its supper, but I stood at the rail of that roof the way I had stood at the rail of the boat, looking the one way I had to look, west and then up the road east of it where a reign I knew nothing of was walking toward us at the speed of an army, and I could no more turn it back than I could turn back the tide I had ridden in on, and I had not the first idea, standing there, what it would want of me, or whether the wanting would leave me alive.

---

## PART FIVE — Starting Over

---

# Chapter 32: Proclaimed at Antioch

The first I heard of what the boy meant to do, I heard from a man selling fish.

It was the cold end of winter, the wharf greasy with a thaw that had no warmth in it, and I had gone down for no better reason than that the press had run out of a thing the press needed and I wanted to walk while it was fetched. There is a fish-seller near the Aemilian bridge who keeps his prices on a slate in the new figures, has done for years, one of the first men in Rome to do it without my asking him to, and I stop there sometimes the way you go back to look at a wall you helped lay. He was not minding his slate that morning. He was telling the man beside him, across a tray of greying mullet, that the new one was giving it all back.

"All of it," he said. "Armenia. The river country. The lot. Pulling the legions home off the far side of everything Caesar took, and the men out there are to march back and pretend they were never there."

The other man said something about the cost of getting there, the men buried getting there, and the fish-seller said the new one had a different idea of what a thing was worth, and went back to his fish, and I stood with my hands on the cold edge of his stall and could not for a moment make my fingers let go of it.

I had heard a great many things since I came home. The city ran on talk that winter the way a mill runs on water, and most of it turned the wheel and ground nothing. A fish-seller is not a dispatch. But he had the names in the right order, and the right scorn, and a man at a fish-stall does not invent Armenia.

I bought a fish I did not want and carried it back up to the yard and did not taste it.

---

What the fish-seller had I got better the next day, and worse, from men who knew. It came in pieces all that spring, the way news comes when the source of it is a thousand miles off and arrives through the mouths of soldiers and the letters of frightened men, and the pieces did not flatter the man who had ordered it. He was abandoning the east. Not losing it, which a city forgives a young emperor, the gods send a bad year. Giving it. The conquests the old man had spent his last strength taking, the cities, the rivers with the unpronounceable names, the new province they had cut their coins to celebrate before the ink on the dispatches was dry, the boy was handing back across the line to the Parthians as a man hands back a tool he has decided he never needed. The border was to come home to the river it had always been before the old man pushed it. The thing was to be done quietly and quickly and as if it were obvious, which was the part that turned the conquest men purple, because to do a thing quietly is to say it was never worth shouting about, and they had shouted about it for four years.

I went down to the Forum to feel the size of it.

You could feel it. The men who had made their careers carrying Caesar east stood about in their good wool with the particular stillness of men who have been told the road they walked is to be unbuilt behind them. A triumph had been voted, was still owed, would still be held, for a war whose ground was at that moment being given away, and nobody could decide whether to celebrate the taking or mourn the leaving, so they did a thing in between that looked like indigestion. I heard a senator's freedman, a sleek one I half knew, say to another that it was sound generalship, that the old man had reached past his arm and the new one was only drawing the arm back to the body where it could be defended, and the words were reasonable and the man saying them had the face of someone reciting a thing he had been handed that morning and did not yet dare disbelieve. The reasonable men said it was reasonable. The army men said nothing in the Forum and a great deal in the wine shops, where I did not go, because a freedman with a Trajan name drinking in an army wine shop the spring the army was being told its war was a mistake is a man writing his own line in a clerk's roll.

I had said it myself. That was the thing I could not get my hands off of, like the cold edge of the fish-stall. Two winters back, sitting in a tent behind Antioch with a friend's fever-smell still in my clothes, I had thought, cold and clear, that somebody was going to have to let the conquered ground go, that the east was a dead hand closing on sand, that no emperor with sense would keep paying men into that country to hold cities that would not stay knelt. I had thought it the way you think a true thing you are ashamed of. And now a man I had never seen, with no idea I existed, sat down at the start of his reign and did the exact arithmetic I had done in the dark and reached the exact total and acted on it with a stroke of a pen, and was right, and I knew he was right, and I wanted to be sick.

Because Celer had died on a road in that country. He had taken a thrust below the ribs holding a supply road open to a province, and the wound had gone bad on the way to me, and I had cleaned it with my own hands and packed it and posca'd it and dressed it and he had died anyway, before dawn, with no last word, while I held the cup. He had held a road to a place. And the place was now to be given back, the road abandoned, the province struck off, as if the line had never been drawn there, as if a man at a desk had rubbed it out with his thumb. He was right to do it. The road went nowhere worth a man's life. I had known that road went nowhere before the boy was ever told he was emperor. None of that put one breath back in Celer, and all of it made his death a thing the empire had now officially decided it had not needed.

I wore the strap at my shoulder, where my hand found it. I worked the buckle of it with my thumb while the freedman talked sound generalship, the way Macer worked his ring, and I did not say anything to anyone, and I went home.

---

Heras I went to because Heras was Greek, and the new emperor, by every account that reached the wharf, loved Greek things the way the old one had loved roads.

He was past sixty now, sixty-four or five, dried down the way men dry rather than soften, his hands as clean as they had been the day he first cut my rope of an argument out from under me in a cell. He had a fire going small and a cup of something heated, and he let me sit a long time before he made me talk, which is a courtesy he extends to no one he does not love and would die before admitting.

"So," he said at last. "You have heard. The barbarian gives the east away, and the Greek-lover sits in the city your old man built, and you do not know which way to stand. You have the face of a man doing sums that will not come out even."

"Tell me about him."

"I have never met him." He turned the cup. "I am from Pergamon, Daniel, not from his bedchamber. What I have is what every Greek in this city has, which is gossip and pride, and we Greeks dress our gossip in better cloth than your Romans do, but it is the same washing under the cloth." He drank. "Still. You want it, you may have it. He is a reader. The old man read what a contract required and no more, and was honest about it, and built things you could walk on. This one reads for the pleasure of it. He composes. He has opinions about poets dead three hundred years and he will tell you the wrong opinion is an offense against him personally. They say he speaks Greek better than half the men in Athens and is prouder of it than any man in Athens would be, because a man born to a thing wears it loose and a man who climbed to it wears it buttoned to the throat."

"That sounds like a small thing to be afraid of."

"You did not let me finish." He held up a clean finger. "The poet is the part the dinner tables love. Under it there is a different animal. He has soldiered, hard, on the cold rivers, and the men who soldiered with him do not call him soft, they call him exact. He misses nothing and he forgives the man who made him notice the miss least of all. You are thinking, a philosopher, a man of books, this is a man I can talk to. Daniel. The most dangerous master a clever man ever serves is the one clever enough to know exactly how clever the servant is being." He set the cup down. "The old soldier could be flattered, because a flattered general thinks the flatterer a fool he can use. This one cannot be flattered, because he has read every flattery ever written and graded them. You will have to be plainer with him than you have ever been with anyone, and plain is the one thing you have never once been since I have known you."

I let that go by, because it was true and there was no profit in saying so.

"He gave the east away," I said. "You think that's sense or weakness."

"I think," Heras said, "that he looked at a map and saw what it cost to hold and what it returned, and decided to keep the part that pays. Which is sense. And I think a Roman of the old conquering kind cannot tell the difference between sense and weakness, because to him the holding is the point and the cost is glory, and a man who counts the cost out loud has insulted the dead." He looked at me, and there was the old cut in it, the skeptic's blade he had never let dull. "It is the thing you have been telling this city for twenty years, you understand. Count the cost. Do the thing that pays. You taught a fish-seller to keep his prices in marks that cannot be cheated and you taught a quartermaster to weigh men against water. And now there is an emperor who counts the cost of an empire the way you count the cost of a wagon, and you sit in my room looking as though the gods had played a joke on you. Perhaps they have. It is the joke they specialize in. They give you the thing you asked for and they wait to see your face."

He was not wrong, and I had not come to be told he was not wrong. But there was a use in it, sharp under the salt. A man who counts. A man who will keep the part that pays and let the rest go without a tear for the men who took it.

I sat with that a while, turning Celer's buckle, and Heras let me.

"He won't want to expand," I said, mostly to myself.

"He has just told the world he won't, at the cost of looking like a coward to every general the old man left him. A man does not pay that price to lie. No. He wants to pull the arms in to the body and lock the doors and walk the rooms of what he has and put them in order. He is a man, I think, who would rather have a small house perfectly kept than a great one half built and leaking." Heras picked the cup up again. "Why does that frighten you more than the giving-away did? You have gone a color."

I had not meant to go a color. I got up and put a log on his small fire that did not need a log, to have my back to him a moment.

Because everything I had, I thought. Everything I had built and shown and sold and frightened men with was a tool for the great half-built leaking house. The eye in the sky was for scouting an enemy you meant to march on. The bursting tube was an argument for a weapon you meant to point at a wall you meant to take. The figures that fed a quartermaster fed an army that was going somewhere. The whole of me, the whole of the thing I had made myself into in this city, was a man who made the reaching out cheaper and surer and more terrible. I had sold Rome a better way to push.

And the man on the road did not want to push. He wanted to pull in.

I had spent twenty years becoming exactly the wrong man for the emperor I was about to have.

"Daniel," Heras said behind me. "The fire is lit. Sit down before you set the roof on it, as is your custom."

---

I did not say any of that to Heras. I said it, in the end, to Marcia, because Marcia is the only person I have ever been able to say the cold things to without dressing them, she having no patience for the cloth Heras spoke of.

She heard me out in the tally-room with the lying clock dripping, and when I had laid it out, the eye, the tube, the figures, all of it built to serve a reaching that the new reign did not want, she did not waste a breath comforting me.

"Then half of what you are is worth nothing to him," she said. "Worse than nothing. It's a smell." She had her tablet and for once she wrote on it, a short column, the way she works a thing by making it a list. "Say it the way a man drawing up the rolls says it. Who is Marcus Ulpius Danihel. He is the dead emperor's marvel. He wears the dead emperor's name. He flew the dead emperor's wars and fed the dead emperor's wars and made the dead emperor a thing that burst and threw a stone across a river for the dead emperor's officers to clap at. Every line of it has the dead man's name on it. You came to this city as the old man's wonder and you have spent your favor being useful to the kind of empire this new one has just told the world he does not want."

"Yes," I said.

"A new prefect drawing a line through the rolls," she said, "looking for the old regime's men, will not have to look hard for you. You're at the top of the page in large letters." She set the stylus down. "So. We are not going to sell him a balloon."

It was the first thing anyone had said in a month that had a forward in it instead of a behind, and I sat up to it like a dog to a door.

"What do we sell a man who wants to keep the part that pays," she said, and it was not a question to me, it was Marcia turning a thing she had already started turning. "Not a way to take more. He has too much already, that's the whole of his complaint. A way to be worth more without being bigger. A way to hold what he keeps so it holds harder. Clean water in a city is worth more to a man who means to stay home and govern it than a flying basket is to a man who never means to march again. The figures that cannot be cheated are worth more to a treasury settling thirty years of war debt than to a quartermaster on the move. The press." She touched the wall, the old habit, the way she touches a thing to weigh it. "A man who reads. A man who composes. A man proud of his Greek and prouder of his judgment, who would rather have a small house perfectly kept. Daniel. You have spent five years making it cheaper to put a true thing on a page fifty times over and you have been selling it to schoolmasters for an as. There is an emperor coming who reads."

"He owes me nothing," I said. "The old man owed me. He'd seen me drink foul water to prove a filter. This one has never heard my name except as a thing in a roll he wants to be rid of."

"Then we make the name worth keeping for what it does for his house, and not for what it did for the dead one's wars." She wrote one more line. "We have a year, near enough, before he walks through the gate. A year is a thing. We do not use it sitting on a roof being sad about a soldier, which is what you have been doing, and don't tell me you haven't, you've worn that buckle to a shine." She did not look up when she said the part about the buckle, which was her mercy. "We use it to become a different kind of useful before he arrives. And we hope to every god the colleges have a hook in that he does not arrive in a mood to clear the table of everything the old man set on it."

I looked at her list. It was four lines and it was the first map of any of this I had been given, drawn cold by a woman who had never been east of Ostia, and it was righter than anything I had thought up in five weeks at sea.

There was one thing on it I did not say to her. I had carried it home in my head from the supply camps, half-formed and recoiled from, the thing about the western lands on the map I had drawn for a magistrate's wax twenty years gone, the two badly-drawn continents across the blank ocean. There was a version of me that wanted to take the empire's whole bottomless reaching and turn it the one direction it had never reached, out west, across the water, away from the people it ground up in the east. I had told it to no one. I had nearly told it to Celer and the chance had died with him. And sitting in the tally-room with Marcia's four cold lines in front of me, I turned it over once more and set it down again, because the whole of her point, the whole of Heras's animal, was a man who wanted to pull the arms in to the body. You do not sell a man who is locking his doors a map to a country a year's sail past the edge of the sea. It was the wrong gift for the wrong emperor. It fit nothing he had told the world he was. I put it back where I kept it, under the floor of my head with the dead glass and the ratios, a thing I had no door for yet.

"What's the year for," I said.

"To make you the kind of man," Marcia said, "that a careful emperor keeps because dropping him would cost more than feeding him. We have a press, an enemy who's been waiting for the lock to change, a senator who doesn't know whose man he is, and a college that holds a door into everything we own. And we have an emperor coming who counts. So we are going to do the one thing this house has always done. We are going to be the cheapest way to keep something that the man with the money has already decided he wants to keep." She stood. "Eat. The boy asked for you twice today. He's decided you're his because you let him hold the strap. Don't undecide it."

---

Lucanus had the buckle when I came out, sitting on the floor of the press room turning it the way I turn it, the way Macer turns his ring, learning my one nervous trick off me without either of us teaching it, and he held it up when he saw me, not to give it back, to show me he had it, the way a child shows you a thing to make you part of having it.

"Whose was it," he said. He had learned the question off the grown people that month. It was the question the whole city was asking at every door.

"A soldier's," I said. "A friend of mine."

He thought about that with Marcia's square weighing face. "Is he coming," he said. Meaning, I think, the friend, and I understood the whole city was in the boy's small mouth, asking when the one who was coming would come, and I sat down on the floor of the press room beside my son who had decided I was his, in the ink smell, under a year that had not arrived yet, and I told him no, the soldier was not coming, but somebody was, and I did not yet know what to make for the one who was, and that was the truest thing I said to anyone all that long cold spring, and the only person I could say it to was five and turning a dead man's buckle in the lamplight, asking when.

---

# Chapter 33: The Four Consulars

The first I heard, I did not hear it. I felt the city stop talking.

It came on over a span of days the way a smell comes into a room before you find the dead thing making it. The wharf was loud as it always was, carters cursing and gulls fighting over guts, and then it was loud in a way that had the wrong shape to it, men talking close and short and not laughing at the ends of things the way men laugh to show a stranger they mean him no harm. The bread-woman who would not gossip in the winter now would not take the coin from your hand without looking at who was behind you in the line. A grain-factor I had dealt with for years, a fat easy man who told the same three jokes, came to settle an account and said nothing else, and left a thing he had brought to give me sitting in his own basket, having decided on the walk over that it was a bad year to be seen giving the Thulean a gift.

I did not know the names yet. I knew the weather of them. The whole of Rome had taken a step back from every doorway at once, and I stood in the middle of it not knowing which way the wind that moved them was blowing, which is the worst place a frightened man can stand, because he cannot even make himself small in the right direction.

---

Marcia had the names before I did. She had them the way she had everything, off the rolls and off the men who kept the rolls, the factors and the bank-table men who lend at the bridge and hear a thing the morning it is true.

"Four," she said. We were in the tally-room with the door shut, which we did not do, the lying clock dripping into its bowl. "Four of the consulars. Killed."

I did not understand the word at first the way she meant it. I knew consulars. Men who had held the consulship, the top of the heap, the names you put on a year. I had stood in rooms with them and not been spoken to.

"Tried?" I said. It was a fool's question. I asked it because I wanted the answer to be yes.

"No." She wrote nothing. That was how I knew. Marcia writes when a thing can be worked. "Taken and killed. On the prefect's word. Attianus. While the boy is still on the road." She said the names the way you set down stones, to be sure of the count. Palma. Celsus. A third I had heard in the winter as a soldier's name, the Moor, the one the army out east had loved, Quietus. A fourth she gave and I lost, a long name, Nigrinus, that I could not have picked out of a crowd if you had paid me, and that is the one that frightened me most, because I understood I could die for being on the wrong side of a man whose name I could not even hold in my head.

"For what," I said.

"A plot." She let the word sit there with us. "There is always a plot. Four of the best men of the dead emperor's making, and a plot was found, and they were dealt with before the new one is even in the city to be asked whether he wanted it done." She put her two hands flat on the table, which she does when she is making herself be still. "This is the house being cleaned before the master gets home. By the steward, so the master's hands are clean of it. Whether the boy ordered it or will arrive and grieve over it and lay it at Attianus's door does not matter to the men in the ground. It is being done by men with no name on the year who want to be useful to the one coming, and a man trying to be useful to a new master before the master arrives is the most dangerous animal there is, because he has to guess what will please, and he guesses big."

I had built a life on guessing what would please a man with power. It is the only trade I have ever truly had.

"They were senators," I said. "Consulars. I'm a freedman who flew a bag."

"You're a freedman who flew a bag," she said, "with the dead emperor's own name. Whose work is dedicated into a god's keeping with a college that can ask a question any day it likes. Who is, on three rolls I could fetch you before the lamp burns down, written as the man of a senator of the conquest party." She picked the stylus up, and put it down again without writing, which I had never once seen her do. "They are not coming for you. You are not a fish that size.
