But the net does not care what it catches up on the way to the fish it wants. And there is a man in this city who has wanted you in a net for fifteen years and now has a season where nobody is asking too closely why a thing dies."

She meant Crispus. She did not need to name him. We had both lived under that name long enough that it had stopped needing saying, the way you stop saying the name of the river when everyone in the room is wet.

---

I went to Scaeva.

Scaeva had leaned in on the Caelian and tipped a room and made me his. I had hated the public collar of it, *Scaeva's man*, but a collar that a strong hand holds the other end of is a thing you are glad of when the dogs are out. He held my sulfur secret, and we held debts to each other, and the arithmetic of all that was tangled but it came to a thing I could lean on, or I had thought it did, for five years.

I went at the dawn hour, with the clients, in a clean toga, the way a client goes. His house on the Quirinal stood open the way a great man's door stands open in the morning, to show the world how many men want a word.

I was not let in.

It was done so that no one could ever point at the doing of it. The doorman knew me. He had taken my name into that house a dozen times. He looked at me as though working out where he had seen the face, and then a freedman came out, a sleek young one I half knew from the dinners, with a face arranged into regret, and he did not take my hand.

"The senator is not receiving this morning," he said.

"I'll wait," I said. There were thirty men waiting. Waiting is what the dawn is for.

"He is not receiving," the freedman said again, and there was a small weight on the second saying that I understood in my body before my head caught it up, "for some while. He has a great deal of business this season. He has asked that those who can manage their own affairs manage them, and not trouble the house until the city is quieter." He looked, very briefly, at the purple I did not wear and could never wear, at the freedman's clothes under my citizen's toga, and the regret on his face did not change at all, which was how I knew it had never been regret. "I'll tell him you came. He'll be glad you're well."

Glad I was well. A man tells you he is glad you are well when he has decided he will not stand between you and not being well.

I sent a tablet, two days on. A short one, nothing in it a clerk could read sideways, only that I would value a quarter of an hour on a matter of the house's business, the river-gate business, said in the words we had used for years. It came back, in the hand of a slave I did not know, the seal unbroken, with a word from the freedman that the senator's affairs at the gate were settled and would need no further attention from me, and that he advised me, as a friend advises a friend, to attend to my own and trouble no one with what could be carried alone.

The river-gate business was the sulfur. *Settled and would need no further attention from me.* He was telling me, in the clean words of a man who does not put a knife in writing, that the favor was withdrawn, the gate no longer smoothed. And the other thing under it, which a man hears with the back of his neck. *Trouble no one.* You are not my man. You were never my man. If a clerk drawing a line through the rolls comes to this door and asks was the Thulean yours, this door will be surprised to hear the name.

He had not denounced me. A denunciation that season was a coin you could spend on your own safety, and he did not spend it. He let go of the rope and stepped back and dusted his hands where men could see him do it, so that anyone watching the senator Scaeva that spring would see a man with no barbarian on his leash. He was a conquest-party man with a dead emperor's wars all over his career, exactly the kind the net was out for, and he had looked at me and seen a smell on his own clothes, and taken off the coat. I had spent two months drawing up the very same sums about myself in Marcia's tally-room. I had just not yet thought of myself as a smell on someone else's coat.

I walked home with my own toga feeling like a costume a child had put on, and did not go down the wide way, and kept finding myself reading the faces of men coming the other way to see whether any of them were reading mine.

---

Macer I went to last, because Macer was the one I could least afford to lose, which is the order a coward does things in, saving the worst for when there is nothing else to try.

Twenty years I had gone to that door, the door of the man who owned me, then of my patron, and somewhere in there, without either of us ever saying a soft word about it, it had become a door I went to the way you go to a thing that will be where you left it. He was past seventy now and built like a beam that has carried a roof so long the roof would fall before the beam did. He kept me standing in the atrium, which he had never done, and came out himself, which he also had never done, he sent the steward, and the two wrong things together told me before he opened his mouth.

"You shouldn't have come up the hill," he said. No *boy*. No *Thulean*. He had a way of saying those that was the nearest thing to fondness he let himself, and they were gone, and the room was colder for the lack of them than for anything he said after.

"I needed to talk to you."

"I know what you need." He turned his ring, the heavy gold one, his thumb working it the way it always worked it, and I thought of Lucanus on the press-room floor turning the dead man's buckle the same way, learning a frightened man's trick off the men around him. "You need an old man with frontier names in his account-books to stand in a door this spring and say the Thulean is a good fellow, leave him be. That's what you need." He stopped turning the ring. "I'm not going to do it."

"I didn't ask you to."

"You came up the hill. That's the asking." He came a step nearer and dropped his voice, and that was the worst of it, because Macer does not drop his voice, Macer says the crude thing at the volume of a man selling timber, and a Macer who has learned to be quiet is a Macer who has been made afraid, and I had only seen the edge of that in the winter and now I was seeing the whole of it. "Listen to me, because I'm going to say it once and then you're going to go home the back way. There are four of the best men in this city in the river or under quicklime this month, not one of them tried, and I am a leather-contractor whose name is on the legion rolls for thirty years of feeding the dead man's army. I am exactly the size of fish a hungry steward keeps for the new master. I have spent the winter making myself small and dull and loyal to nobody anyone can name, and I am not undoing it by being seen a friend to the one man in Rome whose every brick has the dead emperor's name baked into it."

"Titus." I had not called him that in years.

"Don't." He said it flat. "I sold you to Caesar fair and I was glad, you know I was glad, you ungrateful barbarian. I'm not your enemy. If a man comes to my door and asks was the Thulean ever mine, I'll tell him the truth, that the emperor bought you off me a long time ago and I've had nothing to do with your sky-bags and your burning tubes since, and that's no lie, and it'll cost you nothing and me nothing, which is the only kind of true thing worth telling this year." He looked at me, and there was an old man behind the fear of his, one who had taken a cheap oddity for less than a season's mules and watched it turn into this. "But I will not burn for you. I've got a household and grandsons and a name to leave them and I am not setting a torch to all of it to keep one clever foreigner out of a net he flew himself into the middle of. So stop coming to my door. Make yourself small. Make yourself dull. Make yourself the kind of nothing nobody bothers to denounce, because that, boy," and the *boy* came out then, the only one, and it landed like a hand on the back of the neck, not unkind, "is the one thing that's going to save you, and the one thing you have never once in your life known how to be."

He turned the ring once more. Then he had the steward show me out, by the back, through the yard where the hides were stacked, into a lane that smelled of tanning, and the door shut behind me with the ordinary sound a door makes.

He had warned me. That was the thing I carried down the hill, turning it the way I turned the buckle. He had looked at the smell of me and chosen not to denounce, only to warn, and the difference between those two things was the whole of the bond we had, worn to a wire, and the wire had held. It was not nothing. It only felt like nothing, in the lane, with the door shut.

---

The scare came at the yard, eleven days later, and it came in through the front, smiling, which is how it comes.

A man I did not know wanted his account looked at, got up as a small importer, oils and the like, with a tablet of his own and a slave behind him, and a manner that was a hair too easy, the ease of a man who has practiced it. Marcia took him, because Marcia takes the front, and I was at the back of the press room and saw the shape of him through the door and felt my skin go before I knew why. He was not buying oil. His questions wandered. He admired the press, asked could he see it work, asked what we printed, asked whether it was true the Thulean's marks were said before a god each spring on the Aventine, asked it the way a man asks a thing he already knows, to watch your face while you give it. He let drop that a friend of his in the colleges had said an interesting thing about the dedication, about whether everything off the Thulean's press fell rightly under the goddess's keeping or whether some of it went somewhere the rite did not cover. He used the word *impiety* once, lightly, the way you set a coin on a table to see if the other man's eyes go to it.

I did not let my eyes go to it. I do not know how. I think I was too frightened to do anything but the thing I had done my whole life, which is to put a stupid pleasant Thulean over my face and stand behind it.

Marcia was faster than I was, and colder, and magnificent in the way that does not look like anything, which is the only way that works. She did not throw him out. She bored him. She answered everything long and dull, the prices of cloth, the cost of pearwood, the schoolmasters who bought the times-tables, the exact denarius the heifer had cost on the Aventine and who the priest was and how the rite went and how proud the house was, how very proud, to have its little marks under the goddess's eye, and would the gentleman like a sheet, here, a gift, a true thing about wheat-weights any honest grain-man could use. She buried him in the harmless until the harm could not breathe in it. The press she walked him to was the school press; the man who ran the other one, the one Eros kept the cut blocks for, had been sent on an errand the moment Marcia heard the front-door manner; and the room where I keep the work that is mine, the box under the floor, the leaves in the homeland script with the ratios in them, was shut, with a girl sitting against the door of it mending a basket as if she had sat there all her life.

That was Tyche. She had moved the box the night the city went quiet, without being told, and she sat against the shut door mending a basket with her short-cut hair and her face that gives away exactly nothing, a freedwoman of no account at all, and she let the oil-man's slave glance over her head into the room and see a girl and a basket and shelves of ordinary scrolls, and she did not look up.

The man left. He took the gift sheet. He said he would think about the oil. We never saw him again, and I never learned for certain whose he was, the colleges' or the prefect's or Crispus's or some little delator's own, a man who sniffed at doors on his own account hoping to sell what he found to whoever would buy that season, which was the worst kind, because he was hungry and had no master to be reasoned with.

I did not win it. I did not best him with a clever word. Marcia bored him and Tyche hid the dangerous half of my whole life behind a basket and a girl's blank face, and the rest was the pure dumb luck that he found, on the day he came, a dull cloth-house with a school-press and a proud little rite and no purchase for a man's hand to close on. If he had come the week before, with the second press running and the wrong leaves out, I would have had no clever word for that either. I sat on the floor of the press room after, my heart going like a fist on a door.

For a month after, I made myself small the way Macer had told me to. I sent no tablets to senators. I let the contest rite be prepared exactly to the colleges' liking and stood at the back of my own thing and thanked the goddess in the words a priest gave me, and bowed lower than I had ever bowed. I took my name off things. Marcia put the new contracts under hers, *Marcia, liberta*, the widow of the merchant Caepio, a respectable nobody of a name, and where mine had been the security on a thing she found another to be it. I went down to the wharf in a plain tunic and let men forget they had been afraid to be seen near me, by giving them less of me to be seen near.

I had drunk foul water in front of an emperor to make that name. Now I was teaching myself to walk down a lane in such a way that no one would think to put the name to me, and I did it, because a man who will not be a beetle when the boot is coming down is a dead one.

---

The press was running when I came up from the wharf in my plain tunic at the end of it, the school press, the only one I dared run that spring, putting wheat-weights and times-tables onto cheap gray sheets for an as apiece, the smallest and safest thing the house made.

I had caught myself reaching for Heras out of habit and stopped. I had stopped going to his door too, because a man who loved me was a thing I could no longer afford to be seen to have. Celer was three years in the eastern ground for a province struck off a map. Scaeva had taken off the coat. Macer had warned me through a shut door, which was the most a friend had given me in a month. No emperor knew my name except as a line a tired clerk wanted to draw through. The thing I had spent twenty years building, the un-ignorable Thulean who flew and counted and burst tubes for great men, was the very thing that could get me killed, and the only freedom I had left was the freedom to be forgotten, which I now wanted with my whole body, the way I had once wanted to be seen.

Lucanus came and leaned on me while I checked a sheet. He had the buckle. He always had the buckle now. He was sounding out the figures under his finger, slow, getting the seven and the one the wrong way round the way they all did at first.

"That's a seven," I said, and turned his finger to it.

He fixed it and went on counting, and did not ask whose anything was, because he had not heard a frightened thing said over his head that week, we had been careful, and a child who hears no fear asks no questions about it. He counted the sheet to the bottom and got it right and looked up to be told so, and I told him, and he leaned his whole small weight against my arm the way they do when they have decided the leaning is allowed, and went back to the top to do it again.

I let him count, and made myself as small and dull and nothing as a man can make himself while a child counts against his arm, and the press came down, and came down, putting the safe true small thing onto the cheap gray page, an as at a time, in a city that had shut its doors, and no one was coming up the hill to mine, which was the thing I had wanted. The room with the one press running was about the size of a cell, and I knew exactly how that compared, having begun in one.

---

# Chapter 34: From Scratch

The man who flew was no use to me anymore, so I went looking for work as the man who could add.

The autumn the new emperor finally came up the road into the city, I started again at the bottom of doors I would not have crossed the street for five years before. Not senators; I had learned what a senator's open door was worth that spring. I went to the men who keep the men who keep the rolls. Procurators' freedmen. Clerks of bureaus whose names I had to ask twice and still got wrong. Functionaries with a half-room and a chest of tablets, who in the old days would have crossed a yard to be presented to the Thulean and now kept me standing while they finished a column of figures, because keeping a famous man standing while you finish a column is one of the few pleasures a small office allows.

I learned the geography of waiting. The portico, where the cold comes up through the stone into the soles of your feet and you keep shifting your weight and pretending you are not. The inner room you sometimes reach, where the clerk talks past you to the doorway, watching for someone better. The message left and not answered, which is its own room you wait in for days without leaving your house. I was thirty-seven, and my knees had begun to keep their own account of the cold. Day after day I was told the procurator was at his villa, that the bureau did not see private men in the morning, that my matter had been noted and would be raised at a convenient time, which is the Roman for never.

---

The thing that kept the lamps lit while I stood in porticoes being nobody was that the enterprise no longer needed me to be anybody.

Marcia had seen to that, the spring the city went quiet. The contracts came under her name, *Marcia, liberta*, the widow of Caepio, a respectable nothing on the rolls, a woman who factored cloth and rented dry storage and had a little press that printed schoolmasters' sheets. The new figures were hers to license. The contest belonged on paper to nobody, dedicated to a goddess, run by a widow and a freedwoman, which the men who watched such things found beneath their watching. I had spent twenty years making my name the thing everything hung from, and now the most useful thing about me was that my name was nowhere on it. It worked because the two of them were better at it than I had ever been, and I had been good.

I came back from a wasted morning at the grain-bureau door and found them in the tally-room with a carter who was being loud about a short load. Marcia let him be loud. She had the two day-books open side by side, hers and the wharf's, both in the new figures, and when he ran out of breath she laid her ink-stained finger on a line.

"Eleven loads booked in. Nine delivered to weight. One short by a third, which I am charging you. The eleventh you will tell me about now or I will have Tyche find it, and Tyche enjoys finding things, which you will not enjoy."

The carter looked at Tyche. She was at the wall with a tablet and did not look up from it. "It went out the Ostian gate the same morning under a different bill," she said, in the flat clean city Latin she has never once slowed for anyone. "Same mules, same driver. One man paid twice for one load." She turned the tablet around, the columns ranked and totaled. "He's not arguing the third. He's arguing the eleventh, and the eleventh is the one that hangs him."

The carter paid the third and was gone inside the time it would have taken me to find the right tablet.

I said something with my name in it, about how the contract had been mine before it was hers. Marcia put the books square on the table with the edges lined up, which is what she does instead of raising her voice. "It was yours. And yours is a word that gets a man's house looked at this year. So it is mine, and the bureau when it comes will see a widow with clean accounts and no dead emperor in her family. You can have your name back when having it is not a thing men die of." She picked up the stylus and went back to the column. "Go wash your feet. You smell like the portico."

Tyche, at the wall, said nothing, but the corner of her mouth did the thing that is the closest she comes to a smile in a room where there is work to be done. She had moved the box again that month, the leaves in the homeland script, the ratios only she could read, and had not told me where. I had stopped needing to know, and minded it less than a younger man would have.

---

I went to Heras because I could not afford to, and could afford the staying away even less.

I had kept away from his door for the better part of a year. I told myself it was protection. Heras was old, sixty-five and more, his hands no longer steady on the lancet, his household small, and to be seen my friend could cost him in that season. It was also the plain cowardice of not wanting to see his face when I came at last to ask. I had let the months go by trading that against my need, and my need had won.

He was in his garden, a strip behind the house with a fig tree and pots of the herbs he still grew himself though he could have bought them. He watched me come through the gate and did not get up.

"The Thulean remembers the road to my house," he said. "I had a wager with myself about the season. I have lost; I said winter."

"Heras."

"Sit. You look like a man who has been standing." He poured posca without asking, the cheap sour stuff he drank to spite the wine his patients sent him. "You stayed away to keep the smell off me. I worked that out in the summer. It was thoughtful and it was insulting, and we will not discuss it. You have come to ask me for something. Let us get to it."

I told him the shape of it. That the work survived under Marcia's name but could go on printing schoolmasters' sheets for fifty years and never again get near a man who could decide anything. That the new figures were the one thing I had that a man cleaning up a war's debts might want, and that I could not get them in front of such a man, because every door to him was guarded by men whose whole art was keeping the marked out, and I was as marked as a man could be.

Heras let me finish. He ate a fig, slowly, which from him was a form of argument.

"You want a Greek," he said.

"I want a way in."

"Those are the same sentence, in this city, this year." He wiped his fingers on the cloth at his belt. "The boy emperor keeps Greek freedmen at his accounts, because they are literate and cheap and have nowhere to run, and the rolls of his treasury are thick with men who learned their letters in Pergamon and Alexandria and would no more trust a Roman senator than fly." He looked at me sideways. "You will appreciate the figure of speech."

"Do you know one."

"I know a man who knows one." He said it without weight, an old man passing a thing across a garden, and it was the most valuable sentence anyone had said to me in a year. "A patient. A liver I keep an eye on. He has a man he calls a nephew, the way we call any younger man from the same town a nephew when we are far from it. The younger one is in the *fiscus*." He gave the Latin its full dry weight. The treasury. "A clerk who keeps accounts of accounts. Not a great man, but one who is in the room when the great men decide how much there is, which this year is the only room that matters, because the boy has just struck a line through more debt than I can count and somebody has to know what is left." He picked up another fig and did not eat it. "I will not pretend I am glad to do this. You will go in clean, as a man with a method, and leave my name and the patient's out of it, and if it goes wrong you will not have heard the word *fiscus* in this garden. Agreed?"

"Agreed."

"Then I will send a word." He ate the fig after all. "It is good you came. I am too old to be left out of the dangerous part of your life. It is the only interesting part."

---

The man's name was Eudemus.

I had it from a slave who came to the wharf with no message, only a place and an hour, a side door behind the imperial offices, an hour after they closed and a small man could be seen by another small man without either being noticed.

He was younger than I expected, not yet forty, a long careful Greek face, ink to the second knuckle on both hands, a freedman of the emperor's house by the cut of his plain good tunic and the way he did not stand when I came in. Tablets in racks to the ceiling, the smell of wax and old papyrus, a single lamp trimmed close because oil is reckoned even in the emperor's bureau, especially this year.

"You are the Thulean," he said. In Greek. A test, I understood later. I answered in the careful slow Greek Heras had spent twenty years beating into me, and he heard the gaps and went on in Latin, which told me he wanted no part of pretending we were countrymen. "The one who flew. The one with the burning tube. The one whose marks the fish-sellers use. And the one who is on a list, in a chest, in a building I could walk to from here, as a freedman of the dead emperor's house with too many friends among the wrong men."

"I had friends among the wrong men," I said. "Most of them are dead or have stopped knowing me. It has been a quiet year for my friendships."

Something moved behind the long face. Not warmth. The recognition one careful man gives another who has said the true hard thing first, to save them both an hour.

"I have read your marks," he said. He pulled a tablet from the rack without looking, a man who knows where everything is. Two columns, the same sum done two ways, the Roman numerals crabbed and the new figures clean beside them, written in his own ink. "I taught myself in an evening. A child could learn it in a week. That is the first thing I want to say to you. It is not clever. A clever thing I would not trust, because a clever thing is a place to hide a lie." He set the tablet between us. "You gave Rome a thing that cannot hide one. Put the figures in a column and a man cannot bill a load twice without the column showing a hole where the load should be. With the old marks a clerk who knows them can make any sum say what his master wants, and his master is at his mercy, and the emperor is at the mercy of every clerk in every province." He folded his ink-stained hands. "Do you know what the emperor did this year, before he reached the city?"

"He cancelled debts to the treasury." I had heard it forty times, the last from a fish-seller. "All of it. Burned the bonds in the Forum, they say, so no one could quietly un-cancel them."

"He cancelled what was owed because he could not be sure what was owed," Eudemus said, and for the first time there was something under the dryness. "A war's worth of accounts in a dozen hands and a dozen scripts, every one cookable, none checkable against another. You cannot collect a debt you cannot prove. So you forgive the whole and look generous doing it, and then go looking for a way to keep accounts a man cannot cook." He looked at me. "And a physician's patient mentions to him, over a bad liver, that the Thulean's marks are exactly such a way."

We sat with that.

"I want to be plain with you," he said, "because you were plain first. I am not your friend. I will not be your patron; I have nothing to patronize you with. I will risk nothing for you. If you are taken up next month I will have met you once on a matter of figures and remembered nothing else." He turned the lamp a fraction, eking the wick. "What I can do is this. The men I serve want exactly the thing you happen to have made, for their own reasons, which have nothing to do with you. If your marks can be shown to do for a province's accounts what they do for a fish-seller's slate, a day will come, not soon, when a man higher than me asks where they came from, and I will be in the room, and I will say a name. Yours, or the widow's; probably the widow's, it travels better this year." He let that sit, the casual knowledge that my own name had become a liability a careful man would route around. "A name said in a room you will not be in, to men you have not met, on a day I do not control. It is a crack in a door. It is not the door."

"A crack in a door," I said, "is more than I have had since spring."

"Then we understand each other." He pushed a clean tablet and the stylus across the table. "Show me the marks doing something a clerk would call hard. Not a fish-seller's slate. A real account, with a remainder and a fraction and a running balance. If it is a trick, I will know inside an hour, and you will have wasted an evening, which is the cheapest thing you can waste this year."

I did the figures. My hand was not as steady as Tyche's; I flubbed a carry and caught it and ground it out, the way I had in front of Heras in a cell twenty years before, and he watched me catch my own error with something almost like approval, a man being shown the method has no hiding place in it even for the one who brought it. When I finished he looked at the column a long time, then scraped the wax flat with the back of the stylus, every line, until the tablet was blank.

"I keep nothing in your hand," he said. "I will redo it in mine. You will not come here again unless I send. You do not know my name."

"I do not know your name," I agreed, and went out into the dark with a crack in a door behind me, which I carried the way you carry a coal begged off a fire, cupped, afraid of the wind.

---

I told Marcia and Tyche that night, with the lamp trimmed close out of a habit I had caught off the clerk that very evening. Lucanus was asleep; the wharf had gone quiet but for the river working at the piles.

"A clerk," Marcia said, when I had given it to her flat. "A treasury secretary who will say a name he has not yet chosen, on a day he does not control, to men you will never meet." She had her hands flat on the table. "You walked home happy from that."

"I walked home with a crack in a door."

"You walked home with a clerk who was honest with you, which you are starving for, and which is not yet a way in." But she did not say it to crush it. "The figures are right for it. A treasury that cannot trust its own sums will buy a thing that cannot lie, and pay in the only coin we want, the attention of a man who can decide things." She moved a tablet a finger's width, lining the edge. "So we feed the clerk. Quietly. A real account, a province's worth, both ways, until the right liver gets reminded. That is months. That is patient, and you are not good at patient. Go to the bureau's portico, not the senators'. Be the dull man with the dull marks. Let the marks be the famous one."

Tyche had been quiet, building something behind her eyes the way she does.

"He'll want the thing under the marks, in the end," she said, and came down off the wall and sat, which she does not often do. "He doesn't want a better way to add. He wants to know the true shape of a thing too big to hold in one head, and he can't, because the treasury can't see its own size." She picked at the wax with a thumbnail. "And that's a different want than the dead emperor had. That one wanted more. This one wants to know exactly what he has, down to the last as, and stop there, and keep it. He's not a hungry man. He's a man taking an inventory."

I did not answer her at once, because something had turned over in me, a stone I had set down a year before. The map. The western lands across the blank ocean, the gambit I had carried back from the east and filed away, because you do not sell a man locking his doors a country a year's sail past the edge of the world.

But a man taking an inventory.

"Say that again," I said to Tyche. "About knowing the shape of a thing too big to hold in one head."

She said it again, slower, watching me.

"That's not adding," I said. "That's a map." The word came out too loud, and I brought it down. "It's the same want. The treasury can't see its own size; Rome can't see the world's. What if the thing no one has ever counted is the shape of the sea, and what lies past it?"

Marcia had gone very still, the way she goes when a number is doing something she has not yet decided she likes.

"You can't sell him a conquest," I said. The words came slow, finding their order. "He just paid to end one. But I was selling the wrong thing all along. You don't say, take the lands. You say, no one alive knows the true shape of the world. A man who knew it would be that man forever, the way Alexander is the man who reached the edge of the east. You sell him knowing. You sell him the inventory."

"And the army," Tyche said, ahead of me again.

"And the army." She had reached it before I had. "He's got legions gone idle, men he won't spend on conquest, and idle soldiers turn on the nearest thing. You point them at a voyage instead. Ships, timber-yards, rope by the mile, a mapping. A great reaching-out that takes no province and bleeds no city. You don't ask him to expand." I sat back down; I had got to my feet without meaning to. "You ask him to find out."

The lamp guttered. Nobody trimmed it.

"That," Marcia said at last, "is a different thing than you brought back from the east. That was a war you dressed as a voyage. This is a voyage. He might hear a voyage. From a man he trusts, which you are not, which is why we feed a clerk for a year first."

"I'm not pitching it," I said. "There's no one to pitch it to. I just see how it would have to be said, if there were ever a room to say it in." The smallness of that sat in me, a man who had worked out the perfect words for a door that did not exist. "It goes in the box. With the rest of the things I can't build yet."

Tyche got up, took down a fresh tablet, and sat to write, in the homeland script only she could read, setting the shape of it down before I forgot it.

"Knowing, not taking," she said, writing. "Prestige, not province. The idle legions at a yard, not a wall. I'll keep it where I keep the rest." The stylus moved. "You should sleep. You have a portico in the morning."

---

I stood at the bureau's portico most of the mornings of that winter, a widow's agent with a fistful of sheets. Twice a doorman who had turned me away in the spring let me past without a flicker, because the man he had turned away had been somebody and this one plainly was not. I sent no tablets to great men. I waited on a crack in a door behind a clerk whose name I did not know, and once a fortnight ruined good wax working out, again, the exact words for a voyage I had no one to propose it to. It was the smallest I had been since the cell, and the strange thing was the strength it gave me, the way going to ground gives an animal back its hearing.

One of those mornings the slave who had brought me the place and the hour came again with no message, only a place and an hour, the same side door. I sent the boy back with a word that I would come, washed the ink off my hands at the well, and looked at the long way still to go, and at the one finger I had got over the lip of the world after a year of clawing at the wood.

Lucanus was by the rack, sounding out a printed sheet, getting the seven and the one the wrong way round, the way he always did.

"That's a seven," I said, and turned his finger to it, and went out to keep my appointment with a man who was not my friend.

---

# Chapter 35: Hadrian

The crack in the door opened the way Eudemus had promised it would, which is to say not for me at all.

Past midwinter the slave came to the wharf a fourth time with a place and an hour and, this once, a word. A man higher than Eudemus had asked the question Eudemus had waited a year to be asked, where the new figures came from, and had not been satisfied with the widow's name, because the widow could not say how the marks behaved when a province's accounts ran to six scripts and as many lies. So the question had gone up another step, and another, until a hand somewhere closed on it and said, in effect, bring me the man who made them. Eudemus's word was four sentences and one of them mattered. *You will not say my name. You will not say the physician's. You came up through the rolls like everyone else.*

Marcia made the story with me by the lamp, going at it the way she went at a short load, looking for the seam that would split under weight. Not the balloon, dead weight now. Not the powder. Not the contest, which belonged to a goddess and a widow. The water if asked and not before. The figures were the whole of me, and I was to be a method walking on two legs and as little else as I could manage.

"He'll have had a freedman dig the chest out and read him the list. The flying barbarian, the burning tube, Caesar's wonder. You can't make that not true." She lined the edge of the tablet with her finger. "So you let him think he's caught you out, being more than figures, and you let him be pleased about it, and then you give him the figures anyway. A clever man's vanity is that he sees through you. Let him see through you to exactly the thing you want him looking at."

"And if it's the emperor."

I had not meant to say it. She did not pretend I had not.

"Then you will be twice as dull, and you will come home," she said, and put the stylus down. "You are bad at dull. Practice on Lucanus. He'll keep you humble."

---

It was not announced as the emperor, which is how I should have known.

The slave took me not to the Palatine but to a great old senatorial pile on the slope below it, the kind of place the new reign used for business too unfinished for the palace. I was made to wait, a finer waiting than the bureau porticoes, a colonnade off a garden where the cold still came up through the stone but a slave brought watered wine, and I counted the columns out of the old habit and got the same number twice and was almost steadied by it. Others waited. A pair of architects with rolls, arguing in undertones about a revetment. A Greek I took for a rhetor by his hands. We did not speak. The man beside you in such a room is a competitor for the same finite attention, and we took each other's measure in silence and gave nothing.

Then a chamberlain named me and I went in alone, and the protocol caught me at once, because I had braced for the soldier's plainness of fifteen years before and walked instead into a working library. Scrolls in racks to the ceiling, a model of something in cork and wax at one end, half a dozen men standing about it the way men stand about a thing they are being made to admire. And no obvious center to it, no thick-necked man drying his hands, so that I did the wrong thing and looked for the emperor among the standing men and did not find him, and a voice came from the table where a man sat bent over a drawing with a reed, not looking up.

"You bow to the room," the voice said, in Greek, amused, "when you do not know which of us is worth bowing to. It is a sound policy and it tells me you are not a courtier. Sit. No, there, where the light is on you. I like to see a man I am talking to."

I sat where the light was on me, which put him in shadow against the window, which I understood was the point of it.

---

He let me sit a while before he looked up, finishing whatever line he was drawing, and when he did the beard was the first thing, full and curled and oiled in the Greek manner on a Roman face, which tells you before he speaks that he has decided who he is and means you to find it remarkable. He was past forty, heavy through the shoulder, restless even seated, his hands wanting always the next thing while the first still went. There was a draughtsman's roll at his elbow under the scroll he had pretended to consult, and I registered all of it and connected none of it, busy with the small drowning arithmetic of a man who has just understood whose room he is in.

"You are the Thulean," he said. In Greek still, and quick, then, before I could answer, in Latin, watching which language I caught myself in. "The one who flew. I have read the report. A bag of hot air, very ingenious, and a man burned to death under it on the Danube, which the report puts at the end, where a man hopes it will not be read." He set the reed down. "Did you fly yourself?"

"Once tethered, Caesar, years ago, and never since. I put two men up and burned them both. I do not put men up anymore."

"So the report says, and admires you for it. I do not, particularly. A man who builds a thing that kills men and then refuses to ride it himself has decided his own skin is worth more than his curiosity. Alexander rode what he built." He let that sit, watching. "But you are not here about the bag. The bag is a dead man's enthusiasm. You are here because someone in my treasury cannot stop talking about your marks, and I want to know whether I am being sold a conjuror's trick by a barbarian who flew once and has dined out on it ever since, or whether there is a real thing under the smoke. Persuade me there is. You have until I am bored."

A dozen breaths in the room and he had laid out three traps, the flying, the dead man, the conjuror, and stood over them to watch which I stepped in. I left them where they lay.

"I will give you a sum, Caesar," I said, "and you will do it twice, once the way your clerks do and once the way my marks do, and see the difference with your own hand and take none of it on my word. The marks are not clever. That is their worth. A clever thing can hide a lie. These can't."

His eyes sharpened, and I understood I had said something he liked and feared at once. He liked being handed a tool and told to use it himself. He feared, very slightly, being told a thing he had not already thought, by a man beneath him, before witnesses.

"Give me the sum," he said.

---

I gave him a real one, a province's grain levy built the night before out of the shape of true accounts, with a remainder and a running balance and two places where a clerk's thumb could quietly add a stroke. I laid it the long Roman way first, the wall, then in the new figures, the stair, and put the reed in his hand and let him climb the stair himself.

He was fast, faster than I had let myself expect. He took the figures the way Tyche had taken them, by the throat, ran the column, caught the place where the Roman version had let a stroke be added, and his head came up.

"There," he said. "In yours the hole shows. A man cannot add the grain back without the total betraying him. In the old marks he hides it in the crowd of strokes, and I am robbed and cannot prove it." He looked at me with the full force of his attention, which was like standing too close to a forge. "I worked this out for myself in the time it took to say it. You did not have to lead me."

"No, Caesar."

"Do not flatter me by agreeing too quickly. I dislike it." But he was pleased, and let me see it, and turned the tablet to one of the standing men. "Look. The barbarian's marks. You have all been making me forgive debts I could not prove because your fathers' fathers counted on their fingers and called it the dignity of Rome." He turned back. "Who taught them to you."

"No one, Caesar. They are from my country."

"Your country." He smiled into the beard, delighted and merciless. "Thule. The land at the back of the north wind, which Pytheas saw and Strabo called him a liar for, and which you alone among living men managed to leave and reach Rome and forget the road home to. You see I have read more than the report. I read Strabo as a boy. There is no such country, or if there is, it does not write in marks an Egyptian abacus-monger could not have given you in an afternoon." He said it lightly, swinging the reed, Crispus's whole charge said better and from a height Crispus could only dream of, a noose laid on the table for me to put my own head in by arguing.

I did not argue. "Caesar reads more than I ever will," I said. "I cannot win an argument about Thule against a man who has read Strabo, and I have stopped trying. What I can do is hand you an account no man in your service can cook, and where it came from is the least useful question you could ask about it, and the only one your enemies will want you to spend an afternoon on."

He went still, then laughed, once, a bark. "You are telling Caesar how to spend his attention. You are telling me my own suspicion is a waste of my afternoon." The reed lowered. "It is, of course. That is what makes it insolent. Go on. The press. I am told you can print a thing many times."

---

We were a long while at it and I knew the whole time that I was being hunted and was enjoying it, which was the worst sign of all.

He moved. He did not sit through a thing the way Trajan had sat on the edge of the working table and waited to be impressed or wasted. He prowled the cork-and-wax model while I talked, and circled back, and set little tests in my path the way you set a snare in a run you have watched an animal use. He asked how the press inked, and when I told him truly that we wasted one sheet in four he pressed on the waste like a man pressing a bruise, because he had hoped I would gild it and I had not. He asked whether a printed law was more dangerous than a copied one, a single error pressed a thousand times, and I told him yes, the press multiplied the corrector's mistakes along with his truths, which was why the thing it should first carry was figures, where an error shows itself, and not opinions, where it does not. He liked that so much he gave it back to me as his own a quarter-hour later, and I let him, and saw him watch me let him.

"You give ground gracefully," he said, not as praise. "Three times now. You have a true thing and you soften it the instant I lean on it. Why."

The reed tapped the table. The standing men had gone quiet. I felt the floor shift the way it had when Trajan's affable weather went out of him and left the cold sum, except this was a man who wanted to know whether I would lie about why I deferred to him, in front of witnesses, having already half-guessed.

"Because you are the emperor, Caesar," I said, "and I am a freedman who flew a bag once, and a true thing said too hard by a man like me to a man like you is a barbarian forgetting his place, and you would be right to remember it about me later. The thing is true whether I say it loud or soft. So I say it soft, and it stays true, and I stay useful, and we both keep what we want."

Something passed over the bearded face, amusement and a colder thing under it, the appraisal of a man deciding where to file a tool that has just told him how it means to be handled. "You are honest about your dishonesty," he said. "It is a rare vice. I will allow it for now."

---

The recognition came on a thing he said about a roof.

One of the standing men had unrolled a drawing on the far table, and the talk among them drifted, the way it does when the emperor's attention has wandered off the petitioner, to a temple the reign meant to raise from the burned bones of an old one. The emperor crossed to it mid-sentence and left me in the light, and bent over the drawing, and took the reed up again, and began to sketch over the man's careful lines with two fingers in the air and then with the wet reed on the margin, talking fast.

"Not a flat coffered ceiling, every temple in Rome wears the same tired flat hat. A single span. One vault closing over the whole round of it like the half of an egg, heavy at the foot where it pushes out and light at the crown where it has only itself to carry, and lighten it further, here, sink the panels in courses so the stone above is no more than it needs, segments, do you see, like the rind of a fruit drawn in over the top." The two fingers came together and apart, scooping the curve out of the air, and on the margin the reed laid down a dome in stepped segments, one course swallowing into the next, lines no flat Roman ceiling had ever made.

I had seen those two fingers sketch a cornice in the air before, in a white shadow at the foot of a column, in a crowd where no one else carried work.

It went through me from the spine out, the way cold water does, all at once and in a place you cannot warm. The beard, Greek on a Roman face among clean-shaven men. The draughtsman's roll. *Domes like cut melons, one swallowing another.* A temple with the roof in segments like a squash, shown to Caesar, and the man very proud of it. *Go and paint his gourds.* I had stood twenty feet from him eight years before, at the dedication of a Forum, and watched Apollodorus spit gently into a well, and felt the cold of it then and read it half right. I had read that the proud man had the more to lose. I had not read that the well was a throne.

He was still drawing his gourds, emperor of the world bent over a margin sketching the roof a master had told him to go away and paint, while somewhere in the city the master inspected drains and said true things loud enough to be heard, the way he was certain was enough.

The emperor straightened and caught me looking. He could not have known what I had understood. But he saw that I had understood something, that my face had done a thing while his back was turned, and a man who tests everyone he meets does not let that go.

"You have an opinion about my roof," he said. It was not a question, and it was a trap finer than any he had laid, because I had just learned the most dangerous fact about him and had it written all over me, and any opinion at all about his roof, praise or doubt, was a step toward a place I now knew better than to stand. He builds. He prides himself on it. He had been told to go and paint his gourds by the one man whose work outweighed his, and had become the one man able to settle that account, and kept a ledger, and was drawing the gourds anyway, in front of witnesses, daring the room to remember who had mocked them.

"I build things that float and burst, Caesar," I said. "I have no opinion about what holds a roof up. I know enough to know that I do not, which about roofs is the most I have ever known."

He let it stand, and I saw in the letting that he had wanted to be deferred to about the roof, that the deference was the whole point of having drawn it in front of me, and that a man who agreed too readily that he knew nothing about roofs was nearly as suspect as one who claimed to know more. I had to give him my smallness and make him feel he had won it off me. "Though I would pay to see the half-egg stand," I added into the silence. "I have failed at smaller spans and gone home cursing."

That landed. The failure landed. He liked a man who had failed and would name it, the way Trajan had, except where Trajan had liked the honesty Hadrian liked the smallness it admitted beside his success. I steered by that the rest of the hour, and hated that I could.

---

He came back to the figures at the end, because under all the beard and the Greek and the gourds he was a man taking an inventory, and the inventory was the thing he could not get from his clerks and could get from me.

"I will have a province's accounts done in your marks," he said. "Both ways, side by side, and if the marks find me a thief I will know they are real, and if they find me nothing I will know my province is honest, which I will not believe, so they had better find me a thief." He was prowling again, near done with me. "You will have wax, clerks, the use of your press if it comes to that. You will not be paid in coin, which I am told you do not need, being rich, which is itself a thing I find suspect in a freedman and will think about another day." He stopped. "What do you want. Every man who comes up the rolls wants a thing. You have asked for nothing, which is either the cleverest ask there is or you are saving it. Which."

The map was in my mouth.

I want to be exact, because it is the part I came closest to ruining. The room was right. The man was bent toward knowing, toward the inventory, toward being the one who knew the true size of a thing too big to hold in a head. Tyche's words were in my chest, knowing not taking, prestige not province, the idle legions at a yard not a wall, and I could feel the shape of the door, the words worked out over a winter of ruined wax for a room that had not existed and now did, and he had opened his hand and asked me what I wanted, and the true shape of the world lay a sentence away from the one living man with the appetite and the power to go and find it.

And I held it. I held it because of a roof. I had just watched him draw his gourds in the teeth of the man who mocked them, and I knew in the body, before the mind had words for it, that this was a man who would take a glittering idea and make it his within the hour, the way he had made my sentence about the press his own. Hand him the edge of the world, fresh from a barbarian who flew a bag once, and he would either seize it whole as his own thought and leave me a tool that had served its turn, or remember in some later mood that the barbarian had known the shape of the world before the emperor did, and file that with a small mark beside it, for the day he could settle it. I had watched, eight years gone, what such a ledger does to the man it is kept against, and that man was inspecting drains across the city, sure that being right was a shelter.

"I want to be useful and forgotten, Caesar," I said. "I have had a year of being remembered and it nearly killed me. Give me a province's accounts to break and a clerk to break them with and a name on nothing, and let great men have the marks and let me be the dull foreigner who ruined wax in a back room. That is the whole of it. I have had the other kind of life. It is loud and it ends badly."

I could not read whether he believed it, or believed I believed it, or had simply decided to be charmed by a man asking to be left alone in a room full of men asking to be advanced.

"A man who asks to be forgotten is asking to be remembered for the asking," he said. "I will remember you for it. You see how it works." He took the reed up again, done with me, half turned back to the gourds. "Go and break me a province. Eudemus will see to your wax." He had the name out before he caught it, and his eye flicked to me to see if I marked that he knew exactly which clerk had been talking of my marks, that there were no secrets in his house and he wished me to know there were none. I let my face do nothing. "Eudemus, or whoever keeps that side of the rolls. They are interchangeable to me. That is the point of them."

---

They took me down the slope as the light went, into the noise of the city coming up to meet me the way it had come up the Palatine fifteen years before. I had what I had gone up for. A province to break in the new figures, the use of the press, the attention of a man who could decide things, the crack swung wide enough now to walk through. By every measure Marcia kept I had won the winter's whole campaign in an afternoon, and I went down cold to the bone and not from the wind.

A patron tries to catch you out so he can trust you. A predator tries to catch you out so he can price you. They do the same things with their hands and their questions, and Hadrian had done all of them, and at the end I knew the shape of his appetite and the place of his ledger and not one thing about which of the two he meant to be to me, and I suspected he did not know either, and would decide it some morning years off in a mood I could not read from a back room full of wax.

And under that, the colder thing. Somewhere in the city Apollodorus was inspecting his drains, white-haired and certain, saying true things at full volume because true things at full volume had built him everything he could see. He had told a young Spaniard with a draughtsman's roll to go and paint his squashes and leave temples to men who knew what held a thing up, and the young Spaniard had inclined his head a precise small distance and become the master of the world. Apollodorus did not know it, or knew it and did not believe it could matter, which in him came to the same thing. I could not warn him. A man who has been right for sixty years does not take warning from the barbarian with the bag, and to be heard near him saying the emperor's name and the word danger in one breath was to put my own thin walls under the one hand I now most needed to think me dull.

At my own gate the lamp was lit and the day's loads stood waiting to be tallied, and Marcia looked at me across the wax and did not ask the loud question, whether I had pleased him, whether we were bought or saved. She asked the quiet one.

"Did you keep the map in the box."

"I kept it in the box," I said.

She made a mark and slid the loads across to me, the bales and the barrels and the stamped sheets, real things with numbers that stayed where you set them. I sat down and counted them, and up on the Palatine a man who could follow my every thought to the end of it and put a sword through me on a morning of his choosing was deciding, in a mood I would never be let to see, which of those two he had a use for. I counted the loads through. Then, because my hands would not quiet, I counted them again, and they came out the same, and that was the one thing in the whole day that had.

---

# Chapter 36: The Ocean Gambit

I broke his province in eight months, and the breaking of it bought me the door I had spent a winter pretending not to want.

It was Lugdunensis, in Gaul, a fat tidy province with a fat tidy lie running under its grain figures. I ran the accounts both ways, the Roman wall and the new stair side by side, and where they disagreed I marked it and went no further. Three places. Two were a clerk skimming cartage. The third was older and ran up into a name I did not write down, and I left a clean gap in the column where a stroke had gone missing across nine years, and let the gap do the accusing.

I did not see Hadrian for the better part of a year, which was its own message, and I read it the way Marcia read a slow-paying client, that he can pay and is teaching you that you cannot make him. So I was the dull foreigner in the back room who had ruined a great deal of wax, exactly as I had asked to be, and the asking had been true when I made it and a lie within the month. The door was open now, and a man does not stand at an open door forever telling himself he prefers the wall. What I wanted was past it. I had carried it twenty-three years.

---

The summons, when it came, came badly dressed. High summer, the city stewing, the river dropped to its stones, and a clerk arrived at the wharf in the worst hour of the afternoon with an escort of two and the manner of a man told he is carrying something but not what. The tablet wanted the accounts of a second province in the marks, Belgica this time, at a house I knew. More wax and more clerks and the same back room, said the tablet, and I knew it was not, because a thing that is only more of the same does not come with an escort of two.

Marcia knew it too. She read the tablet twice and set it down flat.

"He has decided what you are for. Or he is about to, and wants to look at you while he does it. You will give him Belgica." I had Celer's strap on the bench and had been working the buckle without knowing. "He had a year to be bored by how well you keep your head down. A man like that gets suspicious of a quiet man for the same reason he gets suspicious of a loud one. He thinks the quiet is for something." She slid the strap away from me, the way you take a thing from a child without making it a contest. "Is it for something."

I did not answer her, which was an answer, and she let it be one. That night I went down to the press-room where Tyche kept the second key, and we took the box out from where I am not allowed to know it lives now, and I unrolled the map for the first time in years.

It had been recopied twice, the linen gone soft. The Mediterranean was honest. Africa was a guess that ran too narrow to a point I had no right to. India was small and wrong. And across the western edge, past a blank I had drawn wide on purpose, the two continents sat where a seventeen-year-old had put them from the memory of a screen, badly, unmistakably, a long broken shore curving away into ice and into nothing, a strait at the waist I could not have placed within five hundred miles and had drawn anyway because I knew it was there.

Tyche held the lamp. She had read the framing back to me in the cipher a hundred times and said none of it now. She looked at the western shore the way you look at a thing a man should not have, and said, in the cipher, so the words could not be in the room in Latin, "You are going to point him at your house."

"I'm going to point him at the ocean."

"Your house is on the other side of the ocean." She rolled it for me, careful of the soft linen, and put it in my hand. "You spent twenty-three years making sure no one could follow you home. Now you are going to draw the road."

There was nothing to say to it that would not have been the loud thing, and we did not do that to each other.

---

The house on the slope was the same and the man in it had aged the way a hard man ages, barely and all at once, gray come into the beard at the jaw, the restlessness banked down into something heavier. He was past forty-five now and I past forty, and we had both been emperor and barbarian for a year and knew the parts. He let me get the bow done before he spoke.

"Belgica," he said. "Sit where the light is. You found me a thief in Gaul."

"I found Caesar a gap in a column. Caesar found the thief."

"You left the gap exactly the width of a thief." He almost smiled. "You did not write the name. You will tell me you did not know it."

"I knew the name, Caesar. I am a freedman who counts. The naming of great men is for great men."

He waved Belgica's tablets to a corner where they sat untouched the whole afternoon. "Then a thing crossed my table I cannot forget," he said, coming around the table, the forge-heat of his attention full on, "and it has your smell on it though your name is nowhere near it. A man copying your Gaul accounts said the foreigner once drew a map of the whole world on a wax tablet, before he could speak a word of Latin, to save his neck. Was it true."

So the door had opened itself. I felt the whole winter of work tip and start to slide, and made myself slow.

"It was true, Caesar. A frightened boy with a stylus, drawing badly, to seem worth keeping alive. The magistrate copied it onto a leaf, and I have regretted some of what I drew ever since."

"Regretted." The word caught him the way I had known a word would. He collected the things other men regretted, because a regret is a door left ajar. "Show me. Not the boy's. The man's. You have had twenty-three years to get it less wrong. Show me the world."

---

A whole winter of work had assumed I would pry his hand open, and instead he had put the thing in my hand and asked, and the danger in being asked is that you answer fast. I drew it slowly, the Mediterranean first, because the whole of it had to ride on the part he could check.

He checked it. He put his own knowing against my coast, Spain where he was born, the gulf of Gaul, the boot, Greece, the long African shore his grain fleets ran, and found it righter than the maps in his own library, and his breath changed. Then Egypt, the Red Sea, Arabia, on east, and here I stayed honest, told him India was a guess and likely wrong, that I did not know where the eastern lands stopped and would not pretend. The honesty about the east bought the rest. He liked a man who said *I do not know* about the right thing.

"And the war the dead emperor spent himself on," he said, "lay there." He set two fingers on the rivers, on the cities he had given back. "You served it. You buried men there for a province I struck off the rolls in a morning." This was a trap and an honesty at once, the way his best ones were.

"I did, Caesar." Celer's road was under those two fingers. I did not let it into my face. "I buried a friend there, holding a road to a city you were right to let go. I have thought about which way the error ran. It did not run in the letting go."

He took his fingers off the map. Something in him settled, because I had handed him without being asked the thing he most wanted told and could not be seen to ask for, that abandoning the east had been wisdom and not weakness, said by a man who had paid for it. I had not planned to spend Celer there. I spent him.

"Go on," he said, lower. "You stopped at the edge. There is more of the leaf."

"There is the ocean, Caesar."

"There is the Ocean. The river that runs round the world. Homer's Ocean," he said, fond, a scholar's word.

"No, Caesar." It was the one place I would correct him, chosen in advance, because the gambit needed him to learn one true thing from me and feel its size. "A width. Water with a far side." And I drew it, the wide blank I had always drawn, and then across it, slow, the long broken shore, north into ice, south into ice, the strait at the waist, the two great unnamed lands where no Roman map had ever put a thing but the word *Oceanus* and a drawing of waves.

The room went quiet the way it had when he caught the stolen stroke. He bent over the western shore the way he had bent over his dome, and when he straightened his face had hunger in it, which I had wanted and been afraid of.

---

"Land," he said.

"Land. A man's height of guess in every line of it, Caesar. There is a far shore. I do not know its true distance, nor its true shape, nor whether the strait is one strait or fifty. A boy drew the bones of it and a man has not improved them much." I gave him the doubt straight, because the doubt was the hook; a thing offered with honest doubt is a problem, and he could not leave a problem alone. "What I know is that it is there. Rome has reached east until the east threw her off, south until the sand stopped her, north until the cold and the trees stopped her, and the one direction she has never put a keel into is west, into the open sea, where the world's true size is written and no one has read it."

"Because no one comes back," he said. "Sailors who go west of the Pillars come back along the coast or do not come back. You cannot crawl along a shore that is not there."

"No, Caesar. You cannot." Now the turn, the one Marcia and I had built and rebuilt, the one that had to be his idea inside the hour. "Which is why no one will ever go for plunder. There is nothing out there a man would cross a year of open water to steal that he could not steal closer. A general would be a fool to want it, and it would eat his army. But you have just sold three provinces. You are the emperor who counts. The one thing out there worth a freedman's afternoon is the thing no army can carry home, which is the true shape of the world, measured, mapped, in Rome's hand, the first and last time it will ever be done, because there is only one first time, and the man whose reign does it owns it forever, the way Alexander owns the East in men's mouths though the East threw his heirs off inside a lifetime."

I had said it too fast at the end, and I shut my mouth and let the room hold it. Then he did the thing I had watched him do with the press, and I let him, and watched myself let him.

"You are describing," he said slowly, as if the thought rose in him for the first time, "a thing no general could be trusted with and no merchant would pay for, that returns nothing but knowledge. A thing only a state can do, and only a certain kind of master of a state would think worth doing." He looked up. "Alexander conquered what he could reach and wept that there was no more. A greater man would have measured the part he could not reach, and not wept." The two fingers scooped the air over the western blank, the gourd-drawing gesture, possession made with the hands. "I would be the man who knew the shape of the world."

"Caesar would be that man," I said. Let him be the one who knows.

"Do not agree so quickly. I have told you." But he was already gone into it, and I had lost him to the idea, and that was the winning of it, and it tasted of ash, because he had taken it whole inside a quarter-hour, exactly as I had feared a year ago, and the only difference between that fear and this triumph was that this time I had wanted him to.

---

Then he came back, because his hunger had teeth on both ends.

"Thule," he said. I had known it would come and still flinched under the skin.

"You drew the north too." He put his finger above Britain, on the blank where the boy's tablet had once made a physician say a flat two-syllable word. "There is your country, that writes in marks, that no one has reached and you alone have left. And here, west." The finger swung down to the great shore. "You will tell me Thule is out there, and that this is how you know there is a far side, because you crossed it once and forgot the way. Scouts, the report says. Thulean scouts who know the western water. Tell me about your scouts."

The whole lie stood up in the room and asked to be defended, and I had learned on this floor a year before that the way to die in front of this man was to defend a thing he had already half-seen through. So I gave him a third of it and let him keep the suspicion, because a man allowed his suspicion stops digging for the rest.

"Caesar, I will tell you what I tell no one, since you have read most of it." I let the wax leaf lie between us. "I do not know if any man living has crossed that water. I have said scouts because a frightened man says scouts, the way a frightened man draws a map. What I had, coming here with nothing, was the knowledge that the far shore is there. How I came by it is the one thing I have never made a Roman believe, the same as Thule. Write me down a liar about the road and you will not be wrong. The shore is still there. The marks still cannot be cooked. A man can be a liar about where he is from and a true map still be a true map. Strabo called Pytheas a liar, and Pytheas had still seen the north."

He held my eyes a long time. I had handed him permission to disbelieve the lie, in order to keep the truth, and he was clever enough to feel the trade and take it knowing it was a trade.

"I think you are a liar," he said, without heat, almost with pleasure. "I think there are no scouts and no Thule, and you came from somewhere you will carry to your grave, and I find I do not care, because the map is the map and a man's hidden country is his own affair until it is mine." He tapped the western shore. "But I will keep the matter of where you are from." He meant the ledger, and let me see he meant it. "Now. Tell me why no state has done it, since the why is always the true price."

---

This was the part I could not lie my way past, and did not try, because the wall was real and I had broken myself on it already and he would find that out the moment his own men touched it.

"Two reasons, Caesar, and the second has beaten me." I drew a hull on the wax, crude. "First, ships. Yours crawl from headland to headland and run for harbor when the sky turns. The open Ocean will break them. You would need hulls and rigging Rome does not build, fit to take a far sea for weeks out of sight of land and claw back up into a wind. That is years and money, and it is the easy reason." I set the reed down. "The hard reason is this. A ship out of sight of land does not know where it is. How far north or south, any pilot finds by the sun and the stars; that is old. How far east or west he has come, no man can find. There is no star for it. A fleet could sail a year toward your shore and not know on any day whether the shore was a week ahead or a month, or whether the storm in the night had set them a hundred miles off the line into open water forever."

He was following it exactly. "And the solution."

"Is a clock, Caesar." Here I had to be most careful, because for once the survival law and the truth pointed the same way. "Carry on the ship a perfect clock set to the hour of the home port, and read the true hour of the sun where the ship is. The gap between the two is your westing, exactly, every day, how far around the world you have come." I let it sit. "And I must tell Caesar, because his own men will tell him inside a season, that I have tried for years to build a clock that keeps true time standing still in a warm room, and failed. I have a water-clock in my tally-room that a girl corrects against the sun twice a day, because it cannot be trusted past a morning. The thing the voyage needs is a thing I cannot make. I know no living man who can."

The room was silent. I had told the emperor the gift was gated on a wall I had broken my own head against, on purpose. A thing offered as easy he would seize and squander and blame me when it failed; a hard problem the size of a reign he could not leave alone, would make his own, and would not blame me for, because I had warned him. Warning him was the price of surviving the day the wall held.

He stood a long while over the crude hull and the word *clock* scratched in the wax. "So you bring me," he said at last, "a far shore you cannot reach, in ships I do not have, steered by an instrument no man can build." Something in the beard was almost a grin and was mostly the cold appraisal under it. "It is the most honest thing anyone has tried to sell me in a year."

"It is the truest thing I have, Caesar."

---

He did not launch a fleet. I had not let myself believe he would, and he did not. He gave it the shape of a consolidator who counts. No armada, no year's voyage west, not now, not soon, perhaps not in his reign or mine, and he said so in those words, that he was no Trajan and would not feed an ocean what Trajan fed a desert. What he sanctioned, over that summer and into the next year as the orders worked down through the fiscus, was bounded and exact, and I learned it in pieces, from clerks.

A survey first. The world Rome already held was to be mapped properly, the true coasts taken and set down in the new figures to a single scale, beginning with the seas he already sailed, the African shore pushed south of where the grain fleets went, a season at a time, recording and returning, no man sent past where he could come home from. A school of pilots and shipwrights at a yard on the western coast, he had not settled where, to build and try hulls for the outer sea, slowly, at a cost he named and would not pass. And the clock made a standing problem of the reign, with a purse behind it, offered to any craftsman who could keep true time through a month at sea, and I was to write down everything I knew of why it was hard and where I had failed, so the men who came after would not waste their years on my dead ends.

It was a tenth of the thing in my chest, the cautious near edge of it, and it was real, it had imperial wax on it, and Rome had turned her face, by a few degrees and for the first time on my account, toward the water she had always run from.

"You will write the why of the clock," Hadrian said, gathering the leaves, mine among them now, the western shore folded into his keeping where I would never get it back. "And you will tell no one the part about your scouts, since you have admitted there are none, and I prefer my survey to rest on a true map and not a barbarian's tale. The far shore is mine to find. Thule is yours to keep lying about. We will each have what we want." He paused at the door of his own attention, half gone from me. "You brought it to me and not to a general, which tells me you were afraid of what a general would do with it, which tells me you are afraid of the right things. Go and do Belgica. And send up the clock."

---

They walked me down the slope at dusk, the smell of the city coming up to meet me, fish and lamp-soot and the day's heat off the stones, and the lie was loose now, going up the rolls and out to the pilots and into the reign, the uninhabited shore, the year's reachable sail, the country a frightened boy had drawn to stay alive. Men would die against it eventually, in good hulls, sailing toward an empty shore that was not empty and a year's water that would not be enough, trusting a map a kid had drawn from a screen, steered by no clock. Or they would never sail at all, the yard would rot, and the whole of it come to a line in some clerk's accounts. I could not tell which, and I had aimed it anyway.

At the wharf the lamp was lit and Marcia sat at the table with the Belgica tablets already squared, and she saw what I was carrying before I set it down, the way she saw a short load through a sealed crate.

"He took it," she said.

"A tenth of it. A survey. A yard. He would not feed the ocean." I sat. "He kept the map."

"He kept the map." She did not make it a reproach. She made a mark and slid the Belgica accounts across, and I should have taken them up. Instead I went down to the press-room where Tyche was banking the lamps, and stood a while at the cold press with the empty box open on the bench where the map had lived for twenty-three years. The box was the size and shape of the thing no longer in it. I closed the lid on the nothing inside, and went up, finally, to count Belgica, where the numbers stayed where I set them and did not have a far side.

---

# Chapter 37: Apollodorus Falls

The temple came to the wharf before the news did, the way these things always reached me, in the mouth of a man delivering rope.

He was a carter I had used for years, a freedman with a head for stone because he hauled it, and he set down his tally and stayed to talk because the city was talking and a man likes to be the one who tells it. Caesar had drawn a temple, he said. The biggest in Rome, two goddesses back to back under one roof, Venus and the City herself, sitting, each in her own half. Drawn it with his own hand, they said, no architect's between. The carter thought it grand. I thought, while I checked his count, that Hadrian had been at that very drawing the afternoon I first sat in the light he chose for me, the standing men around the cork model, the reed going at the margin. He had been telling me about a roof while he meant me to admire it. He had built it now, or begun to, and the city was admiring.

I marked the rope received and said it sounded grand, and meant nothing by it, and the carter went off pleased to have been believed.

---

What reached me a few weeks on was not grand.

It came through Heras, who was past sixty-five now and slower on the stairs and still the man Rome's Greeks told things to, because a physician is in every house and forgets nothing. He came to the yard out of his way, which he did not do for small matters, and we sat in the tally-room where the water-clock dripped its unreliable hours, and he turned a cup of my watered wine in his hands a long time before he used it.

"Your friend the bridge-builder," he said, "has been telling the city about the emperor's temple."

He was not my friend, and Heras knew it, and used the word because there was no other for what Apollodorus was to me, a thing the language had not made a word for, a man I had stood beside reading the same lie off the same stone and known, in the standing-beside, more than I knew about men I loved.

"What has he said."

"That the gods are sitting too low. That if Venus and Roma ever wished to rise from their thrones and walk out of their own temple, they would crack their heads on the ceiling, because Caesar set the floor of the building above the natural rise of the ground and gave the seated figures no height to stand into." Heras drank, finally. "He said it as a man names a fault in a wall. To engineers, at a yard. But he said it, and a thing said to engineers does not stay at the yard, and Caesar is the architect."

I sat with the clock dripping between us.
