I knew the joke at once for what it was, true and unanswerable and exactly the kind of true thing he had built sixty years on saying out loud, the seated goddesses too big for the box their maker had drawn, no recourse in stone, the fault plain to anyone who would put a man's height against a span. It was the gourds again, said better, said about a finished thing the whole city could walk into and see.

"When," I said.

"A while ago. It is only now that the man who carried it to me thought it worth carrying." Heras set the cup down. "I do not bring it to make you do anything. There is nothing to do. I bring it because you will hear it from a worse mouth and I would rather you heard it from mine."

"There may be something to do."

"There is not," Heras said, with the flat finality he had used on me about miasma thirty years before, "and you know there is not, and you are going to go and try the thing that is not there to do, because you cannot help it. I have watched you do it with water and with wounds. Go, then. But go knowing he will not take it from you, and come back, because I am too old to teach another barbarian his manners."

---

I went the next morning, on foot, with no escort and no tablet, dressed plainly, which was itself a calculation, because a freedman in good wool calling on a fallen master draws an eye and I wanted no eye on me at his door.

He was not yet fallen, that was the thing I did not understand walking there. He was at a yard near the river where they were squaring blocks for some other work of the reign, and he had a place in it still, a stool under an awning and men bringing him their plumb-lines to settle disputes, white-headed, the bad knuckles worse, the same weather-left-out frame holding the same certainty. He was past seventy and looked like a thing they would have to take apart to move. When he saw me he did not get up.

"The flying man," he said. "Come to watch real work. Sit, you make the boy nervous standing."

I sat on an upturned half-block where the dust was. We talked stone a while, because there was no reaching him any other way, and because I wanted it, the way you want a thing you are about to lose and have not let yourself say is going. He told me a wall I would not have known to look at had a flaw in its footing that would show in twenty years and that no one would thank him for naming, because the man who pays for a wall wants to be told it will stand, not when it will not. He asked, the way he always asked, when I had last failed at something with my own hands, and I told him I had promised an emperor a clock for the open sea and could not build one for a dry room, and he made the short hard sound that was his laugh and said the sea would teach me the depth of my well faster than I could lower the bucket.

Then I said it, because there was no clean way in and the dust was getting in my throat.

"They are saying in the city what you said about the temple. The goddesses cracking their heads."

He was not ashamed. I had hoped, walking there, for some flicker of a man who knew he had gone too far, and there was none. There was a small flat pleasure, the pleasure of a man whose true thing has traveled.

"Because it is true," he said. "He set them too big for the room. A child with a stick and a wall could show you. You drop a height of stone where a goddess is to stand and you measure the standing, you do not draw the goddess to fill the pretty oval and then build the roof down on her. He drew the picture first and asked the stone to agree. The stone will not agree. The stone never agrees. That is the only honest thing in the trade." He looked at me sidelong, the old appraisal. "You came to tell me that."

"I came to tell you to stop saying it."

The awning-shadow moved on his face. He understood me; he had always understood me faster than I wished.

"Ah," he said. "The barbarian has come to teach me how to live."

"I have come to tell you who the man is." I kept my voice down, which with him was a confession in itself, because he despised a lowered voice. "The man you told to go and paint his gourds, at the Forum, the year they dedicated it. The bearded Spaniard with the roll under his arm. You remember telling him."

"I remember. I would say it again. He drew melons and called them domes."

"He is the emperor."

I had thought the word would do something. I had carried it across the city imagining the moment it landed, the certainty going out of his face the way it had never once gone out of it. It did not go out of it now. He looked at me as a man looks at a slow apprentice who has at last arrived, breathless, at a thing the master settled an hour ago.

"I know what he is," Apollodorus said. "I knew what he was when he had the roll under his arm. A man who designs is a man who designs, and his rank is the least true thing about him. The melons are still melons. He is emperor of melons now, that is all." He spread the bad hand on his knee, the thickened knuckles, the old break that had set wrong because he had gone on working through it. "You think I do not know I said it loud. I said it loud on purpose, at the Forum, so the men around him would hear an old man tell the truth and remember that the truth could still be told. That is worth more than the temple. The temple will fall down on schedule whether I say so or not. What I am for is to be the man who said so."

"You are for staying alive," I said. "He keeps a ledger. He kept the small mark thirty years and now he can settle it whenever he likes, and you are handing him the morning."

"And you," Apollodorus said, and here the appraisal in his eyes was something worse than contempt, something close to a thing he pitied, "you bend. I have watched you do it for twenty years. You have a true thing and you fold it small enough to fit through a great man's door and you call that surviving. It is surviving. You will survive me, and you will survive him, and you will fold your true things smaller and smaller until there is nothing left to fold, and you will die a rich man no one ever heard say a single thing straight." He picked up a plumb-line a boy had left and let it find its level, watching the bob settle, the one motion in the world he trusted. "A man right for sixty years does not start lying to power at seventy because the power has learned his face. I would have to become you to take your advice. I would rather take my chances with the stone."

There was nothing under that I could get a finger into. I had come to spit, gently, into a well, and found the well had a man already standing at the bottom of it looking up, perfectly at peace, with no intention of climbing out. We talked a little more, stone again, because we were two men of one trade and there was nowhere else to put the thing between us, and when I rose to go he gave me the plumb-line.

"Take it," he said. "You build things that float. You will never once in your life have a use for a tool that finds the truth straight down. Keep it to remember which of us was the better engineer." The hard sound again. "Go fold something."

I took it. It was the only gift either of us ever gave the other, and he gave it as an insult, and I have it.

---

I tried the other thing too. I want it on the record that I tried it, because the trying was small and the smallness was the whole of what I learned about myself that year.

I had a province half-broken in the new figures by then, the second one, and the work put me in rooms near men who were near Hadrian, fiscus freedmen and a procurator's secretary and, once, a man who carried drawings up to the Palatine and brought corrections down. This last was a Greek named Theophanes, a careful man who valued my marks because they made his accounts of stone and labor uncheatable, and who owed me nothing and risked nothing, which is the only kind of door a man like me is given.

I went at it the way Marcia would have, sidelong, dull, a thing dropped between two real things so it would not stand up and be seen. We were over a tally of cartage for the temple footings, and I asked, as a man asks who is only making conversation while the ink dries, whether the master of the old bridges was consulted on the great work, since no one living knew foundations as he did, and it would be a shame to lose the use of such a man over a quarrel about a roof.

Theophanes did not look up from his figures.

"There is no quarrel," he said. "The emperor designs. The work proceeds. Old men say things at yards." He made a mark, and then, because he was not unkind and had understood me exactly, he added, "I would not carry that sentence up the hill, friend. Neither the part about the bridges nor the part about the roof. A man who reminds Caesar that another man knows foundations better than he does has done the other man no favor, and himself less. The emperor does not need to be told whom he is angry at. He keeps that himself."

That was all of it. That was my word to Hadrian for Apollodorus, said to a clerk three rooms from him, hedged so far down it was barely a word at all, and turned aside before it could become one, by a man doing me the kindness of pretending I had not said it. I did not push it further. I want to be honest about why. It was not that pushing it would not have worked, though it would not have. It was that pushing it would have put my own name in a sentence beside Apollodorus's, in front of a man who carried sentences up the hill, in the season when the one thing keeping me alive was being too dull to carry. I weighed the master of the bridges against my own thin walls, in the half of a breath it takes to weigh such a thing, and I let the sentence die in Theophanes's careful Greek, and I went back to the cartage, and the figures stayed where I set them.

---

He was exiled first. That was how it reached me, in pieces, over a season, the way a slow load comes apart in a sealed crate and you only know by the shifting weight.

Banished from the city, his works left to other hands, the bridges and the Forum and the column going on without him as though they had built themselves, because a thing once standing forgets the man who held the plumb-line. Then, a while after, the other word, carried in the same flat manner, a man dead in exile, an old man, the cause not named because the cause is never named, only that he had said something he should not have, about the temple, about a roof, a man who did not know his place at the end and would not learn it. They put it that way, the ones who carried it. A man who would not learn his place. As if there were a place, drawn somewhere in advance, with a height marked on it that a man could stand into and not crack his head.

I do not know the morning it was decided. I never saw the room. I have built nothing in my life that I trust more than I would have trusted his account of why a wall stands, and I do not have his account of his own falling, because there is no relief that carves the man the chisel was told to leave out, and the man who could have told me how it truly went was the man it went to.

---

Marcia knew the day the second word came, before I said it, the way she knew a short load through wood.

"He would not bend," she said. It was not a question. She had heard me come back from the yard months before with the plumb-line in my hand and had asked nothing, and she asked nothing now, only stated the figure at the bottom of the column.

"He would not bend."

"And you bent." Also not a question. Marcia did not soften things; she found their level and set them down. "You went and you said the careful thing to the careful man and it died where you wanted it safe to die, and you came home, and we still have the wharf." She made a mark in her tally, the day's loads, the bales and the barrels. "I am not reproaching you. I would have told you to do exactly that, and I am glad you did it without my telling you, because it means I do not have to be the one in this house who counts what a friend is worth against the walls." She set the stylus down. "Eat. You have not eaten since the carter."

I ate. The clock dripped its bad hours. Lucanus, who was ten now and knew the new figures faster than the clerks and did not know yet what kind of world he had been born a citizen of, came in and asked who had died, because the house had the smell of it, and Marcia told him a builder, an old one, the man who built the bridges in the war his father served, and Lucanus asked, reasonably, whether the bridges were still standing, and I told him yes, the bridges were still standing.

"Then he did all right," Lucanus said, and went to find his bread, and he was not wrong, and it was the only thing said in the house all that day that I could not fold any smaller and so left whole.

---

That winter the survey went on. I had its reports through the fiscus, the African coast measured a headland further south than the grain fleets ran, the figures coming back in the new marks to the single scale, a yard breaking ground on the western shore where the pilots would learn hulls that could not yet be built for a sea no one would let them cross. The thing I had pointed an emperor at was crawling forward exactly as I had built it to crawl, slow and bounded and gated on a clock I could not make, and it was the only one of all the things in my life that was doing what I had meant it to do, and I could not take any pleasure in it, because the man who would have understood precisely what it cost to aim it and stand back was the man I had let the careful sentence die rather than be killed for.

I sat in the press-room one night where the second key lived and took the plumb-line out and hung it from a nail in the roof-beam, away from the door, where no one would ask. It found its level and held it. A bob on a string knows one true thing and tells it without being asked, plumb down, the way water finds its floor, and it does not bend, and it has never once in the whole history of the trade been wrong, and it cannot fly, and a man who could only build things that floated had hung it up to watch it be right in the dark.

I left it hanging. Then I went and found Tyche where she was banking the lamps and asked her to write, in the cipher, at the bottom of the page where I had been setting down the dead ends of the clock for whoever came after me, the thing Apollodorus had told me about drawing the picture first and asking the stone to agree. She asked whose words they were. I told her a better engineer's. She wrote them in the marks that only the two of us could read, and they went into the book among my failures, where no chisel and no emperor would ever be told to leave them out, which was the one place in Rome I had built where a man could put a true thing and have it stay.

---

# Chapter 38: Consolidation

The accounts of Aquitania came back to me in the spring with a column I could not read, and for three days I blamed the press.

It was a fair thing to blame. We had pulled the sheets in a hurry, the ink had been too thin, and the small figures at the foot of a tally, where I packed the carry-overs in tight to save a line, had come up faint and broken. I held the page at the window and got the lamplight on it from the side, the way you read a worn stamp on a coin, and the numbers swam and resolved and swam again, sixes that might be eights, a four that wanted to be a nine, the whole bottom course of the column gone to a gray smear that my eye would not settle. I sent the sheet back to Eros to re-pull. He re-pulled it. It came back the same. I sent it back again, and Eros, who had set type and cut blocks for me for twenty years and did not frighten, came himself this time and stood in the door of the tally-room with the sheet held out and said, carefully, the way you speak to a man you have decided is being a fool about something, that the figures were clean. He laid it on the bench. He put his thumb under the carry-over line. They were clean.

I bent close, and close, and at the distance where I had read figures my whole life the page was fog, and at the length of my own arm, where Eros stood reading them upside down without trouble, they came clear.

I had been holding the work farther off for a year. I had told myself the light was bad, the ink was thin, the clerks wrote small. I had moved the lamp, trimmed the wick, had Eros ink heavier, and never once said the plain thing, which was that the trouble was not on the page.

"It's the light," I said to Eros, and took the sheet, and held it out at arm's length, and read it to him to prove the light, and he agreed it was the light, and went back to his press, and was kind enough not to mention that he had read it at a hand's distance while I read it at a cubit.

---

I will tell you what a man of my country would have done, because it is the joke of the thing, and I am the only one who will ever get it.

He would have walked into any shop on any street and bought a pair of glass disks in a frame and hooked them over his ears and gone back to reading. A few coins. A thing so common that children wore them and old men lost them down the backs of couches and no one thought it a marvel at all. I had ground lenses. I had spent four years and a fortune of Demetrios's patience grinding lenses, and I had a drawer of them, dished and bubbled and striped, that made the moon a smear and a senator's wife see colored fog, and not one of them, held at any distance from my own failing eye, did the one small honest thing a lens is good for, which is to take a near thing that has gone soft and make its edges hard again. I knew the shape it wanted. A reading lens is the easy lens, the fat one, the one a beginner grinds. I knew the principle the way I knew the shape of the thing in my hand at the hardware counter, the rack of them by the register, the little turning stand. And I sat in my own tally-room holding Aquitania at arm's length and could not make the glass do it, because the glass we could pour in Rome was full of seeds and waves and would not take a true curve from Demetrios's iron dish, and the thing that defeated me was the same thing that had defeated the spyglass, only now it was defeating me where I lived, at the one table where the whole of what I was good for got done.

I had made a glass to look at the moon. I could not make a glass to look at my own ledger.

Heras, when I finally said it to him, did not find it as funny as I did. He held my face in two fingers under a window the way he had held it the day he was sent to decide whether I was mad, turned it to the light, looked into the eye and not at it.

"The eye hardens," he said. "It is the commonest thing there is in a man past forty. The lens behind there grows stiff and will not pull itself round to a near thing anymore. I have it. Every man I have ever physicked who lived to read by lamplight has it." He let go my face. "There is nothing for it. You hold the work farther off until your arm is too short, and then you have a boy read it to you, and then you go on by what you remember and what they tell you. That is the road. I am further down it than you."

"There's a thing my people had for it," I said.

"Of course there was," Heras said, without heat, because he had stopped, years ago, being surprised that my people had a thing for everything and that I could make none of it, and had decided this was either the truest part of my whole strange story or the cruelest part of my lie, and had made his peace with not knowing which. "And you cannot make it."

"I cannot make the glass."

"Then it is a thing your people had and you do not," he said, "which is most of what your people had, as far as I have ever been able to tell," and he said it gently, and it was the gentleness that got under the ribs.

---

What I had, in those years, was a settled place, and it was a smaller place than I had once meant to stand in, and I had learned to stand in it.

I rendered provinces. That was the work the emperor kept me for, and it was good work, and I will not pretend it was not, because there is a clean pleasure in taking a province's accounts both ways, the Roman letters and the new figures, and finding the gap where a procurator's freedman had been eating, and laying it out so plain that the man could be lifted out of his post without a word spoken about why, the column itself the only accuser, naming the year and the sum and never the man. Hadrian liked that. He was a man who wanted the doors of his house counted and the count to be true, and I gave him true counts, and he gave me, in return, the use of my own press and my own clerks and my own wax, and no coin, and his memory, which he had told me twice he would not lose, and which I had learned to treat as a thing kept on a high shelf that could come down on me in any season.

He was away most of those years. He was a traveling emperor, gone north to the wall they were building across Britain to keep the painted men out, gone to the Rhine, gone to walk the frontiers he had decided to hold instead of widen, building everywhere he went, a basilica here and a bath there and his own great round temple rising in Rome with its eye open to the sky. I felt him as you feel weather coming, by the way the men nearer the top of the hill carried themselves, tightening or easing as word came down of where he was and what mood the road had left him in. The terror was behind us. The Four were nine years dead and the city had stopped flinching at a knock, and a man could walk to the baths again without rehearsing what he would say to a stranger who asked him the wrong question. I was not hunted. I was useful, and leashed, and let alone, and I had a wharf that made money and a press that made more, and a wife who ran the books better than I ever had, and I was, if you had asked the carters and the wharf-women, a fortunate man.

The ocean program crawled. I had its reports through the fiscus, dry as bone. The African coast was mapped a few headlands further south than the season before, the survey resting on the map and never on the scouts, as the emperor had ordered. The yard on the western shore had built two hulls and lost one in a gale within sight of its own slip and was building a third. The prize for a clock that would keep true time a month at sea stood unwon, and would stand unwon, I thought, after I was in the ground, because I had hung the whole gift on a wall I had told the emperor plainly I could not climb. It was the only thing in my life doing what I had meant it to do, which was almost nothing, very slowly, in the right direction, for a long time.

I was six-and-forty. Macer was past seventy-five and would not die. Heras was past seventy and aging the way an old wall ages, the footing still sound and the face going. And I was holding my own ledger at arm's length, and would soon need a boy.

---

I came to the thing in Marcia's storeroom, of all places, the driest room on the wharf, the one that smelled of pepper and rope, because that was where she did the part of the books she would not do where the clerks could see, and where I went when I wanted to talk to her and not to the partner who ran my accounts.

She had Aquitania in front of her, my Aquitania, the sheet I could not read, and she was reading it with no trouble at all, her own near sight not yet gone the way mine had, and she had found something in it I had missed, a freight charge entered twice in two quarters under two carters' names that were the same man.

"You missed this," she said. "A year ago you would not have missed this."

"A year ago I could see it."

She put the stylus down. Marcia did not waste a beat pretending I had not said what I said. She had heard the whole story from Heras, who told her things he did not tell me he told her, and she had watched me hold the work off, and she had been waiting, I think, for me to bring it into a room myself rather than carry it around the yard like a stone in my shoe.

"So," she said. "You will go blind to small figures by fifty, near enough, the way Heras's are gone. And you cannot fix it. And the work you are kept for is small figures." She said it the way she said everything, flat, finding the level. "We should talk about what you are building, because you have been building as though you had thirty years and good eyes, and you have neither."

"I have the press," I said. "I have the contest. I have the survey crawling at the coast."

"You have things that are yours," Marcia said, "and a thing that is yours dies when you do. The contest is yours. You fund it and Macer takes the credit and you judge it, and the day you die it is a story Macer's heirs will not pay for. The press is yours, and Eros's hands are old, and the second pressman is a borrowed man, and there is no boy learning to cut blocks because you have not made it anyone's living to teach him. The survey is the emperor's, and emperors change their minds about the sea between one bad mood and the next. You have spent twenty years making yourself the one man who knows how to do each of these things, and that was clever when you needed to be too valuable to kill, and it is stupid now, because you are going to die with all of it still living only in your head and your hands, and your head is starting to go, and your hands will follow."

I want to be exact about what happened in me there, because it was not a flash and it was not a grief, though there was grief under it. It was the way a sum comes out when you have been adding the wrong column and you finally add the right one and the figure that drops out is the figure that was always going to drop out, the one you had been refusing to total. I had known it since the rail carts at the quarry, that I would not win in my lifetime. I had said it to Heras and to myself a dozen times, the steel will not come, the clock will not come, the ocean will not be crossed by men I taught, and I had said it the way you say a thing you do not yet believe in your body. I believed it in my body in Marcia's storeroom, holding a sheet I could not read, listening to a woman whose eyes still held tell me my head was going.

"Then it comes down to the people," I said slowly. "Whoever does the things after me."

"Now you have it," Marcia said, and went back to her column.

---

I went to Heras with it, because Marcia had given me the figure and I wanted to hear the argument, and Heras was the only man left who would argue with me without being afraid of where the argument went.

He was in his own room off the colonnade, with his feet up because the stairs had cost him, and he had a lamp pulled close and was reading a Hippocrates he had read four hundred times and could not have needed the words of, and he had it at arm's length, the scroll held out the way I held Aquitania, and neither of us said anything about that.

"Marcia says my head's going," I told him, "and that I've built everything so it only works while I'm alive to work it. She's right. I've been thinking about it wrong for twenty years. I kept trying to do the thing. Get the steel. Get the clock. Cross the sea. Win it before I die." I sat on the stool by his feet. "I'm not going to win it before I die."

"No," Heras agreed comfortably, as a man agrees that it is raining.

"So the work changes. It stops being the steel. It becomes the men who'll get the steel after me. And the way you keep men at a thing for a hundred years after the man who started it is dead is to build a body. A thing with a name and money of its own and rules and a door, that goes on hiring the next boy whether I'm in the ground or not." I was finding it as I said it, the way I always found the true shape of a thing only out loud. "The press has to be a body. The contest has to be a body, with its own coin, not mine. There has to be a thing that pays a craftsman to chase steel for ten years and fail and chase it again, because no one man can afford to fail at steel for ten years and that's exactly why no one has the steel. And the books." I stopped. "The books most of all. The ladder. I've been writing it like a private thing, a few pages when Tyche has time. It's the only one of these that doesn't need money or an emperor or a body to outlive me. It only needs to get written and hidden well enough to be found. And it's the one I keep putting last, because the figures pay and the books don't, and now my eyes are going and I've spent the good years of them on a province's cartage."

Heras let the Hippocrates roll shut.

"You have just described," he said, "the work of the rest of your life, in a tone of voice a man uses to describe a funeral. Why?"

"Because it's all for after I'm dead," I said. "Every part of it. I won't see any of it work. I'll spend the last twenty years I've got building a staircase and never climb a step of it, and the steps I'm putting in I'll never see anyone stand on."

Heras was quiet a moment. Then he did the thing he did rarely, which was to drop the irony entirely, and it always landed harder than the irony.

"You are six-and-forty," he said. "I am past seventy. I have spent my whole life keeping men alive who would die anyway, a year sooner or a year later, and burying most of them in the end, and I have never once in fifty years cured a thing that did not come back. You think this is a sorrow I have not sat with." He tapped the scroll against his knee. "The man who wrote this died six hundred years ago, and I read his words tonight by a lamp, with my arm out straight because my eyes are gone, and he is in the room. That is the only kind of living-after there is. The rest is statues, and statues fall down on schedule, as your bridge-builder liked to say." He set the scroll on the table. "Write the books. Build your bodies. Stop mourning at your own work like a fool. You are not building a thing you will not see. You are being the man another man reads by a lamp in six hundred years with his arm held out straight. Now go home, it is late, and I am too old to comfort a barbarian past the second cup."

---

It was late when I came back across the yard, and there was a lamp still in the tally-room, and it was Ulpia at the bench, my daughter, who was nine that year and should have been asleep, with a printed sheet of the times-tables in front of her and a stub of charcoal, copying figures.

She was the second child, born the year I came back broken from the East, freeborn where I was made and Lucanus was made, a small fierce square-faced thing with Marcia's flat way of looking at you as if pricing you, and her own name, which had cost an argument, because the law gives a freedman's daughter his nomen and a feminine of nothing else, so she was Ulpia and only Ulpia on the rolls, and I had wanted a cognomen for her the way Lucanus had one, and Marcia had said a girl needs no cognomen, no one will ever write her on a contract, and I had said this one will, and we had named her Ulpia Severa, the grave one, the serious one, because she had come out of Marcia unsmiling and stayed that way, and the name had turned out to fit her like a poured thing.

"You should be asleep," I said.

"Lucanus said girls can't do the tables past the seven," she said, not looking up. "I'm doing the nine."

I looked over her shoulder, by reflex, to check her work, and the figures were a soft gray fog, and I stepped back the length of my arm, and then back again, and from there, at the wrong end of my own reach, in the bad lamplight, I could just make out that she had it right, the whole nine course down the page, the carry where the carry went, in a child's hand that pressed too hard.

"You have it right," I said.

"I know," said Ulpia Severa, and did the next line, and did not ask why her father had backed halfway across the room to read a thing a span from her hand.

I stood there at the far edge of the lamp's reach and watched her fill the page from a distance that had nothing to do with the dark, and I thought of the book that was a few pages long in a script no Roman could read, that started with these same nine courses and meant to climb from them to the shape of the moon's road and the engines I would never build, and how it would have to be written soon, while there was still an eye to write it and a hand to copy it and a child in a lamp who could be taught the cipher and would not ask why.

She would not ask why. That was the thing about her, even then. She would just do the next line.

---

## PART SIX — The Ladder

---

# Chapter 39: Institutions

A man cannot leave money to a press. I learned this from Marcia in the storeroom, the way I learned most law, by proposing something stupid and watching her decide whether it was worth the breath to tell me why.

I had said I would put the press in my will, leave it Eros's wages and a sum to cut new blocks, and let it run. She asked who received the bequest. The press, I said. She said a press is a thing, like a hammer, and you do not leave a hammer money, you leave it to a man, and the man sells it when his son wants the timber for a boat. A will moves things to people. It does not make a thing that holds itself.

"Then how does a thing hold itself," I said.

"It doesn't. People hold it. You want a thing that goes on when the people who care about it are dead, and the law was not written by men who wanted that. It was written to move a dead man's property to his living kin without a fight." She pulled a fresh tablet toward her. "So we do not give the law a thing it hates. We give it one it knows. A burial club."

I did not follow, and said so.

"A man dies," she said, "and his trade fellows want him buried decent and his name said once a year over wine, so they make a *collegium*. They pay in monthly. They own a strongbox and a meeting room and a slave to sweep it, and they take in new members as the old ones die, and the thing goes on a hundred years burying men who weren't born when it started. The law likes a funeral. Nobody fears a funeral club." She tapped the wax. "A club can hold property. A club can take a gift. A club can pay wages out of rents and never die, because it is not a man. We will build a society of men devoted to printing, with you as their dead founder before you are even dead, and the press is the thing the society keeps, the way a burial club keeps the cup it drinks from."

"And I'm the corpse they toast."

"You're the corpse who left them a quarry's worth of pearwood," she said, "so they'll toast you sincerely a while."

---

It took the better part of a year, and most of it was not mine. I stood beside the thing while Marcia made it, because the work had stopped being mine to make and I had not stopped reaching for the tools.

She found the form in an old guild of letter-copyers, a sleepy society of a dozen freedmen who met four times a year, drank bad wine, and copied wills and petitions for the courts at fixed rates. They had a charter, a strongbox, two rooms behind a fuller's, and a god, which mattered. Marcia bought their goodwill with a hall of their own and a promise that the new craft would not undercut their court work, and folded the press into them as a new branch, the cutters and pressmen and inkers entering as members who paid their monthly coin and drew their wages from the society's rents and the sale of sheets. I endowed it. That dry word covered an hour in a notary's room while a man read out in formula-Latin that Marcus Ulpius Danihel conveyed to the society a half-share in the rents of two wharf-front shops, in perpetuity, the income applied to the wages of the printing-branch, the cutting of blocks, and the teaching of apprentices, and that I reserved no power to revoke it. That last clause was the whole point. To sign away the power to take it back is to admit, on a tablet, before a notary who does not care, that you have begun to plan a world you are not in.

I signed it. Eros witnessed it, because the law wanted citizen witnesses and he had been one since before I was, and when the notary finished he said, dry, that he had cut blocks for twenty years for a man who could be sold, and now he was a brick in a building, and he wasn't sure which of us had come up in the world. Then he asked, because Eros never let a sentiment stand without putting a tool in its hand, who would teach the boy.

---

There was no boy. That was the hole in the whole structure, the one Marcia had named in the storeroom.

Eros cut blocks. He had cut every block we ever printed from, and he cut them the way an old man does a thing he has done forty thousand times, well and slowly, his eyes worse than mine from twenty years of pearwood by lamplight, holding the gouge at the length of his arm now and feeling the line as much as seeing it. The second pressman could pull sheets. He could not cut. Cutting a block in reverse, a whole page of letters backward and clean so they print forward, is a thing you learn in years or not at all, and we had no one learning it, because I had never made it anyone's livelihood to spend three years cutting spoiled blocks before he cut a good one. I had needed Eros and I had had Eros, and a man who has what he needs does not train the man who will need it after him.

We took an apprentice the autumn the charter was sealed. His name was Vitalis, and he was twelve, a wood-carver's son from the Subura, freeborn, his father dead of the autumn fever, his mother glad of a place that fed him. He came with his own small knives wrapped in a rag and a way of holding wood up to the light to read the grain before he set the edge to it, which his father had taught him, and which Eros watched once and grunted at, and that grunt was the boy hired.

The bargain the society made with him was the thing I had not understood I was building until I watched it signed. Three years bound, fed and housed and taught, no wage; then journeyman's wages and a member's share; and a clause that he would in his turn teach two before he drew his pension from the funeral fund. He was bound to the society, which would outlast me, feed him when his eyes went the way Eros's had gone and mine were going, and which he would feed in turn. I had spent twenty years being the only man who knew how to do a thing so no one could afford to kill me. I sat in the fuller's back room and watched a freeborn boy bind himself to a club so the knowing would be in more than one pair of hands, and the cleverness I had lived by all that time turned over in my chest and lay the other way up.

The first month he ruined eleven blocks. Eros made him cut the same line of the times-table backward until the seven came clean, and the boy cut it eighty times, and on the eighty-first Eros pulled a sheet and laid it under the boy's nose and said nothing, and the boy looked at his own seven printed forward and true. I heard it from Eros that evening, the way I now heard most of what the press did, secondhand, from the men who did it.

---

The contest was harder, because the contest had a god in it, and the god had a tax man.

It ran, still, the way it had run for twenty years. I funded it from my own coin, Macer took the credit and gave the reading in his domus, and I stood as the tame barbarian judge. It was held every spring on the Quinquatrus, Minerva's festival, chosen long ago as cover, a thing crowned on the goddess of true craft so no haruspex could call it impious. And since the year they nearly broke me on the Caelian, since the white heifer and the dedication I had led with my own hand, the priestly colleges held a hook in it. A right to be consulted. A priest the colleges sent each spring, who watched the reading, took an offering, said his words over the winners before they were crowned, and was given a sum everyone called incense and everyone knew was for the colleges.

To outlive me the contest needed its own coin, the way the press now drew its wages from rents. And the moment it had its own coin it became a property with an income, and a property the priestly colleges held a standing right in was a thing they could, on a bad day, with the right enemy, claim to administer for the gods. Endow it as we had the press, Marcia said, and we would have built the colleges a permanent revenue and handed them, in the same act, the argument for taking it.

"So we don't endow it," I said.

"So we endow it carefully," she said, "and we feed the priest before he is hungry."

What she built I would not have thought of in ten years, and a Roman widow who had fought her husband's cousins for her own warehouse thought of in an afternoon. She did not endow the contest. She endowed Minerva. A sum was settled, through the same society dressed for the purpose, as a perpetual gift to the goddess for the keeping of her spring rite, the cleaning of a small shrine of hers on the Aventine, the incense, the priest's portion. And bound into the deed, as the founder's wish for how the gift be administered, was the contest: the prize said yearly on her festival, the winners blessed by her priest, the whole machine of it folded inside a pious endowment as the works a temple does with a temple's money. The colleges got their revenue named and fixed and theirs, which made them the contest's protectors against any later priest who might meddle, because you do not let another man tax what is already taxed to you. And the contest got walls. It became a rite, and a rite is harder to abolish in Rome than a funeral.

It cost more than funding it from my purse ever had. I said as much, that we had taken a thing I paid for out of pocket and built it a temple's overhead, a priest's stipend, incense by the pound forever.

"You paid for it out of pocket," she said, "and your pocket dies. Now Minerva pays for it, and she is very old and shows no sign of dying. She is expensive and she does nothing and she will outlast every man who ever tried to break you. For a freedman with enemies and a barbarian's name, she is the cheapest senator you will ever keep."

Vibenius was four years dead by then. I do not know what he would have made of a thing built half to honor the hook he had set in me and half to slip out of it, a prayer with a clause. I had told him once, in the worst hour of my life, that building the ladder was thanking the gods in the only language that did not lie, which was work, and I had not meant a word of it, and now I had built the contest a permanent priest out of that lie made law. He had said what I did with his words was not in my hands. Here was a thing I had done with them, and no one left who could say whether it counted as the lie coming true or the truth put to use.

---

The patronage fund was Marcia's whole skill and almost nothing of mine. The idea I had carried for years. No one man can afford to fail at steel for ten years, which is precisely why no man has the steel. Hermes had chased it since before I was free, gorgeous one batch and brittle garbage the next, and every wasted batch was bread out of his own mouth, so he chased it only in the slack hours, between the plowshares and gate-bands that paid. A man who must eat cannot fail long enough to win. So build a thing that pays a man to fail, a fund to keep a craftsman at one hard problem for ten years, fed, supplied, free to ruin a hundred crucibles, asked for nothing but the trying and the writing-down of what failed.

Saying it was the easy part. Making it a thing the law would hold and a man could not pocket and walk away from took Marcia three drafts and a quarrel with a notary. A foundation in trust, the income of a settled capital tied to a purpose, administered by the same society that held the press, with a board of three who chose the craftsmen, judged the writing-down, and paid the next year's coin only against the last year's labor honestly recorded, win or fail. The capital could not be touched, only its income spent. A craftsman drew a living wage, enough to keep him at the bench and not enough to make the bench worth abandoning. And he held his post against his record of attempts, not his successes, which made the whole thing strange and made it work, because the moment you pay a man for results he stops telling you about his failures, and his failures are the rungs.

I put Hermes on it first, which was no decision, only a debt paid. He was past sixty and his shoulder had gone bad and he took the steel commission the way an old soldier takes a pension that lets him keep marching, with no thanks and much complaint about the board's record-keeping. He would chase repeatable steel until he died, and fail, and how he failed, batch by batch, the iron and the charcoal and the color of the heat his eye could no longer trust, went into a book the society kept and Tyche kept a copy of in the cipher, worth more to whoever came after than any blade Hermes ever drew gorgeous and broke in the quench.

---

I had not seen Felix in years when he came to the wharf about the fund. He had been a boy when he won the contest, a counting-house slave nineteen or twenty, sharp and starved-looking, who had written a tale of a ship that fed its own fire and drove itself across the sea on a metal that did not yet exist, and who had said, at the year's end, that he thought he could fix the pot. I had laundered his prize toward his peculium because the law would not let me simply give a slave a hundred denarii, and had not thought hard about him since.

He was forty now, near enough, broad through the chest where the boy had been all elbows, a free man six years and a bronze-caster with a furnace of his own behind the cattle market. He had bought himself out at thirty-four with the peculium the prize had helped fatten, and carried his freedom lightly, without the wound the freed-by-favor carry. He had heard, the way the cattle-market end of the river hears everything, that the Thulean's widow was paying men to fail at hard things, and walked over to ask whether the pot counted.

I asked if he still had the tale. He had burned it years ago, he said, it embarrassed him, a boy's drawing of a ship with no oars and no sail and a stack belching smoke like a kitchen. What he had not burned was the principle, that fire makes water into a wind, and a wind can push a thing, and if you could catch the wind from a boiling pot and make it shove and shove again instead of just lifting a lid, you would have a horse that ate wood and never tired. He had built a dozen pots in the years between. He showed me his hands while he said it, the old burns layered under the new, the way mine had once. The pots all tore, or the wind got out the wrong way, or the bronze went soft at the seam where the heat lived, and that last was the wall, the same wall the old smith was at. You needed a metal that held its strength when it glowed, and there was none in Rome.

"There isn't," I said. "Hermes has been failing to make it for thirty years. We're paying him to keep failing at it now."

"Then I will fail next to him," Felix said, "and use whatever he gets."

I gave him a wage, a term of years, a furnace allowance, and an order to write down every pot that tore and how. I told him, because Marcia made me say it to his face so there could be no later grievance, that the board judged him by the writing and not the working, that he would almost certainly die at this, that I had a wall I would die at too, that the steel which held its strength in the fire might not come in his grandchildren's lives, and that he was being paid, in plain words, to climb a ladder whose top neither of us would see and leave the rungs cut clean for whoever came after.

He heard all of it. Then he asked the only question that mattered to him, how many pots a year the allowance bought.

I told him. He did the sum faster than I could now, holding nothing at arm's length, his eyes still good, and said it bought enough to fail honestly, which was all he had ever wanted, that he had spent six years failing dishonestly in the slack hours, lying to himself the next pot was the one, and a man paid to say the pot tore was richer than a man who had to pretend it hadn't. Then he went off to talk to Hermes about charcoal, two men who would fail at the same wall from opposite sides for the rest of their lives, on my coin, with my blessing. The strange thing was that I envied them, watching them go, both still able to read their work close.

---

Lucanus did not want the workshop. I let myself know it the spring of the apprentice, when he was sixteen and came to the press once because I asked him to and stood in the ink-smell with his hands behind his back the way a guest stands in a temple he does not worship in. He was a good boy, freeborn, citizen-clear where I would always carry the freedman's stain, and he wanted the things the freeborn want, the law and the rolls and a seat in the basilica, to be a man who decides cases and not a man who cuts blocks. He had Marcia's flat way of pricing a room and none of her hunger for the warehouse, and he looked at the press, the engine of everything I would leave, and saw a shed full of stained men, and was too kind to say so and too honest to pretend. I did not push him. I had a daughter who could read close.

Ulpia was eleven and did the sums Lucanus would not. She came to the yard when Marcia let her and stood at Tyche's elbow at the tally-bench, and Tyche, who suffered no one gladly and the child least of all at first, had begun to hand her real columns, the wharf's own, because the girl was faster than the second clerk and made fewer errors. There was no place for her in any of it. A freedman's daughter does not keep a counting-house. She marries where her mother can place her and brings her figures to her own kitchen accounts. Marcia said this without heat, the way she said the level of a thing, that the girl was wasted and would stay wasted and I should not fill her head with a future the law had bolted shut. I said the law had bolted a great many doors I had walked through anyway. Marcia said yes, and I had walked through them as a man, and the girl was not one, and would I please not teach my daughter to break herself against a wall I could see and she could not. I had no answer. I taught her the nine-times course and then the elevens, and did not teach her the cipher, not yet, though Tyche had begun to look at the child the way you look at a hand you might one day need.

---

I came to the press one evening late, the autumn the charter was a year old, to find Eros gone home and the lamp left burning over the cutting-bench, against every rule of the shed, fire and oil and a hundred dry blocks. The apprentice was asleep over his work, his head on his folded arm, a half-cut block under his slack hand and the gouge fallen on the boards. He had been cutting a prize-sheet for the spring, a tale of a cart that ran on a road of iron, and had gotten the wheels and one cart and was at the smoking stack when sleep took him.

I trimmed the wick to nothing and pinched it out, and took the boy by the shoulder, not hard, the way you wake a son, and told him to go to his pallet, the block would keep. He went mumbling that he had the stack nearly right. I picked the gouge up off the floor and felt the edge with my thumb in the dark, the way Hermes had taught me to read a blade I could not see, and it was keen, he kept it keen, the boy who would cut the blocks when Eros and I were under the ground. I set it on the bench where his hand would find it in the morning, and left, and did not look at the block, because I could not have read it close enough to spoil my own pleasure in not needing to.

---

# Chapter 40: The Books

The first page took me eleven days, and the first page was the easy one, because the first page was counting.

I had thought it would be the bottom that gave me trouble, the high rungs, the things I half-knew dressed up as things I knew. The bottom was where I had to be slowest. A man writing the top of a ladder can lean on the man who will read it being already most of the way up. The bottom assumes nothing. I sat at the cutting-bench Eros let me use in the slack hour after the lamp-trim, with a stack of cheap palimpsest I scraped myself because I would not waste a clerk on this, and I wrote for a reader who could not yet count past his fingers, in a language no one within a thousand years would be able to sound out, and I found I did not know how to begin a thing that began with nothing.

Tyche read the eleven days' work in an evening. She read it the way she read a tally she suspected, finger under the line, lips not moving. She had read my homeland script for twenty years and could write it faster than I could now, my hand having gone the way of my eyes, the letters wanting to be larger every season. When she finished she set it down and said it was good, and then said the seven was wrong on the third page, I had drawn the curve of it backward, and that a child copying it would learn a backward seven and teach it to his children, and did I want a thousand-year line of people writing the seven the wrong way because the Thulean's hand shook on a winter night.

I fixed the seven.

---

This is the way the thing was ordered, because the order was the whole of it.

You climb a ladder by the bottom rung. There is no other place to put your foot. I had learned that the hard way over thirty years, watching numerals crawl into Rome at the speed of a fish-seller's slate while the men who could have used them best, the senators with their abacus-slaves, kept the old marks for prestige. You cannot hand a man the top of the thing. He has nowhere to stand. So the books began where a person begins, and went up only as fast as a foot can follow a foot.

First the numbers, and the great trick of writing them so their place tells their size, which is the trick I came in on and the trick that asks nothing of a reader but patience. Then the four workings on them, and I wrote out the long division in full, every step, the way I had once flubbed it under a Greek physician's eye and ground it back to the truth by hand, because the grinding-back is the lesson and the answer is nothing. Then the unknown thing, the letter that stands for a number you do not yet have, which Tyche took to in an afternoon the way she had taken to everything, and which I knew would stop most readers cold, so I built ten rungs where one would do. Then shapes and the measuring of them, the right triangle and its squared sides, the circle and the number that will not come out clean. Then the idea I never had the rigor of and could only draw the shape of, the thing about the line that touches a curve at a point, and the area trapped under a curve that you find by chopping it into pieces too thin to count, and I wrote at the head of that page, in plain words, that I did not have the proof of this and never had, that I was handing down the shape of a tool and not the making of it, and that a man who built on it without finding the proof himself was building on my word, which was the word of a man who had it secondhand from teachers he half-remembered.

Then the three laws of how things move, which I had carried since I was a boy and which I wrote down one rainy afternoon with great confidence and read back the next morning with none.

---

Here is the excerpt as it stood when I left it, the entry on motion, in the homeland script, set down plain for a reader I will never meet. I render it here in the homeland tongue. It is flatter than the rest of this account, because it was meant to teach and not to remember.

> *If you have read the pages before this one, you know how to weigh a thing and how to measure how far it goes and how long it takes. Now you will learn how things move, which is three rules, and a man named Newton found them, who lived in my country long after my time and long before yours, and whom I never met and have read about only a little.*
>
> *The first rule. A thing that is still stays still. A thing that moves keeps moving in a straight line at the same speed, forever, unless something pushes or pulls it. You will say this is false, because a rolling ball stops. The ball stops because the ground drags on it and the air pushes back. Take those away and it never stops. There is no such place on the earth, so you must imagine it. The imagining is the lesson.*
>
> *The second rule. To change how a thing moves, you push it. A bigger push changes it more. A heavier thing changes less for the same push. I believe these three quantities, the push, the change, and the weight, can be set in one true line of figures, and that the push equals the weight times how fast the moving changes, but I am not certain I have the form of it right, and I never could derive it, only use it. Find the true form before you trust it. Do not trust it because I wrote it.*
>
> *The third rule. When you push on a thing, it pushes back on you, the same, the other way. Stand on a boat and shove the dock and the boat goes out from under you. This one I am sure of.*

I wrote *I am not certain* into the second law and left it there. I could have smoothed it. A man writing for the unborn wants to sound like a god, and the temptation to write the second law clean and sure, to let the reader believe the Thulean had it whole, was the strongest pull I felt in the whole long labor. But a clean false rung is the worst thing a ladder can have. A missing rung a climber sees and reaches past. A false one holds his weight until it doesn't, and by then he is high up. So I wrote the doubt into the wood.

---

The chemistry was where the floor went out from under me.

I had meant to write how the steam engine worked. It was the keystone of the whole upper structure, the thing every prize-tale dreamed of, the horse that ate wood and never tired, the metal that did not yet exist. Felix was failing at it on my coin a quarter-mile off and I owed his grandchildren a true account of what the pot was supposed to do. I knew the shape of it cold. Fire heats water, water becomes steam, steam wants more room than water, that wanting is a push, you catch the push and make it shove a thing back and forth, and the back and forth turns a wheel. I had said this aloud a hundred times. I sat down to write the middle of it, the part where the wanting becomes the shoving, why the steam pushes and keeps pushing instead of simply lifting a lid once and going slack, what it is in the heat that becomes the motion, and I came to the middle of the explanation and there was nothing there.

I knew that heat was the little ones moving, the smallest pieces of a thing, jostling faster when hot. I had written that on an earlier page and believed it. I knew steam pushed. I could not put the one to the other. The bridge between the jostling and the push, the thing my teachers must have drawn on a board for a bored boy on a spring afternoon while he watched the clock, was simply gone, scraped clean as the palimpsest, and I sat there at fifty years old with a stick of charcoal in my hand and reached for it the way your tongue reaches for a name you have known your whole life and find you cannot say, and it would not come, and it has not come since.

I wrote what I had. I wrote that fire makes the little ones move faster, that the faster moving is what we feel as heat, that steam pushes hard and an engine catches the push, and that *I do not know what joins the moving to the pushing. There is a thing here I knew once and have lost. A man who finds it has found the heart of the engine. I have only the two ends of the rope and not the middle.* And I sat with that a while, because it was one thing to know the books would have holes and another to watch a hole open under my own hand in the rung I had thought the soundest.

---

It got worse before I made my peace with it, and I did not entirely make my peace with it.

The quadratic. I had carried the quadratic from the cell, from the second night in this world, scratched into my first panicked notes for whoever found my body, and even then I had written beside it that I was not sure I had the sign right. Thirty-three years. I sat down to set it on the page clean, the formula for the unknown that solves the squared thing, a thing I could once have written in my sleep at seventeen, and I wrote it, and the minus before the first part looked wrong, and I changed it to a plus, and the plus looked wrong, and I worked a problem I knew the answer to forward to check it and got the answer, then worked it with the sign flipped and got the answer too, because I had chosen a problem where it came out the same either way, like a fool, and I had to build three more problems by hand to trap the sign, and it took me most of a morning to be sure of a thing a boy is made to memorize in a week. I got it right in the end. But I had needed the morning, and I had not needed the morning at seventeen, and the difference between those two mornings is the whole of what these books were a race against.

The planets undid me the same way and I could not fix it by working. I had drawn the sun in the middle, which I was sure of, and the earth going round it, which had cost men their lives to say and which I gave away on a page for a child. I set the others in their order out from the sun and got to the count of them and stopped. There was an inner pair, hot and quick, then us, then the red one, then the giant, then the ringed one, and out past those a cold pair I could no longer keep straight, their order and even how many, two or three, and the names I had for them were homeland names that meant nothing and that I could not be sure I had hung on the right worlds. I sat and tried to walk it out from the sun the way you walk a familiar street in the dark and found the street went strange past the ringed one, the houses out of order, a turning I could not place.

So I wrote the ones I was sure of in their order and drew them to no scale, with the gaps marked as gaps, and at the cold far end I wrote *out here are more, two or three, I am no longer certain of their number or their order, and the names I knew are no use to you. I have set down only what I can swear to. Do not fill the rest from my drawing. Go and look.* It is a poor thing, an account of the heavens with the far rooms left dark. But a man who drew the far worlds wrong would send some later astronomer hunting a planet that was not where the Thulean swore it would be, and call the Thulean a liar, and rightly, and throw out the sun in the middle along with the lie.

---

That was the principle, and it cost on every page, and I held to it because the alternative was worse. The books would say what I knew, and mark plainly where I did not, and leave the dark rooms dark rather than furnish them with guesses dressed as facts. Tyche enforced it harder than I did. She had a small flat way of reading a too-confident sentence back to me, and asking, in the tone she used on a clerk's rounded-up figure, whether I knew that or only felt it, and the number of times the honest answer was *felt it* shamed me. Where I felt it, we marked it felt. The marking became its own small language in the script, a hooked stroke before a sentence that meant *the Thulean is guessing here*, so that a reader four hundred years on would know which of his rungs were oak and which were the shape of oak drawn in the air.

The biology I could barely begin. I knew bodies were built of small rooms too small to see, and that the same little ones that climbed into a wound to fester were everywhere, in water, in breath, and that creatures over long ages changed into other creatures by the slow weeding of which ones lived to breed, and that this last was a thing men had killed each other over in my country and that I believed it and could not prove a word of it. I wrote it as a story and called it a story. I wrote that I could not show him the little rooms because the glass to see them did not exist, that I had once claimed a glass that could and that the claim had been a lie I told to live, and that he should not believe the rooms are there because the Thulean says so, but should build the glass and look, and that the day he looks will be a greater day than any I have had.

---

It had to live past me, and one copy in one hand is a fire away from never having been. So the plan, which was Marcia's and Tyche's far more than mine.

There would be three copies of the whole, in the homeland script, and a fourth begun in Latin only as far up as the shapes, because past the shapes the Latin failed me and the cipher was the safer keeping anyway. The three would not sit in one room. One Tyche kept, moved when the wind changed, its place hers alone since the bad year when the informer came smelling at the wharf. One went into the keeping of the printers' society, sealed, listed in their charter among the founder's papers as a thing not to be opened or sold, a brick in a wall built to stand a hundred years burying men. One we meant to put where no living interest touched it, sealed in lead and pitch and laid up in a dry place, a wall, a tomb, a foundation, against the day the upper rungs caught up to it and someone living could read them, and against the likelier day they did not and it waited longer.

And the script itself, which was the lock, had to be a lock that opened. A cipher one person can read dies with the person, and then the books are stone that no one in the world can lift. So the homeland script had to pass to more than one trusted hand, taught the way Vitalis was taught the gouge, slowly, to someone young enough to carry it forty years.

---

Tyche was forty-six and the only living soul who read it whole, and she said so to me one evening over the third copy, not as a complaint, as a figure. One hand, she said. You have built the most patient thing in Rome and put its only key in one aging head, mine, and you are no younger, and if a cart takes me on the wharf the books are a box of marks and you are an old man who cannot see to read his own writing.

I said I knew. I said I had been thinking on it.

She did not say the name. She let her eyes go where the name was, which was the tally-bench in the corner where Ulpia stood most evenings now that Marcia let her come, eleven going on twelve, square and grave, working a column of the wharf's own figures faster than the second clerk and checking it twice because Tyche had drilled the checking into her until it was a thing her hand did without her. The child did not look up. She had the same flat refusal to be hurried that Tyche had, that Marcia had, the long-suffering frown of a small person doing a large person's work and finding the large persons careless. She wrote a seven, and it was the right way round, because Tyche had taught her, and I watched my daughter draw the seven the way I had finally fixed it on the third page after the curve came out backward, and said nothing, because Marcia had told me not to teach the girl to break herself against a door the law had bolted, and a counting-house was a bolted door, and the homeland script was a deeper room behind a door I had not yet decided to open.

Tyche had decided. I could see she had decided, watching the girl the way you watch a hand you mean to need. She did not say it and I did not ask it and we left it where it lay, a thing for another evening, the key and the young head it might pass to, the both of us looking at a child who did not know she was being weighed.

---

I went on writing through that winter and into the spring, slower as the light failed earlier and my arm grew tired of holding the work out where my eyes could find it. Some nights I wrote a single sound rung and felt I had done a day's good work. Some nights I scraped more than I kept. The upper pages, the engine and the lights in the sky and the little rooms in the blood, filled with the hooked guessing-stroke until they looked, in the lamplight, like a thing more chewed than written. The lower pages, the counting and the shapes, stood clean and sure, oak all the way up, because the bottom of a ladder is the part a man keeps longest, the part they put in him before the world had a chance to take it back out.

Near the end of the writing season I came to a page I had left blank on purpose, the page where the chemistry should have given the names of the things the world is built from, the small unsplittable pieces and how they joined, and I had nothing for it but a handful of words and the shape of a picture, little balls hooking onto little balls, that I was three-quarters sure was wrong in some way I could no longer locate. I sat with the blank page a long time. Then I wrote across the top of it, in the largest letters my hand had come to, so a reader could not miss them and call them an oversight, the one true thing I had to put there: *Here is a great room I cannot light for you. I knew there was knowledge here and I have lost the shape of it past saving. Do not take my silence for there being nothing. Take it that the Thulean reached this rung and his hand was empty. Cut this rung yourselves. You will, and on the day you do you will have passed me, and that is the whole reason I wrote any of it down.* And I left the rest of the page bare under the words, white and waiting, and set the next page after it as if the empty one were full, and went up to the house, where Marcia had kept a lamp.

---

# Chapter 41: Teaching

The hardest part of teaching a language with no speakers is that there is no one to be wrong in front of.

When I learned Latin I learned it by saying a thing badly to a man who winced, and saying it less badly the next day because the wince had taught me where my mouth was lying. The homeland script had no winces in it. Tyche read it and wrote it cold and silent, the way you read a tally, and the two new hands I set down beside her that spring read it the same way, lips still, because there was no sound to put to it. I had spent thirty years making sure of that. The script was a wall, and a wall does not talk back. So they learned it the way you learn a lock, by the turning, and not one of them ever knew whether the words in their mouths would have made a man on the far side of the world laugh.

I had thought, when Tyche pressed me over the third copy, that the danger was time. It was time. But the thing I had not counted was that I would have to sit in a room and watch other people be handed the inside of my head, a piece at a time, and feel each piece leave.

---

We did it at the wharf in the slack hour, the same bench, the same cheap palimpsest, after Eros trimmed the lamps and the press went quiet under its oiled cloth. Tyche taught. I had wanted to teach it myself and she told me no, in the tone she used on a clerk, that my hand shook and my eyes were going and the children would learn my errors as gospel, and that a thing taught by the man who made it is taught wrong, because he teaches what he meant and not what he wrote. So I sat at the end of the bench and was a thing to be consulted, and mostly was not consulted, and that was correct, and I hated it the clean way you hate a true figure.

Three of them. Ulpia, who came most evenings now. Vitalis, who was fifteen by then and cutting blocks clean as any letterer in Rome, bound to the society for one more year and required by his indenture to teach two others, which made him a keeper before he knew the word. And a girl Marcia had bought that winter to keep the storeroom day-book, a Bithynian, eleven or twelve, collared when she came and uncollared within the month because Marcia would not have iron on a child who handled her ledgers, named Chloe by the man who sold her and kept by the name because she had had no other long enough to remember. Chloe could not read Latin when she came. By spring she read it better than Vitalis, having come to it hungry, the way Tyche had come to the figures in a week behind Macer's back, and Tyche watched her the first month the way a smith watches a heat he does not trust and then stopped watching, which from Tyche was a coronation.

They started where everyone starts. Tyche gave them the letters of the homeland script paired to sounds they would never hear spoken, and the first text she set them to break was not a poem and not a prayer. It was the bottom of the ladder. The numbers and the trick of writing them by place, the four workings, the long division in full. They learned the script by decoding the thing the script was built to carry, so that the lock and the room behind it came into them together. By the time Chloe could sound the homeland letters in her head she had already, without being told it was strange, read a thing no living person but the four of them and me could read, which was the order of the world set out for a reader not yet born.

Vitalis treated it as a craft, which it was for him, a third skill stacked on the gouge and the press, and he was sound and slow and never once asked what the words were *for*. Chloe treated it as a door, and went through fast and hungry and did not look at the frame. Ulpia treated it as a problem with a wrong answer in it somewhere, and she was the one who gave me trouble.

---

She found the order first. That was the thing.

She came to me on a night Tyche had set them to copy a clean page of the lower rungs, the counting, and she stood at my end of the bench with the palimpsest held out at the careful distance she had learned to hold a thing she wanted checked, and she said the script was too tidy.

I asked her what she meant.

She said a man making marks up to stand for sounds would make a mess of it. She had watched Vitalis invent a shorthand for his block-orders, she said, and it was a heap, a stroke here for a word he used often, a scratch there, no two parts of it built the same. The homeland script was not a heap. It had the same number of marks always, give or take, for a thing of a size; the marks for the small joining-words were short and the marks for the big words were long, the way a thing settles when it has been used by many hands for a long time and worn to fit the hand. She said the figures were tidy because numbers are tidy, the world made them so. But the words were tidy too, and words are not tidy, words are a mess everywhere, she had heard Greek and Syrian and the Subura's gutter-Latin and they were all a mess. Mine was not a mess.

I told her it had been used a long time where I came from. Which was true, and was the first true thing I had said to one of my own children about it, and it cost me nothing and it cost me everything, the way a true thing said sideways does.

She let it sit. She had Marcia's habit of letting a thing sit to see if it would say more on its own. Then she asked how long, and I said longer than Rome, and she looked at the page in her hand and you could see her doing the figure, the only figure she had ever met that she could not close, and not liking it, and putting it down half-worked the way she never put a tally down half-worked.

---

The argument with Marcia came the same week and it was not about the script. It was about all of it.

Marcia kept the storeroom warm with a brazier against the river damp, and the pepper and rope and hide smell of Caepio's old concern, which was hers, and she did her hardest talking there standing up with a ledger open under her hand because she liked to have a true thing in front of her while she said a thing that could not be made true. She said I was teaching the girl the whole ladder. The figures, the shapes, now the script, all of it, the same I would teach a son meant to run the thing after me. And there was no after-me the girl could run. A freedman's daughter could not keep a counting-house under her own name; could not hold the press-society's charter; could not stand in the basilica where Lucanus meant to stand. I was fitting Ulpia for a world that did not exist, Marcia said, and a child fitted for a world that does not exist is unfit for the one she has to live in, and that is a cruelty dressed as a gift, and she would not have it called a gift in her storeroom.

I said the bottlenecks had never been permission. I said I had walked through every bolted door I had ever met and that the locks had all been the same lock, which was a man's fear and a man's ignorance, and that nobody had handed me the key, I had been too stupid to know the door was bolted and walked at it and found the bolt was rust.

Marcia said I had walked through them as a man, and a strange one, a marvel the emperor had a use for, and that the law did not bolt a marvel's door the way it bolted a girl's. She said the rust was rust for me. For Ulpia it was iron, and teaching her to read iron as rust was how you got a girl to walk at a thing that would not give and break herself against it, smiling, because her father had told her doors were rust.

She was not wrong. That was the trouble with Marcia, she was almost never wrong about the world as it stood, and I was arguing the world as it might be moved, and there is no honest way to weigh a thing you can hold against a thing you only believe in. I had spent thirty years on the second kind of weighing and lost most of the bouts and gone on. But I was not the one who would do the breaking, and I had no answer to that, and I did not pretend to have one. I said I would not stop teaching her. Marcia said she had not asked me to stop. She had asked me to know what I was doing, so that when the door held and the girl came home with her face broken I would not stand there surprised, as if the law were a thing I had only just heard of.

We left it there. Nothing was decided, because the only thing that could decide it was a world neither of us could see, and we both knew the other was partly right and neither of us would say so.

---

Ulpia decided it herself, which I should have known she would, having made her.

She had heard the storeroom through the wall, or some of it, the way children hear the thing meant to be over their heads. She did not come to me with it. She came to Tyche, at the bench, and I was at my end pretending to mend a stylus and heard the whole of it, because they did not lower their voices and a man my age learns to be furniture.

She asked Tyche straight whether it was true a woman could not keep the books under her own name. Tyche said it was true. Ulpia asked whose name the books were kept under now, the wharf's books, Caepio's old concern. Tyche said Marcia's, the widow's. Ulpia asked how, if a woman could not. And Tyche, who had been freed by a man whose name the law had stapled to her against her will and who signed nothing with it, set down her pen and gave the girl the real answer, which was that the law said a great many things a woman could not do and that the world was full of women doing them under a man's name, a dead husband's, a son's, a tame freedman's, a name borrowed and held in front of the work like a shield, and that the trick was never to own the door but to be the one hand in the house that knew how to work the lock, because a name can be killed and a knowing cannot, and there is always, always a name to be had if you have the knowing, and the names go cheap and the knowing does not.

Ulpia thought about that. Then she said she did not want a borrowed name. She wanted to know the thing so completely that whatever name stood in front of it, the knowing under it would be hers, and they could put any name they liked on the door and it would still be her counting-house behind it whether the law could see her or not. She said this flatly, the way she said a sum, and went back to her copying.
