Tyche looked down the bench at me. I went on with the stylus. There is no answer to make to your own argument coming back out of a child's mouth in a harder form than you ever had the nerve for, except to be glad and to be afraid, which feel, when they come together, almost exactly the same.

---

So the script passed to her, and to the other two, that spring and the next, slow, the way Vitalis got the gouge. By the time the work was three years gone the lock opened to four living hands besides mine and Tyche's, and the books did not die when I did, which had been the whole point, and was a comfort, and was the strangest grief I knew, to sit at the end of a bench and watch the inside of my skull walk off in pieces inside three children, and to be glad of it, and to be, in the room where it was happening, more alone than I had been since the cell.

Because they would carry it all and they would never carry the one thing.

It came to a head over a word. Late, one of the upper pages, the cold sky, the part I had left half-dark on purpose. Ulpia was copying it and stopped on a word and brought it to me, the careful distance again, and asked what it was, because it was not a word for a thing. I had a word, in the homeland script, for a kind of weather that is not rain and not snow, a thing that falls grey and wet and freezing and is neither, a thing the homeland had so much of it had worn a short fat word smooth to name it, and Rome had never once needed the word because Rome had never once had the weather, and I had used it without thinking, on an upper page, describing cold, because my hand still reached for the homeland word for cold things the way your tongue reaches for the name of a street you grew up on.

She asked what it was. I told her a kind of cold rain. She said there was a word for cold rain three lines up and it was a different word, why two words. I said in the homeland there were many kinds of the cold falling, and many words. She asked why, if it was so cold there, anyone lived there, and where it was, and why I had a word for a thing Rome had no thing for, and how a place could be so far that no ship had ever brought one man of it but me, in thirty years, in a city that ate the whole world up the river every morning. She asked it the way she asked a sum that would not close, leaning into the wrongness of it, certain there was a true figure if she only pushed, and looking at me to push back, the way I had taught her to push back, because the pushing was the lesson.

And I had nothing to push with. I gave her the road-lie, Thule, the far north, the ice, the dark half the year, the same lie I had given a Greek physician's flat unconvinced face thirty-four years before in a cell with a wax tablet, and she took it the way Heras had taken it, which is to say she did not, quite, and was too well-mannered or too uncertain of her own arithmetic to say so. She put the word back on the page. She copied it faithfully, the smooth fat homeland word for a wet grey thing that fell on no street she would ever walk, into a book three children would carry a thousand years past a place that had the word and not the weather, and she went back to her bench.

I sat with that. There was a thing I could have said that would have closed her figure clean, and it was the one thing in the world I could not say to a living soul and least of all to her, and I had built a whole language to keep from having to, and the language had a word in it for sleet, and the word had given me away to my own daughter as far as it could give me away and no farther, which was: not at all, and entirely.

---

Heras came down to the wharf near the end of that season, which he did rarely now, seventy-nine and walking with two sticks and a boy at each elbow, to see the thing he had been arguing me toward for ten years, the man read by a lamp six hundred years on. He sat where I sat, at the end of the bench, and watched Tyche set the three of them a page, and watched Chloe break a line of the homeland letters into a true sum and not look up for praise because there was no praise to be had at that bench, only the next line. He watched the Bithynian girl, the bought child with the day-book hand, read a thing no senator in Rome could read.

He did not say anything clever. He had stopped, mostly, saving the cleverness for when his breath was up to it. He watched a long while, and the lamp burned down, and Eros came to trim it and Heras waved him off with a stick so as not to break the children's heads with the light, and we sat in the failing of it.

Then he said, low, that he had spent his life teaching boys what he knew, and that every one of them had got some of it and lost some of it and added a little wrong of their own, and that a thing taught is a thing handed to a thief, the student steals it and runs and you never see the whole of it together again, it lives now in the running.

I said I knew.

He said no, he did not think I did, not yet. He said the books were a fine thing and the lead box a fine thing, but that a book is a dead man's voice and the children were a live one, and that a live voice is the only kind that argues back, and that I should look at the small one with the borrowed name, the Bithynian, and consider that she had read my account of the order of the world before she was twelve and would spend forty years quarreling with it in her own head and teaching it to someone who would quarrel with her, and that this was the thing, not the lead box, this was the fire actually traveling, and that he was old and would not see where it went and neither would I and that this was, he had decided lately, the correct way for a fire to be carried, by people who would not see the far end of it, because a fire carried only by men who needed to see the far end would have gone out in the first generation that did not.

Then he asked Chloe, in Greek, what the sum on her page came to, to see if the child could turn her head from one tongue to another fast, and she answered him in the new figures, in Latin, without looking up, and got it right, and Heras laughed, the dry up-from-the-chest laugh I had not heard out of him in a year, and the two boys at his elbows startled, and the child finally looked up, at the old man laughing at her right answer, and went a little pink, and bent back to the next line.

I walked him to his litter. He took my arm on the wharf where it was uneven, which he had never done, and at the litter he held it a moment longer than the footing wanted, his hand light and dry and very cold even through my sleeve, and said only that I should keep the small one in figures, that one, she was the one, and then he was folded in and the bearers took him up the dark toward the Caelian, and I stood on the wharf in the smell of the cold river with my sleeve still cold where his hand had been, and went back in to where Tyche was sending the children home, one by one, into the streets that would never have a word for what fell on them, each carrying off a piece of a country that had no place on any map but the one I drew wrong on a wax tablet to save my life, and that none of them would ever miss, because you cannot miss a thing you were never told you were carrying.

---

# Chapter 42: Heras

He sent for me the way he sent for the wine merchant, by a boy with a list.

Marcia had the list from the boy and read it to me at the storeroom door, flat, the way she read a manifest, because she had known Heras almost as long as I had and would not have wept in front of his messenger to save her life. He wanted, the boy said, the account of what was owed on the press-society's physic chest, which he had stocked for years out of his own purse and meant now to be paid back into the foundation so it did not lapse when he did. He wanted to know whether Tyche had the right figure for it. He wanted me to come up the Caelian in the morning and not before, because he slept badly and the morning was the only part of the day his head was his own. And he wanted, the boy added, plainly reciting, for me not to bring a physician, on the grounds that he was one.

So I knew the shape of it before I went, which was a mercy he had arranged on purpose, being a man who had spent fifty years deciding how to tell people things they did not want to hear.

---

He was eighty that year, or near it; he had never been sure of his own birth and had made a joke of it that I had heard a hundred times, that a slave is born on the day he is bought and he had been bought twice, so he was either old or very old and would let me choose. He was in the small room off the colonnade where he read, propped against the wall on cushions Marcia had sent up over the years and he had never thrown out, and the lamp was unlit because it was full morning and the light came in clean off the white of the wall opposite. His hands were folded on the blanket. They were the same hands. That was the thing that caught me at the door and that I did not let catch me, the cleanest hands I had ever seen in this world or the other one, gone to spotted paper over the bones, folded on a blanket, and still, somehow, a doctor's, the way a tool keeps the shape of the work after the hand that held it is gone.

"You came up the steps too fast," he said, before I had said anything. "I can hear you breathing. You are fifty-two and you came up an old man's stairs like a boy late for a lesson. Sit down before you make more work for whoever has me next."

I sat down. Marcia had sent up bread and the soft white cheese he could still eat and a jug of the boiled water, our water, the wharf water through sand and charcoal and the long boil, which he had drunk for thirty years while telling me the boiling was a Greek discipline and the creatures were a barbarian's nightmare. He did not touch the cheese. He had me pour him a cup of the water and he held it and did not drink that either, just held it, for the warmth of the cup I think, his hands being cold.

"The chest," he said. "Tell me Tyche has the figure."

I told him she had the figure.

"To the as?"

"To the as."

"Good." He shut his eyes a moment, satisfied the way he was satisfied by a clean prognosis, a thing known and squared. "Then that is done, and you can stop pretending you came up the hill for an account. Eat the cheese, it will only go to the slaves and they have their own."

I ate the cheese so as to have something to do with my hands.

---

I had sat by a great many dying men by then. I want to be exact about that, because it matters to what this was and what it was not. I had knelt in churned dirt on a frontier holding a fistful of burning cloth, and I had sat five nights in a supply tent with a cup of water and a wound I had cleaned and packed and dressed perfectly and watched the perfect dressing fail, and I had stood in a yard and taken a plumb-line from a man who would not bend and would be dead in exile by the next season because of it. I knew what it was to sit by a death I had built, or failed to stop, or let happen by holding my tongue. I knew the particular taste of that, the way you keep doing arithmetic because your hands will not be still, adding the saved against the one lost and getting an answer that slams the scale and means nothing.

This was not that. There was no arithmetic in it. He was eighty and his heart was tired and his lungs filled a little at night, and there was nothing I had built that had reached him and nothing I had failed to build that could have turned it, because no one has ever built the thing that turns it, not in his world and not in mine, where it is only pushed a few years down a hill and arrives anyway. I sat there with my hands full of nothing to fix, and I want to say that was easier, and it was not. It was its own thing. I had spent thirty-five years being a man with a tool for everything, and I sat by the one death where the only correct thing to do was to be a man in a chair, and I had forgotten how, or had never known.

"You are looking at me," he said, eyes still shut, "the way you look at a machine that has stopped and you do not yet know why."

"No."

"Yes. I have watched you look at things for thirty-five years. You are doing the face." He opened his eyes. They were the same too, gone a little milky at the rims now, the lens stiffening the way he had told me mine was stiffening, both of us reading at arm's length down the same road, him further along it. "There is nothing in your kit for this, Daniel. I am telling you so you can put the kit down. It is the one complaint I have never been able to do anything for in fifty years either, and I have had longer to make my peace with the failure than you have, so let me have the dignity of it and do not sit there inventing a filter for old age."

I told him I was not inventing anything.

"You are inventing it behind your eyes. I can see the wheel going round." He shifted on the cushions, a small careful negotiation with his own ribs. "Pour yourself the water. You are going to want something in your hand for this and it had better not be wine, you have a hill to go back down."

---

He talked, that morning and the two mornings after, in the way he had always talked, which was around a thing and then suddenly through the middle of it. He did not talk about dying except as a clinical matter, the way he might have discussed a patient who happened to be in the room, and once he caught himself doing exactly that, narrating the slowing of his own pulse to me with two fingers on his own wrist, and stopped, and said it was a hard habit to break, watching, even when the body on the couch was the one you had been issued.

He wanted the medicine settled. That was the work of those mornings, under the talk. He had carried it for thirty years, the boiling and the washing and the clean cloth, all of it framed for Rome as Greek discipline and Hippocratic rigor and never once as creatures, because he had told me at the start that the creatures were witch-talk that would get me killed and had been proven right by the fact that I was not dead. He had taught it to a string of younger physicians and freedman surgeons and one army medicus who had taken it east, and he was clear-eyed to the point of cruelty about what would happen to it now.

"They will keep the parts that work and lose the reason," he said. "They have already begun. There is a man on the Esquiline, a good cutter, hands like a butcher and a butcher's luck which is better than skill, and he boils his instruments and his water and washes his hands raw and tells his patients it is to please Asclepius, that the god loves a clean altar. He does not believe your creatures. He saw that the men he cut lived more often when he did the cleaning, and he kept the cleaning and threw away everything else, and he has put a god under it because a god is what his patients understand." He turned the cup. "That is what becomes of a true thing in the world. It goes loose. It survives by forgetting why it is true. In a hundred years there will be men boiling water and washing wounds across half the empire and not one of them will know it was a barbarian's nightmare about little animals, and they will be right to do it and wrong about the reason, and the men they save will not care which."

I said it was better than nothing.

"It is much better than nothing. It is most of everything." He looked at me. "It is more than the flying thing ever did, and more than the bursting tube, and you should think about that on your way down the hill. The thing of yours that will save the most lives is the dullest thing you ever made and the one no one will ever put your name on, and I have been telling you that for thirty years and you have been not listening for thirty years, and I would like, before I am too tired to enjoy it, to be told that I was right."

"You were right."

"Say it as though it costs you something."

"You were right, you Greek bastard, the water was the whole thing, the rest was noise." It came out rough and he laughed at it, the dry up-from-the-chest laugh, shorter than it used to be, and it cost him and he did not care that it cost him.

---

The doctoring itself, the actual sitting-by and the hand on the wrist and the knowing what a body is going to do before it does it, that he could not hand to anyone and he knew it, and the losing of that was the only thing those mornings that I think genuinely grieved him. He had no quarrel with the dying itself. What grieved him was the eye going out of the world. He had a freedman of his own, a quiet Cappadocian named Zoticus who had been his man and his apprentice for fifteen years and was sound and careful and would carry on the practice. But Zoticus was a craftsman and Heras had been something else, a mind that had read a man's death in his color across a courtyard and had also read mine across a wax tablet in a cell when I was a frightened boy doing long division to prove I was real, and that, the seeing, did not pass down a line the way the boiling did. It went out with the man. He knew it. He had watched it go out of better physicians than himself, the teacher dying and leaving the method and taking the eye, and he had made his peace with being on the near side of it and now he was on the far.

"Zoticus will keep them alive," he said. "He will keep the chest stocked and the figures square and your foundation will pay him and he will be a good physician for forty years." He set the cup down on the table beside him, precisely, the way he set down a thing he was finished with. "And there will be no one left in Rome who can look at you across a room and tell you that you are guessing."

I did not say anything.

"You know what I am talking about."

"I know what you are talking about."

"I am not being modest. I am the last one. Macer could tell you that a thing would not make money, but Macer believes you about everything else because the flying convinced him forty years ago and an old man does not revise. The wife sees you clearer than I do, and she will tell you when you are wrong about a contract or a man, and she will not tell you when you are wrong about the world, because she does not argue the world, she takes it as it is and works it, that is her genius and it is not mine." He looked at the wall, at the clean morning light on it. "I argued the world. I argued you. I have spent thirty-five years telling you that you cannot see the small creatures and you have no proof of them and your map is a guess and your ladder is a prayer with a workman's apron on, and you went on building, and a good half of what you built was sound and the other half I could never be sure of and neither could you, and the only reason the unsound half did not run away with you entirely is that there was a man in the room who would say, out loud, in front of you, that you were guessing." He turned his head back to me. "When I am gone you will guess, and it will sound like knowing, because it sounds like knowing in your own head, it always has, that is the disease, and there will be no one to read the too-certain sentence back to you and ask whether you know it or only feel it."

It was Tyche's phrase, or she had made it into a mark on a page, but he had handed it to me at the start before either of us had a word for it. The hooked stroke. The thing I had built into the books precisely so that a reader four hundred years on would know which rungs were oak and which were oak drawn in the air. I had thought, when we made that mark, that it was a clever solution to the problem of being honest in a book. Sitting in that white room I understood that it was a clay copy of a living man, that I had spent my whole life being kept honest by a Greek who would not be impressed, and that I had tried to fold the Greek into a stroke of cipher because some animal part of me had known he was running out, and that a mark on a page cannot ask you a question, and cannot stop you, and cannot laugh in your face when you deserve it, which is the only correction that ever actually worked on me.

"You will have to do it yourself," he said. "Be your own skeptic. You will be bad at it. Everyone is bad at it. That is why we keep each other." He shut his eyes. "I am tired now. Come tomorrow. Bring the figure on the chest in your own hand and not Tyche's, I want to see whether your sevens have stopped looking like ones."

---

He died on the third morning, before I came up the hill, so I was not in the room, which I have decided to be glad of and have mostly succeeded.

Zoticus told me how it went, plainly, because Heras had trained him to tell a death plainly. He had drunk a little of the boiled water in the night and slept and his breathing had gone shallow and long-spaced toward dawn and then between one space and the next had simply not taken the next, and Zoticus, who had been sitting with him, had waited the way you wait, counting, and the count had run out. There had been no word. Heras had said his last clever thing the evening before, to Zoticus, who reported it to me with a straight face because the man had no idea it was funny. Heras had asked for the lamp moved closer and Zoticus had moved it and Heras had looked at the Hippocrates he could no longer read at any distance and said that it was a great waste, to spend a life learning to see and then have the eyes go before the man, and that if there were gods they had a low sense of humor and if there were none then it was only physics and physics had no sense of humor at all, which was worse, and that on the whole he preferred the gods, as company, even not believing in them, and would Zoticus please blow out the lamp, there was nothing in the book he had not read.

That was the last of it. No god invoked, except as a joke at a god's expense. I would not have had it otherwise and he knew I would not, which is why, I think, he made sure there was a witness to write it down for me.

---

Marcia handled the rites. There was a guild of physicians he had paid into for fifty years and they did it correctly, the Greek and the Roman both, and the pyre was on the appointed ground outside the gate and I stood where a long-standing friend stands, which is close but not closest, because the guild had the closest places, and that was correct too. I am not going to write what it was to watch it. There is a rule in this place that I learned from Heras as much as from anyone, which is that you do not say the loud true thing out loud, you do the rite and let the smoke carry it and keep the loud thing in a box under a board, and he would have had no patience at all for a barbarian narrating his own grief at a graveside like a hired mourner with a contract.

What I will write is the part that did not happen at the pyre and that I did not see coming.

It was three or four days after. I was at the wharf, at the bench, in the slack hour, and Eros had trimmed the lamps and the press was under its cloth, and I had a page of the upper book in front of me, the chemistry I had left half-dark, and I had written a sentence about the joining of the small parts of things that was more confident than I had any right to be, and I read it back, and I reached for the cup of boiled water at my elbow to wet my throat, and the cup was there, the wharf water, sand and charcoal and the long boil, and I drank it.

And there was a thing I always did, when I drank that water at that bench in the slack hour, which was that there was no one at the other end of the bench to say it to, but I had spent thirty years drinking that water within reach of a man who would, on a good evening, say something cutting about the Greekness of the discipline, and I had drunk it ten thousand times without thinking and would have said, without thinking, some old worn answer, the way you say a thing to a man you have sat beside for half your life. I drank the water and the answer came up in me, shaped and ready, the worn old retort about whose discipline it really was, and there was the whole length of the empty bench, and the answer had nowhere to go, and it sat in my mouth, and I did not say it, because you do not say a thing aloud in an empty press-room like a man who has lost his wits.

So I swallowed it with the water. And then I picked up the stylus, and I went back to the too-confident sentence about the joining of small things, and I read it again, and I made myself ask, out loud, in the empty room, in Greek, because Greek was the language we had argued in, whether I knew that or only felt it. My voice sounded wrong in the empty room, an old man talking to himself in a dead language. I felt it. I marked the sentence with the hooked stroke that meant the Thulean is guessing here, and I read the next sentence, and I asked the room again, and the room did not answer, and I marked that one too, and I went on down the page asking a question into a silence that used to answer back, and marking my own guesses, badly, the way everyone is bad at it, because that is why we keep each other, and there was no one to keep, and the water in the cup went cold while I worked, and I drank it cold rather than get up for more.

---

# Chapter 43: Family

There is a thing that happens in a house in winter, in the dark before the lamps are trimmed, when the meal is on and the rain is on the tiles and everyone is somewhere in it making noise. I have no Latin word for it and no English one either, which tells you something about how much I had thought about it before this world handed it to me at the age of fifty-three.

Our house was the upper floor of the wharf building Caepio had left Marcia, which she had refused to leave for anything grander on the grounds that the warehouse was below her feet and a woman should sleep over her money. So the smell came up through the boards, pepper and rope-tar and the dry resinous smell of the hides, and over it the kitchen, and in the cold months the brazier and the wet-wool reek of everyone's cloaks steaming on the pegs by the stair. It was loud. There was no hour of the day when it was not loud. After thirty years I had stopped hearing most of it the way you stop hearing a river, and then some evening it would come back to me whole, and I would stand on the stair with my hand on the rail unable to account for the fact that any of it was mine.

---

We ate together more than was strictly Roman. Marcia had decided this early, by which I mean she had decided it and informed me of it. A proper house dined apart, the master reclining, the women and children fed earlier, the slaves after; we did not have the room and Marcia did not have the patience, and so we ate at a long scrubbed table that had come up from the warehouse and still had a chandler's tally cut into one end, all of us on benches, like a crew.

That winter the table was Marcia at the head, because it was her table and her warehouse and there was never any question; me at the foot, where I could get up to the lamps; Lucanus on one side when he came, which was less and less; Ulpia on the other, with her wax tablet beside her plate, which Marcia permitted at the table and nowhere else, having lost that particular war and not the others. Tyche came up most evenings, being family in everything but the law. The girl Chloe brought the food and then ate at the corner once Marcia had told her twice to sit down, a Bithynian raised in a strict house being unpersuadable in fewer than two tellings that she was allowed to.

Marcia said the blessing, such as it was. There was a household Lar in a painted niche by the door, a cheerful crude little figure with a drinking-horn, and she gave it its due in about the time it takes to clear your throat, because the gods, in Marcia's theology, were a creditor you paid promptly and did not flatter. Then she said what was in front of us and what it had cost, by habit, because she could not look at a bowl of lentils without pricing the lentils.

"Lentils," she said, that evening. "And the last of the salt pork, which Chloe will not get into her head is the last of it however many times I say. And bread. The bread is dear this month and will be dearer, so eat it as though you respect it."

"To the Lar," I said, "and to the price of bread."

"Don't be clever at the table," she said. "You're bad at it in front of the children."

---

Lucanus came that night, which was why there was salt pork.

He was two-and-twenty and he came in out of the rain in a toga he had paid to have cleaned, which he wore now even on a wet night because a man who means to stand in a basilica gets used to the weight of it early. He had Marcia's square build and her unhurried way of filling a doorway, and my long hands, which were the only thing of mine he had taken that he could not give back. He was clean-shaven and close-cropped, and the emperor had grown a beard, and Lucanus had not, and I understood why and did not say so, because a freeborn citizen reading law in Rome does not need a Greek beard making him look like he reads anything but law.

He kissed his mother and put two fingers to the Lar without looking at it and sat, and the toga had to be arranged before he could eat, which took a while, and Ulpia watched him do it the way you watch someone wind something up.

"You've folded it wrong," she said.

"I have not folded it wrong."

"The fall's wrong. You look like a man carrying a sheep."

"Ulpia," said Marcia.

He was reading with an advocate named Caesius now, with a practice in the Basilica Julia, taking notes and learning the forms, which magistrate hated to be kept waiting and which loved it. He had a memory for the human weather of a courtroom that I would have given a great deal for at his age, who could read whether a city was inland or coastal but not whether a soft-jawed magistrate had eaten. He told us about a property suit, two brothers and a wall and a drain, and made his mother laugh twice, which was the household's true coin, and I sat at the foot of the table and ate the salt pork and was as happy as a man gets, which is the trouble.

Because there was a moment, there was always a moment, when the law-talk turned to the thing under it. Caesius was freeborn of freeborn, three generations clean. Lucanus was freeborn too, full citizen, born in lawful marriage, which had cost me a great deal to give him. But his father was a freedman, which was on no document anyone would show him and on every document about him forever. There is a height above which a freedman's son does not climb, and it is not a high height, and Lucanus had found it already, with the soft hands of a man only just begun to feel for the ceiling of the room he is in.

"Caesius says I could stand at the lower bar in three years," he said. "Small suits. Freedmen's business, mostly. Estates with no one to fight over them." He said it lightly. He had learned lightness from his mother and irony from me and used both as armor, and I knew the armor because I had forged it. "Caesius says a man with a head for figures and a clean Latin can make a good living and never be asked his grandfather's name, so long as he never wants to be asked it."

"That's a living," said Marcia.

"It's a good living."

"It is," she said, and meant it, because to Marcia a thing that fed you and could not be taken from you was very near the top of the world's goods, and she was almost never wrong, and I sat there wanting to put my son's hand on something higher and having nothing in this world to put it on that would not burn him.

What I had to give him was downstairs. The press, the figures, the contest, the long cold lunatic project of the books, a workshop full of people who would have died for the strangeness of it. And he did not want it. He had told me so at sixteen, gently, and meant it more at two-and-twenty, and he was right not to want it. The work had my name on it where my name was a stain. He wanted to walk into a building and have no one able to say what he was. I had spent thirty-six years being a thing no one could name, and I was not going to be the one to tell him that the clean room he wanted was also a small one.

So I asked him about the drain the two brothers were fighting over, and made him explain the easement law to me twice because the second time he stopped performing and started thinking, and his face when he was thinking was Marcia's, and I had that.

---

After, when Lucanus had gone back out into the rain to his rented room nearer the courts, because a man on the respectability track did not sleep over a warehouse, the table thinned to the women and the work.

This was the other half of my evenings and the better-kept secret of them. Ulpia would clear her plate to the side, not away, never away when there was a tablet involved, and pull the lamp close, and we would do the work I was no longer fit to do alone. I could not read my own small figures by lamplight anymore; I needed the light close and the page far and could not have both. Ulpia read them to me, and had for two years, since she was small enough to kneel up on the bench to see over the page, fast and flat, catching my errors before I made them, which only two people in the world had ever done and the other was Tyche.

That night it was the African coast, the survey that came up the river twice a year, leagues and soundings and the lie of the headlands, set into the new figures to one scale by, in the actual fact of the lamplit evening, a sixteen-year-old girl reading numbers to a half-blind man.

"You've the league wrong here," she said. "Or he has. Six hundred where the run before it is two hundred and the coast doesn't bend. Dropped a mark, six for two, the same stroke twice." She had it before I did. She always had it before I did now. "Correct it or note it?"

"Note it. Never correct another man's count, you query it. You don't know what he saw."

"He saw two and wrote six," she said, "but yes." And she noted it, in the small careful hand that was already better than mine had ever been, and somewhere a season's sail down a coast no Roman would walk for four hundred years a headland got its true distance back because a girl in Rome would not let a stroke lie.

She was very good. I want to be plain about how good, because it is the whole of the thing. Better than Lucanus, better than the second clerk, better than I had been at her age and getting better at a rate that frightened me on her behalf. She had the cipher two years; she read the books; she could take Newton's second law in my failing English and tell me where I had hedged it and where I had cheated, more Tyche's pupil than mine in that.

And there was nowhere for any of it to go. A freedman's daughter could not keep a counting-house under her own name, could not plead, could not hold a magistracy that did not exist for women in the first place, could not do one public thing with the best mind I had met in this world since Apollodorus went into the ground. She could do it all under a borrowed name, her mother's, a husband's, the way Marcia did, the way the whole enterprise already did. She had decided this was enough, at fourteen, with a coldness that was entirely her mother's. Marcia had warned me to teach her no further and Marcia had been right and I had gone on anyway, and there we were at the lamp, the two of us, having both gotten what we wanted and not the thing under it.

---

The marriage talk had started.

It had started the polite way, which is to say Marcia had begun to mention names, the way she costed lentils, who had a son and what the son would come to and what a girl of ours could be made to be worth to such a house. Ulpia was sixteen. A girl of sixteen unbespoken was a girl whose father was dead, broke, or strange, and I was the third, and the strangeness had bought her a few extra years that were running out.

"The Ennii have a son," Marcia said, that same night, after Ulpia had gone to bed and Tyche had gone down to her own room and it was the two of us and the dying brazier. "Grain factors. Decent house. The boy's dull but he's kind, which lasts longer."

"She'd eat him alive."

"She'd eat most of them alive. That's not a reason. That's an argument for finding her a big enough plate." Marcia worked a knot out of her shoulder with her thumb. "I married a dull man the first time and buried him and he left me a warehouse. I married a strange one the second time and he gives me headaches and a son who won't take the business and a daughter too good for the world she's stuck in, and I'd call the strange one the better bargain, but I had the warehouse first. She has no warehouse, Daniel. She has a head no one will pay for and a name that's half yours."

"She has the cipher. She has the books."

"She has a secret she can never use in the daylight, and a husband she hasn't met yet who will own her in the law whatever we write in private, and you and I will be dead, and Lucanus off being clean, and there will be a man who decides whether she keeps a tablet at her own table." She let that sit. She was the least cruel person I knew and the most exact, and the two together could take the wind out of you. "I'm not saying give her up. I'm saying I'm shopping for the man who'll let her be what she is, and that man costs more than the dull kind one, and we should know it going in."

I said I didn't know how to be told this.

"You're not being told it well. Heras was the one who could say a hard thing soft, and he's gone, so you and I are stuck saying them hard." She got up and banked the brazier and stood with her back to me a moment, and then said, not turned around, "Find her a man who can stand a clever wife. I'll do the rest. I always do the rest." Which was as close as she came that year to saying it grieved her, and was more than I could have managed, and I had a country full of people who said such things plainly and could tell none of them to her.

---

The roots came up, when they came up, sideways, the way Heras used to say the truth comes.

It was a few nights on, a hard cold after the rain, the brazier being mean with its heat, and I was doing the dull patient work of the cipher with Ulpia, copying a clean rung of the lower ladder, the four workings, into the fair copy that would go into the lead and the pitch. It is slow work and the hand wanders and the mind wanders with it, and I was humming.

I do that. I only know I am doing it when someone tells me, because in the world I came from a man could hum and the air around him was full of the same songs and no one heard him. I was humming a thing I had hummed for thirty-six years, that I no longer had whole, that came up in pieces with the shape of a tune and the wrong words in the gaps, and I patched the gaps with sounds, the way everyone does with a half-remembered song, except that everyone else can go and find the song.

Ulpia stopped writing.

"What is that?"

"What's what."

"That. You were making it. With the breath." She set the stylus down. She had her mother's flat patience and my refusal to leave a thing alone, which is a bad combination to be questioned by. "You do it when you copy. The same one. It goes up and then it falls and there's a part in the middle you never reach, you go around it. What is it?"

And I opened my mouth to lie, because I always lied, the road-lie, the far north, the songs they sing in the long dark of Thule, worn smooth from use. And I did not tell it, I am not sure why, except that it was cold and the girl had just spent two hours setting down knowledge I would die owning the only true source of, and I was tired in the particular way that makes a man honest at the wrong moment.

"It's a song from where I'm from," I said. "I don't remember it."

"You're singing it."

"I'm singing what's left. There were words. I had them, going on forty years with no one to sing them to and no way to fetch them back, and they've worn off the way a coin wears, you can see there was a face on it." I hummed the up-and-the-fall, and went around the hole in the middle the way I always went around it, and stopped. "There's a word in it. I know there's a word right there and I've lost it. I'd know it if I heard it. I'll never hear it."

She watched me. She did not believe in Thule, none of them did, too well-mannered to say so and too sharp not to know, and I watched her decide not to ask the question she was too smart not to have, which was: a song from *where*. Where there are no caravans and no ships and no other man who knows the tune.

"Teach me the part you have," she said instead.

So I did. I hummed it and she hummed it back, flat and exact, better than I had it, and we went up and we fell and we went around the hole in the middle, the two of us, in a cold room over a warehouse, and there was a word missing in the heart of it that had belonged to a country she would never see and that I could no longer give her, and she hummed the gap faithfully around the nothing, and I had to get up and see to the lamp because my eyes were doing a thing the eyes of a man of fifty-three are permitted to do over a poor lamp.

She never asked again. She filed it where she had filed the cipher's too-tidy word for sleet the year before, and made of it whatever a brilliant girl makes of a father with a song he cannot finish and a word for weather Rome does not have. She did not press. She had decided, somewhere I could not see, that there was a locked room in me and that she would not pick the lock, which was the gentlest thing anyone could have done to me and the one I could thank no one for.

---

Macer came to dinner at the turn of the year, which was a production. He was eighty-six, and being eighty-six was the only thing left that genuinely interested him; he had to be carried up the stair in a chair by two of his men, complaining the whole way that the warehouse stank, which it did. He called me Thulean still, as he had for thirty-five years and boy before that, and liked Ulpia in the way a hard old man likes a hard young thing, and told her, with the cruelty old men are forgiven, that it was a pity she was a girl because she'd have made him a quartermaster.

"It's a pity for your quartermaster," Ulpia said. "Not for me."

Macer laughed until he had to be thumped on the back. "Where did you get this one, Thulean. Not from you. You never said a sharp thing in your life you didn't apologize for after."

"Her mother," I said, and he agreed, and turned the gold ring on his finger with his thumb, the gesture I had watched him make for thirty-five years over deals and debts and the corpses of his rivals, and made now over a plate of lentils he could barely eat, in a warehouse he despised, having outlived almost everyone who knew what either of us had been. He fell asleep before the bread was done. He woke once, found me across the loud bright room, said, "You did all right, boy," in a voice I had not heard him use, and went back to sleep, and in the morning denied it.

---

There is the thing in the dark before the lamps, that I started with and could not name.

I will not name it now either. But I will say what it was made of, on a cold clear night when the rain had quit and the warehouse breathed its pepper and tar up through the floor. A tablet face-down where Ulpia had left it with a queried mark she would not let lie, and Lucanus's wet footprints by the door already dried to nothing, and Marcia somewhere below counting the day's loads against tomorrow's, and a song in my chest with a hole in the middle of it that the whole house could now hum and none of them could fill.

I went around the house at the end the way I went around the song. The lamps. The brazier banked. The door barred, the Lar with its little horn catching the last of the light. Up the stair to the room where Marcia was already asleep over a tally she'd dropped on the floor, and I picked it up because the figures would smear if it lay open, and I could not read the figures and it did not matter, I knew the hand, and I set it on the chest where she'd find it. Then I lay down in the warm dark of a house that was mine in a country that was not, and listened to the rain start again on the tiles of a roof two thousand years before I was born, over the people I loved and could not tell where I had come from, and I did not finish the song, because there was no finishing it, and slept.

---

# Chapter 44: The World Bends

The new figures came to the Aurelian milestone the spring I turned four-and-fifty, and I learned it from a carter who could not read them.

He had stopped his mule at the wharf gate to ask Pamphilus the way to a warehouse, and while they argued about which lane it was he jerked his thumb back up the road and said the magistrates had cut a new stone at the second mile, and a man could tell his distance off it now in shopkeeper's marks, which he supposed was progress if you were a shopkeeper. He said it the way you say a thing that has nothing to do with you. Pamphilus sent him down the right lane and came in and told me, because Pamphilus knew, alone of nearly everyone, where the shopkeeper's marks had come from.

I walked out to see it. It is a thing I would not have done at forty, walked an hour up a hard road to look at a rock, but my eyes were going and my legs were still good and I had reached the age where I would rather use the part of me that worked.

The stone was new-faced, the chisel-marks still pale, an aedile's name carved across the top in proper Roman letters, the whole apparatus of who had paid for the road's repair and under which consuls. And below it, where the older milestones gave the distance to the city in numerals the size of a man's hand, IIII and the rest, this one gave it in mine. A two and a circle. The circle was the trouble. Zero is the mark no Roman wants, the mark for the nothing that is somehow a something, the one I had set down on a wax tablet thirty-seven years before in a cell to a Greek physician who thought I was a lunatic, and here it was cut a thumb deep in stone on the public road, official, paid for, a magistrate's reckoning of how far a man stood from Rome.

I put my hand on it. The cut was sharp under my fingers, the grit of fresh stone, and I stood there long enough that a woman driving a goat went around me wide, the way you go around a man talking to a wall.

I had won that fight cheap and lost it complete. Cheap, because the figures cost me nothing to give and spread themselves on speed alone, fish-sellers' slates and grain-factors' day-books, because a man cheating you with Roman numerals can hide a mistake in a column and a man cheating you in mine cannot. Complete, because somewhere in the years between the slate and the stone they had stopped being the Thulean's marks and become simply the marks, the way you write a number, and no one cutting that milestone had thought of me any more than the carter had, and that was the whole of what I had wanted and it sat in my chest like a swallowed stone of its own. A man wants to be the one who taught the world to count and also wants the world to count without thinking of him, and he cannot have both, and I had the second, which is the one that lasts.

I walked home. The figure stayed in my hand the way the cold of a coin stays.

---

Theophanes took me to the portico the next month, which he did from kindness and a Greek's pride in a collection, not knowing he was showing me my own reflection in a bad mirror.

It stood off the Argiletum behind the booksellers, a colonnade a rich freedman of the imperial house had endowed with his name on the architrave, two long rooms of shelving open to any man who could read and behave. I had heard of it. I had not gone, because I had a horror of going, the way you do not visit a grown child you are afraid you have ruined. Theophanes wanted my eye on a question of cataloguing in the figures, and I went for that, and forgot it the moment I came through the door.

The smell hit first. Not the smell of a library, which is dust and cedar oil and old papyrus going to powder. This was ink, the flat metallic ink we mixed with lampblack and gum at the press, the smell of the wet sheet coming off the block, and under it the rawer smell of new-cut pine boards and the size in fresh-bound covers, the smell of a thing being made faster than it can age.

The shelves on the left were the old library, scrolls in their pigeonholes, a few hundred of them, the accumulated copying of generations of slaves' wrists, each one different, each one a man's eyes and a man's errors. The shelves on the right were mine.

I do not mean they were mine. I mean they had come off the press, and there were more of them than there were scrolls on the other wall, and they were the same. That was the thing the eye could not stop on. A scroll is a single creature; you cannot mistake one for its neighbor. These were ranks of the identical, the times-tables Eros had cut twenty years ago, the rules of the new figures, the geometry primers, cheap Homer and cheaper Aesop, the printed prize-tales from the contest sold for an as and bound up by some industrious soul into a fat ugly codex of pure invention, sixty copies of a thing where there had once been one. Bound flat, in the codex form, because you cannot shelve a stack of printed sheets like a scroll and the form had had to change to hold the flood. The columns of them, the same spine and the same spine and the same spine, made my eyes do a thing I did not like, a kind of vertigo, the multiplication of a single object until it stops being an object.

And there were boys reading them. Not the sons of senators with a Greek tutor at the elbow. A wool-dyer's apprentice, by the stains to the wrist, mouthing the geometry primer with his finger under the line. A girl, which stopped me cold, a slave by her dress and free to be there reading because no one minded a girl reading a printed sheet the way they would have minded her in a scriptorium handling the master's Cicero. The cost had come down until literacy was a thing a poor man could spend an idle hour on, and the printed sheet, being worth almost nothing, had escaped the lock and key that guards the things worth something.

Theophanes was talking about the catalogue. I told him to set the printed works on the figures and the scrolls on the Greek alphabet and the two would have to learn to live on the same shelf, and he laughed and said that was very like me, and I let him think it was a joke about cataloguing.

I had wanted books cheap so that knowledge would stop being a thing you had to be rich to climb toward. I got it. The wool-dyer's boy had it under his finger. I stood in the new room with the new smell and could not make the gladness sit still, because every one of those sixty identical spines was a thing that did exactly what it was set to do, said exactly the same words to every hand that opened it, and a press does not know whether the words are a times-table or a libel, and the same flood that put geometry in a poor boy's idle hour would put whatever a powerful man paid to have set, sixty times, the same, into sixty hands at once, and no scribe's tired wrist to slow it or get a word wrong and break the spell. I had handed Rome a thing that says one thing to everyone, faithfully, and would never once ask what the thing was.

---

I would not have gone to the western yard at all if Hadrian had not sent for the survey in person, and the survey lived at the yard, and so I went down to the coast in the late summer in a wagon that hurt my back, an old man fetched to read his own map to younger men who could read it better.

It was south of the Garonne mouth, a working yard the program had bought into, sheds and slipways and a stink of pitch and oak-sap and the green reek of new-sawn timber, and pilots' talk going in three languages over the noise of the mauls. The survey itself was nothing to look at, leaves of the African coast in Ulpia's clean copying and the men's rougher field hands, leagues and soundings to the one scale in the figures, a coast crawling south a headland a season the way a snail crosses a road. I read what I was wanted to read, and answered what I could, and felt the old fraud's sweat that I knew less than the men I was instructing.

Then the yard-master, a leather-faced Gaul named Bituitus who treated me as a clerk's curiosity and the program as a slow miracle, took me out to the long slip to see what they were building, because he was proud of it and wanted a Roman to be proud with him.

She was up on the ways, half-planked, ribs to the sky like the inside of a whale, and she was wrong. Wrong is what I mean. She was nothing the Romans built. The grain ships that crossed to Alexandria are broad shallow baskets that hug a coast and run before a known wind and would open like a flower in a real sea; everyone at that yard knew it, had buried men who learned it. This thing was deep and narrow and high in the bow, double-planked, the timbers heavier than reason, built to be hit by water from any direction and shrug and stay shut, built by men who had lost two hulls already to the open gray and were building the third to lose better. They had a name for the seam-method that I will not pretend I followed. They had drowned a man getting here. The thing on the ways was an honest answer to a question Rome had never thought to ask before I asked it for them in a senator's house with a lying map.

I stood at her keel and looked up through her ribs at the sky and could not speak for a while, and Bituitus took it for the right kind of silence and let me have it.

She could not cross. I knew that, standing there. She might float in a sea that would sink the grain fleet, she might claw off a lee shore that would have ended a Roman captain, and she could not point her bow at the western blank on my map and arrive, because no man aboard her could tell how far west he had come. Latitude they had, off the sun and the pole, the easy half. The hard half, the westing, wanted a clock that would keep true time through a month of pitching and salt and cold, and the prize for that clock had stood unwon since the year I named it and would go on standing, because I had failed to build a clock that kept true time across a still tally-room, and I knew no man living who could. The hull was real. The dream the hull was built toward was a coast I had drawn from a dying phone's memory and a course no instrument on earth could yet hold.

I had told Hadrian the west was uninhabited and a year's sail off, and neither of those was a thing I knew, and one of them was very likely a lie, and these men were spending their lives and now and then a life learning to build the ship that would carry that lie out past the point where a man could turn back. Bituitus put his hand on a rib the way I had put mine on the milestone, and said, in his coast-Latin, that the third one would swim, he could feel it in her, and I said I believed him, and meant it, and that was the worse half of the day.

---

The boy I met on the road back, near the second mile, by chance, by the milestone with the circle cut in it.

He was perhaps nine, a carter's get by the look of him, left to mind a parked wagon and a dozing mule while his father did business off the road, and he was doing what a boy does with an empty hour, which is making a world. He had a stick. He was drawing in the dust of the road's edge, and talking, half to the mule and half to no one, and I stopped my wagon because my driver wanted to water the beasts at the trough by the stone and because I had heard the boy say a word I knew.

He was building a ship in the dust. A ship with no oars and no sail, he was explaining to the mule, which did not care, a ship that ate fire in its belly and breathed smoke out a stack and walked on the water faster than the wind whether the wind blew or not, and it would go past the edge of the sea to the country no one had found yet, and he, the boy, would be the captain, and he would be rich, and he had seen it in a tale his uncle had bought printed for two coins and read to him until he had it by heart.

He had the steam-ship. He had Felix's steam-ship, near enough, the contest-tale of a self-fueling pot that drives a hull, the one that won the year a counting-house slave stood up and named the metal that does not yet exist, printed cheap and sold for an as and read aloud by a carter's brother to a boy of nine who now believed, with the whole of his nine-year-old belief, that such a ship was a thing the world contained and that he might one day stand on its deck.

It does not exist. I want to be exact about that, the way I was exact with the milestone and the hull. There is no pot in the world that drives a ship; the trick of it, the bridge from the fire to the turning, had been in my head once at seventeen and had worn out of it years ago and not come back, and the steel to hold the fire if I could remember the trick is the steel Hermes has failed at for thirty years and will die failing at, and Felix after him. The boy in the dust had the dream a clean generation ahead of any hope of the thing. He had it whole, with the confidence of a child, the way I once had a country whole, and he drew its stack in the dust and made the smoke-noise with his mouth and steered it past the trough where my mules were drinking, out over the edge of the only world he knew, toward a coast that I had put on a map.

I gave him a coin, which frightened him, an old stranger leaning down off a wagon, and his father came around the wagon-tail at the wrong moment and started apologizing for the boy bothering a gentleman, and I said the boy had not bothered me, and we went on, and behind us I heard the steam-noise start up again the moment the wagon was a safe distance off.

---

The thing I carried back up the road was not one thing and would not resolve into one.

Felix was a quarter-mile from the cattle market that month, on my foundation's coin, failing at the pot the boy had just sailed in the dust, and I had paid for both ends of it, the failure and the dream, the man who could not build the thing and the printed tale that taught a nation of children to want it. Hermes was failing at the steel that the pot needs and the hull needs and the cannon needed and I had not built and never would. The cannon I had built, the one that burst on purpose to argue for the steel, had taught Rome a thing too, taught it that a tube of metal and the right gray powder will throw a stone across a river and that the powder is dung and ash and the yellow rock from the volcano country, and that knowledge was loose now, off in the world doing whatever loose knowledge does, sixty copies the same, faithful to whoever held the page.

I had set out to teach a slave-holding empire to count, and read, and dream, and build ships that go where its masters had never let it imagine going, and I had done some fraction of all of it, and the milestone had my figures and the portico had my books and the western slip had my hull and the boy in the dust had my lie, and not one of those things knew my name or cared what I had wanted it for. They served. That was the property I had not thought hard enough about when I started giving things away in a cell to stay alive. A figure serves the man who reckons with it, an honest grain-factor or a tax-farmer skinning a province. A book serves the page it carries, true or false. A faster ship goes wherever its owner points the bow. A powder throws the stone the gunner aims. I had spent thirty-seven years making Rome stronger at the things Rome chose to do, and I had never once been asked, and would never be asked, what Rome chose to do with strength.

The wagon brought me back through the gate at dusk, into the wharf-stink and the noise of my own house, and I did not go up. I went into the press-room, where the day-shift was breaking up and Vitalis was setting the bench to rights, and stood among the racked blocks and the smell of the ink that was now the smell of a public portico off the Argiletum, and I watched the boy rack the day's cut blocks and damp the inking-pads and bank the stove, the ordinary closing of an ordinary day's work, the machine I had built to outlive me being put to bed by a hand that was not mine and would do it the same tomorrow whether I lived or not. Vitalis asked if I needed light to see by, since my eyes, and I said no, I knew the room, and he wished me good night in the easy way of a young man to an old one, and went, and left me in it.

---

# Chapter 45: What It Serves

The accounts came to me out of Judaea the way everything came to me now, late and on someone else's errand. Eudemus sent them down to the wharf with a clerk and a covering note that asked me to cast a column the fiscus had got into a tangle, because the war had thrown the eastern books out of true and the man who balanced them had died of a flux, and the figures were mine and so was the only living hand that could be trusted to find where a province had stopped adding up.

I had not asked to see them. I want that set down plainly, the way I would have wanted it of any man I was reckoning. I did not go looking. The leaves came to me because I had taught Rome to keep its sums in marks a tired clerk could not fudge, and a war is the largest sum an empire ever does, and so the war came to my bench in my own figures, fair-copied in Ulpia's clean hand off the field rolls, the two and the circle and the rest, the marks they had cut into the milestone, adding up the thing.

I read them at arm's length by the window because my eyes would no longer take small marks by lamp. Ulpia read the smaller hands to me where the field clerks had crowded them. We sat at it three mornings.

The first leaves were grain. Modii by the hundred thousand, drawn from Egypt and Syria and shipped to the staging ports and carted inland to feed the legions that had come up into the province, three of them and the vexillations of more, a number of mouths I knew the daily ration of because I had once stood in a marching camp and watched a quartermaster fail to feed them and learned the arithmetic of it under Celer's eye. So I could do the work in my head before Ulpia read me the total. So many men eat so much bread a day, and the bread is so much grain, and the grain is so many ships, and the ships are so many days, and the days were now into the fourth year. The column balanced. It was a good column. Whoever had drawn it had used the carry-mark correctly and had not once hidden a shortfall in a Roman tail where it could rot unseen, which was the whole of what I had built the figures to prevent, and they had prevented it, and the thing they had made legible was the feeding of the army that was burning Judaea down to its roots.

There is a kind of reading where the eye runs ahead of the man. I had read three more leaves before I understood what the fourth column was.

It was headed in the field clerk's abbreviation, which Ulpia read out flat because she did not know the country and the word was only a word to her. Captives. The fiscus took its tithe of the captives the way it took its tithe of everything, and the captives were a revenue, and a revenue is reckoned, and so they had been counted and valued and the value entered, and because the price of a thing falls when the market floods with it, there was a second column beside the first that tracked the price, and the price was falling. I sat and watched the price of a human being fall down a column of my own marks across the seasons of the war, the way you watch the price of grain fall after a good harvest, because there were so many of them. The clerk had noted, in the margin, in a tradesman's shorthand, that the markets at the staging ports could not absorb the supply and that lots were being driven north and west to find a price, and that even so the price was a fraction of the standing rate, and he had given the fraction in my figures, exact, because my figures are exact, that is what they are for.

Ulpia stopped reading. She had got to the bottom of the column and there was nothing under it but the rule and the total, and she did not read the total. She is a grave girl. She put her finger on the rule under the last figure and waited, and I told her we would do the grain again, the grain leaves, I wanted to be sure of the grain. So we did the grain again, which balanced, which had always balanced, and I did not have her read the other column a second time, and that is a thing I let myself do and will not dress up as anything better than what it was.

---

The reports came in pieces over that winter and the next, the way the front always reached Rome, secondhand, thirdhand, worn smooth by the men who carried them.

A discharged man at the wharf, an auxiliary mustered out and working a grain-hull for his passage home, told Pamphilus a thing about the country that Pamphilus brought to me because Pamphilus knew the eastern books had crossed my bench. The man said the legions were not fighting battles any more, that there were no battles to fight, that the rebels would not stand. He said they had gone into the ground, into the dry hills, into caves and old cisterns and the diggings under the towns, whole families of them with their grain and their water and their goats, and that the army had stopped trying to dig them out, which cost men, and had instead sat down at the cave-mouths and waited. He said it took a long time. He did not say it as a horror. He said it as a tedium, a thing that had bored him, sitting a season at the mouth of a hole in the rock on short rations himself while the people inside it were brought to the end of theirs. He had the contempt of a man who has been frightened and lived, for the people who frightened him and did not. Pamphilus stood in my tally-room and gave it to me in the man's own words, and then said, because Pamphilus is a kind man and had watched my face, that it was a long way off and none of my doing, and went back to the cloth.

A dispatch reached the city in the spring, read out and chalked up and gone over in the porticoes, that the war was as good as won and that the emperor would refound the rebel capital. Aelia Capitolina, after his own name and the god of the Capitol, a colony on the bones of the old city, the plough run round its boundary the way you found any colony, a temple where their temple had been, and a standing prohibition that no Jew set foot in it on pain of death. The men in the portico thought it a fine settlement of a tiresome matter. A city was a city; a colony was prestige and land for veterans; the emperor was a great founder of cities, it was the thing he loved, he had founded them all across the world and put up his wall in the north and his temples in Greece, and here was another, and the only difference any of them remarked on was the strangeness of a people who would rather die in a hole than live under a new and well-drained street plan.

I knew the street plan they meant. I had drawn that grid for other colonies, the cardo and the decumanus square to the compass, the falls worked out so the drains ran, the aqueduct sited off a survey done to the one scale in my figures. I did not draw this one. But I had drawn the kind of it, and taught the men who drew it, and a colony laid out clean over the place where a city had been razed and its people driven off or dead is a thing my survey serves as well as it serves anything, because a survey does not know what was on the ground before the chain was run across it.

---

I tried to take it to Heras and he was a year and more in the ground.

I do not mean I forgot. I mean the want of him came up in me the way a tongue goes to a pulled tooth, a reaching to the place where the thing had been. He would have sat on the bench under the window with his bad chest and his arms folded and let me talk myself out, and then he would have found the soft place in what I said and put his thumb on it. He was good at that. He was the only one who had ever been good at that with me, because Marcia weighs a thing for what it will do and Tyche is on my side past the point of use to me and the rest of them think I am the Thulean and cannot help it.

So I did it without him, which is to say badly. I argued it on the cold bench in the empty room after the day shift had gone. I said, to no one, in the Greek I used to use with him because it kept my own voice strange enough to hear, that a man is not answerable for the use another man makes of a true thing, that the figures are only counting and counting is good, that a clean column is a defense against the tax-farmer as much as a tool of him, that I had fought slavery with the only weapons I had and a slave market crashing under its own weight is the thing I had wanted, was it not, the thing made too cheap to be worth keeping, except that this was not the market dying of a rail cart that did the work of twenty men, this was the market drowned in the bodies of a destroyed people, and the figure was the same figure either way, falling, and the figure did not know the difference and neither, it turned out, did the world.

I asked the empty bench whether a man who carries water into a camp so the men do not die of the flux is answerable for what the men he kept alive then do with their hands. Heras would have made me say what I meant by answerable. He would have asked me whether I was looking for the truth of it or for permission, and I would not have been able to lie to his face the way I can lie to a bench, and that was the use of him, and it was gone.

I had taught four children to read my secret script so that the knowing would outlive me. I had not taught one of them to argue with me, because there had been no time, and because in truth I had not wanted it, a man wants a thing kept and does not want it questioned, and now I had no one to question it and the questioning was the part I could not do without.

---

The powder I could not settle, and the not settling it was the worst of the work.

Here is the honest shape of what I knew and did not know. The powder is loose. It has been loose since the day Hermes cast a bronze tube thick enough to burst and we burst it on purpose downriver before the Guard's officers, to argue that the force wanted better metal than any we could make. Rome learned that day that dung and ash and the yellow rock from the volcano country, ground fine and mixed and corned, will throw a stone across a river. The steel to make a tube that does not burst has never come, not from Hermes in thirty years, not from Felix after him, and it will not come in my life; there is no gun in the world, no cannon a gunner can trust, the thing I am most often credited with and have most completely failed to make. So no one threw a stone at a wall in Judaea out of a tube of mine. Of that much I am sure, and it is a small sure thing.

But a wall does not have to be thrown down from a distance. It can be undermined. A gallery is dug under it and the props are fired and the wall comes down into the hole, and men have done that with timber and fire since before Rome was a village. And a charge of the gray powder set in a bored hole will crack rock and bring down an overhang and seal or open a way into the ground far faster than a man with a pick, which is a thing any quarryman would see in an afternoon, and Maximus saw it at his quarry years ago when I let a little of it loose to split a block, and quarrymen talk, and the powder is loose. I do not know that anyone carried a keg of it into the dry hills of Judaea to bring down the roof of a cave or to open one that men did not want to enter with their bodies. I do not know that they did not. The accounts that crossed my bench were grain and captives and pay; no clerk enters a thing like that in a revenue column. I asked no one, because the only way to learn it was to ask in a way that named me to it, and I found that I would rather not know than be told, and I despise that in a man and found it in myself.

So it sits, and will sit. Either my powder helped close a hole in the rock over a family that had gone into it to live, or it did not, and I will go to my grave not knowing which, and the not knowing is its own kind of weight, heavier some nights than a thing I could be sure of would be. A thing you are sure of has edges. You can set your hand on it. This had no edges. It was everywhere the powder might be and nowhere I could point.

---

They call me the Thulean still, in the markets and the porticoes, the man who flew, the man who taught the figures, the wonder-worker from the far cold north. It is a good name to have. It opens doors and it has more than once kept me out of a cell, and I built it on purpose, day by day, out of a balloon and a map and a string of demonstrations, because a man with no city and no people in this world needs a name that does the work a city does.

I had thought of the name as mine, a thing I wore. I sat on the cold bench that winter and understood it the other way. A man useful to power is a tool in power's hand, and a tool is used for the work the hand is doing, and the hand in Judaea was doing what it was doing, and my figures were in its grip, and my drains were under its camps keeping it healthy enough to do it, and my survey was square to the compass over the razed ground, and somewhere out past the edge of what I would let myself ask the gray powder might be cracking the rock. The Thulean is not a man who hands the world gifts and stands clear. The Thulean is the name a slaughtering empire gives the foreigner who makes the slaughter run cleaner, and I had spent thirty-seven years polishing that name and presenting it at every door, and the doors had opened, and this was the inside of the house the doors led to.

I had wanted to be useful and forgotten. I had got the useful. The forgotten I had wanted for vanity's sake, the small clean immortality of a mark with no name on it, and now I wanted it for cover, and could not have it, because a man does not get to lay down a name once it has started doing work without him, any more than he gets to call back a figure once it is cut in the public stone.

I could not tell, sitting there, and cannot tell now, writing it, whether I had been a man doing the only duty a man with my knowledge could do, which is to lift the bottom rungs and trust the climbing to better hands than mine, or a boy who decided in a cell to stay alive that he knew better than the whole turning world what the world should have, and gave it, and it served. Both arguments are good arguments. I have made them both to the empty bench in the dark and neither has ever once won, and I no longer have the man who could have told me whether either was even the right question.

---

I went up to the house late, past the hour the gate is barred, and Marcia was awake.

She was at the storeroom table with a single lamp drawn close and the day's tallies under it, working them because her eyes were still good enough to and mine were not, and she did not ask where I had been, because she knew the eastern leaves had been on my bench for three mornings and she is not a woman who asks a question she already has the answer to. She moved the lamp so it would not be in my face when I sat down. That was all. She finished the column she was on, and ruled it off, and set the stylus down, and we sat a while in the smell of pepper and rope and warm oil that her dead husband's warehouse had never lost.

After a time she said the price of eastern grain would come down hard once the province was settled and the levies eased, and that we should not be long on grain contracts this season, and I said no, we should not, and we talked about grain, the price of it, the ships, the season, two people who reckon for their living talking the thing they both knew how to talk while the lamp burned down, and neither of us said the other column, the one beside the grain, the one that had been falling, and I was grateful to her for the grain the way a drowning man is grateful for a plank, and she knew that too, and let me hold it, and we sat with the figures that stayed where they were put and meant only themselves, until the oil was low and she banked the lamp and we went in.

---

# Chapter 46: Hadrian Dies

The news came to me at my own bench, in my own city, on a day I had spent on a dispute about the price of pearwood.

That is the whole of the difference, and I knew it was the whole of the difference while it was happening, which is not a thing I could have said the other time. The other time I had been a sea away on a road that wanted me dead, and a baggage clerk had asked me a question he could not make sit flat, and the bottom had gone out of me before the water boiled. This time a carter put his head in at the wharf gate in the middle of the afternoon, a man I bought river-mud bricks from twice a year, and said the emperor was dead at last, said it the way he might have told me a bridge was down on the Tibur road, a thing that would make my morning longer and was no concern of his beyond that. At last. The two words carried the whole shape of it. Everyone had known for a year that the man was dying. The only news in it was that the dying had finished.

I thanked him and went back to the pearwood.

I am not going to pretend the afternoon held. It did not hold, and I will get to where it stopped holding. But the first hour of it held, and the first hour is where the man I had become was, and I want it on the page before the rest, because the rest is older and easier to write and a reader will trust the rest too much. I went on with the pearwood because a block-cutter named Vitalis had ruined a face of Greek setting it wet against the grain, and the society that owned the press would dock the cost of the board against the quarter's rents whether an emperor lived or died, and the figure of it did not change. So I stood in the press-room with the boy and we worked out what the board had cost and what his next month would cost him for the waste of it, and the marks I drew were the same marks I would have drawn yesterday, and the press behind us went on biting its slow sheets the way it bit them whether I was in the room or not, because I had built it years ago to do exactly that, to not need me, and on the day it mattered it did not need me.

That was the point of all of it. I had only never watched the point come true on the day it was for.

---

The dying had been a long public business, the kind the city follows the way it follows a chariot team, knowing the horses by name and laying money on the outcome. The man had swollen up with the water-sickness, the dropsy, the body filling with the fluid the body cannot pass, the legs going and then the belly and then, they said, the lungs, so that toward the end he could not lie flat for drowning in himself by inches in his own bed. He had gone down to Baiae for the warm shore and the soft air and the baths the rich go to die at, and he had not got better, because there is no getting better from that, and a man as clever as he was had known there was no getting better, and the part the city told and retold, with the relish it keeps for the great brought low, was that he had wanted to die and been kept from it.

He had asked for the knife and been refused. He had asked a physician for a poison and the physician had killed himself rather than hand it to Caesar, which the porticoes thought either the noblest or the stupidest thing a Greek ever did, depending on the portico.
