He had bared his own breast to a hunting-servant who knew the killing spot, a Goth or a Jazyge, some northern man kept for the boar, and the man had bolted. A philosopher would have told you a god does not get to choose his hour. The man himself, I think, would have told you that an emperor who could order the death of any man in the world had discovered the one death he could not order was his own, and would have written an epigram on it, because he wrote epigrams on everything, and one of his, four small lines to his own soul, his little wandering pleasant soul, going off now into the cold and the dark, was already going round the city in two translations before the body was cold. Little soul, little wanderer, the guest and companion of the body. I heard it from a wine-seller. Even his dying he made into a thing other men would copy.

It reached me clean and slow and entire, in other words. Not a rumor I had to triangulate off three soldiers' mouths. A death long expected, in a known place, of a known sickness, told by men with nothing at stake in the telling, arriving at the end of a known road. There was nothing in it for me to be afraid of, and I went looking, the way you press a bruise, for the fear, and it was not there.

---

I had spent two decades being careful of that man.

I want to set down what the carefulness had been, because its ending is the strange part, stranger than the fear's not-coming. From the first hour in a working library on the Palatine slope, when he baited me with Strabo to see if I would lie about Thule and repeated my own good line back at me as his own to see if I would let him, I had built my whole conduct around a single read of him: that he kept a ledger, that he forgave nothing he wrote in it, and that the thing he wrote down hardest was any morning a man had made him feel small. I had governed every audience by it. Show him enough to be worth keeping and never enough to be out-known. Let him take your map and call it his thought. Praise the half-egg dome you privately think will crack and pay to watch it not. I had watched what he did to a better man than me, an old engineer with a plumb-line and no give in him, who would not learn to bend and was exiled for it and died out of Rome of nothing anyone would name. I had bent. I had bent so reliably for so long that the bending had stopped being a decision and become a posture, the way a man who has carried a load on one shoulder for years stands crooked when the load is off.

The load was off. That was what I could not get my body to credit, that first afternoon and for some days after. The eye that kept the ledger was shut. The matter of where I was from, which he had told me to my face he would keep and remember, which had sat in his accounting of me for seventeen years like a coin he had not yet decided to spend, was now in no one's keeping. He had died without spending it. He had died, as far as I would ever know, without writing the line about me that I had spent twenty years making sure he would never have cause to write.

And the wariness did not know that. The wariness had nowhere to go and went on standing crooked. I caught myself, walking home along the wharf in the dusk, framing the careful thing I would say if I were called to the Palatine, the small useful truth and the smaller deference, the way you go on chewing after the food is gone. There was no Palatine to be called to. There was a body at Baiae and a will I had not read and a city holding its breath, and I stood at my own gate composing answers to a question no living man was any longer going to ask me, and that, more than the carter's two words, was when the afternoon stopped holding.

Because under the relief, and it was relief, I will not lie about it being relief, there was a thing I had not budgeted for and did not like to look at. He was the only other restless mind I had met in this world. The only one who lived the way I lived inside my own head, never landing, always reaching past the thing in front of him for the thing behind it, building and counting and unsatisfied. We had circled each other for years as two predators circle, each knowing the other could kill him and each finding the other too interesting to do it yet, and there is a kind of company in being hunted by a man who understands you, that you do not get from being loved by men who do not. He had taken my map and made it his and I had let him because letting him was how I lived, and somewhere in the letting there had been a thing close to the pleasure of being read correctly, the only correct reading in two decades, even though the reader meant me no good. Heras had kept me honest and was dead. Apollodorus could have met me as a peer and was dead, and partly of this man. And now this man was dead, and the count of people in this world who could see me whole, friend or enemy, had gone from one to none, and the relief and the grief were the same size and the same shape and I could not get a knife between them.

---

The new wind I read in a week, the way you read any new wind, off the men whose living depends on guessing it before it blows.

He had named the heir already, months back, in the spring, a thing the city had chewed over and largely approved. Not a soldier. Not a young conqueror with a marshal's hunger. A grave, decent, unhurried senator of middle years, rich, well-liked, of no faction anyone feared, a man who had managed a province without scandal and buried two children without theatre and was known chiefly for never having put a foot wrong, which in Rome is a career. Antoninus, they were calling him, and the porticoes liked him for the reason they had been uneasy about the last one: he was not interesting. He would not go anywhere. He had no wall he burned to build in the cold north, no Greek shrine he ached to roof, no shore he meant to stand on with the wind off the unknown. He would stay in Rome and in his villas a day's ride from Rome and govern the small house perfectly kept that the last man had always said he wanted and never once stopped traveling long enough to keep. A man who would die, you felt already, in his own bed, of age, having gone nowhere and lost nothing and refused every adventure that came up the road to him.

I did the cold arithmetic Marcia would have done if I had not, and most of it came out warm. My institutions did not care who wore the purple. The press was a printing-branch of a burial-club and ran on rents I had tied to it with a deed no emperor would bother to untie. The contest was Minerva's now, endowed in the goddess's own name, its old enemies converted into its paid keepers, and a stay-at-home pious emperor was if anything a friendlier sky for a thing dedicated to a goddess than the restless cosmopolite had been. The figures were cut in the public stone and could no more be called back than a river. The patronage fund paid Hermes's heirs and paid Felix to fail at the pot for another term of years off settled capital that asked nothing of any living man's favor. I had spent the back half of my life building precisely the thing a man builds so that an emperor's death cannot reach in and take it, and on the test day it did not reach in. A grave decent emperor would let it alone, and a wicked clever one would have let it alone too, because there was nothing in it any more for a clever man to want from me by force; I had given it all away into structures, on purpose, to be unworth the trouble of seizing.

All of it came out warm but the one thing, and the one thing was the one thing that had ever needed a living emperor's whim, and now there was a different living emperor with a different whim, and his whim went nowhere near the sea.

---

The ocean was his. The dead man's. It had been his prestige from the first hour I had threaded the map into his hunger for being the one who knew the shape of the world, and it had lived these seventeen years on exactly that, on a restless emperor's wish to have done a thing no Caesar had done, the African coast pushed a headland further south each season because he liked to be told it had been, a yard on the Garonne mouth building deep hulls because he liked to think of them, a standing prize for a sea-clock no one had won because the unwon prize itself was a kind of glory, a question Rome had posed that the world had not yet answered. It had never been a war or a colony or a road, a thing that paid. It had been one man's long reach past the edge, funded because the man wanted to be the kind of man who reached.

The new man did not want to be that kind of man. The new man wanted to be the kind of man who balanced the books and mended the roads and did not gamble. I knew, standing at my gate composing answers no one would ask, that within a year or two some procurator going through the dead reign's commitments with a sharp pen and a mandate to stop the bleeding would come to a line that read: a yard in Gaul, building ships that do not return, against a prize no man has claimed, toward a coast no living person has seen, on the word of a barbarian about a map. And the pen would pause, and the procurator would ask whose project it had been, and the answer would be a dead emperor's, which is the answer that ends projects. It would not be killed in a rage. It would lapse. The next season's grant would be smaller and the season after that there would be none, and the hulls would rot on the ways for want of pitch, and Bituitus the yard-master would go back to building grain-ships for men who wanted grain-ships, and the survey would stop at the headland it had reached, and the prize would be quietly let drop, and no one would ever again, in my lifetime, be paid by the empire to ask how far it was to the place I had drawn on a wax tablet to a magistrate forty years ago and regretted drawing the moment the clerk copied it loose.

The road west. The quiet thing I had never quite said out loud even to Marcia, that under the prestige and the survey and the careful talk of knowing the shape of the world, I had wanted there to be a yard, and hulls, and a prize for a clock, because someday a hull might cross, and a thing that could cross to a country I had drawn from memory was the only road I had ever been able to imagine, however far-fetched, to the only country that was mine. I had never believed in it the way you believe in a thing you will do. I had believed in it the way you keep a key to a house you can no longer find. And the man who had paid for the key without knowing what it unlocked was dead, and the man taking his place would melt the key down for the bronze in it, and I found, standing there, that I did not even have the standing any more to argue for it, because to argue for it I would have to be the dead emperor's marvel again, the Trajan-creature, the Hadrian-creature, and I had spent the last twenty years stripping that name off myself so the regimes could not use it against me, and a stripped name cannot ask a new emperor to keep an old emperor's ships.

So I let it go. Not all at once and not cleanly, but I let it go the way you let go of most large things, by going home to a smaller one.

---

Ulpia was at the storeroom table when I came in, with the lamp drawn close, reading the African survey to herself in the low voice she used for figures, the headlands and the soundings I could no longer take by lamp, a coast I had wanted pushed south for forty years and would not see pushed much further now. She was twenty that summer, grave the way her cognomen promised, doing a man's reckoning under a borrowed name because the law would let her do it no other way, and she would go on doing it after I could not, and she would teach it to Chloe, who was learning the cipher fast, and Chloe would teach it to someone I would never meet. The script that held the bottom rungs of the ladder lived in four young heads now and a copy cased in lead, and would outlive the next emperor and the one after that and the yard on the Garonne and me. That had been the wager. That was the wager paying.

She heard me and stopped, and asked, not whether I was well, because she does not ask a question she has the answer to either, she had got that from her mother. She asked whether the western survey went on now, the African coast, whether she should keep the soundings current or close the column. A practical girl. She had understood, faster than I had wanted her to, that an emperor's death is a thing that closes columns.

I told her to keep it current a while yet. Keep it as it stood, in good marks, true to the last headland, so that whoever opened it after both of us, in whatever year, under whatever sky, would find the coast where we had left it and not have to start the chain again from the river-mouth. I could not pay for one more league of it to be sailed. I could pay for the league already sailed to be kept honest and waiting, the way you keep a road graded that no one is traveling, against a traveler.

She wrote down what I told her, in my own marks, and ruled it off, and asked me to check the rule, because her mother had taught her never to trust a tired hand, even her own. I told her the figure stood. Then she carried the leaf with the open columns to the end of the table and set it down face-up, square to the edge the way Marcia squared a tally, and weighted the curling corner with a stone loom-weight out of her mother's basket so the draft off the wharf could not take it in the night, and left it lying there in the dark, true to the last headland, a thing not closed, only laid down.

---

## PART SEVEN — The Long Way Home

---

# Chapter 47: Old

There is a step on the wharf, the third up from the water, where the stone has worn to a dish from two hundred years of men's heels, and that is the step that told me.

I had gone down to the boats for a thing I could have sent Chloe for, a tally of the morning's hides off a barge out of Aquileia, and I went down myself because I had been sitting since the lamp-trim and a man my age stiffens at a bench like a cart left out in the rain. Going down was nothing. The water was bright and the wharf stank the good familiar stink of pitch and fish and wet rope, and I took the count off the bargeman in the new figures and corrected him twice and felt about as clever as I ever feel anymore, which is to say like a man at a party remembering a joke from a country no one in the room has heard of. Then I turned to come up, and at the dished step my chest closed like a fist.

It was not pain, exactly. It was the wind going out of me and not coming back when I called for it. I stood on the worn stone with one hand on the mooring-post and breathed at the air and the air would not fill me, and there was a roaring high up behind my ears, and the bright water went gray at its edges the way a lamp goes when the oil is low. I have seen men die of the chest. I have stood in army camps and watched older legionaries fold quietly at the latrine and never get up, and I had filed it, the way I file everything, under *the heart, or the great vessel off the heart, nothing to be done.* I stood on the step and did to myself what I had done to a thousand frightened soldiers. I told myself the thing I could not prove, which is that it would pass. And it passed. The fist opened. The air came. I went up the rest of the steps one at a time with both hands going post to post like a child on a ladder, and at the top I leaned on the gate of my own yard and waited for my legs to belong to me, and a carter I have known thirty years asked was I well and I said the heat, and it was not hot.

I knew exactly what it was and exactly that I could not touch it. That is the part I had never managed to picture, in all the years of picturing my death. I had given this city water you could drink without it killing your children. I had stood over Celer with clean cloth and boiled water and washed hands and watched the foulness in him win anyway, and learned the floor of what I had, learned there was a wall I could build against the little ones coming in and no door in it for what was already inside. The heart is inside. The years are inside. There is no boiling them out. The man who frightened a whole priesthood claiming to know the invisible could stand on a worn step with his own death rehearsing itself in his chest and do nothing but wait to see whether it was the performance or the real night.

---

The body had been going for years, of course, in the slow way, before the step made it sudden.

The eyes first, long back, so long I have stopped grieving them and only work around them, the work held out at arm's length until my arm tired and then read to me by Ulpia in the flat voice she uses for figures. That was a clean loss, a thing taken all at once a decade gone, and a man can make peace with a clean loss. It is the slow ones that wear you, because they ask you to lose the same thing every morning.

The hands. My hands are cold now in a way that has nothing to do with the weather, cold from the inside, the cold of a stone that the sun cannot reach the middle of, and they ache in the joints in the wet, the knuckles thickened so the old gold ring Marcia gave me will not come past the swell of them and I have stopped trying to take it off. In the morning the fingers will not open straight. I work them against each other under the blanket before I rise, the way you'd work a stiff hinge, until they will hold a stylus, and even then they hold it like a man holding a child's hand, afraid of crushing and afraid of dropping. The letters I make have grown until ten of mine fill a line that once held forty. I cannot write small. That is the plain fact of it, said without weather, and it is the fact that changed how the books got finished.

The teeth go on their own schedule, which is to say badly. I have lost four and the rest are a daily negotiation, and there is no Heras to pull the worst of them clean, only Zoticus, who is careful and good and lacks the thing Heras had, the eye, so that he treats the tooth in front of him very well and never sees the man it is killing. Bread I soak now. Meat I leave. Marcia has the cook do me a barley pap like an infant's and says nothing about it, sets it down by my hand and takes hers and we eat, and she is sixty-eight and her own back has gone crooked from forty years over a tally-board, so we are a fine matched pair of ruins at our own table, and there is a kind of company in that I would not trade for a young body in an empty room.

And the sleep. The sleep is the meanest of them. It will not come when I lie down and it will not leave when the light does. I wake in the black hours certain it is dawn and lie listening to the rats in the warehouse below and the river and Marcia breathing, and the thoughts that come in those hours are the bad accounting, the column of all the men a thing of mine has killed, Sabinus and the burning cloth, Geta's hand, Celer under my hands, the price of captives going down a column I had set the figures for, and there is no skeptic at the other end of the bench to read it back and ask do I know that or only feel it. I have learned the trick of getting up and going to the cold filtered water and drinking it standing in the dark, which does nothing for the sleep but is a thing to do with the hands.

---

So I dictated. There was no help for it.

It went badly at first and then it went well, and the going-well of it was the strangest grace of that whole long last labor. I would sit at the bench with my eyes shut, which I found I could do now without shame, an old man with his eyes shut talking into a quiet room, and Tyche or Ulpia would sit across with the homeland script and a clean leaf, and I would speak the rungs in the homeland tongue, slowly, and they would write them small and true where my hand no longer could.

Tyche is fifty-seven and her hand is faster than mine ever was and steady as a level, and she takes my words and sets them down and stops me. She stops me more than ever. *You said this surer than you wrote it last winter,* she says, and reads me back the old page where I had marked the same thing with the hooked stroke, the little crook that means *the Thulean is guessing here,* and asks which of the two of me she should believe, the one who is tired tonight and wants to sound certain, or the one who marked his own doubt three years ago with a sharp eye. I tell her the younger one. We keep the crook. She has read my secret language for thirty-odd years and I think she does not entirely forgive me for the years she read it as a slave who could be sold for knowing it, and that unforgiveness is part of why she is the best keeper a thing could have. She owes the books no flattery. She will not let me lie to the unborn to spare my old man's pride.

Ulpia takes it differently. She is four-and-twenty and she does not stop me, she questions, after, when the leaf is done, the way you'd test a beam by leaning your weight on it. *You said the push equals the weight times the change in the moving. You said it with a crook. Is the crook for the rule or for your having it?* And I have to tell her the truth, which is that I am sure such a rule exists and unsure I have its true face, that I have the two ends of the rope and a man wiser than I am must find the middle, and she writes that down too, my uncertainty about my own uncertainty, because a true account of what I know includes how I know it. She is better at this than I am. She was raised by Marcia and Tyche to trust no hand, even her own, even her father's, and the law that will not let her keep a counting-house under her own name has made her ferocious about the one thing it cannot bar her from, which is being right.

Between the two of them, in the slack hours of that winter and the spring of my one-and-sixtieth year, the upper pages got down. The lights in the sky as far as I could swear to them and the dark past that marked dark. The little rooms in the blood, written as a story and called a story. The little ones in the water and the wound. The slow changing of creatures over ages that men in my country killed each other over and that I believe and cannot prove. All of it down now, small and true in a hand that was not mine, the bottom rungs oak and the top rungs honestly chewed, and the relief of it was a real thing I could feel in my chest some evenings, a slackening, like setting down a load I had carried so long I had forgotten my arms were holding it. And braided into the relief, the same size, the thing I have learned not to reach for a name for: that the load was knowledge of a country two thousand years from this room, and the only hands I could set it into were the hands of people who would never once know what country it was, who took my dictation for the ravings of a clever old foreigner with a made-up alphabet and loved me anyway and wrote it down because I asked.

---

There was one rung I would not get, and that spring I stopped pretending I would.

The clock. The true clock, the one that ticks the same in summer and winter, the one with the little catch that lets the wheel go one tooth at a time and no faster, that I have wanted since I was nine-and-twenty and had Hermes cut me a set of gears and watched the regulator run down or run wild and never once keep an honest hour. I had carried it forty years. I had told Apollodorus to his face I could not build it, and told Hadrian I could not build it and watched the whole ocean program break against the not-building of it, because a ship cannot find its westing without a clock that keeps true at sea, and a man cannot reach a country he drew from memory without finding his westing. The clock was the lock on the door at the bottom of everything I had wanted for myself, and I have known for years it would not open in my life, and knowing a thing and putting it down are two different acts.

I put it down on a clear cold morning when my hands would barely hold the stylus. I had Ulpia bring me the cipher-book where my failures live, the clock dead-ends among them, the gears that ran wild, Apollodorus's plumb-line maxim that I had her copy in there the year he died, *you draw the picture first and ask the stone to agree.* I had her read me back what I had written about the escapement over the years, which was a great deal of wanting and no answer, page after page of a man circling a thing he could feel the shape of and could not seize, the way your tongue circles the name it has lost. And I told her to write, under the last of it, in the plain teaching voice, not the wanting voice: *I never found this. I believe a small catch can be made that lets a wheel pass one tooth at a measured beat and holds it true against a falling weight or a coiled spring, and that the man who makes it will be able to keep time on a still bench and then, if he is very good, on a moving deck, and that the moving deck unlocks the sea and the sea unlocks the whole western half of the world. I could not make the catch. I have drawn here every wrong shape I tried so you need not waste your years trying them. Begin where I failed. Do not begin where I began.*

She read it back. She asked, because she has Marcia's habit of asking the cost of a thing before she'll sign it, whether I wanted it left in the books at all, since it was a confession of failure in a work I meant to teach by. I told her to leave it. A ladder that hides where its maker fell teaches a man to fall there again. We ruled it off. Then I had her take the loose drawings of the escapement, the wild gears and the failed catches, the forty years of it, the things Hermes had cut for me that I had kept in a box at the bench because I could not throw away the only proof I had ever truly tried, and I told her to give the box to Felix's workshop, to the craft-failure fund, with a note that here was a rung I had not been able to cut and a man should be paid to fail at it for ten years and then another man after him. And she carried the box out, and I sat at the bench with my eyes shut and listened to her go, and did not feel what I had braced to feel. I had thought it would be like a death. It was like setting down the water-cup after Celer stopped needing it. The thing was over and my hands were empty and the emptiness, after the first strangeness of it, was only quiet.

---

Macer is ninety-four and not for this world much longer, and they carried him to my table at the turn of spring because Marcia thought I should see him while there was a him to see.

He is a husk now, the big shoulders gone to bone under the skin, the broken-veined face fallen in around the jaw that was once like a beam, and he sat where they set him and turned the heavy gold ring on his finger with his thumb the way he has turned it for half a century, except now the ring turns loose on the wasted finger and he turns it anyway, the gesture outliving the hand it was made for. His mind comes and goes. For a while he thought I was a steward and asked me sharply about a grain contract on the Rhine that I think was settled before I was born into this world, and I answered him in figures and he grew calm. Then he came back to himself all at once, the way the light comes back when the cloud goes off the sun, and looked at me down the table and knew me, knew everything, the cell and the balloon and the map and the boy he had bought for less than a season's mules, and he said, *You still here, Thulean.* As a question and not a question. And I said I was. And he said, with the old mean dry pleasure that is the last thing in him to go, *Outlived the lot of them, you and me. The clever ones go first.* And turned his loose ring, and went away again behind his eyes, and they carried him home before the meal was over, and Marcia and I sat at the wreck of the table and did not say the thing we both knew about how few more turns of spring there were in him.

---

I will tell you what I do now in the last light of the day, because it is the truest thing I have to set down and it is not a grand thing.

When the writing is done and the keepers have gone home with their leaves and the press below has finished biting its sheets without me, I climb to the flat roof of Caepio's warehouse, which is Marcia's, where she dries her pepper and her rope. I climb it slowly, post to post inside my own chest now, resting on the worn step. I do not go up for the view, though Rome from there at dusk is a thing, the smoke of a hundred thousand suppers and the river going copper and the long shadow of the column I bled for laid across the Forum. I go up because the warehouse roof holds the heat of the day after the wharf has gone cold, and an old man's hands take a long time to warm, and up there the brick under my palms is still warm from a sun that has already set, and I stand with my two cold ruined hands flat on the warm brick and let the day's heat that the stone is letting go come up into me, a borrowed warmth, going out of the brick into the dark whether I take it or not, and I take what I can hold before it is gone, and stay up there with my hands down on the warm stone until the brick is as cold as I am, and then I go down the dark stair by feel, and lie down beside Marcia, and wait to see what the night will be.

---

# Chapter 48: The Subtraction

The world I had built around myself began to come apart from the edges in, the way the cheap wool tunics did, the hem first, a thread at a time, so that you go on calling the thing a tunic long after it has stopped being one.

Pamphilus went that spring, the spring of my three-and-sixtieth year. He had been the first man I ever bought free, with four thousand of Macer's sesterces and Hermes's cold chisel against the rivet of his collar, and he had answered my question about where he would go with another question, *go where,* and stayed at the wharf forty years on real wages and outlived a wife and a child and most of his teeth, and he died in his bed in the room between the storerooms one night without troubling anyone. He was past seventy. There was nothing to say it was but the years. I paid for the burial out of my own purse and not the house's, and could not have told you why it mattered the coin came from me and not Marcia's tally, except that I had bought him once with another man's money and wanted, the second time, to pay with my own.

Eros went the next winter, the letterer, the man who had cut the first crooked Greek Homer into pearwood under my eye and then spent thirty years pulling sheets off the press until his right shoulder hung lower than his left. He had taught Vitalis the cutting before he died, which was the only thing I had asked of him at the end and the only thing he had cared about either, that the boy's lines were clean. So the press did not so much as cough when he stopped. It went on biting its sheets. I stood in the press-room the morning after his pyre and watched the new pressman, whose name I am ashamed to say I had to be told twice, lay a sheet and pull and lay another, and the thing I felt was a cold thing, the machine carrying on across the gap where a man had stood as if the gap were a thing it had been designed to close.

There were others, that stretch of years, the small subtractions, an old carter, the woman who had sewn the first show-bag's seams a lifetime ago and whom I had thought already dead, a clerk of Onesimus's line. You reach an age where the names you have known the longest start going out, one and then a dark stretch and then another, and the room you are standing in gets larger and colder by exactly the light each one took with it, and you stop being surprised, which is its own kind of grief and a meaner one than surprise.

---

Macer did not go out like a lamp. Macer went out like a beam coming down, a thing you have leaned a roof against for so long you have forgotten it was ever a separate object, and then it is on the floor and the roof is on the floor and you are standing in the open with the sky where the dark used to be.

He was ninety-six. Men spoke of it as a freakish thing, an obscenity almost, the way they speak of a flood out of season; the priests of his guild had a word for a man that old that was not quite a compliment. He had outlived three emperors and the use of his own legs and the whole generation of contractors he had cheated and been cheated by, and he had gone on turning the loose gold ring on his wasted finger in a chair by a brazier in his son's house, the son a soft heavy man of sixty who feared him still, and I went to see him in the last of it because Marcia said go and because a man does not refuse Marcia twice in the same season.

The room smelled of leather even then. I do not know how. He had not sold a hide in fifteen years and the house was his son's and reeked of the son's perfume, and under it, faint, banked, the leather, as if the man had soaked in it so long the smell was leaving him slowly. He knew me at once that day, which was not always so anymore. He looked at me down his ruined nose with the broken veins gone gray in the fallen skin and said, *They told me you were sick in the chest.*

I said I had been, in the spring, and it had passed.

*It does not pass,* he said. *It waits.* He turned the ring. *I have had it waiting on me thirty years. You learn to keep it standing in the corner like a creditor you are richer than.* And he laughed, or did the thing that was left of laughing, a dry working of the chest with no sound to it, pleased with himself, pleased to be the one telling the Thulean how a thing worked for once instead of the other way.

We did not say tender things. We had never once in forty-five years said a tender thing and we did not start with him dying, which would have shamed us both. He asked me what the press cleared in a year now and I told him, roughly, in the new figures, and he made me say it twice and did the arithmetic against the cost of the cloth and the wages in his head, the head still doing that when the legs and the bladder and the rest of him had quit, and he said I was paying my pressmen too much, which was true, and that I always had, *and look,* he said, *look where it got you, a warehouse and a wife and your figures on a milestone and not one denarius more than a tight-fisted man would have, you sentimental fool.* He was the man who had owned me. He had bought me for less than a season's mules off a physician's tip, when I could not speak the language well enough to argue the price, and he had penned me in a guarded yard and let me use a peculium that was *not yours, I let you use it as your own,* and he had stood at the wall on the Caelian the day they tried me for my life and spent nothing to save me, and told me later to make myself small and that he would not burn for me. And he had never, in all the years he was afraid, given me up. He could have. There were seasons it would have cost him nothing and bought him a great deal. He let me stand in his atrium and told me to my face he would betray me slow, at an old man's memory, if they pressed him, and then they pressed him, twice that I knew of and likely more I never heard, and the memory stayed slow.

I have tried, since, to make a single clean shape of what I owed him and what he owed me, and I cannot, and I have stopped. He was the cage. He was also the only wall between me and worse cages for the years I needed a wall and could not build my own. A man does not weep for the bars of his cell, and I did not weep, and a man does not entirely fail to feel the cell go either, because the cell is where he learned the language and made the thing and met the wife and lived, in the end, the only life he was going to get. The grief, if it was grief, had no clean edge to take hold of.

He died about a month after, in the chair, in the afternoon, the ring still on him. His son sent word by a slave and I went and stood the correct distance at the pyre, a client at a patron's burning, which is what I legally was to the end, his man, freed past him by an emperor but his man in the rolls and at the rite. There were not many of us. The men who would have stood close were all ash themselves.

The will was the most Macer thing he ever did. He left me the heavy gold ring. Nothing else, no coin, no share, no word of affection for a clerk to read out, just the ring, worth a fair sum in gold, the one he had turned on his thumb when he was thinking for as long as I had known him, which I had assumed without once thinking about it would go to the soft frightened son. The clause, his clerk told me, drafted in Macer's own brutal commercial Latin, ran that the ring should go *to the Thulean, who will know what it cost.* That was all. No name. He had known me forty-five years and put me in his will as the Thulean, the curiosity he had bought cheap and that had made him rich, and that had never once stopped being, to Macer, a barbarian from a place that probably did not exist. And the ring would not go past the swell of my own ruined knuckles either. I could not wear it any more than he could at the end. I had Tyche put it in the box under the floorboard where the dead glass of my first country lies wrapped in oiled wool, two things from two men who never met, one of whom owned me, mine now and no use to anyone, *who will know what it cost.*

---

After Macer the house was running well past me and I knew it.

I would come to the bench in the slack hour with some scheme half-made in my head, a better way to gum the binding so the codices did not split at the spine, and Vitalis, who was thirty now and ran the press as if it were a limb of his own body, would hear me out with the patient face a grown son gives an old father, and then tell me, kindly, that they had tried it two years back when I was abed with the winter cough, and it cracked worse, and the better way was already in use on every sheet in the room. Chloe kept the storeroom day-book in a hand so clean it shamed the clerks of my prime, free now, manumitted out of Marcia's own will against the day, and she read the English cipher better than I could write it. Ulpia had the survey, the African coast laid to the last headland and held there, true and unworked, against a future hand. And Tyche held the whole of it, the cipher and the books and the figures and the thread of the enterprise gathered in her sixty years like reins in a driver's fist, and she did not need me to tell her how to drive.

I had become a passenger in the cart I built. There is a particular humiliation in it that no one warns you of, that the success of a thing you make to outlast you is the exact measure of how little it needs you while you are still alive to watch. I had wanted, since I was a frightened boy writing notes for whoever found his body, to leave a ladder that would stand without me. It was standing. People were climbing it. And I stood at the bottom of it now with my hands too stiff to grip the rung and watched them go up, and was glad, and was the loneliest I had been since the cell, and neither of those cancelled the other.

---

Marcia I had thought would bury me.

She was younger by thirteen years and built like a thing meant to last, square and unhurried, and she had run her own warehouse and then ours through three reigns and a terror and never once been ill that I saw past a day in bed with a flux she beat by force of contempt. She had said it herself, years back in the storeroom, that a thing that is yours dies when you do, and she had meant me, plainly, that I was the perishable one, my head going and my hands following, and she had built the whole succession of the enterprise on the assumption that she would be there to hand it on after me. We both built on it. It was the floor we stood on.

It came on as an ache in the gut that summer, the summer I was sixty-six and she was three-and-seventy, a deep ache that woke her in the night, and then she could not keep her food, and then she went yellow at the eyes and under the nails the way a thing goes that has something gone wrong deep in it where no hand can reach, the kind of slow rot inside that Heras would have named in a breath and shrugged at, because there was no door into it, the same wall I had stood against over Celer, no medicine in this whole world to make. Zoticus came and was careful and good and did the thing Heras taught him, which was to tell it plainly. He told us both, in the storeroom, that it was in the liver, that it would not turn, that it would take some months and they would not be easy, and there were poppy things he could give for the worst of the pain and nothing for the rest.

Marcia heard him out the way she heard a man quote a price she had already decided was too high. Then she asked him how many months, because she had a thing to finish, and he said he could not say, before the cold, likely, and she said that would do, and sent him for the poppy, and went back to the tally-board.

She finished it. It was the deed of trust that moved the warehouse and her own estate clear of Caepio's cousins forever and into the society and the children, the legal wall around the work, drawn so tight no relative and no procurator and no emperor's reaching freedman could pry it loose, and she sat over it with Ulpia reading her the small clauses she could no longer see for the pain and the poppy, and made Ulpia read each twice, and corrected the lawyer's man once on a wording that would have left a finger's-width gap a clever cousin could get a hand into, and closed it. She closed the gap she had spent thirty-five years teaching me to find. Then she let them put her to bed, which she had refused to do until the deed was sealed, and she did not get up again to any purpose.

I sat with her. The one thing I could still do with my ruined hands was sit by a bed and put a cup to a mouth, which I had learned on a road in the east a long way from her, and I did it. She did not want talk. She had never wanted talk. We had been married thirty-five years and most of what passed between us had passed in the medium of work, a tally slid across a table, a contract she would not let me sign, a figure I could not read that she read me in the flat assessing voice that took the world as it was and gave it back at its true weight with nothing added and nothing forgiven. In the bad hours, when the poppy was thinning and before the next dose, she talked a little, and it was never of love, which would have sounded like a man pricing a thing he was afraid of losing. She talked of the house. She told me which of the cousins would try, and how, and which clause would stop them, and that I was not to soften toward the Ennii in the matter of Ulpia, who could marry where she liked or not at all now the deed was hers. She told me Tyche was to be obeyed in everything to do with the money and I was to be humored and ignored, in those words, *humored and ignored,* with the ghost of the old dryness in it, because she knew exactly what I had become in my own house and loved me without one grain of the illusion that I still ran it.

She died before the cold, as Zoticus had said, on a gray morning with the river fog at the window. The breathing went long and shallow toward the end the way Zoticus had told me Heras's had, the spaces between getting wider, and I sat counting them the way you cannot help counting, the arithmetic that is the thing my hands and my head do when the rest of me cannot be trusted, and one space ran out and did not fill, and I went on counting it for a while into the quiet before I understood the count had no more numbers in it.

---

There is a side of the bed.

For thirty-five years there had been a weight on the other side of the bed at night, a warmth, a particular shifting and settling that I had stopped hearing the way you stop hearing the river, a breathing that was the floor under my own bad sleep. The bad sleep was the same after. The black hours and the rats in the warehouse and the river and the bad accounting were all the same. What was gone was the breathing. I would surface in the dark certain it was dawn, the way I always had, and lie there with the old column of the dead running its figures behind my eyes, Sabinus and Geta's hand and Celer and the price of captives, and I would reach, with the worn animal habit of forty years, for the sound of her breathing to set the floor back under it, and there was the river, and there was no floor.

And there were the tallies. She kept the day's loads on a board she would not let the clerks touch, and balanced it last thing and left it by her hand for the morning, and I had a habit, the years my eyes were going and after they were gone, of finding the board she had dropped asleep over and setting it where her hand would fall on it when she woke, because I could not read the figures but I knew the hand, knew the shape of her sevens that did not look like ones, and I would square the board under the dropped stylus by feel and leave it. There was no board now, no hand to drop it and none to find it. I went on, the first nights, half-reaching in the dark for a board that was not there, my fingers patting the empty table by the bed for a thing my body had been doing for years and did not know yet how to stop, and I let them, because telling them to stop felt like a second burial, and they stopped on their own after a while, and that was worse again.

---

I am alone in the house now in the way a man is alone in a house full of other people. Tyche comes most days and runs what I no longer run. Ulpia has the books and the deed and a household of her own forming around her, free of the Ennii. Chloe keeps the day-book. Vitalis runs the press. Zoticus looks in. The children of the children make noise on the storeroom floor. The work goes on without me and the keepers climb the ladder, and I sit at the edge of it, an old foreigner with two relics under a floorboard, the dead glass of a country no one will ever believe in and a dead man's ring that will not fit my hand.

I had thought, when I was young and frightened in this place, that the hardest thing here would be to be the only one who knew. I had not reckoned on this. To be bought and freed and married and surrounded for forty years, and then to come out the far side of it with the work standing and the people gone, and the room around me as large and as cold as the field I woke in.

There is a thing I do now in the slack hour. I have Chloe set the storeroom day-book open on the table before me, and I put my two stiff hands flat on the leaf, on the figures I cannot read, the marks I taught this house and this city and a milestone on the Aurelian road, and I leave them there a while on the cool page. The page gives nothing back. It is only the work, going on, in a hand that is not Marcia's and not mine. After a time I take my hands off it and tell Chloe she may close it, and she closes it, and squares it on the table, and sets the stylus across it for the morning the way someone in this house has always done, and goes home to her own evening, and I sit in the chair until the light is gone and there is nothing left to look at that I could see anyway.

---

# Chapter 49: Securing the Ladder

A man who has spent his life finding the gap a clever cousin could get a hand into does not stop because his eyes have. He only does it slower, and aloud, and with other people's hands.

I had thought, those first dull weeks after Marcia, that the securing was done. The deed was sealed. The press ran. The contest had Minerva's priests for paid watchmen and the patronage fund had its board of three and the books were written nearly to the top of the ladder. I was a passenger. But a passenger can still count, and one night with the old column running behind my eyes I counted the books, where the copies were and were not, and sat up and could not lie back down, because I had built a thing to outlast me and left its only readers in one city, in heads that would also stop.

So I sent for Ulpia in the morning and told her we were not finished.

She had her mother's way of hearing a thing she thought she had already settled, the slight stillness, the held breath. She was two-and-thirty by then and held the books and the deed and the survey and a household forming around her, and she said, in the flat voice that was Marcia's voice come down a generation, "We have three copies and the Latin. You wrote it yourself into the plan."

"I wrote a plan," I said. "A plan is a thing on a leaf. I want to put my hands on each copy before my hands are no use for anything but lying flat on a page."

---

We did it across that winter, the four of us who could read the cipher and I who could no longer see it, in the slack hour after the press shut and before the household ate, in the back of the storeroom where Marcia had finished her deed.

There were the three full copies and the partial Latin, and I made each of them be brought and read to me, a passage at random, so I would know with the only sense I had left that the marks on the leaf were the right marks and not a fair clean copy of a wrong one. Tyche read me a page of the second working, the long division with the remainder drawn as an *r*, and I stopped her on a carry she had set a column wide, and she looked, and it was hers that was right and my ear that was old, and she let me hear in her silence exactly how often that was now the case.

Tyche's copy was the working one, the mother of the rest, and it did not stay in one place. It moved as it had always moved, the way she had moved the box through the bad years of the terror, a thing in oiled wool that lived now under the floor of one room and now another, known to her and to Chloe and to no clerk. It was the living text. When a child of the keepers found a clearer Latin word for a rung, or when one of them caught a thing of mine that was wrong, the correction went first into Tyche's copy and then out to the others at the year's turn, the way a temple keeps a true measure against which the market measures are checked.

The society's copy was sealed. It went into the collegium's chest under the same seal as the deed, lodged in law, a thing the burial-club's own rules forbade them to open except in the presence of three of the board, so that no single keeper's death and no single keeper's treason could lose it or sell it. It was the copy that existed on paper that it existed. A magistrate could be shown the chest. He could not be shown inside it without the three, and the three would never all be one man's creature. Marcia had taught me that shape. I had only to apply it to the thing that mattered most.

The third copy was the one for the dark.

I had Vitalis case it. He was the right hand for it, a press-man with a wood-carver's father in him, and he made the thing the way you make a thing you mean to forget on purpose. The leaves were waxed against the wet and folded into oiled hide and that into a vessel of lead, the seams run with pitch and the whole sealed and weighted, so a man digging a foundation a hundred years on, or three hundred, would strike it and pry it and find inside a fat case of strange marks he could not read and would either burn for a witch's leavings or carry, if the gods were kind, to someone who would sit with it. We argued where to put it. We put it under the cold floor of the press-room itself, beneath the stone the great screw is bedded in, because the press is the one part of all of it I could not see going away. A man may forget a tomb. He does not forget the machine that feeds him, and where a press stands men come back to lay another, and where they dig they find.

And the partial Latin, up through the geometry, that one I did not hide. That one I let breathe. It went into the printed library off the Argiletum among the codices, a strange treatise by the Thulean, mathematics and the four workings and the figures and the lines and angles, all of it true and all of it tellable, with the cipher's deeper rungs left out of it on purpose. A man who climbed the Latin to the top of the geometry and wanted the next rung would find at the head of the next blank page a note, in plain Latin, that the rest was written and kept and could be sought. The Latin was the open door. It said, *there is a stair, and it goes higher, and the way up is not lost.* It did not say in whose floorboard. It only had to make a hungry man ask.

---

The honest marks I made them keep in every one.

This was the thing I would not bend on, and the only thing in the whole securing that Tyche enforced harder than I did. A rung I was sure of got no mark. A rung I had built but doubted got the hooked stroke, the little dragging tail off the last letter that meant *the Thulean is guessing here, prove it before you stand on it.* And the rungs I did not have at all, the joining of heat to motion that I had owned once and lost, the far rooms of the planets, the way the small pieces of a thing fasten to each other to make a new thing, those stayed white. Blank, with a note across the white in a hand large enough that even I could nearly see it, that the rung was missing, that I had not faked it, that the climber must build it himself and not look for it here and fall.

Chloe read me one of those blank-page notes, the chemistry one, the year we sealed the copies. She read it the way she read everything, fast and clean and without weighing it, and then she stopped, and she said, "You could fill it. Some of it. You could guess and we would never know it was a guess."

"You would," I said. "Tyche taught you the mark. The mark is so you would."

"A future man would not."

"A future man is the whole point," I said. "A faked rung does not break in the room where it is faked. It breaks under the weight of the one who trusted it, a hundred years up, where no one I will ever meet can catch him." I had her read the note again, and the hooked stroke before it, and I made her promise me the thing I had already made Tyche promise and Ulpia promise and would make Vitalis promise, that the blanks stayed blank and the hooks stayed hooked through every copying, world without end, that no kind hand smoothed my doubt into a lie to make the book look finished. A finished-looking book is a beautiful thing. It has killed more climbers than any honest gap.

---

The keepers I confirmed the way you check a rope you mean to hang a life on, hand over hand, end to end.

Tyche was five-and-sixty, and old, and the oldest rung of the chain herself, and the danger I had sat up over was that the chain still bent back to her in too many places. She knew it before I said it. She had said it to me years gone, *you put its only key in one aging head, mine,* and we had answered it once with the children, Ulpia and Vitalis and Chloe, four young hands for one old one. But four hands in one city in one generation is still a thing a fire or a plague or a bad emperor can take in a season, and I wanted the chain to run past all of us, down into hands neither of us would live to shake.

So we wrote the chain into the thing as a rule, not a hope. The collegium's deed bound it: that the cipher should be taught, always, by a keeper to no fewer than two, that no keeper should be the last to know it, that a keeper who held it and taught it none was in breach. Vitalis was bound already to teach two block-cutters their craft; we bound him to teach two the script besides. Chloe, free now, took a girl of the storeroom and a boy of the press under her, the way Heras had once said of her, *keep her in figures,* and she kept them. The thing would not narrow to a point again. It would fan, downward, into the workshop and the press and the trade, into hands that came up through the craft and not through my blood, into a Felix not yet born who would one day read a strange treatise in a portico, and want the next rung, and go asking.

Lucanus would not be a keeper. I had long stopped grieving it. He had the law and the basilica and his unbearded citizen face, and he wanted nothing of the workshop or the script, and his line, if he had one, would climb the cleaner ladder that I could never climb, the one whose lowest rung was being born free. I did not begrudge him the door I had been shut out of. The ladder I had built was for whoever was hungry, and it would not check a man's birth before it let him up. That was the whole quiet argument of the thing, and I never once said it aloud to him, and he never once asked.

---

I sent for Lucanus on the matter of the law, because it was the one thing in the securing that wanted his trade and not mine.

He came in his advocate's clothes from the Basilica Julia, eight-and-thirty and stiff with me the way a son is stiff who has measured the exact length of the chain his father's name puts on him. We did not warm to each other in an hour. But he sat at the storeroom table where his mother had drawn her deed, and read the deed of trust through with the eye of a man trained to find the gap, and found two.

He found that the board of three could, by the deed's own wording, be filled by men a future procurator might lean on, and we tightened it, so that one of the three must always be a keeper of the cipher and one a master of the press, men with the work in their own hands and not the favor in their purse. And he found that the contest's endowment to Minerva, that beautiful trap of Marcia's that turned the priests into the contest's paid guards, could be undone by a priest who stopped believing the goddess was owed, and we could not tighten that one, because faith is the one wall you cannot draft, and we left it, with the gap named, the way I left a rung blank in the books. Some things you secure. Some things you only confess you could not.

He drew the new wording himself, in his fine basilica Latin, and his hand on the leaf was a freeborn citizen's hand setting in law a thing his freedman father had built, and neither of us said anything about that either. He asked me, before he rolled it, where the share of the ring was to go, the gold Macer left me, the worth of it. I told him to put the worth of the ring into the patronage fund, into Felix's failing pot, so that a dead contractor's gold would pay a free smith to fail at steel for ten more years, which would have made Macer call me a sentimental fool, which it was. The ring itself was not in the deed. The ring was a private matter, and I had not yet settled it, and there was a thing I had to do with the ring and the glass beside it that I would do with only one other soul in the world.

---

I sent the rest of the household home early the night I settled the box, and kept Tyche.

We sat in the room over the floorboard, the two of us, an old man and a woman not much younger, the only two living souls who had ever known the box was there or what the box held. She had kept it through the terror when she would not tell even me where it was. She had laid Macer's ring into it the spring of the will, beside the dead glass, and nailed the board down over the both of them. There was no one else I could do this with. There was no one else who would not need it explained, and the thing could not be explained, and that was the whole of why it had to go the way it was going to go.

She pried the board up without my asking and lifted the box out and set it on the table between us and worked the oiled wool back, and there it was. The dead glass. Black and smooth and cold, the thing I had carried in my hand the morning I woke in a stubble field with a city two thousand years too young at the bottom of the valley, the thing that had held my mother's number it could no longer call and a picture of the Colosseum new, and that had gone dark in a loft on the fourth night and never woken, and that I had kept, wrapped in wool, for fifty years, the one object in this world that was proof, if proof could be read off it, of where I was truly from. The ring lay against it, gold against dead glass, two things from two men who never met. And under them the citizenship tablet, *Marcus Ulpius Danihel,* the name a dead emperor's law had made me, the proof of the country I had been forced to become.

Tyche did not touch the glass. She never had. She looked at it on the table the way you look at a thing you have guarded longer than you have understood it, and she said, "You have decided."

"I have decided."

"You will tell me the deciding," she said, "or you will not, but you will not pretend to me there was no deciding. I have known you fifty years. You do not handle this thing in the open with anyone."

So I told her. Not the country. I had told her, over the decades, in slips and half-sentences, a *back home,* an *at home,* a smooth word for a kind of weather Rome did not have, enough that she had built her own true picture and was too good a person to make me confirm it. I told her the deciding. The books go on, I said. The books are the gift. Every rung of them is a thing a man can be told and use, the figures and the workings and the lines and the laws and the honest blanks where I ran out, and they will stand in three copies and a lead case and an open door off the Argiletum, true and tellable forever. And this, I said, and I put one stiff finger on the cold glass and did not pick it up, this cannot be told. There is no rung under it. A man who is shown it cannot climb to it. It is the one thing I carry that is only a danger to whoever holds it, a witch's relic, a thing that would get you burned or get you taken apart by men who wanted to know how it was made and could not be told because the making of it is a thousand years of rungs not yet built. The figures I can leave to the world. This I can only leave to the ground.

"Bury it with me," I said. "In the wrappings. No one opens the box after. The ring goes back to its worth in the fund, as I told Lucanus, and the gold itself can melt into a smith's hearth where it does some good. The tablet goes with the glass, both of them, into the wrappings, into the ground. The two papers of where I am from, the false one Caesar made and the true one I cannot prove, they go down together where they cannot do any harm and cannot say anything to anyone, and the part of me that can speak stays up here in a book a child can read."

Tyche was quiet a long time. The lamp wanted oil and I did not call for it.

"You could give it to me," she said at last. "I have kept it fifty years. I could keep it fifty more, if the gods were absurd about it, and then it would be someone's to keep after."

"And what would you tell them it was?"

She did not answer that, because there was no answer, and we both knew there was no answer, and the not-answering was the closest we ever came in fifty years to saying the whole thing plain. She had carried the weight of the secret almost as long as I had, the only one who had, and she was telling me she would carry it still, to spare me the ground, and I was telling her no, that the carrying ended with the carrier, that I would not hand her or any child after her a thing that could only ever be a danger and never once be a rung.

"Then it goes down," she said, "and the work stays up," and she folded the wool back over the glass and the ring and the tablet the way she had folded it ten thousand times, her hands sure where mine were not, and she did not nail the board this time. She set the box back into the floor and laid the board loose over it, because the next time it came up would be the last time, and there would be other hands than ours for that, and we both knew whose, and we did not say it.

---

We sat after, with the lamp guttering, and did not talk of it again.

She told me instead what the press had cleared that quarter, in the new figures, the way Macer used to make me say it twice, and I made her say it twice, and did the old arithmetic in my head against the wages and the cloth, the head still doing that one thing clean when the eyes and the hands had quit, and I told her she was paying her pressmen too much, which was true, and which I had taught the whole house to do, and she laughed the dry working laugh of someone who has heard a man's only joke for fifty years and finds it funny anyway because it is his.

When she left I sat on alone in the dark over the loose board. There was nothing left to secure. The rope was checked end to end. My mind had let go of the glass under the floor already and gone a quarter-mile off to the lead case under the cold press-room stone, the strange marks folded in their oiled hide and their pitch and their lead, lying there patient as a seed under the one machine men always come back to, waiting for a foundation-digger not yet born to strike it with his spade and stop, and pry, and carry it up into the light to someone who would sit with it and begin to climb.

---

# Chapter 50: Going

The body goes the way the press went when a bearing wore out, not all at once and not in the part you were watching. You oil the screw and the frame cracks. You guard the heart and it is the lungs that fill, slow, over a season, so that by the autumn I could climb the stair to the room over the loose board only if I stopped at the turn and held the wall and let the air come back in its own time, which it did, and then later did not, fully, ever again.

I had thought, when the wind first caught me on the wharf step three winters back, that I knew which door it would be. I was wrong about that the way I have been wrong about most of the timing in my life. It was not the heart that stopped me. It was the breath. The water came up under it. Zoticus, who was Heras's pupil's pupil and had Heras's flat refusal to lie to me, listened with his ear to my back the way I had taught Heras's whole line to listen, and said the wet was in the bottom of both, and that it would rise, and that he could give me the poppy when I wanted it and could give me nothing for the rising itself, because no one could, because the rung was not built, was not anywhere near built, and would not be built for a length of time he could not have counted and I would not have told him.

So we did not pretend. That was the one decent thing left to do with it, and we did it.

---

They moved my bed down to the storeroom in the last weeks, because I could not do the stair, and because I asked for it, though I did not say why I asked.

It was the driest room on the wharf. Marcia had chosen it for the pepper and the hides forty years before, and finished her deed in it, and it smelled still of rope and old vellum and the river coming through the wall, and the table where she had drawn the trust stood against the far side with the day-book on it that I could no longer read. I wanted to be near the work. I told Ulpia that was the reason and it was a true reason and it was not the whole of it. The press ran on the other side of that wall. I could feel it through the floor when I lay still, the long shudder of the screw coming down and the lift of it coming up, fifty times a page and a page in the time it took me to take a few of my bad breaths, a thing in the world that did not need me to make it move and went on moving, and I lay with my hand flat on the boards to take it the way an old man takes the heat off a brick.

The mind held longer than the breath. That was the cruelty of the particular door I was given. For a while there were whole afternoons clear as the wharf in summer, where I could do the arithmetic of the quarter in my head while Tyche read me the figures, the head doing its one clean thing while the rest of me drowned by inches. And then there were the other afternoons, later, when the room went strange and the Latin would not come and I reached past it, in the dark behind my eyes, for the words of the other tongue, the first tongue, the one I had not spoken aloud to a living soul in fifty-seven years, and found that those were going too. I had a song once. I had a great many songs once. I went looking for one, the way you go looking through a chest you have not opened, and found three words of it and a shape where the rest had been, and could not say whether the tune I put under the three words was the true tune or one I was making up on the spot to cover the hole, and there was, as there had been for a very long time, no one in the room or in the world I could ask.

The things that did not come went the same way as the song, in pieces, with the shape left where the thing had been. The heat made to turn a wheel without a horse or a slave hung on it. The little teeth that would have caught and let go and caught and let go and kept the hours true, that I drew a thousand ways and never once made stand still. The glass that should have shown the small living ones swimming and only ever showed a smeared rainbow and a lie. A great hull driven west across the blank water on no wind, to a coast I drew before I had any business drawing it. A wire with the lightning tamed and running quiet down the length of it into every house in a city, lighting the dark, which I could see whole and bright in the failing room and could not have so much as begun, not the first rung of it, not in five lifetimes of the body I had left. They came and stood at the foot of the bed, all of them, the way the unbuilt dead stand, and I had nothing to say to them either. I had moved what I could reach. The grain, the figures, the clean water, the press going through the wall. The rest stood at the foot of the bed.

---

Ulpia was there most of the days. She had her mother's face by then and her mother's way of doing the necessary thing without making a scene of it, and she sat at Marcia's table with the books and did the work where I could hear her stylus, which I think she understood was a kindness, the sound of it, the small scratch of the figures going down.

She read to me from the upper rungs in the evenings, in the homeland tongue, in the cipher I could no longer see, the chemistry passage with the blank under it and the note across the blank, the long passage on the lines and the angles, the workings, the figures her grandfather's clerks had called shopkeeper's marks. She read it flat and clean and asked her questions after, the way she always had, and some of the questions I could still answer and some I could not, and on the bad evenings I could not tell which was which, and she did not let me know when I had answered the wrong thing. She only set it down and asked the next. She was keeping the honesty even there, in the dark, at the end, the way I had made the whole house promise to keep it: she would not smooth a wrong answer of mine into a right one to comfort a dying man, because a comforted dying man is worth nothing to a climber a hundred years up. I had built that into her when she was a child and now it was being done back to me and it was the right thing and it was cold and I was glad of it.

She asked me once, on a clear evening, where I had learned the workings. She had asked me a hundred times across the years, in a hundred ways, the way they all had, and I had given them all the same shaped non-answer for fifty-seven years, the cold far north, the place that may not exist, the long way nobody came back along. She did not ask it the old way that evening. She asked it gently, knowing I was going, giving me the door to put down a thing she could see I had carried, and I lay there and looked at the shape of her against the lamp and wanted, with everything that was left, to give it to her. To say it once. To have one person in the world hold the true weight of it before the weight went into the ground with me.

I told her the cold north. I said the long way. I gave my own daughter the lie I had given the magistrate the second morning, because the truth had no rung under it and would only frighten her or shame her or get her looked at sideways in the basilica where her brother worked, and because there are gifts you cannot give a person you love, the box being one and this being the other. She heard the old answer and she knew it for the old answer and she let it lie, the way her mother had, the way Tyche had, all of them too good to make me say it and none of them ever told. She put her hand over my hand on the boards. Her hand was warm and the boards were warm under it where the press came through and I shut my eyes and let her think I slept.

---

Lucanus came on the rest-days from the basilica, in his good toga, three-and-forty and gray at the temples and stiff with me to the last, though the stiffness had gone soft the way a rope goes soft, frayed rather than cut.

We had never warmed in an hour and we did not warm in these. But he sat the wrong way on the stool, the way he had sat the wrong way on everything since he was a boy, and he told me the business of the courts in his fine careful Latin, the cases, the men, the long Roman game of obligation he played better than I ever had because he had been born holding the right end of it, born free where I was made, his whole clean life lived up a ladder I was shut off the bottom of. He brought me a thing once from a market stall without saying why he had brought it. It was a child's clay toy, a little smoking-stack cart on four wheels, a Thulean engine, the thing the contest had set Rome's children dreaming of forty years before, the cart that ran on its own fire and was never built and would not be built in his lifetime or his children's, and he set it on the table by the day-book and turned it once with one finger and watched the painted wheels go round and said nothing, and I said nothing, and it was the nearest the two of us ever came.

I had wanted other things for him once. I had wanted, for a while, when he was small, to make him the keeper, to put the script and the books into my own blood so that the one person who carried it all the way down would be mine. He had refused it the way you refuse a coat that is the wrong shape for you, without cruelty, simply taking up his own life instead, the law and the standing and the free birth, and I had grieved that for years and then stopped, because the ladder I built was never going to ask a climber his father's name before it let him up, and it would have been a poor thing if it asked it first of my own son. So the books went down the workshop line and not the blood line, to a Felix not yet born who would one day stand in a portico off the Argiletum and read a strange treatise and want the next rung. And my son sat the wrong way on a stool and turned a clay toy with one finger and we did not speak of any of it, and it was, in the end, enough, or as close to enough as a man gets.

---

It was Tyche who was there at the night of it. Of course it was Tyche.

She was seventy and slower than she had been and her own eyes had begun to go, and she came in the deep of the night when Chloe sent the storeroom boy running, because Chloe had been sitting the watch and heard the breathing change. The breathing had been changing for days. This was a different change. The wet had risen to where I could not lie flat at all and they had me propped on the bolsters Marcia used to pile against the small of her back, and I was working at the air the way you work a stiff pump, drawing it and getting half of it, and there was a sound in it I had heard before, once, on a frontier, on a green-willow basket coming down out of the sky in fire, a sound I would not write then and could not have stopped my own chest making now if I had wanted to.

Tyche sat where Ulpia sat. She took my hand off the boards and held it in both of hers, which were cold, both of ours cold, the two oldest things in the room. She did not tell me it would be well. She had never once in fifty years told me a thing that was not so to make me feel better, and she did not start at the end. She held my hand and she said the figures of the quarter, low, in the new numbers, the way I had made her say them twice, and she did the arithmetic against the wages and the cloth out loud where I could follow it, because it was the one thing my mind could still take hold of, the one rope still strung end to end across the dark, and I followed it down, the columns, the carry, the remainder drawn as a small *r*, all the way down to the bottom of the page where it came out clean.

She was paying the pressmen too much. I could not get the breath to tell her so. She knew I was thinking it. She said, against my ear, that I had taught the whole house to overpay, that it was the most expensive lesson I ever gave them and they kept it on purpose, and she made the dry sound that had stood in for her laugh for fifty years, and I think I made a sound back. I meant it to be a sound back.

---

It is hard to say now where the order of it went.

There was the room and the lamp and the wet sound and Tyche's two cold hands, and then the room was thinner, the way the show bag went thin and pale when you held it up against the sun and saw the light come through the weave, and I was lighter, the way the threadbare feed-sack went light on its string over a boy's head in a slaughter-lot, rising while the heat held, and I knew even then, the part of me that always knows, that the lightness is not staying up, the lightness is only the lift, the staying up is the fight and there is no more heat to feed it. So I was going down. I have always known the difference between rising and not falling. It was the one thing I learned before anything else.

The press is going through the floor. I can feel it in the hand she is holding, the long shudder down and the lift up, a page in the time of a few bad breaths, the screw Vitalis beds in the stone with the lead case patient underneath it, the marks folded in their hide in the dark, waiting, a thing that does not need me, that has not needed me in years, going on and on against my back. Tyche's thumb is moving on the back of my hand the way you smooth a leaf flat before you write on it. She is saying the figures still. I have lost the figures. I have the sound of the figures.

There is grain. There is a field of cut grain in the early light and the stubble of it pale and sharp and the dew not yet off it, gold going gray going gold as the sun comes up the valley, and the cold of the ground coming up through the boots, the good boots I bought with my first paycheck, and far off down the long slope a great pale ring of new stone catching the first of the light, and the thing warm and smooth and heavy in my hand that will not call anyone, that has a picture in it of this very ring when it was new, and the four men coming up the rise with their spears low and the lead one watching my hands, and I am seventeen, and I am frightened, and there is no one, in the whole turning world, who knows where I am.

Tyche's hands are warm. I was wrong about that. They were cold before and now they are warm. The grain is warm. The light is coming up the valley and the breath is shorter than the light and the next one is

---

# Chapter 51: A Hand Not His

GAIUS NORBANUS RUFUS TO HIS FRIEND SEXTUS CAECILIUS PRISCUS, GREETING.

You ask me, with the studied carelessness of a man who has already decided his own opinion, what I made of the printed library in the portico off the Argiletum, and whether the Thulean was the prodigy the stall-keepers swear he was or merely the most successful charlatan of our fathers' age. I have let your letter sit a market-day on my table while I turned the question, and I find I can give you both answers and neither, which is the only honest report a man can make of a thing everyone already knows.
